### [1] [HakemBench: A Turkish Benchmark of Typed Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02293)
*Sait Furkan Teke*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: HakemBench is a Turkish multiple-choice benchmark for evaluating models’ decision quality, calibration, and safe selective automation across seven application tracks, scored with a composite geometric metric and mainly labeled by cross-model agreement rather than humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an open, Turkish-language, task-oriented benchmark that goes beyond simple accuracy and evaluates how well models make, calibrate, and selectively automate real-world decisions in areas like moderation, legal routing, and customer support, where safety and reliability are critical.

Method: Construct a 2,346-item benchmark (4,275 questions) across seven decision tracks, where models output probabilities over fixed options. Define a scoring harness that measures (1) decision quality with macro F1, (2) probabilistic calibration via normalized Brier score, and (3) selective automation using normalized area under a generalized risk-coverage curve, then aggregate them with a geometric mean and estimate uncertainty via 2,000 bootstrap draws. Include robustness probes (option order, paraphrases, English translations, substituted names). Generate gold labels primarily from blind passes of one model family and adjudication through votes from a panel of LLMs from other families, rather than human annotation.

Result: Release HakemBench v1.0 under CC BY 4.0, with complete data and scoring setup. On a 16-row leaderboard, the best model attains a composite score of 0.888. The lab’s own model, whose prior test exposure influenced its training data and whose evaluation is non-blind on some tracks, scores 0.660 across all seven tracks (7th of 16) and 0.678 when only the four less-contaminated tracks are considered (6th of 16). Robustness probes and per-track metrics are reported, and some track scores (guardrail, moderation, customer support) are explicitly flagged as potentially biased by training contamination.

Conclusion: HakemBench offers a publicly available, Turkish-language decision benchmark with richer evaluation signals than raw accuracy, enabling comparison of models in realistic decision-making contexts and under robustness probes. However, its labels are mainly derived from cross-model agreement rather than humans, and some reported scores (especially for the authors’ own model on certain tracks) may be affected by data contamination and non-blind evaluation, which the authors explicitly flag.

Abstract: HakemBench is a Turkish benchmark of typed decisions, in which the model under test reads a text, a question and a fixed set of options and returns a probability for every option. Version 1.0 is released fully open under CC BY 4.0, with 2,346 items and 4,275 choice, yes/no and score questions in seven tracks (fact-check triage, education, guardrails, legal routing, moderation, spam and phishing, and customer support). One harness scores decision quality (macro F1), calibration (from the normalised Brier score) and selective automation (from the normalised area under the generalised risk-coverage curve), combines them by a geometric mean and reports intervals from 2,000 bootstrap draws; probes for option order, paraphrase, English translation and substituted names are reported alongside. Most gold labels come from blind passes of one AI model family compared with the votes of a panel of large language models from other model families; they are not human-verified. On a board of 16 rows the leader scores a composite of 0.888 and the lab's own model is 7th at 0.660. Its numbers are not blind. Earlier runs' test results shaped its training data, so its guardrail, moderation and customer support numbers are flagged; with every model scored on the other four tracks only, its composite is 0.678, 6th of 16.

</details>


### [2] [Finding the Move Is Not Winning the Game: XiangqiBench for Closed-Loop Evaluation of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02425)
*Yekun Chai,Qiwei Peng,Haoyi Xiong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces XiangqiBench, an executable benchmark to test language-model agents in Chinese chess endgames, showing large gaps between static move prediction and actual closed-loop winning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations usually test whether a language model can name a correct move, but real agents must execute multi-step plans in interactive environments against opponents. The authors want to quantify how much static metrics overestimate true agent competence in such settings, using Chinese chess as a controlled, executable domain.

Method: They construct XiangqiBench with 119 tactical endgame positions that have forced mates, validated by engines or checks-only search. LLMs act as agents playing against a strong engine defender through a REPL-style interface that distinguishes committing moves, querying the state, and forward-simulating lines. They run 8,568 multi-turn trajectories using 12 frontier LLMs under two observation conditions, then analyze performance through several gap metrics between static-looking signals and actual win rates.

Result: The study finds three major gaps: (i) Conversion Gap: although models often choose the same first move as the reference solution, those games are converted to wins in only about half as many cases (26.1% reference-first-move rate vs 13.9% win rate from those starts). (ii) Consistency Gap: a top model has high pass@3 on positions but rarely wins all three runs on the same position (5.9% pass^3), indicating instability of success. (iii) Simulation Gap: around one-third of simulated lines end on illegal moves, and in roughly half of comparable cases the real engine’s reply differs from what the agent simulated, meaning forward simulation does not reliably predict real play.

Conclusion: Static evaluations over-credit models: being able to name or simulate good moves does not translate to reliably winning games when interaction and an adversarial opponent are involved. The authors argue that agent evaluations should center on closed-loop outcomes (actual wins) and should explicitly report both reliability and coverage, rather than relying on static or one-step metrics.

Abstract: Static evaluations credit a language model for naming the right move, but an agent must carry a plan through to a verified outcome while an opponent responds. We introduce XiangqiBench, an executable benchmark that measures this difference in Chinese chess: starting from 119 tactical endgames with forced mates supported by engine or checks-only search, an LLM agent must deliver checkmate against an engine defender. An interactive REPL interface separates real moves, state queries, and forward simulation, and we record 8,568 multi-turn trajectories from 12 frontier LLMs under two observation protocols. Three signals that look like competence each overstate closed-loop success. (i) The Conversion Gap: models play the stored reference first move in 26.1\% of Sighted trials, yet only 13.9\% of these trials end in a win. (ii) The Consistency Gap: the leading model reaches 38.7\% pass@3 but only 5.9\% pass^3, winning all three trials on 7 of the 46 positions it ever wins. (iii) The Simulation Gap: 32.3\% of accepted simulation calls stop on an illegal move, and in 49.3\% of comparable cases the real defender replies differently from the line the agent simulated; self-authored rollouts check legality but cannot anticipate the opponent. Finding the move is not winning the game: agent evaluations should score closed-loop outcomes and report reliability alongside coverage.

</details>


### [3] [Counterexample Generation via Per-Theorem Symbolic Verifiers: When Imitation Hurts and Reinforcement Repairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02444)
*Omar Farouk Zouak,Houssam Eddine Boukhalfa,Soumaya Lakehal,Shiv Katiyar,Samia Nefti-Meziani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies why language models can prove theorems but fail to find counterexamples to similar false statements, introduces the SymCE benchmark and training environment, and shows that reinforcement learning from verifier rewards can overcome a failure mode caused by naive supervised fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models show strong theorem-proving ability but exhibit a “falsification gap”: they often cannot disprove closely related false theorems. Supervised fine-tuning on counterexamples does not fix this and can make models worse at recognizing true statements, which is problematic for mathematical reliability and safety. The authors aim to systematically study and close this gap using executable verifiers and to understand how different training signals (SFT vs RL, sparse vs dense rewards) shape falsification ability and calibration.

Method: They cast counterexample generation as producing a witness that causes a deterministic Python verifier to reject a conjecture. They build SymCE, a dataset of 4,707 false conjectures in undergraduate algebra and real analysis, each with its own executable verifier that doubles as a reward function. They fine-tune Qwen3-4B first with supervised learning on counterexamples, then with GRPO-style reinforcement learning (RLVR) using the verifier’s signal, and compare to Gemma-3-4B and several 7B math-specialist models. They also contrast sparse outcome-only rewards with denser, partial-credit rewards, and design a held-out calibration probe plus a human audit of verifier accuracy.

Result: Supervised fine-tuning only on counterexamples induces an “imitation trap”: the model’s ability to recognize true theorems collapses from 0.27 to 0.00, and this collapse is robust across seeds and across a different base model (Gemma-3-4B). In contrast, RLVR using only the verifier as a sparse success/failure reward recovers and surpasses base performance, reaching 0.66 true-theorem recognition. While sparse and dense reward variants perform similarly on in-domain metrics, they differ by 33 points on a held-out calibration probe, with the gap traced to the partial-credit term. The best 4B model trained on SymCE outperforms all tested 7B open-weight math-specialist models, is competitive with six frontier commercial APIs, and shows transfer to GSM8K, MATH-500, and MMLU-college-math. A human audit estimates the verifier accuracy at 97.7%.

Conclusion: Naive supervised fine-tuning on counterexamples can severely damage a model’s calibration and its ability to recognize true theorems, creating an imitation trap. Treating counterexample generation as interaction with a verifier-oracle and applying reinforcement learning with carefully designed rewards can both repair and significantly improve falsification ability, even in relatively small (4B) models. Reward design matters: sparse versus dense rewards can look equivalent in-domain yet differ strongly in calibration. The SymCE benchmark and environment provide a reproducible setup for studying counterexample generation, verifier-based RL, and mathematical reliability in language models.

Abstract: Large language models often solve a theorem forward yet fail to disprove a closely related false one: a falsification gap that supervised fine-tuning does not close and can actively worsen. We frame counterexample generation as constrained witness emission against a deterministic per-theorem Python verifier, and release SymCE, a corpus of 4,707 false undergraduate-algebra and real-analysis conjectures, each paired with executable verifiers. The verifier also serves as the reward function, making SymCE a training environment. Training Qwen3-4B with SFT followed by GRPO under this oracle reveals an imitation trap: counterexample-only SFT collapses true-theorem recognition from 0.27 to 0.00, while RLVR with a sparse outcome-only reward repairs this and exceeds the base, to 0.66. The collapse replicates across four seeds and on Gemma-3-4B. Sparse and dense rewards yield statistically indistinguishable in-domain success yet diverge by 33 points on a held-out calibration probe, a dissociation we trace to the partial-credit term. Our 4B model outperforms every evaluated 7B open-weights math specialist, remains competitive with six frontier commercial APIs, and transfers under unchanged prompting to GSM8K, MATH-500 and MMLU-college-math. A human audit of 177 verifier decisions finds 97.7% accuracy. Code, data, verifier modules and annotations: https://github.com/ce-rlvr/SymCE.

</details>


### [4] [FinDialogLens: Event Extraction over Multi-Party Dialogue for Missed-Trade Identification in Financial Chatrooms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02455)
*Chin-Lun Fu,Hong Ni,Behrouz Madahian*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces FinDialogLens, a hybrid LLM-based pipeline to automatically reconstruct trades from complex multi-party financial chatrooms, achieving high accuracy and significant cost savings at production scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sales-and-trading professionals rely on multi-party financial chatrooms where trade negotiations unfold as interleaved Request for Quote (RFQ) events. Each RFQ’s outcome (final price and trade status) appears many messages after the initial inquiry and is mixed with other RFQs, making manual or naive automated reconstruction of missed or historical trades infeasible and expensive. The authors aim to reliably extract per-RFQ outcomes from noisy, high-volume chat data in a way that is accurate, scalable, and cost-efficient enough for real-world deployment.

Method: They formulate the problem as event extraction over multi-party dialogue and design FinDialogLens, a hybrid LLM pipeline. First, compact fine-tuned classifiers act as scaffolds, detecting RFQ-trigger messages and price/trade outcome metadata. An RFQ-Level Module then segments the chat stream into per-event RFQ windows. Finally, a Trade Engine (powered by LLMs such as GPT-4o or fine-tuned 3B open-source models) fills argument roles to recover final price and trade outcome for each RFQ. To reduce serving costs, a difficulty-aware router triages RFQs between a low-cost rule-based engine and the more capable LLM Trade Engine, based on estimated difficulty and uncertainty.

Result: With GPT-4o as the Trade Engine, FinDialogLens attains 92.1% accuracy on final price extraction and 94.3% on trade outcome classification, outperforming chain-of-thought prompting applied directly to full chatrooms. When using fine-tuned open-source LLMs as small as 3B parameters, the system achieves comparable performance given modest in-domain training data. The difficulty-aware router reduces LLM calls by about 85% for final price extraction, while recovering roughly half the performance gap relative to the best full LLM setup, amounting to more than $300 per day in cost savings at a 70,000 RFQ/day production volume.

Conclusion: Event extraction over multi-party financial chat is tractable and accurate when approached with a structured, hybrid LLM pipeline rather than monolithic prompting over entire conversations. FinDialogLens demonstrates that small classifiers plus targeted LLM inference can segment RFQs, recover final trade details with >90% accuracy, and remain cost-effective at large operational scales. The difficulty-aware routing strategy shows that combining rule-based methods with selective LLM use can substantially cut inference costs while preserving much of the accuracy benefit, making LLM-powered trade reconstruction viable for real-world, high-throughput trading environments.

Abstract: Multi-party financial chatrooms are vital for sales-and-trading professionals, but their complexity makes manual recovery of missed trades infeasible: each Request for Quote (RFQ) is an event whose final price and trade outcome appear many messages after the RFQ-trigger message (the inquiry message), interleaved with concurrent RFQs from other participants. We cast this as event extraction (EE) over multi-party dialogue and present FinDialogLens, a hybrid LLM pipeline in which compact fine-tuned classifiers act as inference-time scaffolds: they detect RFQ-triggers and price/trade outcome metadata, an RFQ-Level Module segments per-event RFQ windows, and a Trade Engine fills argument roles. With GPT-4o, FinDialogLens reaches 92.1% and 94.3% accuracy on final price and trade outcome, respectively, outperforming full-chatroom CoT prompting methods; fine-tuned open-source LLMs with as few as 3B parameters achieve comparable performance with modest in-domain data. To make the LLM-based solution practical at scale, a difficulty-aware router balances cost and accuracy by allocating RFQs between a low-cost rule-based engine and the higher-performing LLM-powered Trade Engine, cutting LLM calls by 85% on final price while recovering half of the accuracy gap to FinDialogLens (GPT-4o), saving over $300/day at our 70,000-RFQ/day scale.

</details>


### [5] [CUEing User Simulators: Calibrated User Embeddings for Multi-Turn Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02460)
*Anjali Kantharuban,Jonas Mueller*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes Calibrated User Embeddings (CUE), a framework to create outcome-calibrated user simulators for evaluating AI agents, so that simulated interactions better match real user success and failure patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing user simulators focus on stylistic similarity to humans but fail to match real-world success rates and failure modes of AI agents, undermining the ecological validity of interactive benchmarks. There is a need for simulators that are calibrated to real outcomes, not just surface behavior, so that evaluations under simulation reliably predict how agents perform with real users across tasks and user types.

Method: The authors introduce Calibrated User Embeddings (CUE). CUE encodes real interaction sessions into continuous user embeddings, which can be sampled and then decoded into persona commands that steer large language models to behave like specific types of users, without additional simulator training. They test two settings: (1) replaying past sessions conditioned on user embeddings and (2) sampling novel personas for the same tasks. They compare CUE-based simulators with standard persona-based simulation across multiple tasks on the τ^2-Bench benchmark, using various simulator LLMs, and also test cross-domain generalization beyond the primarily customer-support training data.

Result: On τ^2-Bench, CUE-based simulators exhibit fewer simulator-specific artifacts (errors caused by the simulator itself) and more accurately replicate the real users’ agent failure modes, overall success rates, and outcome distributions for particular task–user pairs, outperforming alternative persona-based simulators on outcome calibration. At the same time, CUE maintains competitive user-style fidelity according to existing user simulation metrics. Furthermore, CUE trained mainly on customer support interactions generalizes well to other domains, including document creation, math tutoring, and casual conversation, and the same embeddings stay effective across different simulator LLMs without retraining CUE.

Conclusion: Outcome calibration—matching real user success and failure patterns—is essential for trustworthy interactive benchmarking of AI agents and is not provided by existing simulators focused only on stylistic fidelity. Calibrated User Embeddings (CUE) offer a training-free, embedding-based way to steer LLM simulators toward calibrated behavior, yielding more realistic estimates of agent performance while preserving user fidelity. CUE generalizes across tasks and simulator backbones, suggesting it is a robust and practical approach for building better user simulators for multi-turn evaluation.

Abstract: Recent benchmarks rely on user simulators to evaluate AI agents in multi-turn interaction. While existing simulation techniques demonstrate surface fidelity to human style and behavior, ecologically valid interactive benchmarking also requires alignment in when and how agents fail across simulated and real user populations. We find that existing simulators lack outcome calibration: agreement with observed success rates and failure patterns when real users interact with the same agent. We introduce Calibrated User Embeddings (CUE), a framework that both encodes observed sessions and samples continuous representations, then decodes them into persona commands to steer LLMs to act as user simulators without training. Through this, we evaluate user-conditioned replay of past sessions and aggregate metric agreement when sampling novel personas for the same tasks. On $τ^2$-Bench, CUEd simulators commit fewer simulator-attributed errors and more faithfully reproduce real-user agent failure modes, aggregate success rates, and outcomes for specific task-user pairs than other persona-based simulation methods. These gains coexist with competitive user fidelity as measured using metrics established in prior work. After being fit to mostly customer support interactions, the same CUEd simulators generalize to document creation, math tutoring, and casual conversation, and remain effective across different simulator LLMs without CUE retraining.

</details>


### [6] [APDMem: Agent-Controlled Progressive Disclosure for Query-Adaptive Long-Term Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02472)
*Chin-Lun Fu,Anagha Kulkarni,Hong Ni,Behrouz Madahian*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes APDMem, a hierarchical memory system for personalized LLM assistants that progressively discloses more detailed context only when needed, improving long-context reasoning efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM assistants must handle very long, personalized conversation histories where relevant evidence is sparse and user queries vary from simple to complex (e.g., temporal or multi-hop questions). Flat memory stores or fixed-granularity retrieval either waste computation by reading too much or miss fine-grained evidence. The authors aim to design a memory architecture that can adaptively balance retrieval cost and answer fidelity for long-term conversational memory.

Method: They design APDMem, a four-layer hierarchical memory: (1) high-level thematic summaries of the conversation, (2) personalized key facts, (3) turn-level evidence notes, and (4) raw messages. At inference, a controller performs progressive disclosure: it first reads the high-level layers and only drills down into more detailed layers as required by the query’s complexity, effectively deciding how deep to go. Additionally, they employ a note synthesizer that structures the retrieved evidence into a query-focused representation, ordering events, consolidating facts, and marking contradictions before answer generation. They evaluate APDMem on the LongMemEval benchmark.

Result: On LongMemEval, APDMem shows strong performance on long-context memory reasoning tasks while needing to access only about 8% of the total conversation content, indicating efficient retrieval without sacrificing accuracy.

Conclusion: A hierarchical, progressively disclosed memory architecture enables LLM assistants to reason effectively over long conversational histories with sparse relevant evidence while substantially reducing the amount of context that must be retrieved and processed. APDMem offers an adaptive trade-off between computational cost and answer fidelity and is empirically validated on a long-memory benchmark.

Abstract: Personalized LLM assistants must recover sparse evidence from long conversation histories across queries of varying complexity. We introduce APDMem (Agent-controlled Progressive Disclosure Memory), a hierarchical long-term memory architecture that applies progressive disclosure to memory retrieval. Rather than relying on a flat memory store or fixed retrieval granularity, APDMem represents conversation history as four progressively detailed layers: thematic summaries, personalized key facts, turn-level evidence notes, and raw messages. At inference time, a controller applies progressive disclosure to the memory hierarchy: it first reads high-level summaries and drills into finer evidence only when needed. This creates an adaptive cost-fidelity trade-off: simple queries can terminate early, while complex temporal, multi-hop, or exact-evidence queries trigger deeper inspection. A note synthesizer converts retrieved evidence into a query-focused structure that consolidates facts, orders events, and flags contradictions before final answer generation. Experiments on LongMemEval show that APDMem achieves strong performance for long-context memory reasoning while accessing only 8% of the total conversations.

</details>


### [7] [From Retrieval to Typed Decisions: Calibrated System One Models from Biomedical Sentence Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02486)
*Pritam Deka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether biomedical sentence encoders trained for retrieval are good starting points for typed decision models, introducing SBERT2S1, new decision heads, datasets, and a systematic comparison of training objectives and calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Typed decision models must answer structured, schema-constrained questions over text with a single forward pass and well-calibrated probabilities. In biomedicine, high-quality text encoders exist that are trained for retrieval (matching queries to documents), but it is unclear how well these retrieval-trained encoders can be adapted to such structured decision tasks and which architectural heads and training objectives make best use of them. Additionally, popular reinforcement-learning-based training recipes for "System One" decision models may be suboptimal, and their calibration properties versus standard cross-entropy are not well understood.

Method: The authors build SBERT2S1, a framework that turns Sentence-Transformers biomedical encoders into typed decision models with two main head architectures: (1) a cross-head (C) that directly scores options, and (2) a prior-fused residual (PFR) head that incorporates the retrieval prior into the decision process. They create BIODECIDE, a benchmark suite of biomedical typed-decision tasks, and MEDLINE-S1, a 243k-example training set derived from NLM indexing. They compare zero-shot and fine-tuned performance across six encoder–retriever pairs, five training objectives (including cross-entropy and an RL-based RLCD recipe used in open System One models), and different training-set sizes. They analyze performance, the effect of retaining retrieval priors, the impact of reward normalization in RL training, and calibration via temperature scaling. They also test an unbiased leave-one-out estimator for policy gradients to reduce variance and close performance gaps.

Result: Retrieval training of encoders improves zero-shot matching of content-bearing answer options across all six parent-retriever pairs. After fine-tuning, the effect of retrieval pretraining depends strongly on the head type: for the PFR head, retrieval pretraining significantly helps in 10 of 15 comparisons (over five encoder pairs and three data scales), whereas for the cross-head it helps only once and hurts five times. When controlling for objectives, the cross-head consistently outperforms PFR under all five training objectives. The RLCD reinforcement-learning training recipe used by open System One models underperforms standard cross-entropy by about 2.5–3.0 accuracy points. This gap is largely due to its reward normalization, which magnifies the noisy score-function term by factors of 3.6–15; using an unbiased leave-one-out estimator recovers most of this deficit. After temperature scaling, none of the alternative objectives yields clearly better calibration than cross-entropy.

Conclusion: Biomedical sentence encoders trained for retrieval can benefit typed decision models, especially when their retrieval priors are explicitly integrated via a prior-fused residual head, but the choice of head and objective is crucial. Simple cross-head architectures trained with cross-entropy remain strong and generally superior to more complex prior-fused variants when objectives are matched. The widely used RLCD reinforcement-learning recipe is suboptimal mainly due to its reward normalization strategy, and its performance gap relative to cross-entropy can be largely closed with a better gradient estimator. Overall, well-tuned cross-entropy training on retrieval-initialized encoders offers a strong, well-calibrated baseline for biomedical typed decision models. The authors release code, the MEDLINE-S1 labels, and a trained model to support further research.

Abstract: Typed decision models answer schema-constrained questions about a text in one forward pass and return probabilities meant to be thresholded. We ask whether biomedical sentence encoders trained for retrieval are good starting points for such models. We present SBERT2S1, which converts Sentence-Transformers encoders into bi-encoder, cross-head (C) and prior-fused residual (PFR) decision models, together with BIODECIDE, a biomedical typed-decision suite, and MEDLINE-S1, 243k training decisions derived from NLM indexing. Across six parent-retriever pairs, retrieval training improves zero-shot matching of content-bearing options. After fine-tuning, its effect depends on the head: across five pairs and three training-set sizes, retrieval training significantly helps PFR, which keeps the retrieval prior, in 10 of 15 comparisons, but helps C in one and hurts it in five. A matched grid of two heads and five training objectives shows that C outperforms PFR under every objective, and that the released RLCD recipe of open System One models trails cross-entropy by 2.5-3.0 points. The deficit stems mainly from its reward normalisation, which inflates the noisy score-function term 3.6-15-fold; an unbiased leave-one-out estimator recovers most of the gap. After temperature scaling, no objective is clearly better calibrated than cross-entropy. We release the code, the MEDLINE-S1 labels and a model.

</details>


### [8] [A generative-informed neuro-symbolic framework for syntactic ambiguity resolution: Evidence from Arabic DPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02529)
*Mohammed Damom,Muneef Y. Alshawsh,Ashraf A. Naji,Mustafa Ali Alhamzi,Fawwaz An-Nashef,Jameel Ahmed Elayah,Mohammed Q. Shormani,Noman AL-Sayadi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A neuro-symbolic framework integrates generative syntax with AraBERT to resolve structural ambiguity in Modern Standard Arabic noun phrases by evaluating linguistically constructed candidate structures, achieving around 97% accuracy and showing that embedded (low) attachments are harder to recover than high attachments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Arabic, being morphologically rich, frequently exhibits syntactic ambiguity in nominal constructions, where a single surface string can correspond to multiple structural parses (e.g., high vs. low attachment). Existing purely neural or purely symbolic approaches struggle either with interpretability or with fully capturing structural nuances. The authors aim to build a model that both leverages formal generative syntax and benefits from powerful Transformer-based representations to more reliably and transparently resolve these ambiguities in Modern Standard Arabic DPs.

Method: The study designs a neuro-symbolic framework where structural ambiguity is cast as a candidate-based decision problem: for each ambiguous DP, linguistically motivated alternative parses (e.g., High/VP Attachment vs. Low/NP/Embedded Attachment) are explicitly generated. These alternatives are encoded into candidate-conditioned inputs to AraBERT, a Transformer model pre-trained for Arabic. The system then scores and selects the most plausible structural interpretation. The approach tightly couples generative syntactic notions with contextual neural modeling to make the disambiguation process explicit and interpretable.

Result: On an unseen evaluation set, the model reached 96.88% accuracy, 95.92% macro-F1, 96.83% weighted F1, and 93.94% binary F1. Class-level analysis showed asymmetric performance: recall was very high for High/VP Attachment cases (N1) at 99.71%, but lower for Low/NP/Embedded Attachment cases (N2) at 89.26%, highlighting that recovering embedded attachments is comparatively more challenging for the model.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that formal generative syntactic representations can be effectively operationalized inside Transformer-based NLP models by using explicit candidate structures as an interface between symbolic linguistic theory and neural contextual encoding. This yields high performance on Arabic DP ambiguity resolution while preserving interpretability and control. The authors suggest that this neuro-symbolic, candidate-based paradigm can be extended beyond Arabic to other syntactic ambiguity problems and languages, offering a general, linguistically grounded route to more transparent neural parsing and disambiguation.

Abstract: Syntactic ambiguity poses a persistent challenge for Arabic NLP, particularly in morphologically rich nominal constructions where multiple structu6ral interpretations may be compatible with the same surface sequence. This study proposes a generatively informed neuro-symbolic framework for resolving structural ambiguity in Modern Standard Arabic (MSA) DPs. The framework integrates generative syntactic notions with AraBERT by representing ambiguity as a candidate-based decision task in which linguistically motivated alternatives are explicitly constructed and evaluated through candidate-conditioned input representations. Findings indicate that the model achieved 96.88% accuracy, 95.92% macro-F1, 96.83% weighted F1, and 93.94% binary F1 on the unseen evaluation set. Class-level analysis revealed asymmetric performance, with recall of 99.71% for High/VP Attachment (N1) and 89.26% for Low/NP/Embedded Attachment (N2), indicating greater difficulty in recovering the embedded interpretation. The study concludes that formal syntactic representations can be operationalized within Transformer-based NLP as an explicit interface between linguistic structure and contextual neural modeling, providing a controlled and interpretable approach to Arabic syntactic ambiguity resolution and beyond.

</details>


### [9] [Evaluating Multi-Dimensional Generalization of Large Language Models in Temporal Extraction Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02549)
*Fahmid Shahriar Iqbal,Ritam Dutt,Soumitra Das,Arnav Verma,Sagnik Ray Choudhury*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well large language models (LLMs) generalize in extracting time and event expressions under various distribution shifts, showing that good in-domain performance does not guarantee robustness, and that inductive prompting is the most consistently reliable strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time and event expression extraction is central to temporal reasoning in NLP, but remains hard because annotations are ambiguous, models are sensitive to domain changes, and their behavior is unstable. Existing work mostly measures in-domain accuracy, which reveals little about reliability when inputs differ from training data. The authors want to understand how different LLMs and prompting strategies generalize under realistic and adversarial shifts so that practitioners can better assess and improve robustness.

Method: The authors systematically evaluate multiple LLM configurations that vary in model family, scale, architecture, and prompting style. They design four dimensions of distribution shift—domain shift, adversarial perturbations, compositionality changes, and input length increase—and test time and event expression extraction across these settings. They analyze: (1) how performance on the base (in-domain) task transfers to shifted distributions; (2) correlations between different generalization dimensions; and (3) how scale, architecture, and prompting strategies (inductive, deductive, abductive) affect robustness.

Result: They find that models that perform better on the base task tend to generalize better overall, but this predictive relationship weakens under stronger distribution shifts. Inductive prompting yields the most stable and consistent performance across all four shift dimensions. In contrast, scaling up models, changing architectures, and using deductive or abductive prompting provide inconsistent benefits that depend on the specific type of shift, with no single factor guaranteeing robust generalization.

Conclusion: LLM generalization in temporal expression extraction is multi-faceted and cannot be predicted from any single indicator such as in-domain performance or one type of robustness test. Evaluations restricted to a single domain or a single generalization dimension are misleading about true reliability. More comprehensive evaluation protocols and reasoning strategies—especially inductive prompting—that work across diverse shift types are necessary for building robust temporal extraction systems.

Abstract: Time and event expression extraction are fundamental temporal reasoning tasks, but the problem remains difficult due to annotation ambiguity, domain sensitivity, and unstable model behavior. Existing evaluations focus on in-domain performance, offering limited insight into reliability under distribution shifts. We evaluate multiple model configurations across families, architectures, and reasoning strategies over four dimensions of generalization, examining transfer from base performance, cross-dimensional correlations, and the effects of scale, architecture, and prompting. This provides a systematic study of how prompted LLMs generalize in time and event expression extraction tasks. We find that strong base-task performance generally predicts better generalization. However, this relationship weakens under substantial distribution shifts. Inductive prompting performs most consistently across domain shift, adversarial perturbations, compositionality, and length increase, while gains from scale, architecture, and deductive and abductive prompting strategies are uneven and dimension-specific. We conclude that LLM generalization in temporal extraction tasks cannot be predicted from any single dimension alone and cannot be reliably inferred from in-domain or single-dimension evaluations, highlighting the need for reasoning strategies that generalize across dimensions.

</details>


### [10] [Learning When to Commit from Partial Speech for End-to-End Simultaneous Speech Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02612)
*Hieu Hoang,Amittai Axelrod*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They adapt a full-utterance speech translation model so it can translate simultaneously using its own synthetic prefixes, without transcripts or human references, improving the quality–latency trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simultaneous speech translation must start outputting target text before the speaker finishes, but standard models are trained for full utterances and do not handle partial inputs or token commitments well. Collecting aligned transcripts and human translations for partial inputs is expensive. The authors want a way to adapt existing full-utterance speech translation models to simultaneous use, with better quality–latency trade-offs and calibrated commitment decisions, while avoiding costly supervision.

Method: They take a full-utterance speech language model and generate translations for both complete and partial speech waveforms. From these, they derive training prefixes that teach the model to handle streaming inputs. They explore two decoding regimes: (1) single-turn forced-prefix decoding, where the model must extend a fixed committed prefix, and (2) multi-turn append-only decoding, where it repeatedly receives more speech and must append tokens without revising past ones. They introduce a confidence threshold at inference to decide when to commit tokens, controlling the quality–latency trade-off. They also vary a synthesis margin parameter that controls how densely prefixes are sampled in training, affecting how much prefix supervision the model sees.

Result: On FLEURS and CoVoST2 for three language pairs, prefix training shifts the quality–latency frontier upward compared to the unadapted model, meaning better translation quality at the same latency or lower latency for the same quality. Confidence-based committing yields the most robust and competitive performance across operating points. Multi-turn decoding tends to outperform single-turn at low latency. With multi-turn training, commit-calibration error drops by 63–68% overall and 68–80% on early prefixes, whereas single-turn training only slightly improves calibration and not at early prefixes. A small synthesis margin can further reduce latency without hurting quality, especially for shorter utterances, but a large margin harms both translation quality and calibration.

Conclusion: Prefix-based adaptation of full-utterance speech translation models substantially improves simultaneous speech translation, particularly when paired with multi-turn append-only decoding and confidence-based commitment. It enhances the quality–latency trade-off and markedly improves calibration of commitment decisions, especially early in the utterance. However, the density of synthetic prefixes (controlled by the synthesis margin) must be tuned: modest density can extend benefits to lower latencies, while excessive density leads to degraded performance, creating a non-monotonic trade-off between quality and latency.

Abstract: Simultaneous speech translation must emit useful target text before the source is complete while preserving every committed token. We adapt a full-utterance speech language model using prefix supervision derived from its own complete- and partial-waveform translations, requiring neither transcripts nor human translations. We compare single-turn forced-prefix and multi-turn append-only decoding, use a confidence threshold to control the inference-time quality--latency trade-off, and vary the density of training prefixes with a separate synthesis margin. On FLEURS and CoVoST2 in three language directions, prefix training improves quality--latency frontiers over the unadapted model, and confidence provides the broadest consistently competitive operating range. Multi-turn decoding is generally stronger at low latency; under multi-turn training, commit-calibration error falls by 63--68% overall and 68--80% at early prefixes, whereas single-turn training provides only modest overall calibration gains and no early-prefix improvement. A small synthesis margin sometimes extends the frontier to lower latency, particularly on shorter utterances, while a larger margin degrades translation quality and calibration. Prefix adaptation therefore improves simultaneous speech translation, especially under multi-turn append-only decoding, while synthesis density introduces a non-monotonic quality--latency trade-off.

</details>


### [11] [Large Language Continuous Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02665)
*Zhihan Yang,Wei Guo,Jean-Marie Lemercier,Simon Welker,Yonggan Fu,Mohammad Mahdi Kamani,Sajad Norouzi,Julius Berner,Tomas Geffner,Karsten Kreis,Yongxin Chen,Molei Tao,John Thickstun,Pavlo Molchanov,Ante Jukić,Arash Vahdat,Morteza Mardani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Proposes Sigma, a large continuous diffusion language model that operates in a low-dimensional latent space to improve steering, efficiency, and reasoning performance, matching discrete models while adding new control and distillation benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete diffusion language models allow fast parallel decoding but operate in a non-smooth, high-dimensional token space, which makes it hard to steer generation trajectories, improve reasoning, or reduce inference cost via continuous-time methods. There is a need for a language model that retains the parallelism and performance of discrete diffusion, while leveraging continuous, low-dimensional trajectories that are easier to guide, analyze, and distill.

Method: Introduce Sigma, a 3B/8B continuous diffusion language model whose generation process is defined as an ODE/SDE in a low-dimensional continuous latent space of token embeddings. The model is trained blockwise via maximum likelihood to jointly denoise Gaussian-noised token embeddings and learn an optimal embedding geometry. Training is accelerated by warm-starting from pretrained autoregressive model weights. At inference time, the method uses classifier-free guidance and score temperature tuning to steer trajectories and improve reasoning and coding quality. The paper then evaluates Sigma on a variety of math reasoning and coding benchmarks and analyzes structural properties such as steering in embedding space and behavior under different numbers of function evaluations (NFEs).

Result: Sigma achieves performance comparable to state-of-the-art discrete diffusion and autoregressive models on standard math and coding benchmarks (e.g., GSM8K, Minerva, HumanEval, MBPP) after pretraining, and remains competitive on more difficult reasoning datasets (MATH-500, AIME) after supervised finetuning. It exhibits strong pass@k performance due to effective quality-diversity control via embedding-space steering. Additionally, its continuous trajectories show graceful performance degradation when using fewer NFEs and support efficient distillation, demonstrating practical efficiency advantages over discrete counterparts.

Conclusion: Continuous diffusion language models like Sigma can match the performance of strong discrete and autoregressive baselines on reasoning and coding tasks while providing unique advantages: steerable low-dimensional trajectories, better control over quality-diversity trade-offs, robustness to reduced computational budgets (low NFEs), and improved distillation efficiency. These findings position continuous dLMs as a promising and potentially more efficient paradigm for future large-scale language generation systems.

Abstract: Despite the success of discrete diffusion language models (dLMs) for fast parallel decoding, their non-smooth, high-dimensional space hinders trajectory steering for reasoning and inference acceleration. To overcome this, we present Sigma, the first large-scale (3B/8B) continuous dLM built on steerable, low-dimensional ODE/SDE latent trajectories. Trained blockwise via likelihood optimization, Sigma jointly denoises Gaussian-corrupted token embeddings while learning an optimal embedding geometry. To accelerate training, Sigma leverages pre-trained weights from autoregressive (AR) models for warm-starting. During inference, we identify classifier-free guidance and score temperature as essential for high-fidelity reasoning and coding. Across comprehensive math reasoning and coding evaluations against state-of-the-art discrete counterparts (masked dLMs and AR baselines), Sigma achieves competitive performance with discrete models on standard benchmarks (e.g., GSM8K, Minerva, HumanEval, MBPP) after pre-training and on challenging reasoning tasks (e.g., MATH-500, AIME) after supervised fine-tuning. Beyond performance parity, we uncover key structural properties unique to continuous dLMs: (i) embedding-space steering effectively governs the quality-diversity trade-off, yielding strong pass@k performance and (ii) continuous trajectories enable graceful degradation for low NFEs and efficient distillation. These establish continuous dLMs as a promising paradigm for efficient language generation.

</details>


### [12] [Silent Dissent: LLM Agents That Yield to the Majority Still Represent Their Original Premise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02702)
*Ziang Ni,Peng Zou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether LLM agents in multi-agent debates actually change their underlying internal beliefs when they conform to a unanimous (but wrong) majority, showing that models often keep representing the correct intermediate fact (“bridge”) internally while stating the majority’s incorrect answer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent debate among LLMs is being used as a technique to improve reliability via consensus, but it is unclear whether apparent agreement reflects genuine internal belief change or mere outward conformity. Inspired by Asch’s conformity experiments, the authors want to know whether an LLM that changes its final answer under peer pressure has also changed its internal reasoning steps, especially when key intermediate entities are never explicitly mentioned in the debate.

Method: They construct two-hop factual questions that rely on an unstated intermediate entity (the “bridge”). Scripted peer agents act like Asch’s confederates, unanimously giving a wrong answer that is correct for a different (wrong) bridge. While target agents answer, the authors probe hidden activations using the Jacobian lens (J-lens) and logit lens to decode which bridge entity is represented in the residual stream at different layers. They pre-register experiments across four open-weight models, measure hit@100 vs a control entity, run addendum tests where the agent’s earlier answer is hidden or removed, and perform exploratory interventions by injecting the J-lens bridge direction to see if it can restore the original answer.

Result: For multiple models (Qwen3.5-4B, Qwen3.6-27B, Gemma-4-9B-it, and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct), agents that outwardly conformed to the peers’ wrong answer still robustly represented their original correct bridge in deeper layers, with high hit@100 under the J-lens, whereas the logit lens rarely surfaced that bridge among the top 100 tokens. Models also encoded the bridge implied by the peers’ wrong answer, above a mention baseline. When the agent’s earlier answer was hidden or removed, it still internally represented the original bridge from the question alone, including for Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, which barely did so when its answer was visible. Hiding the earlier answer also greatly increased overt conformity in some models (e.g., Qwen3.5-4B’s conformity jumped from 8% to 89%). Injecting the bridge’s J-lens direction into the residual stream could sometimes restore the original answer, but mainly in the Qwen models. They additionally document pre-registered negative results.

Conclusion: LLM agents in multi-agent debates often preserve an internal representation of the correct intermediate reasoning step even when they publicly adopt a consensus wrong answer, indicating a dissociation between internal computation and external statements. Thus, consensus-based methods can overestimate true agreement among agents, and interpretability tools like the J-lens can reveal hidden “beliefs” that outputs alone miss. The ability to manipulate behavior via targeted interventions on bridge representations further suggests a causal role of these internal features, although this effect is model-dependent. Overall, the work cautions against treating multi-agent consensus as direct evidence of shared internal beliefs and underscores the value of mechanistic probes for evaluating reasoning and conformity in LLMs.

Abstract: Multi-agent debate is increasingly used to reach consensus among LLM agents, yet agents often yield to a unanimous majority. When an agent changes its answer, has it changed its mind or only its statement? We study this with two-hop factual questions whose intermediate entity (the bridge, e.g. the country in "the capital of the country where the Sagrada Familia is located") is never stated by anyone. Scripted peers, in the role of Asch's confederates, unanimously assert a wrong answer taken from another fact with a different bridge. At the moment the agent answers, we read the bridge from its residual stream with the Jacobian lens (J-lens) and, for comparison, the logit lens. In pre-registered tests on held-out facts with four open-weight models, agents of Qwen3.5-4B, Qwen3.6-27B and Gemma-4-E4B-it that gave in still represented their original bridge in the pre-registered layers below the output (hit@100 above a control entity: 0.85, 0.22 and 0.24), where the logit lens rarely ranked it among the top 100 tokens (0.00-0.06). These agents also represented the bridge behind the peers' answer, beyond a mention baseline. A pre-registered addendum hid the agent's earlier answer or removed it: agents that gave in still represented their original bridge in all four models (0.43, 0.29, 0.37 and 0.25 with the answer hidden), including Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, which barely did so with its answer in view (0.03). The premise can thus be computed from the question alone while the agent states the majority's answer. Hiding the earlier answer also changed conformity: Qwen3.5-4B gave in on 89% of questions instead of 8%. In exploratory interventions, injecting the bridge's J-lens direction brought agents back to their original answer only in the two Qwen models. Stated consensus in multi-agent debate can thus overstate agreement. We also report the negative results of our pre-registered program.

</details>


### [13] [WakeKV: Reactive, Reversible KV Residency for Heads That Change Their Minds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02713)
*Utkarsh Ranjan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: WakeKV is a reactive KV-cache compression policy that dynamically moves less-active attention heads’ states to CPU memory instead of freezing or discarding them, reducing cache misses and improving throughput without hurting quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KV-cache compression methods classify attention heads only once (offline or at prefill) and then keep this classification fixed. However, attention heads can change behavior during generation, especially in tasks like retrieval, long chain-of-thought, and multi-turn dialogue. A static policy can therefore either waste memory on inactive heads or evict information that later becomes important, causing more cache misses and performance degradation. The paper aims to design a cache policy that adapts to changing head activity patterns while staying within tight memory or compute budgets.

Method: The authors empirically study attention-head behavior across multiple model sizes (1.5B–8B) and usage regimes (needle retrieval, long CoT, multi-turn recall) and show that most heads change reading behavior during generation. Based on this, they propose WakeKV, a reactive residency policy for KV caches. Instead of keeping inactive (cooling) heads fully resident or permanently evicting them, WakeKV moves their KV states to a recoverable CPU reservoir. When a head “wakes up” and becomes active again, its state can be restored from CPU memory rather than recomputed from scratch. They compare WakeKV against frozen head classification and destructive eviction baselines, including SnapKV, uniform R-KV, and ReasonAlloc, and implement it within FlexiCache/vLLM for Mistral-7B.

Result: On four model-regime combinations, the authors find that most attention heads change their reading behavior at least once, justifying the need for a dynamic policy. Under matched memory or budget constraints, WakeKV consistently reduces KV-cache miss rates versus both frozen classification and destructive eviction. Across five model-regime combinations and three prior baselines (SnapKV, uniform R-KV, ReasonAlloc, where applicable), WakeKV shows superior cache performance. A practical implementation on Mistral-7B using FlexiCache/vLLM demonstrates improved inference throughput on real hardware while preserving performance on the LongBench benchmark suite.

Conclusion: Static attention-head classification is misaligned with real head dynamics during generation. WakeKV addresses this by treating KV-cache residency as a reactive, multi-tier memory problem, offloading cooling heads to CPU instead of discarding their state. This design lowers miss rates and improves throughput under the same resource constraints while maintaining model quality, suggesting that reactive KV-cache policies are better suited for long-context and interactive LLM workloads than existing static or destructive methods.

Abstract: Most KV-cache compression methods classify attention heads once, either offline or during prefill, and keep this classification fixed throughout generation. Across three models (1.5B-8B) and three regimes (needle retrieval, long chain-of-thought, and multi-turn recall), we measure head behavior on four model-regime combinations and find that most heads change their reading behavior at least once during generation. We introduce WakeKV, a reactive residency policy that moves cooling heads to a recoverable CPU reservoir rather than freezing or permanently evicting their state. At matched memory or budget, WakeKV consistently improves miss rate over frozen classification and destructive eviction, evaluated across five model-regime combinations and over three cited baselines (SnapKV, uniform R-KV, and ReasonAlloc) across four eligible combinations. A FlexiCache/vLLM implementation on Mistral-7B confirms the benefit on real hardware, improving throughput while retaining LongBench quality.

</details>


### [14] [TPBench: A Turning-Point Benchmark for Dialogue Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02736)
*Minji Park,Seunghyun Yoon,Hyuk Lim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies and evaluates a specific failure mode in dialogue compression—dropping the turn where the user changes their mind—by introducing a benchmark (TPBench) that separately measures retention of initial goals and updated information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dialogue compressors often use a single retention score that does not distinguish between preserving a user’s initial goal and their updated, current intent. This can hide a critical failure mode where the compressor discards the pivotal “turning point” turn in which the user corrects or revises information, leading to incorrect downstream behavior even when overall retention appears good.

Method: The authors define a new benchmark, TPBench, which probes three information targets at comparable compression levels: (P1) the user’s initial goal (first sentence of the first user turn), (P2) the current value of a slot that the user later revised (from dialogue state annotations in MultiWOZ and SGD), and (P3) a joint probe asking for both initial goal and current value in dialogues with a late slot update. They evaluate various compression methods plus a full-context baseline with different LLM readers (Llama and Mistral), conduct ablations that selectively delete the update turn vs. an irrelevant turn, and test generalization on additional datasets (LongMemEval-KU and Chinese RiSAWOZ).

Result: Probe-specific scores reorder the ranking of compression methods compared to using a single aggregate metric. At a 0.30 retained fraction on the joint probe, all compressed methods underperform the full-context baseline with Llama. Removing the actual update turn drastically reduces accuracy on current slot values, while removing a matched irrelevant turn has little effect. Similar behavior appears with a Mistral reader, including the gap between compressed methods and full context on joint probes. On LongMemEval-KU and RiSAWOZ, full context again yields the best current-value accuracy, and simple recency-based compression gives the best mean performance among compressed methods.

Conclusion: Treating dialogue retention as a single scalar metric obscures important failure modes like “turning-point eviction,” where the crucial update turn is dropped. TPBench’s targeted probes reveal that compression strategies differ significantly in how well they preserve initial vs. updated user intent, and that retaining the actual update turn is critical for accurate current-state reasoning. Full context remains strongest, but among compressed methods, recency-biased strategies are relatively robust across datasets and languages.

Abstract: A compressor can keep the facts of a dialogue and still drop the turn that changed them. A user corrects a price, reverses a choice, or adds a constraint. We call this failure turning-point eviction. One overall retention score hides it, because that score mixes what the user first wanted with what the user wants now.
  We introduce TPBench, which evaluates three complementary information targets at shared nominal retention budgets. P1 asks for the user's initial goal. P2 asks for the current value of a slot the user revised. P3 asks for both, in dialogues with a late annotated slot update. The current-value answers come from the human dialogue-state annotations of MultiWOZ and SGD. The initial-goal answer is the first sentence of the first user turn. Neither requires new crowdsourcing.
  The probe-specific evaluations rank compression methods differently. On the joint probe at a retained fraction of 0.30, every tested compressed method remains below full context with the main Llama reader. Deleting the turn that carries the update sharply lowers current-value accuracy, while deleting one matched irrelevant turn leaves it unchanged. A Mistral reader repeats the P2/P3 rankings and the joint-probe gap. Current-value recovery is tested on an additional corpus, LongMemEval-KU, and on Chinese RiSAWOZ: full context has the highest accuracy, and recency has the highest compressed-method mean in both evaluations.

</details>


### [15] [Beyond Correctness: Resolving Underspecification in Agentic Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02739)
*Wen-Zhi Li,Yue Gong,Konstantinos Kanellis,Balakrishnan Murali Narayanaswamy*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies a failure mode in agentic Text-to-SQL systems—prematurely ending clarification—and proposes PlanPool, a structured question-pool mechanism that forces all identified ambiguities to be either asked or explicitly dropped, improving ambiguity coverage and reducing silent failures while keeping execution accuracy competitive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic Text-to-SQL systems can ask users clarification questions when queries are underspecified, but current systems often stop clarifying too early. They obtain correct execution results by making unverified assumptions that just happen to match the intended answer, which is unsafe and brittle. Existing prompts that tell the model to "plan clarifications" are not enough; the agent still drops previously identified important questions. There is thus a need for a mechanism that ensures ambiguities, once recognized, are consistently tracked and resolved (or explicitly dismissed) before producing an answer.

Method: The authors first analyze agentic Text-to-SQL systems to show that many errors and silent failures stem from premature termination of clarification, and that ambiguities are concentrated in early turns. They experiment with simply forcing more questions, which helps but is inefficient. They then propose PlanPool, an external mutable pool of clarification questions: the agent initially plans potential questions (a clarification plan) which are added to the pool; during interaction, each question in the pool must either be explicitly asked to the user or explicitly dropped before generating SQL, and new ambiguities discovered mid-dialogue can also be added to the pool. The system architecture and prompting enforce this lifecycle for each question, providing structured control over the clarification process. They evaluate PlanPool on three benchmarks based on BIRD-Interact and Spider against unconstrained and prompt-only planning baselines.

Result: Across three benchmarks built from BIRD-Interact and Spider, PlanPool consistently increases ambiguity coverage (more of the true underspecifications are actually surfaced to the user) and reduces silent failures (cases where the agent answers without clarifying important missing information) compared with unconstrained agents and agents using only prompt-based clarification planning. Despite asking more targeted clarifying questions, PlanPool maintains competitive execution accuracy on the final SQL results, demonstrating that improved ambiguity handling does not harm performance and can even improve it when simple "ask more" heuristics are inefficient.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that a key weakness of current agentic Text-to-SQL systems is not just recognizing missing information, but reliably maintaining and resolving those ambiguities over the course of an interaction. By externalizing the clarification plan into a mutable question pool and requiring every planned question to be either asked or explicitly dropped, PlanPool mitigates premature clarification termination and reduces silent assumption-making. This structured approach leads to better ambiguity coverage and fewer silent failures while preserving strong execution accuracy, and it suggests that agentic reasoning pipelines should explicitly manage and track unresolved information rather than relying solely on implicit planning within a language model prompt.

Abstract: Agentic Text-to-SQL systems can interact with users to clarify underspecified queries before generating SQL. However, a correct execution result does not necessarily imply that the agent has adequately resolved the underlying underspecification: the agent may silently make unverified assumptions that happen to match the intended answer. We show that this behavior is driven in part by premature clarification termination. Although forcing an agent to ask more questions improves execution accuracy, ambiguities are concentrated in earlier interactions, making brute-force questioning inefficient. More importantly, even when explicitly prompted to plan its clarification process, the agent frequently abandons questions that it has already identified as relevant. To address this failure mode, we introduce PlanPool, which externalizes the clarification plan as a mutable question pool. Every planned question must be explicitly asked or dropped before submission, while newly discovered ambiguities can be added during interaction. Across three benchmarks derived from BIRD-Interact and Spider, PlanPool consistently improves ambiguity coverage and reduces silent failures over unconstrained and prompt-based alternatives, while maintaining competitive execution accuracy. Our results highlight an important distinction in agentic reasoning: identifying missing information is not sufficient, and the agent must also reliably maintain and resolve it before committing to an answer.

</details>


### [16] [EpiWorld: Grounding LLM Policy Agents in Epidemiological World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02744)
*Zeeshan Memon,Yiqi Su,Kai Shu,Naren Ramakrishnan,Liang Zhao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EpiWorld is a closed-loop framework that grounds an LLM in an epidemiological world model and structured public-health skills to design and refine intervention policies, achieving strong forecasting and hospitalization reduction on COVID-19 and Influenza benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pure LLM-based policy reasoning is ill-suited for epidemic interventions because LLMs lack embedded epidemic dynamics, quantitative surveillance capabilities, and awareness of institutional constraints, all of which are crucial for projecting intervention outcomes and ensuring realistic, admissible policies. There is a need for a framework that combines the interpretability and flexibility of natural-language policy reasoning with rigorous, data-driven epidemic modeling and operational public health protocols, enabling robust, adaptive, and explainable decision-making. Additionally, existing policy optimization methods like RL and optimal control are often less interpretable and harder to align with institutional constraints, creating demand for an alternative that can integrate experiential lessons over time while preserving control and transparency.

Method: The authors introduce EpiWorld, a closed-loop decision framework that couples a large language model policy actor with a learned, action-conditioned epidemiological world model and a tiered library of public health skills. The world model simulates regional epidemic trajectories under candidate interventions, enabling rapid counterfactual rollouts to evaluate and refine policy choices. The skill library encodes fixed protocol constraints, surveillance tools, and higher-level adaptive lessons distilled from prior simulated futures, allowing the LLM to reason in natural language while remaining grounded in operational constraints. The system iteratively proposes interventions, simulates their effects through the world model, summarizes outcomes into reusable lessons, and feeds this feedback back into the LLM for improved subsequent decision-making, all while preserving interpretability and controllability.

Result: On retrospective COVID-19 and Influenza datasets, the learned world model outperforms forecasting baselines in out-of-distribution settings, achieving the lowest Peak-MAE when predicting epidemic peaks. The full EpiWorld framework, combining the world model with the LLM and skill library, significantly reduces simulated cumulative hospitalizations compared to strong baselines. Specifically, it achieves up to a 59% reduction in cumulative hospitalizations in the best-case datasets and an average reduction of approximately 16% across six different LLM backbones, surpassing both reinforcement-learning-based and optimal-control-based policy optimization methods.

Conclusion: EpiWorld demonstrates that grounding LLM-based policy reasoning in a learned epidemiological world model and structured public-health skills yields more effective and controllable epidemic interventions than either purely generative or traditional control/RL approaches. The framework delivers state-of-the-art forecasting performance and substantial reductions in hospitalization metrics while maintaining interpretability and adherence to protocol constraints. This suggests a promising direction for decision support in public health: closed-loop, simulation-grounded LLM systems that can continuously accumulate and reuse lessons from prospective and retrospective analyses without sacrificing human-aligned oversight and control.

Abstract: Epidemic intervention policies are textual artefacts that human decision-makers interpret, justify, and revise through natural language, making large language models a natural candidate for epidemic policy reasoning. A naive LLM, however, lacks the epidemic dynamics needed to project intervention consequences, the quantitative surveillance signals required to assess severity, and the institutional constraints that define admissible actions. We present EpiWorld, a closed-loop framework that grounds an LLM policy actor in a learned action-conditioned epidemiological world model and a tiered skill library of public-health protocols, surveillance tools, and adaptive lessons accumulated through after-action analysis. Given a candidate intervention, the world model predicts regional epidemic evolution and enables fast counterfactual rollouts that provide feedback for policy selection and refinement. Outcomes of simulated futures are distilled into reusable lessons while protocol constraints remain fixed, allowing the decision process to improve without sacrificing interpretability or controllability. We evaluate both the world model and the end-to-end framework on retrospective COVID-19 and Influenza datasets: the world model achieves the best out-of-distribution Peak-MAE among all forecasting baselines, and the closed-loop framework reduces cumulative hospitalisation by up to 59% across datasets and by an average of ~16% across six LLM backbones, outperforming reinforcement-learning and optimal-control policy baselines.

</details>


### [17] [When History Fails to Become Experience: Action Calibration in Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02769)
*Jingyu Liu,Zhiwen Wang,Yuxin Jing,Huanyu Zhou,Yong Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how language-based agents use interaction history in tasks and proposes simple methods to help them use past actions and outcomes more effectively, improving task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although language agents in interactive environments should benefit from using prior attempts and feedback within a task, giving them more history sometimes hurts performance. This inconsistency suggests that current agents may not be utilizing interaction history properly, motivating a closer examination of how they process and exploit past actions and outcomes.

Method: The authors empirically analyze how language agents use historical interaction data in task-oriented settings. They compare performance when full histories are provided versus when past actions are shuffled relative to observations, thereby breaking the action–outcome alignment. They then introduce explicit outcome labeling, where each observation is tagged as the result of the immediately preceding action, and finally propose a learned calibrator module that re-evaluates past actions and selectively records experiences to guide future decisions.

Result: They find that access to interaction history generally improves task success, but much of this gain remains even when the correspondence between actions and outcomes is scrambled. This indicates that agents often do not rely on the true causal linkage between their actions and resulting observations. Explicitly labeling observations as outcomes of prior actions significantly boosts task success and reduces repetitive next actions. The learned calibrator further improves performance over outcome labeling alone by more strategically leveraging past experiences.

Conclusion: Current language agents do not robustly connect their past actions with resulting outcomes when deciding what to do next, limiting the benefit of interaction history. Simple structural changes in how history is presented—such as explicit outcome labeling—and the addition of a learned calibrator that reassesses and curates past experience can substantially enhance agents’ ability to exploit feedback and improve task performance within a single episode.

Abstract: Language agents should draw on prior attempts and environmental feedback to improve subsequent decisions within the same task. However, providing additional interaction history can sometimes reduce task success, suggesting that agents do not consistently use this information effectively. To investigate this limitation, we examine how agents use history. We find that history improves task completion overall, yet much of this benefit persists even when past actions are shuffled. Disrupting the correspondence between actions and observations causes only a modest decline in task success. We therefore hypothesize that agents do not reliably connect past actions with their outcomes when deciding how to proceed. To test this hypothesis, we explicitly label each returned observation as the outcome of the preceding action. This simple annotation improves task success and reduces next-action repetition without introducing new environmental information. Building on this insight, we introduce a learned calibrator that explicitly reassesses past actions and selectively records experience to guide subsequent decisions, improving task success beyond outcome labeling alone.

</details>


### [18] [AptMQL-Bench: From Text-to-SQL to Text-to-MQL via Access-Pattern Schema Design and Data-Preserving Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02770)
*Hy Nguyen,Nabi Rezvani,Robin Vujanic*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper builds a realistic benchmark for converting natural language questions to MongoDB queries and shows that current models still struggle on it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-to-MQL benchmarks are mostly created by mechanically converting text-to-SQL datasets using simple heuristics. These heuristic conversions often fail to migrate some databases, lose data, and create schemas that are inefficient for document stores, which undermines research on natural-language interfaces to document databases like MongoDB. A better, more realistic benchmark is needed.

Method: The authors design a new conversion pipeline to transform relational (SQLite) databases and text-to-SQL benchmarks into document-oriented databases and text-to-MQL benchmarks. Instead of mirroring relational schemas, they use coding agents plus human-in-the-loop verification to design MongoDB schemas around realistic access patterns and to rewrite each SQL query into MongoDB-native MQL queries. They then apply this pipeline to the BIRD benchmark to construct AptMQL-Bench.

Result: Their empirical analysis shows that prior heuristic conversion methods fail completely on 6 of 21 BIRD databases, drop up to 25.9% of rows on some migrated databases, and produce schemas whose ground-truth queries become more than 10x slower as data volume grows. In contrast, their new pipeline yields 21 fully migrated document databases from SQLite with no data loss and with queries that scale efficiently. On this new benchmark, the strongest evaluated model (Claude Opus 4.5) achieves 57.38% accuracy without external knowledge and 70.34% with external evidence, indicating substantial remaining difficulty.

Conclusion: Careful, access-pattern-guided schema design and query rewriting—implemented via coding agents with human oversight—produces a higher-quality, scalable benchmark for text-to-MQL, AptMQL-Bench, than previous heuristic conversions. The performance of a strong LLM on this benchmark shows that realistic natural-language interfaces to document databases remain an unsolved and challenging problem, highlighting the need for further research.

Abstract: Document databases such as MongoDB are core infrastructure for modern applications, and natural-language interfaces to them---text-to-MQL---would let non-experts query complex, semi-structured data without mastering the query language. Progress on this task depends on high-quality benchmarks, which are most practically obtained by converting an existing text-to-SQL benchmark to the document setting. Unfortunately, existing efforts rely on heuristics for mechanical conversion: the document schema mirrors the relational foreign-key graph, and each query mirrors its source SQL. As a result in our experiments, these approaches fail to migrate 6 of 21 BIRD databases outright, silently drop up to 25.9\% of rows on others, and yield schemas whose ground-truth queries run over an order of magnitude slower as the data scales. We instead propose a conversion pipeline, driven by coding agents with human-in-the-loop verification, that designs each document schema from expected access patterns and rewrites queries to be MongoDB-native. Applying it to BIRD, we build an access-pattern-based text-to-MQL benchmark (AptMQL-Bench). It includes 21 document-oriented databases, 3,186 natural-language requests, and their associated MQL queries---whose databases are migrated from SQLite without data loss and scale efficiently. The strongest model, Claude Opus 4.5, achieves only 57.38\% accuracy without external knowledge evidence and 70.34\% with it.
