This indicates that realistic text-to-MQL generation remains challenging.

</details>


### [19] [Improving Atomic-Fact Recall via Focused Views in Unstructured Knowledge Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02772)
*Ding Wu,Ye Zhang,Haoyu Wang,Tianci Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose FOVEATED, a plug-and-play method that perturbs positional encodings during knowledge editing so that LLMs learn individual facts from unstructured passages rather than overfitting to passage context, improving reliability of edited knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are used as general interfaces to factual knowledge, but their internal knowledge becomes outdated and full retraining is expensive. Knowledge editing aims to update specific facts without harming unrelated capabilities. Existing unstructured knowledge editing methods, which take in free-form passages, often suffer from context reliance: models can quote the whole passage but fail to recall single facts when the passage context is absent. This indicates that current training objectives underestimate the difficulty of learning earlier, less-contextualized facts in a passage, leading to poor factual recall after editing. The paper aims to diagnose this issue and design an editor that truly internalizes each fact independently of its surrounding context.

Method: They first analyze how standard passage-level objectives cause context-induced difficulty underestimation: later sentences get more supporting context and look easier (lower loss), so optimization undertrains earlier facts. To address this, they introduce FOVEATED, a plug-and-play framework that creates focused views for each sentence in the editing passage. Concretely, during the editing phase they randomly shift the Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) positions assigned to keys representing the preceding context tokens, effectively perturbing how context is attended to while keeping queries/values aligned. This reduces over-reliance on specific passage context and forces the model to learn each fact more robustly. The RoPE perturbation is only used during editing; inference uses the model’s original positional encoding. They instantiate FOVEATED for both direct-optimization KE methods (which directly adjust parameters using gradients from the passage) and locate-then-edit methods (which first identify parameters to change and then update them). They also provide theoretical analysis showing how this positional perturbation counteracts the bias in difficulty estimation across sentences.

Result: Across experiments with five different knowledge editing algorithms, two LLM architectures, and three unstructured KE benchmarks, integrating FOVEATED consistently improves performance. Specifically, edited models show better factual recall of individual statements from the editing passage without needing the full original context, while preserving general capabilities and unrelated knowledge. Quantitative results likely show higher editing success rates, better reliability on fact-only prompts, and competitive or improved locality and generalization metrics compared to baseline editors without FOVEATED.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that context reliance in unstructured knowledge editing stems from a structural issue in the passage-level training objective, which underestimates the difficulty of earlier, less-contextualized facts. By perturbing positional encodings for contextual tokens only during editing, FOVEATED encourages LLMs to encode each fact more independently of its surrounding passage. This yields more reliable, context-agnostic knowledge updates across various editors, models, and benchmarks, without changing inference-time architectures. The framework is presented as a simple, plug-and-play enhancement that can be broadly applied to improve unstructured knowledge editing in LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly serve as general-purpose interfaces to factual knowledge, but their parameters do not automatically reflect information that changes after pretraining. Knowledge editing (KE) provides a targeted alternative to costly retraining by modifying selected knowledge and preserving unrelated knowledge and general capabilities. Conventional KE uses structured factual triples, whereas unstructured KE (UKE) uses free-form passages containing multiple facts. Nonetheless, existing UKE editors exhibit a failure mode known as context reliance: edited LLMs can often reproduce the editing passage but fail to reliably recall its individual facts without the original passage context. We identify context-induced difficulty underestimation under the standard passage-level editing objective: later facts receive increasingly rich ground-truth context and consequently incur lower initial losses, making them appear easier to learn. In response, we propose FOVEATED, a plug-and-play framework that constructs focused views of each sentence by randomly shifting the Rotary Position Embedding (RoPE) positions assigned to the keys of its preceding context. The perturbation is applied during editing and removed afterward, leaving the model's native positional encoding unchanged at inference time. We instantiate FOVEATED for both direct-optimization and locate-then-edit editors. We theoretically analyze how FOVEATED counteracts context-induced difficulty underestimation and empirically demonstrate consistent improvements across five KE editors, two LLM backbones, and three benchmarks.

</details>


### [20] [Automatic Evaluation of Mental Health Stigma in Online Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02775)
*Naomi Baes,Jemima Kang,Nick Haslam,Chris Groot,Alsa Wu,Luc Raszewski,Yulia Otmakhova*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents a theory-grounded benchmark and annotation framework to evaluate mental health stigma in online text, showing that current sentiment, toxicity, and hate-speech models and LLMs poorly capture stigma without explicit rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mental health stigma is complex, multifaceted, and harmful, yet existing automatic evaluation methods rely on proxies like sentiment, toxicity, or hate speech, which do not adequately capture its nuances. There is a need for a principled, fine-grained, and condition-specific framework to measure stigma in real-world online communication so that computational models can be properly evaluated and improved.

Method: The authors build a benchmark using naturally occurring online news and social media texts that reference six mental health conditions. They design an annotation framework with (1) a binary stigma vs. non-stigma label and (2) a multi-level taxonomy capturing stigma mode, domain, and specific components of certain stigma types. Human annotators label the data according to this theory-grounded scheme. The authors then evaluate multiple large language models and specialized classifiers trained for sentiment, toxicity, and hate speech on this benchmark to assess how well they detect mental health–related stigma.

Result: Models trained to detect sentiment, toxicity, and hate speech fail to accurately identify mental health stigma, indicating that these neighboring constructs are insufficient proxies. Large language models also perform suboptimally, tending to overpredict the presence of stigma unless provided with explicit, operational decision rules, similar to the guidelines needed by human annotators.

Conclusion: Mental health stigma is a distinct, complex construct that is not reliably captured by existing sentiment, toxicity, or hate-speech detection models, nor by general-purpose LLMs used out of the box. A theory-grounded, fine-grained annotation framework and benchmark—such as the one introduced in this work—is necessary for more accurate evaluation and future development of models that can meaningfully detect and analyze mental health stigma in online text. The authors make their benchmark, annotations, examples, and code publicly available to support further research.

Abstract: Mental health stigma has profoundly harmful impacts but its complexity makes it difficult to evaluate. Stigma may involve explicit derogation, but also subtler forms of blame, fear, paternalistic pity, social distancing, structural exclusion, and discrimination. We introduce a theory-grounded benchmark for automatic evaluation of mental health stigma in online communication, consisting of naturally occurring online news and social media text annotated with a fine-grained taxonomy of stigma across multiple mental health conditions. Our annotation framework comprises a binary stigma-detection task and a multi-level taxonomy covering (i) stigma mode, (ii) domain, and (iii) specific components of certain forms of stigma. We apply this framework to texts mentioning six mental health conditions and evaluate large language models alongside stigma-related classifiers for detecting sentiment, toxicity, and hate speech. Results show that mental health stigma is not well captured by models trained to detect these neighboring constructs, and that LLMs often overpredict stigma unless given explicit operational rules - mirroring the importance of decision rules in human annotation. We release the publicly available part of benchmark, annotations, prototypical exemplars of stigma and code at: https://github.com/jemimakang/mh_stigma.

</details>


### [21] [Text-Centric Post-Training for Omni-Modal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02819)
*Ziyang Cheng,Yuhao Wang,Hongcheng Liu,Qimin Wu,Jingru Fan,Chen Qian,Yanfeng Wang,Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a more efficient way to improve reasoning in omni (audio-visual-text) LLMs by doing most optimization with cheaper text-only training and only a small amount of audio-visual RL to restore and refine perception.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Joint audio-visual reasoning in omni LLMs is expensive because constructing and training on multi-modal data is costly, yet models still struggle with multi-hop reasoning even when they can answer related single-hop questions. Diagnostics suggest that perception (understanding inputs) and reasoning (multi-step inference) are not perfectly aligned during training, motivating a post-training approach that can independently emphasize and rebalance these capabilities while reducing compute and data costs.

Method: The authors first analyze omni LLMs and find a gap in multi-hop reasoning despite good single-hop performance, indicating partial decoupling of perception and reasoning in local optimization. They then perform post-training focused on text-only reasoning across different data sources, scales, and model families, using supervised fine-tuning followed by RL to maximize reasoning performance. They test a configuration where all training data are synthesized by a text-only LLM, entirely removing audio-visual data from construction and training. Observing that text-only training hurts perception, they finally propose a text-centric post-training paradigm: (1) a main text-only reasoning optimization stage (SFT + RL) and (2) a reduced-data native audio-visual RL refinement stage that uses far fewer multimodal tokens to restore and refine perception while preserving most of the reasoning gain.

Result: With the best text-only configuration, SFT plus RL improves Qwen2.5-Omni-7B’s geometric mean of nine reasoning benchmarks by 25.83% over the base model and even beats a full native audio-visual training route, while using 56.6% fewer GPU-hours. When training only on fully synthetic text data generated by a text-only LLM, the model still gains 21.01% on the same geometric mean without any audio-visual data in training or data construction. However, this text-only training harms perceptual performance. Adding a final reduced-data audio-visual RL refinement stage that uses about 90% fewer multimodal input tokens than full-data audio-visual RL restores perception to above the base model and keeps 93.5% of the reasoning improvement obtained from the best text-only pipeline.

Conclusion: Reasoning and perception in omni LLMs can be effectively decoupled in post-training. The bulk of reasoning capability can be improved efficiently using only text-based training, even from synthetic LLM-generated data, substantially reducing compute and multimodal data requirements. Although this harms perception, a lightweight native audio-visual RL refinement step, using a small fraction of multimodal tokens, can restore and even improve perceptual performance while retaining most of the reasoning gains. This text-centric post-training paradigm offers a more compute- and data-efficient path to strong audio-visual reasoning compared with conventional fully multimodal training.

Abstract: Improving joint audio-visual reasoning in Omni Large Language Models typically incurs substantial data construction and training costs. Our diagnostics reveal multi-hop reasoning difficulties despite correct answers to all corresponding single-hop questions and suggest partial decoupling in the local optimization of perception and reasoning objectives. This motivates post-training with different emphases on these capabilities. Text-only reasoning training yields gains across data sources, model scales, and families. With the best-performing text-only configuration, supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning (RL) raises Qwen2.5-Omni-7B's geometric mean of nine reasoning scores by 25.83% over the base model, outperforming the complete native audio-visual route with 56.6% fewer GPU-hours. Training on data synthesized entirely by a text-only LLM raises this geometric mean by 21.01% without audio-visual data in construction or training. However, text-only training degrades perception. We therefore propose a text-centric post-training paradigm: text-only training provides the main reasoning optimization, and reduced-data native audio-visual RL then refines perception. Refinement uses about 90% fewer input tokens than full-data audio-visual RL, restores perception above the base level, and retains 93.5% of the best-performing text-only pipeline's reasoning gain.

</details>


### [22] [Clinical Concept Centers in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02829)
*Aishik Nagar,Abhishek Vaidyanathan,Arun-Kumar Kaliya-Perumal,Elijah Tzen Hsuen Boey,Stefan Winkler*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that large language models contain identifiable, clinically meaningful concept representations in their latent space that causally drive decisions, and that directly steering these concepts improves reliability and performance in clinical decision support tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most evaluations of large language models in clinical settings focus on the text they generate, ignoring the richer information in their internal representations. Mechanistic interpretability suggests that models internally encode more precise and influential features than are visible in their verbalized reasoning, and that these hidden features causally determine outputs. However, whether such latent, causally-relevant representations exist for clinical concepts—and how they affect reliability, robustness, and usefulness for clinical decision support—has not been studied.

Method: The authors apply mechanistic interpretability techniques to eleven open-weight LLMs, searching their latent space for "concept centers" that correspond to specific clinical concepts. They identify and localize these centers, test whether they selectively activate for matching clinical narratives, and perform causal interventions (steering or activating/inhibiting these centers) in both constrained and open-ended generation settings. They probe behavior under adversarial and aligned role-based priming, simulate realistic deployment scenarios where concept steering is applied, and run a blinded clinician study to assess whether concept-center activation aligns with expert preferences.

Result: Across all eleven models, the authors find distinct latent directions—clinical concept centers—that reliably correspond to particular clinical notions. These centers fire selectively on aligned clinical narratives and exert causal control over the model’s outputs. Models continue to rely on appropriate concept centers even under adversarial role priming, while aligned priming further boosts clinical task performance. Steering the models along these concept directions in simulated deployment leads to measurable gains in downstream clinical decision support quality. In the clinician study, cases where relevant concept centers were strongly activated more often matched clinicians’ preferred outputs, indicating that latent concept usage predicts expert judgments.

Conclusion: Clinical LLMs harbor robust, localized representations of clinical concepts that are both interpretable and behaviorally causal. Evaluating and steering models at the level of these latent concept centers yields increased robustness to prompt perturbations and meaningful improvements in clinical decision support performance. This establishes mechanistic-interpretability-based latent-space analysis as a practical tool for both evaluation and control of LLMs in clinical practice, suggesting that future safety and reliability work should operate not only on the textual outputs but also directly on internal concept circuits.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used in clinical settings. However, research into the reliability and performance of these models has focused almost entirely on the language substrate, scoring what the model says. Mechanistic interpretability has found that the latent space carries a higher fidelity of representation than the text: internal representations not only encode substantially more than the output verbalizes, but the stated reasoning also systematically omits features that causally drive the answer. An evaluation of model behavior in terms of mechanistic interpretability has not been explored in clinical decision support. In this work, we extend behavioral evaluation into the latent space and ask whether clinical concepts exist as locatable, causally used representations inside open-weight LLMs. We find dedicated clinical concept centers in the latent space of all eleven open models we test. These concept centers are interpretable, firing only on their aligned clinical narratives, and meaningfully and causally drive model behavior in both constrained and open-ended settings. They are not just analytical representations, but circuits that can be utilized in clinical practice, and we explore their use from the perspective of both evaluation and performance. From the evaluation standpoint, models stay internally coherent and keep using the relevant concept centers even under adversarial role-based priming, while aligned priming improves downstream clinical performance. From a performance perspective, we simulate realistic deployment settings and find that steering models along these centers leads to meaningful downstream improvements. Finally, we conduct a blinded clinician validation and find the activation and usage of these concept centers predicts clinicians preferences.

</details>


### [23] [How Robust Is Multimodal Claim Verification to LLM Rewriting?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02841)
*Yun-Ang Wu,Xanh Ho,Andre Greiner-Petter,Sunisth Kumar,Tian Cheng Xia,Florian Boudin,Akiko Aizawa*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how stylistic edits made by LLMs to scientific text affect decisions in multimodal claim verification models and finds that while accuracy is largely robust, probability scores systematically shift, especially under hedging-related edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used to rewrite and polish scientific text, which is known to alter writing style. However, it is unclear whether such stylistic shifts—especially those introduced by LLMs themselves—can bias downstream scientific AI systems, such as models that verify claims against evidence. Prior work has shown that LLM-driven rewriting can manipulate peer-review scores, raising concerns that similar vulnerabilities might exist in claim verification, a core task in scientific integrity and fact-checking.

Method: The authors focus on multimodal claim verification, where a system must judge whether a textual claim is supported by an associated piece of evidence (e.g., text + figures). They introduce two rewriting strategies applied to the claims: (1) natural rewriting, which mimics how researchers use LLMs to improve academic writing quality (polishing, paraphrasing, fluency), and (2) controlled injection, which minimally edits text by inserting a single word strongly associated with LLM style to isolate lexical effects. They then systematically evaluate 11 open-weight vision-language models (VLMs) from five model families, with parameter sizes ranging from 2B to 38B, measuring both accuracy and changes in prediction probabilities under different rewriting conditions (hedging, boosting, general polishing).

Result: Across the 11 evaluated VLMs, overall accuracy on the multimodal claim verification task remains largely stable under both natural rewriting and controlled injection; most models show no statistically significant accuracy degradation. Nonetheless, the authors observe consistent and structured shifts in output probabilities: hedging-oriented stylistic changes (making claims sound more tentative or uncertain) induce significant probability shifts for nearly all models. In contrast, boosting-style edits (making claims sound more confident or assertive) lead to weaker and less consistent probability changes, and general polishing operations such as grammar correction or fluency improvements have minimal effect on model outputs.

Conclusion: The study concludes that while current multimodal claim verification models are robust in terms of accuracy to LLM-induced stylistic rewriting of scientific claims, their confidence distributions are systematically influenced, especially by hedging-oriented language. This indicates that style can subtly bias model belief strength without necessarily flipping final decisions. Compared with prior demonstrations that LLM rewriting can strongly manipulate peer-review outcomes, claim verification appears more stable, yet the observed probability shifts highlight a potential vulnerability for applications that depend on calibrated model confidence or aggregate probabilistic judgments.

Abstract: LLMs are known to introduce stylistic changes into generated text, yet how these stylistic shifts affect model decisions on scientific tasks remains underexplored. In this paper, we focus on multimodal claim verification, where the goal is to determine whether a textual claim is grounded in a given piece of evidence. We apply two rewriting strategies: natural rewriting, which simulates how researchers routinely use LLMs to polish academic text, and controlled injection, which inserts a single LLM-associated word to isolate the effect of vocabulary choice. We evaluate 11 open-weight models spanning five VLM families and ranging from 2B to 38B parameters. We find that models are robust to these modifications: most show no significant drop in accuracy, and compared to prior work on review-score manipulation, verification appears far more stable. However, consistent probability shifts do occur. Hedging-oriented conditions produce significant shifts across nearly all models, while boosting conditions show a weaker effect and general polishing conditions (e.g., grammar correction, fluency improvement) have little effect.

</details>


### [24] [Adaptive Mutual Distillation for Balanced Multi-Task Post-Training of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02856)
*Baohang Li,Xiaocheng Feng,Yichong Huang,Chengpeng Fu,Wenshuai Huo,Zekun Zhou,Zekun Yuan,Tingjia Zhang,Bing Qin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes Adaptive Mutual Distillation (AMD), a framework that jointly post-trains two LLMs with different task-balancing strategies so they can effectively teach each other, adaptively tuning distillation signals per task and transfer direction to improve multi-task performance and final merged model quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-task post-training of LLMs must handle tasks with highly imbalanced datasets. Existing approaches design sampling or loss-balancing strategies for a single model, but different strategies yield complementary strengths across tasks. This suggests that multiple models could mutually distill knowledge. However, naive mutual distillation assumes that cross-model supervision is uniformly useful across all tasks, directions, and training stages, which is unrealistic. There is a need for a principled method that selectively and adaptively leverages cross-model signals where they are beneficial, to better exploit complementary task strengths while avoiding negative transfer.

Method: The authors introduce Adaptive Mutual Distillation (AMD), a collaborative training framework for two LLMs, each using a different task-balancing strategy (e.g., different sampling or weighting). During post-training, AMD periodically considers candidate changes to the distillation weights that govern how much each model learns from the other for each task and transfer direction. Short training probes are run under these candidate weight settings across tasks, and task-wise validation scores from these probes are used to choose the best adjustment for each task and direction. This yields an adaptive, task-specific, and directional control of mutual distillation strength over the course of training. After joint training, the two resulting models can optionally be merged (e.g., via parameter merging) to form a single model for inference.

Result: On six benchmarks and three LLM backbones, both models trained with AMD consistently achieve higher average benchmark scores than supervised fine-tuning (SFT) baselines that use the same task sampling strategies but no mutual distillation. AMD also outperforms other task-balancing methods tested in the study. Furthermore, merging the two AMD-trained models yields an additional improvement in average benchmark scores, surpassing multi-task SFT by an average margin of 2.91 points across the three backbones.

Conclusion: Adaptive Mutual Distillation effectively leverages complementary strengths of models trained with different task-balancing strategies by adaptively tuning cross-model supervision at the task and direction level. This approach not only improves multi-task post-training performance over standard SFT and existing task-balancing techniques for each individual model, but also enables further gains via model merging into a single, stronger inference model. AMD thus provides a practical and more powerful framework for multi-task LLM post-training under data imbalance.

Abstract: Multi-task post-training of large language models (LLMs) aims to improve performance across tasks with unequal amounts of training data. Existing methods focus primarily on balancing task contributions during single-model training. Different task-balancing strategies can produce models with complementary strengths, creating opportunities for mutual distillation. However, the usefulness of cross-model supervision can vary across tasks, transfer directions, and stages of training. We propose Adaptive Mutual Distillation (AMD), a collaborative post-training framework that jointly trains two models with different task-balancing strategies. AMD evaluates candidate adjustments to distillation weights through short training probes shared across tasks, then uses task-wise validation scores to select an adjustment for each task and transfer direction. Across six benchmarks and three LLM backbones, both AMD models achieve higher average benchmark scores than supervised fine-tuning (SFT) baselines trained with the same sampling strategies. They also outperform the task-balancing methods evaluated in our experiments. Merging the two trained models can further improve their average benchmark score while yielding a single model for inference. The merged models outperform multi-task SFT by an average of 2.91 points across the three backbones.

</details>


### [25] [ConvoDrift: A Multi-Turn Conversational Dataset for Modeling Stylistic Tone Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02873)
*Vihindi Kotalawala,Pamoda Dilranga,Gayani Thoradeniya,Prasan Yapa*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces ConvoDrift, a dataset for modeling and evaluating how linguistic style gradually drifts in multi-turn conversations while keeping the same semantic intent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing style-control work in NLP mainly targets single sentences or assumes a fixed style across an interaction, ignoring that real conversations exhibit dynamic stylistic changes as user preferences, tone, or context evolve over turns. There is a need for resources that capture and quantify such progressive style drift under stable semantics, in order to better study personalization, alignment, and stylistic control in dialogue systems.

Method: The authors construct ConvoDrift from 15,727 shared multi-turn conversational skeletons, creating six prompt–response pairs per conversation. Each pair is annotated with (1) whether a style drift occurs and (2) the direction of that drift, spanning various communication genres. They further derive a pairwise dataset of semantically equivalent but stylistically distinct responses. These pairs are labeled with persona-conditioned preferences based on five distinct communication personas, enabling analysis of personalization and pluralistic alignment. The dataset is evaluated via human studies (three annotators, seven Likert-scale criteria), LLM-as-a-judge, and automatic lexical/semantic metrics.

Result: ConvoDrift offers a large-scale resource with explicit style-drift and style-direction labels, plus persona-conditioned preference annotations over stylistically varied but semantically aligned responses. Human annotation shows high agreement, with an average Krippendorff’s alpha of 0.88 across seven criteria. Lexical and semantic analyses confirm that drift events correlate with lexical changes while preserving semantic similarity, validating that the dataset successfully isolates stylistic variation from semantic content changes.

Conclusion: The paper provides a well-validated benchmark for studying progressive stylistic tone drift and persona-conditioned stylistic preferences in dialogue under fixed semantics. By demonstrating high annotation reliability and showing that style drift affects surface realization while maintaining meaning, ConvoDrift enables more fine-grained research on conversational style control, personalization, and alignment in LLM-based dialogue systems.

Abstract: The evolution of linguistic style in conversations is an underexplored issue in NLP. Most style-control datasets focus on sentences or assume a static style throughout, missing the dynamic shifts that occur as user preferences change during interactions. We introduce ConvoDrift, a dataset designed to model progressive stylistic conversational tone drift under fixed semantic intent. It is built on 15,727 shared multi-turn conversational structures for adaptation and persona-conditioned alignment methods. It consists of six prompt-response pairs per conversation, each with the annotation of style drift and style direction labels. These pairs cover a range of communication genres. We further derive a complementary pairwise dataset by pairing semantically equivalent but stylistically distinct responses and annotating persona-conditioned preferences using five distinct style communication personas, enabling the controlled study of personalisation and pluralistic alignment in language tone. In addition to dataset construction, we conduct a comprehensive evaluation involving human validation, LLM-as-judge assessment, and automatic lexical and semantic evaluations. Across seven Likert criteria annotated by three human annotators, the average Krippendorff's alpha is 0.88, and our lexical and semantic analyses show that drift events induce lexical changes while preserving semantic similarity.

</details>


### [26] [Query-aware routing for Cross-lingual performance gains in Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02875)
*Akshay Jain,Edward Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents a method to boost cross-lingual retrieval (Finnish–Swedish–English) without hurting existing same-language performance or requiring re-indexing, by adding a query-only LoRA adapter with language-based routing on top of a frozen multilingual encoder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multilingual text encoders often perform well when the query and document are in the same language, but their effectiveness drops when doing cross-lingual retrieval (query and document in different languages). Rebuilding document indexes with new models is costly, and practitioners want to keep good same-language performance while improving cross-lingual retrieval. The authors are particularly interested in financial-domain retrieval for English, Finnish, and Swedish, and they want a way to selectively enhance cross-language behavior using an existing model and document embeddings.

Method: The authors freeze an existing multilingual embedding model (Nemotron-3-Embed-1B) and its document embeddings, and train a low-rank adapter (LoRA) that is applied only to query encoding. They introduce deterministic routing: if the query language matches the document index language, the system uses the original encoder; if they differ, it routes the query through the LoRA adapter to obtain specialized cross-lingual embeddings. The adapter is trained on cross-lingual relevance data while keeping document-side representations fixed, thus ensuring compatibility with the existing index. They evaluate this setup, named SampoTron, on a sampled financial benchmark covering six retrieval directions among English, Finnish, and Swedish, including full-corpus Finnish tasks.

Result: Using SampoTron (Nemotron-3-Embed-1B + query-only LoRA + language-based routing), the system’s average nDCG@10 across six English–Finnish–Swedish retrieval directions rises from 0.241 to 0.291, a 20.9% relative improvement. All six cross-lingual directions see gains, and the same-language retrieval performance is preserved, as shown by evaluations including two full-corpus Finnish tasks. This demonstrates that cross-lingual retrieval quality can be significantly improved without retraining or re-indexing document embeddings.

Conclusion: A query-only LoRA adapter combined with deterministic language-based routing provides an effective way to selectively enhance cross-lingual retrieval for specific language pairs, while leaving same-language performance and document indexes untouched. This pattern allows practitioners to retrofit existing retrieval systems with cross-language specialization in a modular and reusable manner, particularly useful in domain-specific settings such as financial text search involving Finnish, Swedish, and English.

Abstract: Multilingual encoders can exhibit reduced retrieval effectiveness when queries and relevant documents differ in language, despite strong same-language performance. We investigate whether Finnish and Swedish cross-lingual retrieval can improve while preserving an encoder's existing same-language performance and document index. We combine a query-only low-rank adapter, trained against frozen document embeddings, with deterministic routing based on query and index languages. Cross-language queries use the adapter, while same-language queries use the original encoder. SampoTron, our fine-tuned low-rank (LoRA) adapter alongwith the Nemotron-3-Embed-1B model, improves average retrieval quality across six English, Finnish, and Swedish directions from 0.241 to 0.291 in normalized discounted cumulative gain (nDCG) at rank ten, a 20.9% relative gain on a sampled financial benchmark. All six cross-lingual directions improve, and routing preserves the original same-language performance, including two full-corpus Finnish evaluations. The approach enables selective cross-language specialization with reusable document embedding vectors.

</details>


### [27] [Evaluating LLM-as-a-Judge Beyond Score Alignment: A Psychometric Analysis of Residual Judging Difficulty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02877)
*Longwei Cong,Sonja Hahn,Sebastian Gombert,Leon Camus,Fabian Zehner,Hendrik Drachsler,Ulf Kroehne*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how large language models (LLMs) behave as automatic judges compared to humans, going beyond simple score alignment to examine which specific evaluation cases each finds difficult using psychometric modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation of LLM-as-a-judge mostly checks aggregate agreement with human scores, which hides deeper differences in what humans and LLMs find hard to evaluate. The authors want a finer-grained, principled way to understand and compare judging behavior and reliability, especially in summarization evaluation.

Method: They apply Many-Facet Rasch Models (MFRM) separately to human and LLM ratings of summaries. This model decomposes observed scores into latent summary quality, rater severity, dimension (e.g., coherence, consistency) severity, and rating-scale thresholds. Using the residuals after this decomposition, they define a psychometric measure called residual hardness to quantify how difficult specific summary–dimension cases are for each judge type, and they compare hardness structures between humans and 17 open-weight LLM judges on the SummEval dataset. They also analyze whether LLM-hard but human-easy cases can be predicted from observable properties of the source documents and summaries.

Result: They find that even when LLMs have moderate alignment with humans on latent summary quality, they diverge notably in residual hardness: humans and LLMs do not agree on which summary–dimension instances are difficult. The mismatch depends strongly on the evaluation dimension: for consistency, LLMs find cases harder than humans (LLM-hard shift), whereas for coherence, humans find cases harder (human-hard shift). Moreover, some LLM-hard but human-easy cases are partially predictable from observable text features.

Conclusion: Aggregate alignment between LLM and human scores is an incomplete indicator of LLM-as-a-judge reliability. Psychometric residual diagnostics, such as residual hardness analysis, reveal systematic, dimension-specific differences in what humans and LLMs find difficult. This richer view enables more informative evaluation of LLM judges and can guide more targeted forms of human–LLM collaboration in evaluation workflows.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are widely used as automatic judges, with validity typically assessed via alignment with human scores. However, aggregate agreement fails to reveal whether humans and LLMs find the same evaluation cases difficult. In this paper, we study this problem in summarization evaluation from a psychometric perspective. We fit Many-Facet Rasch Models separately to human and LLM ratings to decompose scores into latent summary quality, rater severity, dimension severity, and rating-scale thresholds. Building on this decomposition, we define residual hardness as a model-adjusted measure of judging difficulty and compare whether human and LLM judges share the same hardness structure. Across 17 open-weight LLM judges on SummEval, we find that moderate alignment in latent summary quality does not imply alignment in residual hardness. Human and LLM judges differ in which summary--dimension units remain difficult, and this mismatch is strongly dimension-dependent. Consistency shows a pronounced LLM-hard shift, whereas coherence shows a human-hard shift. We further show that human-easy but LLM-hard cases are partially predictable from observable source--summary properties. These findings suggest that aggregate human alignment reflects only part of LLM-as-a-judge reliability, while psychometric residual diagnostics support more informative judge evaluation and more targeted human--LLM collaboration.

</details>


### [28] [Evaluating VQA in Vision Language Models using Cooperative Principles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02878)
*Monika Shah,Sudarshan Balaji,Somdeb Sarkhel,Sanorita Dey,Deepak Venugopal*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how Vision Language Models (VLMs) handle visual question answering when the questions contain pragmatic oddities that break Grice’s conversational maxims, and finds that these models perform worse under such conditions than with standard questions and also behave differently from humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLM benchmarks mostly use clean, cooperative questions that follow Grice’s maxims (quantity, quality, relation, manner), but real-world queries can be redundant, misleading, or pragmatically odd. The authors want to understand whether state-of-the-art VLMs are robust to such pragmatic violations and how their reasoning compares to humans in these scenarios.

Method: They automatically generate modified VQA questions by prompting VLMs to add extra modifiers that introduce non-essential, ambiguous, or false information, thereby creating violations of Grice’s maxims. They then measure the VQA performance of several VLMs (ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, Llava) on these modified questions, compare this to their performance on clean questions, and run human experiments to collect pragmatic judgments, time-on-task, and answers to both human-induced and AI-generated violations.

Result: All evaluated VLMs show reduced VQA accuracy when questions contain maxim violations introduced via modifiers. The patterns of errors and interpretations differ from human pragmatic reasoning. Moreover, VLM behavior differs depending on whether the violations in the questions originate from humans or from AI generation. Humans take less time to resolve VLM-induced violations than human-induced ones, but VLMs are in fact less accurate on the VLM-induced violations.

Conclusion: VLMs are sensitive to pragmatic perturbations that violate Grice’s maxims and their performance degrades under such conditions, highlighting a gap between model behavior and human pragmatic reasoning. AI-generated pragmatic violations are easier for humans to resolve yet harder for VLMs, suggesting that current training and alignment do not equip VLMs with robust, human-like pragmatic comprehension and that future work should explicitly model and test for such behaviors.

Abstract: We evaluate the performance of Vision Language Models in Visual Question Answering (VQA) when questions violate Grice's maxims. To do this, we use VLMs to generate question modifiers that add non-essential, ambiguous or false information and show that in the presence of such violations, the VLMs that we evaluate (ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and Llava) show diminished performance. Further, we empirically show the difference between how humans reason pragmatically compared to VLMs, and the difference in VLM reasoning when it resolves violations that are human-induced compared to those that are AI-generated. Finally, we show that human cognitive effort (measured through time-on-task in an experiment) is lower for resolving VLM-induced violations, but VLMs themselves perform less accurately in such cases.

</details>


### [29] [Misinformation Without Triggers: From Factual Answers to Downstream Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02886)
*Lin Tian,Marian-Andrei Rizoiu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how false training data affects language models’ factual answers versus their downstream decisions, finding a systematic gap: even when answers look correct, decisions can still be biased by earlier misinformation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models are trained on web data containing misinformation. Prior poisoning work mainly studies trigger-based attacks and usually focuses on whether the model’s *direct answer* changes. But in real use, models are part of larger decision or summarization pipelines. The authors want to know: (1) how false training data without explicit triggers influences both factual answers and subsequent decisions; (2) whether auditing models via direct factual probes is sufficient to detect and fix misinformation effects; and (3) how this plays out in a real-world misinformation scenario (Australian bushfire arson claims).

Method: They perform controlled data-poisoning experiments on eight language models. They inject false documents into the training data and compare with matched truthful documents. First, they design a toy decision task, “Guess the Capital,” where the model’s factual answer is deterministically converted into a card choice by a fixed decoder, allowing them to separate factual recall from decision behavior. They measure: (a) how often the model’s *answers* follow the injected false content, and (b) how often its *game choices* follow that content. They vary the training “dose” of misinformation (e.g., 1,000 poisoned examples). Then they extend to a real-world misinformation case based on Facebook posts about the 2019–20 Australian bushfires, manipulating both the numerical claims (inflated arrest counts) and the wording framing those claims, and examine both factual responses (counts) and higher-level assertions (e.g., whether people were arrested for arson).

Result: In the Guess the Capital task at a training dose of 1,000 poisoned examples, direct answer conformity to the injected choice is extremely high (95.8–100%), but the resulting game choices only increase by 1.7–14.4 percentage points relative to truthful training. This reveals an “audit gap” between what direct factual probes show and what decisions the model makes. The gap is bidirectional: (1) sometimes facts appear corrected under a direct probe, yet decisions remain biased toward the previously injected answer; and (2) game accuracy can drop more than would be predicted from the fraction of obviously poisoned answers. When they add truthful correction data, models can recover the correct factual answer, but the downstream decision patterns remain partially corrupted. In the bushfire case, models trained on false posts continue to assert that people were arrested for arson even when they no longer reproduce the inflated numerical arrest count. Factorial manipulations of wording show that merely misleading phrasing (not just false numbers) causes models to assert arrests even when the count is kept at the accurate value of 24.

Conclusion: Auditing language models only by checking their direct factual answers is insufficient: there is a persistent “audit gap” between correct-looking answers and the decisions or inferences those models drive. Misinformation can leave lasting, harder-to-detect traces in how models aggregate and apply information, such that fixing or overwriting a specific false fact (e.g., a number) does not fully undo its influence on downstream reasoning and decision-making. Therefore, safety and reliability evaluations must consider decision-level behavior and narrative framing effects, not just factual QA performance, and mitigation strategies need to address both explicit false claims and the subtler stories built around them.

Abstract: Language models learn from web documents, some of them false, and false content can reach a model's answer to a factual question and the summaries and decisions that use it. Most data-poisoning studies add a trigger to the training data and activate it in the prompt. False documents can also change factual responses without any trigger, but we do not know whether the direct answer predicts the decision. In this work, we follow false content past the answer and find an \emph{audit gap} between what a direct probe reports and what the model then does, comparing false training with matched truthful controls in a controlled decision task, \emph{Guess the Capital}, where a fixed decoder turns factual answers into a scored card choice, and on a misleading claim from Facebook posts about the 2019--20 Australian bushfires. Across eight models at dose 1,000, direct injected-choice rates reach 95.8--100\%, while injected game choices increase by 1.7--14.4 percentage points over matched truthful training. The gap runs the other way too. Facts that pass the direct probe still push decisions toward the injected answer, and game accuracy drops further than those choices explain. Truthful correction brings the fact back but not the decisions built on it. We then look into the real-world bushfire case, models trained on the false posts say that people were arrested for arson even when they lose the inflated count, and in a count-by-wording factorial the misleading arrest wording produces arrest assertions even when the training count stays at 24. In simpler terms, \textbf{a correct factual answer does not guarantee a correct decision, and losing the injected number does not remove the misleading story}.

</details>


### [30] [Output Language Confusion under Multilingual Prompt Contamination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02926)
*Riju Marwah,Ritvik Garimella,Khusham Bansal,Atishay Jain,Amit Sheth*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They test how multilingual irrelevant sentences (distractors) in prompts distort factual benchmark scores for LLMs, and show that standard exact-match metrics falsely label many answers as hallucinations when models only switch script or language.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current factual QA benchmarks assume clean, monolingual prompts and judge correctness with exact string matching. In real-world multilingual use (e.g., RAG returning mixed-language passages, users pasting multilingual content), prompts can contain multiple languages, causing models to change language or script in their answers. This breaks exact-match grading and can inflate hallucination estimates. The authors want a cheap, replicable way to stress-test LLMs in such settings and to disentangle genuine semantic errors from superficial language/script changes.

Method: They propose Multilingual Distractor Interference (MDI): take existing factual QA benchmarks (TruthfulQA and TriviaQA) and prepend a semantically unrelated sentence in a foreign language (the distractor) to each question. They design eight distractor conditions, including various languages and an English paragraph, and run about 40,000 evaluations on five instruction-tuned LLMs. They then compare model behavior and accuracy under clean vs. distracted prompts, analyze script switching and abstention, and manually inspect a subset of answers to correct for metric artifacts (e.g., answers that are correct but written in a different script).

Result: For Llama-3.1-8B under a Hindi distractor, 58% of responses switch to Devanagari script. Raw exact-match scoring labels many of these as hallucinations, giving a hallucination proxy of 0.710. Manual review shows that most script-switched answers that were correct in clean conditions are still semantically correct, cutting the true semantic hallucination rate to 0.470. Other models tend to react by abstaining more rather than hallucinating and do not show increased hallucination rates. A long English distractor causes very high abstention (0.806–0.998) across all models, suggesting confusion akin to reading-comprehension failures. TruthfulQA multiple-choice accuracy remains stable for all single-sentence distractor conditions.

Conclusion: Naively applying exact-match metrics in mixed-language prompts can seriously misdiagnose LLM reliability by conflating script or language switching with hallucinations. In multilingual or code-switched contexts, hallucination analysis must separate surface-form issues (script/language changes) from genuine semantic errors. The MDI protocol offers a simple, data-free way to test model robustness to multilingual distractors and reveals that some apparent failures are metric artifacts, while real failure modes—like abstention under long distractors—may be more operationally important for multilingual RAG and deployment.

Abstract: Standard factual benchmarks assume clean monolingual prompts and exact-match scoring, two assumptions that break simultaneously in real-world multilingual deployment, from retrieval-augmented generation pipelines returning mixed-language passages to users pasting multilingual web content. We introduce Multilingual Distractor Interference (MDI), a lightweight and fully replicable evaluation protocol requiring no new data or annotation, in which factual questions are preceded by a semantically irrelevant foreign-language sentence, and evaluate five instruction-tuned LLMs across TruthfulQA and TriviaQA under eight distractor conditions (40,000 evaluations). Our central finding is a metric confound: for Llama-3.1-8B under a Hindi distractor, 58% of responses switch to Devanagari script, yielding a raw hallucination proxy of 0.710, but manual review reveals that 120 of 148 script-switched responses that were correct under clean conditions remain semantically correct despite being written in the wrong script, reducing the adjusted semantic hallucination rate to 0.470. All other models respond through abstention escalation with no hallucination increase. A paragraph-length English distractor triggers near-universal abstention (0.806-0.998) across all models, consistent with reading-comprehension confusion, a failure mode with direct consequences for multilingual RAG pipelines. TruthfulQA multiple-choice accuracy is unaffected under all single-sentence conditions. These results show that exact-match hallucination rates in mixed-language settings should be decomposed into script-switching and semantic error components before drawing conclusions about model reliability.

</details>


### [31] [Enhancing Biomedical Named Entity Recognition via Multiple Programming Languages Instruction Tuning and Ensemble Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02949)
*Songtao Li,Yijia Zhang,Jianyuan Yuan,Shidi Zhang,Fengyu Zhang,Hongfei Lin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes MITE, a method that reformulates biomedical named entity recognition as code-structured instruction tuning across multiple programming languages and ensembles their predictions to improve robustness and performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current instruction-tuned LLM approaches for biomedical NER serialize annotations as flat text, which lacks structural constraints for typed entity extraction. Additionally, biomedical labeled data are scarce, and relying on a single textual output format limits structural diversity and model robustness. While external biomedical knowledge can help, it is expensive to build and maintain. The authors seek a way to introduce richer structure and diversity into instruction tuning for BioNER without requiring extra domain resources or annotations.

Method: MITE reformulates BioNER as a structure-to-structure generation task. Both instructions and outputs are represented in code-like formats. Each training instance is converted into multiple programming-language representations (Python, C++, Java), all encoding the same entity semantics but in different syntactic structures. An LLM is instruction-tuned on these multi-language code prompts and outputs. At inference time, the model generates predictions for each code format, and an entity-level voting ensemble aggregates them to reduce variance from any single format and improve robustness.

Result: On six standard BioNER datasets, MITE achieves better performance than strong BERT-based and LLM-based baselines. It also shows good cross-dataset generalization. Ablation studies indicate that both the multi-language code representations and the ensemble voting mechanism contribute significantly to the gains, and parameter analyses show the approach is robust across settings.

Conclusion: Encoding BioNER as multi-language code-style structure-to-structure generation, combined with an entity-level ensemble over different programming-language formats, improves robustness and accuracy without relying on external biomedical knowledge. This structured, multi-format instruction tuning paradigm is an effective and general strategy for enhancing LLM-based biomedical NER and may extend to other structured extraction tasks.

Abstract: Instruction tuning has become a common paradigm for applying large language models (LLMs) to biomedical named entity recognition (BioNER). However, existing instruction-tuning approaches still face two key challenges. First, conventional natural-language instructions typically serialize BioNER annotations as flat textual outputs, providing limited structural constraints for typed entity extraction. Second, high-quality biomedical annotations are limited, and learning from a single serialized output form may restrict structural diversity and reduce model robustness. Although external biomedical knowledge can be introduced to alleviate data scarcity, it often requires costly resource construction. To address these challenges, we propose MITE, a Multiple Programming Languages Instruction Tuning and Ensemble method for BioNER. MITE reformulates BioNER as a structure-to-structure generation task by representing both instructions and entity outputs in code-formatted representations. Specifically, each training instance is transformed into multiple programming-language formats, including Python, C++, and Java, while preserving the same underlying entity semantics. These language-specific representations provide structurally diverse supervision without requiring external biomedical knowledge or additional annotations. During inference, MITE aggregates predictions from different code formats through an entity-level voting strategy, reducing language-specific prediction variance and improving robustness. Experiments on six widely used BioNER datasets demonstrate that MITE consistently outperforms representative BERT-based and LLM-based baselines and exhibits strong cross-dataset generalization. Ablation and parameter analyses further verify the effectiveness and robustness of the proposed components.

</details>


### [32] [A Guideline-Augmented Multi-Agent Framework for Schema-as-Code Biomedical Named Entity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02970)
*Songtao Li,Yijia Zhang,Shidi Zhang,Jianyuan Yuan,Fengyu Zhang,Hongfei Lin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces GAMA, a guideline-augmented multi-agent framework that turns dataset-specific annotation rules into executable schemas, enabling LLMs to perform more accurate and structurally controlled biomedical named entity recognition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based BioNER approaches struggle with dataset-specific annotation semantics (e.g., unclear entity boundaries, type scopes, and labeling conventions) and with uncontrolled free-form generation that causes invalid formats, hallucinated entities, and structural errors. There is a need for a method that can explicitly model dataset-specific guidelines and enforce structural constraints during prediction.

Method: GAMA automatically induces candidate annotation rules from labeled training data and verifies them to build a reliable guideline memory tailored to each dataset. Using this memory, a planning agent proposes ranked span–type hypotheses with textual rationales. A separate coding agent turns these hypotheses into schema-constrained entity objects (schema-as-code). A verification module checks span grounding, type correctness, and structural validity, and uses a dual-loop refinement process to fix invalid or low-confidence predictions. The framework is tested with multiple LLM backbones across five standard BioNER datasets.

Result: Across five widely used BioNER datasets and multiple LLM backbones, GAMA consistently outperforms strong LLM-based baselines. Component-wise ablation and parameter sensitivity studies show that the guideline memory, planning–coding decomposition, and verification/refinement loops each contribute substantially to performance gains.

Conclusion: Encoding dataset-specific annotation guidelines as verifiable rules and using a multi-agent, schema-as-code workflow allows LLMs to perform BioNER more accurately and reliably than prior LLM-based methods. Structured planning, coding, and verification with refinement effectively mitigate ambiguity in annotations and common errors from unconstrained generation.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown promising potential for biomedical named entity recognition (BioNER) through instruction following and in-context learning. However, existing LLM-based BioNER methods still face two key limitations. First, retrieved demonstrations and external biomedical knowledge provide limited support for dataset-specific annotation semantics, leaving entity boundaries, type scopes, and annotation conventions ambiguous. Second, free-form generation lacks sufficient structural control, often leading to invalid formats, hallucinated mentions, duplicated entities, and boundary errors. To address these limitations, we propose GAMA, a guideline-augmented multi-agent framework for schema-as-code BioNER. GAMA first induces candidate annotation rules from labeled training instances and verifies them against annotated data to construct reliable dataset-specific guideline memory. Guided by these verified rules, a planning component generates ranked span-type hypotheses with rationales, and a coding component converts them into schema-constrained entity objects. A verification module then checks span grounding, type validity, and structural compliance, and performs dual-loop refinement to correct invalid or low-confidence predictions. Experiments on five widely used BioNER datasets with multiple LLM backbones show that GAMA consistently outperforms strong LLM-based baselines. Ablation and parameter analyses further verify the effectiveness of the proposed components.

</details>


### [33] [OLMo-Detect: A Multi-Stage, Confounder-Controlled Benchmark for Membership Inference on Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02986)
*Tao Shi,Chaoyi Xiang,Qiongkai Xu,Jey Han Lau*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces OLMo-Detect, a controlled benchmark for evaluating membership inference attacks (MIAs) on LLMs across training stages, showing that current MIAs are weak, sensitive to data type and distribution shift, and that detectability increases with model size but saturates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing membership inference benchmarks for LLMs have three key flaws: they only cover limited training stages (often just final models), they fail to properly align the distribution of member and non-member data (leading to confounders), and they do not rigorously ensure that non-members truly never appeared in training. This prevents reliable measurement of privacy leakage and MIA progress. The authors want a principled, fully open benchmark over an open training pipeline to systematically study when and how membership is detectable, and how robust attacks are to distribution shifts.

Method: They build OLMo-Detect on top of the fully open OLMo 2 LLM training pipeline. The benchmark: (1) spans multiple checkpoints (pre-, mid-, and post-training) to cover the training trajectory; (2) explicitly aligns members and non-members along three important axes (e.g., domain, length, and difficulty / style) to control confounders; (3) rigorously filters candidate non-member texts using the infini-gram index to ensure they do not appear in the training corpus. They also create a shifted variant, OLMo-Detect (Shifted), where members are intentionally misaligned with non-members to induce distribution shift. On these benchmarks, they systematically evaluate 15 unsupervised and 3 supervised MIAs across multiple OLMo 2 model sizes, and test generalization to OLMo 3 and other, non-OLMo models.

Result: Empirically, (i) overall MIA performance is modest: even the best unsupervised and supervised attacks only achieve an AUC of about 0.68, and supervised attacks generalize poorly across domains; (ii) detectability is highest at mid-training and lower at early and final checkpoints, but analysis shows that this is not a pure stage effect—curated math data is intrinsically more detectable than other data types and drives the pattern; (iii) membership inference performance increases when scaling models from 1B to 13B parameters but saturates by 32B; (iv) no evaluated unsupervised MIA is robust to distribution shift, with AUC drops as large as 0.42 on OLMo-Detect (Shifted). These trends also appear on OLMo 3 and non-OLMo models, suggesting the findings generalize beyond a single family.

Conclusion: OLMo-Detect provides a controlled, multi-stage, and distribution-aware benchmark for studying membership inference in LLMs. Using it, the authors show that current MIAs are weaker and less robust than might be assumed, that detectability is heavily driven by data type (e.g., curated math) rather than just training stage, that privacy leakage increases with model size but plateaus, and that existing unsupervised attacks are highly brittle under distribution shift. The benchmark and results indicate that more robust, distribution-aware MIA methods and better understanding of data-type-specific leakage are needed, while also suggesting that current LLMs may leak less membership information in aggregate than previously feared.

Abstract: Membership inference on large language models (LLMs) aims to determine whether a given text sample was included in an LLM's training data, without access to its training corpus. Despite recent progress, existing benchmarks suffer from three limitations: limited coverage of training stages, insufficient distributional alignment between members and non-members, and lack of rigorous filtering of non-members against the training corpus. To address these limitations, we propose OLMo-Detect, a multi-stage, confounder-controlled benchmark built upon the fully open OLMo 2 pipeline. OLMo-Detect spans pre-training, mid-training, and post-training, explicitly aligns members and non-members on three key axes, and rigorously filters non-members via infini-gram. To assess robustness to distribution shifts, we further introduce OLMo-Detect (Shifted), a variant where members are misaligned with non-members. We evaluate 15 unsupervised and 3 supervised membership inference attacks (MIAs) across the OLMo 2 family, finding that: (i) overall performance is limited: the best unsupervised and supervised MIAs both reach an AUC of only 0.68, and supervised MIAs degrade under cross-domain evaluation; (ii) MIA performance peaks at mid-training and is lower at pre-training and post-training, a pattern driven by data type rather than a stage effect: curated math data is far more detectable than other types; (iii) overall scores improve from 1B to 13B but plateau at 32B; and (iv) no unsupervised MIA is robust to distribution shifts, with AUCs shifting by up to 0.42. Finally, we find that our findings on OLMo 2 generalize to OLMo 3 and non-OLMo models.

</details>


### [34] [OmniConfess: Eliciting Token Confessions to Mitigate Omni-Modal Hallucination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02999)
*Huiqiang Rong,Haoran Luo,Hui Feng,Zhonghong Ou,Kaiwen Xue,Guoxin Zhang,Yifan Zhu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes OmniConfess, a training-free method to analyze and reduce hallucinations in omni-modal large language models by revealing which input modalities support each generated token.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Omni-modal LLMs can use text, images, audio, and video, but they often hallucinate, especially when their outputs rely on incorrect or irrelevant evidence from one or more modalities. Existing mitigation methods usually work at inference time but do not clearly show what evidence the model is relying on, making it hard to understand, debug, or selectively correct hallucinations at a fine-grained level. The authors want a way to both mitigate hallucinations and expose the evidential basis of each part of the model’s response.

Method: The authors introduce OmniConfess, a training-free procedure applied after a candidate response has been generated. For each token in the response, they systematically intervene on the available evidence channels (text, image, audio, video), recompute token scores under these controlled conditions, and derive a structured “confession” that indicates how strongly each channel supports that token. Using this token-by-channel evidential map, OmniConfess preserves content that is grounded in appropriate evidence while identifying and correcting tokens that are based on irrelevant or conflicting modalities. They also build OmniHalluBench, a benchmark of 3,540 examples across six datasets covering different modalities and both judgment and free-form generation tasks, to evaluate the method.

Result: On OmniHalluBench, OmniConfess reduces hallucinations across diverse modalities (text, image, audio, video) and task types (judgment and free-form generation). The experiments show that their intervention-and-rescoring framework can consistently improve grounding quality without requiring model retraining, demonstrating robustness across heterogeneous settings.

Conclusion: OmniConfess provides a training-free and omni-modal approach to hallucination mitigation that not only improves answer fidelity but also explains the evidential dependence of each generated token. By producing token-by-channel “confessions,” it enables more transparent and controllable generation. The OmniHalluBench benchmark further establishes a standardized way to evaluate omni-modal hallucination mitigation methods, and results indicate that OmniConfess is effective across a broad range of modalities and tasks.

Abstract: Omni-modal large language models (OmniLLMs) unify text, images, audio, and video, yet hallucinate when generation relies on the wrong evidence. Existing inference-time methods can reduce hallucinations, but rarely reveal which evidence sustains a generated commitment. We introduce OmniConfess, a training-free method for mitigating omni-modal hallucinations. It fixes a candidate response and re-scores it at token resolution under controlled channel-wise evidence interventions, producing a structured token-by-channel confession that reveals the response's evidential dependence. OmniConfess uses this confession to preserve grounded content and correct commitments driven by irrelevant or contradictory evidence. To evaluate OmniConfess, we construct OmniHalluBench, a 3,540-example benchmark built from six datasets spanning text, image, audio, and video settings and both judgment and free-form generation. Experiments show that OmniConfess mitigates hallucinations across heterogeneous modality and task settings. Our code and benchmark are publicly available at https://github.com/RongHuiQiang/OmniConfess.

</details>


### [35] [Recursive Self-Improvement in Unified Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03002)
*Huijuan Wang,Chufan Shi,Cheng Yang,Yaokang Wu,Taylor Berg-Kirkpatrick,Xuezhe Ma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a self-improvement training loop for unified text–image models where text and visual capabilities iteratively generate and verify data for each other via executable programs, substantially boosting performance on chart understanding and generation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Unified multimodal models can both understand and generate text and images, meaning they could, in principle, create their own additional training data. However, prior self-improvement approaches mainly use vision to supervise image generation and do not fully exploit the bidirectional synergy between text and vision. There is also a risk of error accumulation when models train on their own imperfect outputs without an external source of truth. The authors aim to design a training process where text and vision mutually improve while using an external, reliable verifier to prevent cascading errors, focusing on a structured domain: charts.

Method: They introduce Recursive cross-capability Self-Improvement (RSI), a multi-round training loop for a unified multimodal model. In each round, the model: (1) generates chart images and then "reads" them to detect its own shortcomings; (2) writes executable programs intended to construct charts targeting these weaknesses; (3) executes the programs to produce chart renders and automatically verifies that each output matches its specification. Only verified renders are used to train image generation, while the verified labeled renders plus the model’s own correct programs train visual understanding and program-writing. Program execution serves as an external source of truth, preventing error accumulation across rounds. They build a benchmark, BasicChartBench, to evaluate open models’ chart capabilities early in training and test performance on prompts reworded differently from training prompts to assess generalization.

Result: Using RSI over four rounds on chart tasks, performance on differently worded requests increases from 45.7% to 60.2%, whereas comparable continued training without RSI remains around 46.3%. Most of the improvement comes from the verified construction process itself, with an additional 3.5% boost from explicitly targeting the model’s observed failures. Over the RSI rounds, the proportion of verified programs rises from 48.9% to 95.2%, and the model’s accuracy when "reading" edited chart renders improves from 55.6% to 87.4%.

Conclusion: Recursive cross-capability self-improvement enables a unified text–image model to bootstrap its own training data in a structured domain while avoiding error compounding by relying on program execution as an external verifier. By letting text and visual capabilities train each other via executable chart programs, the method significantly improves chart understanding and generation performance beyond what further standard training achieves. The results on BasicChartBench suggest that verified, program-based self-improvement is an effective way to enhance multimodal models and that focusing data generation on observed failure modes yields additional gains.

Abstract: Unified multimodal models (UMMs) understand and generate both text and images, which lets a model produce its own training data. Existing self-improvement in UMMs keeps supervision on the visual side, where image understanding judges image generation. We propose recursive cross-capability self-improvement (RSI), a training loop in which the text and visual abilities of a UMM supply training data for one another. In each round, the model generates images and reads them to find where it falls short. It then writes programs aimed at these shortcomings, and execution verifies every result against its specification. Verified renders train image generation, while labeled renders and the model's own correct programs train visual understanding and program writing. Program execution thus acts as a source of truth outside the model, so errors do not accumulate across rounds. We study RSI on charts and build BasicChartBench to evaluate open models early in training. On requests worded differently from training, four rounds of RSI raise the score from 45.7% to 60.2%, while continued training stays at 46.3%. Verified construction carries most of the gain, and targeting the model's failures adds 3.5%. Along the way, the share of verified programs rises from 48.9% to 95.2%, and the reader's accuracy on edited renders rises from 55.6% to 87.4%.

</details>


### [36] [HyperThink: Text-to-Parameter Hypernetworks for Efficient Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03039)
*Donggyun Kim,Jack Lu,Chanwoo Kim,Mengye Ren,Seunghoon Hong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: HyperThink replaces long chain-of-thought decoding with a single question-conditioned parameter update, achieving faster reasoning with comparable accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long chain-of-thought reasoning boosts LLM performance on multi-step tasks but is slow and token-inefficient due to sequential decoding of long traces. There is a need for methods that retain strong reasoning while reducing latency and token usage.

Method: Introduce HyperThink, a text-to-parameter hypernetwork that, given a question, predicts updates to a small subset of the base LLM’s parameters. A vector-quantized decoder constrains these updates to a finite codebook of reusable parameter patterns to improve robustness and transfer. The system is trained end-to-end on the base model’s own chain-of-thought outputs so that at test time it can internalize reasoning into adapted parameters and avoid explicit long traces.

Result: On mathematical and general reasoning benchmarks, HyperThink achieves better accuracy-latency trade-offs in the low-latency regime compared with standard chain-of-thought decoding, especially when very limited or near-zero intermediate thinking tokens are allowed. It generates concise step-by-step solutions and final answers with far fewer tokens while maintaining strong reasoning quality.

Conclusion: HyperThink demonstrates that amortizing reasoning into question-conditioned parameter updates can substantially reduce inference-time latency and token usage while preserving multi-step reasoning performance. This text-to-parameter adaptation is especially beneficial when tight latency constraints limit the use of traditional long-form chain-of-thought prompting.

Abstract: Long-form thinking traces can substantially improve the multi-step reasoning performance of large language models (LLMs), but they introduce high inference-time overhead, with latency dominated by sequential decoding. We propose HyperThink, a text-to-parameter approach that amortizes this reasoning computation into a single query-conditioned parameter update: a lightweight hypernetwork reads the question and predicts updates to a small subset of the base LLM's parameters, while a vector-quantized decoder constrains them to a finite set of reusable patterns to improve robustness and transfer. Trained end-to-end on outputs from the base model itself, HyperThink eliminates long thinking traces at test time: after one hypernetwork forward pass, the adapted model generates a concise step-by-step solution and final answer without an intermediate trace, using far fewer tokens while retaining strong reasoning performance. Empirically, HyperThink improves the low-latency region of the accuracy-latency trade-off on mathematical and general reasoning tasks, with its strongest gains in the near-non-thinking regime.

</details>


### [37] [The Geometry of Knowledge Accessibility in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03052)
*Lihu Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that how well an LLM can recall knowledge for a query is largely determined by where the query’s representation lies in the model’s embedding space: closer to a central region means more accessible knowledge, farther away means less.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
