Motivation: LLMs often "know" facts in their parameters but fail to reliably recall them when prompted. Current work lacks a clear, predictive characterization of when the model’s internal knowledge will be accessible for a given query, and how to choose the right intervention (e.g., rewriting, chain-of-thought, retrieval). The authors aim to provide a simple, general, and measurable notion of knowledge accessibility tied to the model’s internal geometry, to better understand and control LLM behavior before generation begins.

Method: 1) Define knowledge accessibility as the model’s ability to correctly answer knowledge queries without external help. 2) Embed queries using the LLM’s internal representations before generation and analyze the geometry of these embeddings. 3) Identify a central point in representation space and measure each query’s distance to this center. 4) Empirically correlate this distance with knowledge accessibility across datasets and tasks, and test whether a clear boundary between more vs. less accessible queries emerges. 5) Perform controlled experiments to compare this geometry with alternative explanations such as reasoning difficulty, and to evaluate which interventions (query rewriting, chain-of-thought prompting, retrieval-augmented generation) help most at different distances from the center/boundary.

Result: 1) Query embeddings exhibit a centered geometric structure: queries whose answers the model can recall lie closer to a central representation, while those it struggles with are farther away. 2) There exists an empirical knowledge boundary in representation space; accessibility declines monotonically with distance from the center. 3) The distance-based ordering of queries generalizes across datasets, even though each dataset may have its own center. 4) The geometric signal aligns more strongly with knowledge accessibility than with general reasoning difficulty. 5) Different inference-time interventions show distinct regimes of effectiveness: rewriting boosts performance mainly for already-accessible queries, chain-of-thought prompting is most helpful near the boundary, and retrieval-augmented methods yield the largest gains for queries well beyond the boundary.

Conclusion: The paper proposes and validates a simple geometric view of knowledge in LLMs: a central region in representation space corresponds to highly accessible knowledge, with accessibility decreasing smoothly as queries move away from this center, forming a knowledge boundary. This geometry can be used as a pre-generation signal to forecast whether a model will be able to recall the needed knowledge and to adaptively select interventions—rewriting, chain-of-thought, or retrieval—based on a query’s location relative to the boundary. This provides both conceptual insight into how knowledge is organized in LLMs and a practical tool for improving inference-time behavior.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) contain broad knowledge, but they cannot access all of it reliably. We study this problem through knowledge accessibility, which describes whether the knowledge needed for a query can be recalled from the model. We find that knowledge accessibility has a simple geometric structure in the model's representation of the query alone, before any generation. More accessible queries are closer to a center in the representation space, while less accessible queries are farther away. This geometry reveals a knowledge boundary that separates more accessible queries from less accessible ones. Accessibility consistently decreases with distance from the center, and this distance-based ordering transfers across datasets even when the centers differ. Controlled experiments further show that the centered geometry is more closely related to knowledge accessibility than to reasoning difficulty. The geometry also reveals when different interventions are useful. Query rewriting helps more for accessible queries, chain-of-thought reasoning helps more near the boundary, and retrieval gives larger gains beyond the boundary. These findings not only provide a new geometric view of how knowledge is organized in language models, but also suggest a useful pre-generation signal for adaptive inference.

</details>


### [38] [HARPO: Hallucination-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Faithful and Creative Language Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03063)
*Tiezheng Yu,Yuxin Jiang,Jinpeng Li,Shuning Sun,Fei Mi,Haoli Bai,Lifeng Shang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: HARPO is a reinforcement learning framework that reduces hallucinations in LLMs while preserving creative writing quality, using a hallucination-aware reward model and selective reward activation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs often hallucinate facts, which undermines their reliability in knowledge-intensive applications. Prior attempts to reduce hallucinations can overly restrict model creativity or rely on weak supervision signals. The paper aims to build a training framework that explicitly balances faithfulness to evidence with creative, high-quality language generation, using stronger verifiable feedback for hallucination detection.

Method: The authors propose HARPO, an RL-based training framework with three key components: (1) HA-GRM, a Hallucination-Aware Generative Reward Model trained using verifiable feedback to jointly score factual faithfulness and writing quality; (2) a Selective Activation Mechanism (SAM) that only activates writing-quality rewards when HA-GRM judges the output as hallucination-free, thereby avoiding rewarding high-quality but unfaithful text; and (3) a data curriculum that gradually transitions training from general creative-writing tasks to hallucination-focused, knowledge-intensive tasks, improving robustness against hallucinations while maintaining stylistic strength.

Result: Using Qwen-family models (1.7B–8B), the authors report that their Qwen3-4B-based HA-GRM attains an F1 of 78.08% on RAGTruth hallucination detection, outperforming a supervised fine-tuning baseline at 66.37%. Applying HARPO to Qwen2.5 and Qwen3 models improves both faithfulness and writing metrics. For Qwen3-4B on MultiHopRAG, the HA-GRM-estimated hallucination rate drops from 3.29% to 1.02%, while Arena-Hard-v2.0 creative-writing scores increase from 16.95% to 27.54%.

Conclusion: The study concludes that HARPO effectively reduces hallucinations in LLM outputs without sacrificing, and even improving, creative-writing quality. By combining a hallucination-aware reward model with selective reward activation and a curriculum over task types, the framework yields models that are both more faithful in knowledge-intensive settings and strong stylists in open-ended generation. The approach demonstrates that carefully designed RL reward structures can mitigate hallucinations while maintaining generative richness.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are prone to generating hallucinated content, which compromises their reliability in knowledge-intensive tasks. To address this challenge without sacrificing creativity, we propose HARPO, a reinforcement learning framework designed to jointly optimize faithfulness and creativity. HARPO incorporates a Hallucination-Aware Generative Reward Model (HA-GRM), trained via verifiable feedback, to assess both faithfulness and writing quality. A Selective Activation Mechanism (SAM) activates writing rewards only for outputs judged hallucination-free by HA-GRM, while a data curriculum progressively shifts training from creative writing to hallucination-centric tasks. On RAGTruth, our Qwen3-4B-based HA-GRM achieves a response-level F1 score of 78.08%, compared with 66.37% for the supervised fine-tuning baseline. Experiments on Qwen2.5 and Qwen3 models from 1.7B to 8B parameters show improvements in both faithful generation and writing quality. On Qwen3-4B, HARPO reduces the HA-GRM-judged hallucination rate on MultiHopRAG from 3.29% to 1.02%, while increasing the Arena-Hard-v2.0 creative-writing score from 16.95% to 27.54%.

</details>


### [39] [Unmasking Propaganda: A Comparative Analysis of Masked and Causal Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03077)
*Claudiu Creanga,Ioachim Lihor,Liviu P. Dinu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares modern language models for detecting fine-grained propaganda techniques and achieves small but real improvements over prior state of the art on SemEval-2020 Task 11.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Propaganda in text is subtle, context-dependent, and difficult to separate from ordinary persuasion. Existing systems struggle to accurately detect specific techniques, limiting the ability to automatically analyze manipulative political communication. The authors want to understand how far current masked and causal LMs can go on this task and where they still fail.

Method: They perform a comparative study on the SemEval-2020 Task 11 propaganda dataset, testing two families of models: (1) masked language models based on XLM-RoBERTa and DeBERTa V3, and (2) large causal language models from several providers (OpenAI, Google, Mistral, Anthropic, Meta). For causal models they use prompting, with two prompting strategies: a simple base prompt and a chain-of-thought (CoT) style prompt that asks the model to reason step by step before predicting a propaganda technique. They evaluate per-technique and overall F1 scores for span-level technique classification.

Result: Their best masked language model reaches 63.18 F1 and their best causal model 63.62 F1 on technique classification, both surpassing earlier state-of-the-art systems on SemEval-2020 Task 11. Performance varies by technique: models are relatively strong on techniques like loaded language and name-calling but noticeably weaker on others such as bandwagon and black-and-white fallacy.

Conclusion: Modern language models, both masked and causal, can modestly advance the state of the art in fine-grained propaganda technique detection, but they still have uneven performance across techniques. The authors conclude that additional work—such as task-specific fine-tuning, model ensembling, and expanding or improving training data—is needed to build more robust and comprehensive propaganda detection systems.

Abstract: Propaganda detection is an essential task in natural language processing (NLP), particularly in the context of manipulative political communications. However, identifying specific propaganda techniques presents a significant challenge due to their often subtle nature and reliance on context, making them difficult to distinguish from legitimate persuasive language. Propaganda often involves highlighting certain facts while downplaying or ignoring others to create a desired perception. This biased communication aims to influence attitudes, beliefs, or behaviors towards a particular cause or position. This paper explores advances in detecting propaganda techniques through a comparative analysis of modern language models, using the SemEval-2020 Task 11 dataset. We evaluated both masked language models (based on XLM-RoBERTa or DeBERTa V3) and causal models (from OpenAI, Google, Mistral, Anthropic and Meta), employing two prompting strategies: base and chain-of-thought prompting. Our results demonstrate improvements over state-of-the-art models, with the best-performing MLM achieving an F1 score of 63.18 in technique classification and the best causal model achieving 63.62. We also observed that certain models excel in specific techniques, such as loaded language and name-calling, while struggling with others like bandwagon and black-and-white fallacy. These findings suggest that fine-tuning, ensemble modeling, and the use of larger datasets can further enhance propaganda detection capabilities.

</details>


### [40] [An automated pipeline for standardised speech-unit annotation in spontaneous dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03078)
*Hanlu He,Harald Vilhelm Skat-Rørdam,Ingvi Örnólfsson,Ivana Konvalinka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces and evaluates an automated pipeline to detect conversational turns and listener backchannels (like short acknowledgments) in dyadic speech recordings, achieving moderate reliability and accurate timing, and proposes it as a first-pass tool in semi-automated annotation workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Studying conversational dynamics (who talks when, how they alternate, and how listeners provide feedback) requires detailed, consistent annotations of turns and backchannels. Manual annotation is time-consuming, subjective, and hard to reproduce. Existing automatic methods based only on speech activity (who is speaking) cannot reliably separate full conversational turns from brief listener feedback or pauses within a turn. The authors want a scalable, standardized way to generate initial annotations that can be refined by humans, improving efficiency and reproducibility in conversation research, including under challenging listening conditions.

Method: They design an automated pipeline that takes two-channel recordings of spontaneous dyadic conversations (each speaker on a separate channel) and processes them in several steps: (1) voice activity detection (VAD) to find speech vs. non-speech; (2) channel-energy filtering to clean up and refine detected speech segments; (3) temporal merging to combine nearby segments into longer candidate stretches; (4) automatic speech recognition (ASR) to obtain text; and (5) context-based post-processing using temporal and contextual cues (derived from patterns in timing, overlap, and ASR output) to classify segments as conversational turns versus backchannels. They evaluate this system on 99 ten-minute conversations (33 dyads, three conversations each) in Danish, recorded in two conditions: normal listening and asymmetric listening where one partner hears added speech-shaped noise via bone conduction. They assess performance using segment-level detection metrics (precision, recall, F1) for both turns and backchannels, and measure temporal boundary accuracy via onset and offset errors (median and mean absolute differences from human annotations). They also conduct a four-conversation case study varying pipeline parameters and comparing pipeline-human agreement against human inter-annotator agreement.

Result: The pipeline achieved overall segment detection reliability of F1 = 0.621, with similar performance for turns (F1 = 0.624) and backchannels (F1 = 0.618). For segments that were correctly matched to human-annotated units, the median absolute timing errors were small: for turns, 0.150 s at onset and 0.160 s at offset; for backchannels, 0.130 s at onset and 0.180 s at offset. However, the mean boundary errors for turns were substantially larger than the medians, indicating that while most boundaries were fairly accurate, a smaller subset had large mismatches. Performance did not show significant differences between normal and asymmetric listening conditions, suggesting robustness to the added bone-conducted noise. In the detailed case study on four conversations, agreement between the pipeline and human annotators was lower and more variable than agreement between human annotators themselves, and it depended on how the pipeline parameters were set.

Conclusion: The proposed pipeline can reliably and automatically identify conversational turns and backchannels with reasonable accuracy in both detection and temporal boundary placement, even under more challenging listening conditions. Although its agreement with humans is lower and more variable than human–human agreement, its performance is adequate for use as an automated first-pass annotator within a semi-automated workflow. This provides a consistent, standardized starting point for human review and correction, which can improve efficiency, comparability, and reproducibility in research on conversational dynamics, especially when dealing with large datasets of dyadic speech.

Abstract: Quantifying conversational dynamics requires reliable identification of interactional units and their temporal boundaries, but speech activity alone does not distinguish conversational turns from listener feedback or within-turn pauses. We present an automated pipeline for extracting turns and backchannels from separate-channel recordings of spontaneous dyadic conversation, designed to provide a consistent first-pass annotation for subsequent human review. The pipeline combines voice activity detection, channel-energy filtering, temporal merging, automatic speech recognition, and context-based post-processing. We evaluated the pipeline on 99 ten-minute Danish conversations from 33 dyads using segment-level detection reliability and temporal boundary error. Conversations were recorded under both normal and asymmetric listening conditions. In the latter, speech-shaped noise was delivered to one participant through bone-conduction headphones. Overall detection reliability was F1=0.621, with similar performance for turns F1=0.624 and backchannels F1=0.618. For successfully matched segments, median absolute onset and offset errors were 0.150 and 0.160s for turns and 0.130 and 0.180s for backchannels, respectively. Mean errors were substantially larger for turn boundaries, indicating a smaller number of large boundary mismatches. Performance did not differ significantly across the two experimental listening conditions. In a four-conversation case study, pipeline-human agreement was lower and more variable than human inter-annotator agreement and varied across parameter settings. These results support the pipeline as an automated first pass within a semi-automated annotation workflow, providing a consistent basis for more standardised and reproducible annotation of conversational dynamics.

</details>


### [41] [Ask, Relax, or Act? Evaluating Actionable Indeterminacy in LLM Preference Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03102)
*Ang Li,Yue Lin,Feifei Kou,Zhan Su,Prayag Tiwari,Wenhao Li,Shuhui Zhu,Hongyuan Zha,Baoxiang Wang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies when LLM agents should act, ask clarification questions, or repair user constraints, and shows that models often intervene unnecessarily even when a valid action is already justified.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are increasingly used to take actions (e.g., booking, scheduling, allocation) under uncertainty, but they frequently fail to decide correctly between acting immediately and asking for clarification. Existing evaluations mainly test correctness of final choices, not whether the model intervenes appropriately or produces usable questions/repairs. The authors aim to formalize and benchmark this decision-making aspect so that LLM agents can behave as reliable, efficient assistants.

Method: The authors formalize the notion of "actionable indeterminacy" to decide between acting, clarifying, or repairing constraints: (1) act if there is at least one accepted action common to all admissible objectives/preferences; (2) ask for clarification if multiple possibilities are feasible but share no common acceptable action; (3) propose minimum-cost constraint repairs when the user’s request is infeasible. They build a solver-grounded benchmark across four domains—object allocation, meeting scheduling, apartment choice, and stable matching—with matched instance pairs that differ only in whether intervention is needed. They then evaluate LLMs along three dimensions: decision correctness (act/clarify/repair), matched-pair reliability (consistency across nearly identical instances), and fully correct responses (both the right decision type and a usable action/question/repair).

Result: Models frequently struggle to detect when no intervention is needed, often asking questions or attempting repairs even when a justified action already exists. While models can recognize cases needing clarification or constraint repair, they still over-intervene on solvable instances. Moreover, correctly labeling the decision type (e.g., “I should act”) does not ensure that the resulting action, clarifying question, or repair is actually valid or usable. The experiments also show that how the response requirements are specified—what the model is told to output—significantly affects both the quality of responses and the actual decisions made.

Conclusion: Reliable LLM agency demands more than recognizing uncertainty: agents must learn when *not* to intervene, and their chosen next step must be rendered as a concrete, verifiable response. The formalization of actionable indeterminacy and the solver-grounded benchmark expose systematic weaknesses in current models’ ability to distinguish between acting, clarifying, and repairing. Explicitly specifying output requirements can substantially improve performance and even shift intervention choices. The work suggests that future systems should jointly design decision policies and response formats to ensure that agents take justified actions and only intervene when truly necessary.

Abstract: An LLM agent can recognize uncertainty yet still choose the wrong next step: asking when action is already justified, or seeking clarification when the constraints must change. We formalize actionable indeterminacy: act when an accepted action is shared across all admissible preferences or objectives, clarify when each possibility is feasible but no action is shared, and propose a minimum-cost permitted constraint repair when the request is infeasible. We construct a solver-grounded benchmark spanning object allocation, meeting scheduling, apartment choice, and stable matching. Matched pairs retain the same source while changing whether intervention is necessary, and evaluation separates decision correctness, matched-pair reliability, and fully correct responses. Our findings reveal a recurring difficulty in recognizing when intervention is unnecessary: models can identify situations requiring clarification or repair yet still intervene when a justified action already exists. Correct decision labels also fail to guarantee usable actions, questions, or repairs. Crucially, response requirements shape not only how decisions are expressed but also which decisions are made. Making the required content explicit substantially improves fully correct responses and can change intervention decisions, even when outputs are already parseable. These findings highlight that reliable agency requires more than recognizing uncertainty: it requires intervening only when necessary and translating the chosen next step into a verifiable response.

</details>


### [42] [Emergent Structure in the Marginal Attention Space of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03109)
*Valentino Maiorca,Walter Nelson,Francesco Locatello*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how attention mechanisms, rather than just representations, align across many language models by defining a “marginal attention space,” and uses it to find text-intrinsic and model-specific patterns that also enable better KV-cache eviction strategies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most prior work focuses on similarity of hidden representations across independently trained language models, but much less is known about whether and how internal mechanisms like attention behave similarly across different models. Understanding these shared and private aspects of attention could both deepen our theoretical grasp of LLMs and lead to practical gains, e.g., in memory-efficient inference via better KV-cache management.

Method: The authors analyze post-softmax attention weights by marginalizing over query positions, creating a joint token-head “marginal attention space.” They then study this space along two axes: token-wise reduction (aggregating over heads) and head-wise reduction (aggregating over tokens). They conduct experiments across 60+ diverse LLMs. They empirically relate token-wise marginal attention to the input-output Jacobian of the network and theoretically prove that, under smoothness assumptions, models with similar next-token distributions must exhibit similar Jacobian statistics. For the head-wise axis, they quantify per-head patterns and leverage them to design a per-head KV-cache eviction budget. This budget is precomputed offline on pretraining-like text and combined with a training-free token scoring rule at inference.

Result: Token-wise reductions of marginal attention reveal a robust, text-intrinsic signal that is highly conserved across models. This conservation is empirically linked and theoretically justified via the similarity of input-output Jacobians in models with similar output token distributions. Head-wise reductions, conversely, show model-private signatures that are stable across documents. Using these signatures, the authors derive per-head KV-cache budgets that, when combined with a simple token scoring mechanism, achieve performance on standard eviction benchmarks comparable to more expensive methods that either recompute budgets per document or require task-specific training.

Conclusion: Marginal attention provides a unified lens on attention behavior across language models, separating a conserved, text-driven token signal from model-specific head patterns. This view not only clarifies why certain aspects of attention are shared when models produce similar outputs, but also yields a practical KV-cache eviction strategy: a fixed, per-head budget learned offline plus a generic token scoring rule can match more complex, adaptive or trained methods. The work suggests that analyzing attention in marginal spaces can bridge mechanistic understanding and efficiency-oriented applications.

Abstract: While representation similarity across independently trained language models is well-documented, how internal mechanics such as attention behave across models remains far less characterized. Inspired by this gap, we examine the structure of post-softmax attention weights by marginalizing over query positions, mapping them into a joint token-head "marginal attention space". Evaluating across 60+ diverse LLMs, we find that different properties emerge when reducing this space along its token and head axes. When reduced token-wise, marginal attention yields a text-intrinsic signal robustly conserved across models. To explain this property, we empirically connect marginal attention to the input-output Jacobian of the network, and prove theoretically that under a smoothness assumption, models with similar next-token distributions are guaranteed to have similar input-output Jacobian statistics. When reduced head-wise, it forms a model-private signature conserved across documents. Practically, this provides a natural way to estimate a per-head budget for key-value (KV) cache eviction, effectively decoupling model-specific budget allocation from text-intrinsic token scoring. On standard eviction benchmarks, a per-head budget precomputed offline on pretraining text, combined with a training-free token score, shows competitive performance with methods that recompute the budget on every document or train it per target. Code available at https://github.com/Flegyas/marginal-attention

</details>


### [43] [Ontological Instability and Statistical Amplification: The Paradox of "Humanizing" LLM-Generated Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03110)
*Claudiu Creanga,Liviu Dinu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Study analyzes what supervised AI-text detectors (RoBERTa-based and event-based latent space methods) actually rely on, and tests their robustness under various perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While AI-text detectors show high benchmark accuracy, it is unclear what linguistic or statistical cues they truly exploit and how robust they are to realistic modifications in text. Understanding this is crucial for reliable AI-generated text detection and for avoiding high false-positive rates on human writing.

Method: The authors probe a RoBERTa-based AI-text detector using semantic, structural, and tokenizer-level perturbations on the M4 dataset (10,000 samples) and a controlled set of 300 generations. They specifically manipulate text properties (e.g., making machine text 'more human') and measure changes in features such as Verb Diversity and detector scores. As a control, they evaluate an alternative 'event-based Latent Space detection' method by paraphrasing texts and applying homoglyphs, then comparing extracted event sequences using Jaccard similarity and reporting AUC across domains.

Result: When Mistral-7B-Instruct is prompted to make machine text more human-like, verb diversity increases (from 0.77 to 0.92), but paradoxically the text becomes easier for the RoBERTa detector to flag as machine-generated. The RoBERTa detector’s scores correlate with statistical complexity, leading to a very high false-positive rate (76.3%) on formal human texts. The event-based latent space method is highly sensitive to paraphrasing and homoglyph attacks (87% and 70% of events/verbs changed, respectively) and performs poorly overall, with a best domain AUC of only 0.577. Structural abstraction did not improve robustness.

Conclusion: State-of-the-art supervised AI-text detectors, like the RoBERTa-based model examined, rely on specific surface-level or statistical features (e.g., complexity) rather than deeper semantic structure, making them brittle and prone to misclassifying formal human text as AI-generated. Alternative event-based latent space approaches are also fragile to common text transformations and do not yet provide robust, high-accuracy detection. Structural abstraction alone is insufficient to create detectors that are both accurate and robust to realistic edits and adversarial changes.

Abstract: Supervised AI-text detectors report high benchmark accuracy, but it is not clear what their decisions are based on. We analyze a RoBERTa-based detector under semantic, structural, and tokenizer-level perturbations, using the M4 dataset (N = 10,000) and controlled generations (N = 300). When Mistral-7B-Instruct was asked to make machine text sound more human, Verb Diversity rose from 0.77 to 0.92 and the outputs became easier to detect. Detection scores appear to track statistical complexity, which also leads to a 76.3% false-positive rate on formal human writing. As a control, we evaluate event-based Latent Space detection. Paraphrasing changed 87% of its event sequences (Jaccard = 0.067), and homoglyphs altered 70% of the extracted verbs even though extraction still ran (Jaccard = 0.30). Its best domain AUC was 0.577. RoBERTa's robustness seems specific to the features it uses, and structural abstraction did not make detection more robust.

</details>


### [44] [Building Interpretable Feature Representations for Resume-Vacancy Matching by Distilling Production LLM Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03112)
*Ilya Chekin,Vyacheslav Malyugin,Vladimir Chirkov,Mikhail Yurushkin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build an explainable candidate–vacancy matching system that turns LLM-generated labels into fast, CPU-serving bi-encoder features along multiple named dimensions, achieving high agreement with recruiter feedback in production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recruiters need more than a single opaque relevance score when matching candidates to vacancies; they need interpretable, actionable evidence about why a candidate fits or not, broken down into meaningful dimensions. Existing systems often provide only black-box scores, limiting trust, controllability, and the ability to incorporate recruiter feedback. This paper aims to create a matching system that is both explainable and operationally efficient in real-world recruitment pipelines.

Method: They propose a two-part approach: (1) an LLM-based labeler that, via carefully engineered prompts and feature definitions refined by recruiter feedback, assigns labels across several interpretable matching dimensions to vacancy–resume pairs. This LLM labeler is used offline only. (2) A distilled feature bi-encoder model: a LoRA-adapted embedding backbone with small per-dimension prediction heads. This student model is trained on the LLM’s labels over 168,772 labeled vacancy–resume pairs (17,921 vacancies and 180,030 resumes) and is optimized to run efficiently on CPU to serve all online requests. The system is continually improved by revising prompts as new recruiter feedback is received and retraining the bi-encoder on updated labels. Recruiters also interact with the system by confirming or adjusting predicted feature values in production, providing further supervision.

Result: The deployed bi-encoder student model, trained on labels from the LLM labeler, shows strong agreement with recruiter-recorded feature values on real production data. On 927 recruiter-recorded feature instances from a curated feedback subset, the model’s predictions match the human decisions in 888 cases, corresponding to a 95.79% agreement rate. These results demonstrate that the distilled model can effectively approximate the LLM labeler and align well with recruiter judgments while remaining efficient enough for CPU-based online serving.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that an interpretable, multi-dimensional candidate–vacancy matching system can be effectively realized by combining an offline LLM-based labeler with an efficient, distilled bi-encoder for online serving. The approach yields high agreement with recruiter feedback and supports continuous improvement through iterative prompt refinement and retraining. This architecture provides recruiters with actionable, named matching dimensions instead of a single opaque score, enhancing transparency and usability in recruitment workflows while maintaining practical performance and scalability.

Abstract: Matching candidates to vacancies is central to recruitment, and a recruiter needs to see why a candidate fits, not only a single opaque relevance score. We provide this evidence as named, interpretable matching dimensions recruiters can act on - eight in our current deployment. We propose a two-part approach. The first is an LLM-based labeler whose prompts and feature definitions were refined from recruiter feedback while it served as an earlier production matching stage. In the current architecture, it is used only for offline labeling and is not called on online requests. The second is a feature bi-encoder distilled from it: a LoRA-adapted embedding backbone with compact per-dimension heads that runs on CPU and serves all online requests. Both parts keep improving: prompts are revised as feedback arrives, and the bi-encoder is retrained on the updated labels. The model is trained on 168,772 labeled vacancy-resume pairs (17,921 vacancies and 180,030 resumes). Recruiters using the service can confirm or revise surfaced feature predictions. On 927 recruiter-recorded values from this selected production-feedback subset, the deployed student agrees with the recorded decisions in 888 cases (95.79%). This is operational, non-blinded agreement rather than an independent human evaluation.

</details>


### [45] [Investigating the Role of Reasoning-Language Alignment in Monolingual Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03136)
*Oliver Hauck,Mario Sanz-Guerrero,Katharina von der Wense*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how the language used for chain-of-thought reasoning affects performance in retrieval-augmented QA, using a monolingual German testbed from a niche tabletop RPG universe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLMs are mostly trained to reason in English, and prior work found that forcing them to reason in other languages hurts accuracy, but only in short-prompt settings. It is unclear whether this also holds in RAG setups where models must process long, language-specific retrieved context. Additionally, benchmarks that truly require retrieval (and not parametric memory) in non-English domains are scarce.

Method: The authors construct a fully monolingual German RAG QA benchmark based on lore from the tabletop role-playing game The Dark Eye, chosen because its German documentation is rich but obscure enough that LLMs cannot answer from memory. They use an agentic RAG system where they can explicitly control the language of the intermediate reasoning traces (e.g., forced German vs. forced French vs. native unconstrained English). They then systematically vary the reasoning language while keeping the query and retrieved documents in German, and compare performance across conditions and under different levels of context richness and structure-awareness in the retrieval pipeline.

Result: When the language of chain-of-thought reasoning is aligned with the query and the retrieved documents (i.e., German for German queries and evidence), RAG QA performance improves compared to using a mismatched reasoning language. Forced German reasoning beats forced French, even though the model is overall stronger in French on standard benchmarks, demonstrating that alignment with context matters more than raw language proficiency in this setting. The performance gain from alignment increases as the retrieved context becomes richer and more structurally organized. However, reasoning in forced German does not surpass the model's native, unconstrained English chain-of-thought; it only approaches it.

Conclusion: Language alignment between queries, retrieved documents, and reasoning traces benefits RAG performance, indicating that cross-language reasoning incurs a cost even for relatively strong languages. Nonetheless, the model’s best performance still occurs with its native unconstrained English reasoning, suggesting that truly strong multilingual RAG requires models that are natively capable of high-quality reasoning in multiple languages, not just translation or forced-language prompting. The authors provide their German The Dark Eye RAG benchmark as a public resource for future research on multilingual retrieval and reasoning.

Abstract: Reasoning traces improve large language models (LLMs), but current models are trained to reason mostly in English. It has been shown that forcing a model to reason in another language degrades accuracy, even when the reasoning language matches the language of the prompt -- but only for a setting where the model reasons over a short prompt. Here, we ask whether the same holds for retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), where the model must read and integrate a large amount of retrieved evidence in the target language. To study this, we build a fully monolingual German RAG question-answering testbed over the fictional world of the tabletop role-playing game The Dark Eye, a domain that is richly documented in German but too niche for the model to answer from memory, so that it has to rely on retrieval. Varying the forced reasoning language of an agentic RAG system on this testbed, we find that aligning the reasoning language with the language of the query and the retrieved documents helps. Forced German reasoning outperforms forced French, although the model benchmarks higher in French, so the benefit comes from alignment and not from language proficiency. The advantage grows when the retrieved context is richer and structure-aware. However, forced German only reaches the level of the model's native, unconstrained English reasoning without surpassing it, showing that native multilingual reasoning is needed. We publicly release the testbed and QA benchmark.

</details>


### [46] [Predicting Steering Vectors and Adapter Weights for Few-Shot Author-Style Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03163)
*Leonard Popp,Danni Liu,Supriti Sinhamahapatra,Jan Niehues*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper explores methods for adapting large language models to mimic individual scientists' abstract-writing styles from only a few examples, introducing steering and hypernetwork-based approaches that balance style imitation with text quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adapting language models to a specific author's style from limited data is difficult, especially in scientific writing where formal constraints reduce stylistic variation and where style is often intertwined with the author's preferred research topics. Existing style-control methods either require large amounts of data, predefined style labels, or compromise text quality. The authors aim to disentangle style from content and develop methods that can condition generation on an author's style with just a few abstracts while preserving fluency and factual quality.

Method: They frame the task as style-conditioned abstract generation using a base language model and a small set of abstracts per author. They introduce three approaches: (1) contrastive activation steering, where they compute steering vectors by contrasting an author's abstracts with style-neutral generations on the same content, then apply these vectors to the model's activations during generation; (2) a predictor network that, given examples of an author's abstracts, learns to output appropriate steering vectors; and (3) a hypernetwork that, again conditioned on a few example abstracts, predicts LoRA adapter weights to plug into the base model. They evaluate style imitation versus output quality across seen and unseen authors and compare against conventional fine-tuning and inventory-based style steering methods.

Result: They observe a clear trade-off between strength of style imitation and overall generation quality. Direct fine-tuning captures the strongest author-style signal but substantially harms fluency and quality. The hypernetwork approach delivers the best balance, maintaining good fluency while achieving strong style matching for both authors seen during training and novel authors. Their contrastive, author-level steering—built by comparing an author's real abstracts with neutral generations for the same content—improves performance over methods that rely on a predefined style inventory. Analysis of the learned representations shows that both manually extracted and automatically predicted steering vectors are nearly orthogonal in representation space yet reach similar style-control performance.

Conclusion: Style conditioning for scientific abstract generation can be effectively achieved from just a few examples by using contrastive, author-level steering mechanisms that separate style from content. Hypernetwork-predicted LoRA adapters provide the best trade-off between mimicking an author's style and preserving output quality, outperforming simple fine-tuning and inventory-based steering approaches. The finding that different, nearly orthogonal steering directions can yield comparable style control suggests that there is no single unique axis for style in this setting, and that style can be captured via multiple independent directions in representation space.

Abstract: Adapting large language models to an individual author's style from a few examples is challenging, and scientific writing sharpens the difficulty: formal conventions leave little surface variation, and authors write about their own topics, so extracted ``style'' easily entangles with content. We study style-conditioned abstract generation from a few example abstracts per author and propose three methods: (1) contrastive activation steering, (2) a network that predicts steering vectors, and (3) a hypernetwork that predicts LoRA adapters. We find a consistent trade-off between style imitation and output quality: fine-tuning buys most of the available style signal but forfeits fluency, while the hypernetwork achieves the best trade-off on both seen and unseen authors. Our steering operates at author level, contrasting an author's abstracts against style-neutral generations for the same content. This holds topic fixed, removes the need for a predefined style inventory, and outperforms inventory-based steering. % [EDIT 1a] softened "no single optimal axis" claim Moreover, our analyses demonstrate that manually extracted and predicted steering vectors are near-orthogonal yet score comparably, indicating that style conditioning here can admit at least two unrelated directions rather than requiring one particular axis.

</details>


### [47] [Hindsight-Guided Rationale Distillation for Rare Disease Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03176)
*Aarav Singh,Animesh Pathak,Navyansh Singh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They investigate hindsight-guided distillation for rare disease diagnosis, showing that a contamination-filtered student model can slightly but significantly outperform its larger teacher, and analyze why.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rare disease diagnosis with language models is challenging, and using a smaller student distilled from a larger teacher could improve performance and efficiency. However, when the teacher conditions on ground-truth labels during chain-of-thought generation, it can introduce artifacts that contaminate the student, so the authors want to understand if and when hindsight-guided distillation genuinely helps.

Method: They fine-tune a 1.5B-parameter student model on chain-of-thought traces from an 8B-parameter teacher that has access to the ground-truth diagnosis while generating reasoning. They compare an unfiltered student (trained directly on these traces) with a filtered variant (StudentF) where regex-based filters remove explicit ground-truth label phrases from the training data. They run statistical tests on diagnosis accuracy across diseases and analyze error patterns, focusing on a contamination artifact they call "GT hallucination."

Result: Overall task accuracy is low for all models, but the filtered student (StudentF) achieves a small yet statistically significant accuracy improvement over the teacher (p < 0.001), especially for diseases with more training examples. The unfiltered student does not significantly outperform the teacher (p = 0.129). They find that explicit "ground truth is X" phrases get copied by SFT; at inference, the unfiltered student hallucinates such phrases in 33.9% of cases, and when these hallucinated labels are wrong, accuracy drops sharply. Regex filtering almost eliminates this contamination and yields the observed gains.

Conclusion: Hindsight-guided distillation alone does not reliably improve diagnostic accuracy; the key benefit comes from filtering out label-contaminated reasoning traces that cause "GT hallucination." While the filtered student can slightly outperform its teacher, the gains are modest and task difficulty remains high. They also show frequency-dependent knowledge transfer and a persistent calibration gap not fixed by supervised fine-tuning, highlighting the need for better approaches to leverage hindsight signals without introducing harmful artifacts.

Abstract: We study hindsight-guided distillation for rare disease diagnosis on ZebraMap: a 1.5B student is fine-tuned on chain-of-thought traces from a 8B teacher that observes the ground-truth diagnosis during generation. Absolute accuracy remains low for all models - the task is hard at this scale - but within this ceiling a filtered variant (StudentF) achieves a small, statistically significant accuracy advantage over the teacher (p < 0.001), concentrated in better-represented diseases. The unfiltered student does not significantly outperform the teacher (p = 0.129), establishing that contamination filtering - not hindsight distillation alone - drives the gain. The gap traces to an artifact we term GT hallucination. Label-visible generation causes the teacher to embed "ground truth is X" phrases in its reasoning chain; SFT copies the pattern. At inference, the unfiltered student reproduces the phrase in 33.9% of cases, with severe accuracy degradation when the hallucinated label is wrong. A regex filter removing these slots reduces contamination to near-zero, producing the observed gain - though the effect remains small. We precisely quantify this gain-cost tradeoff, document frequency-dependent knowledge transfer absent from the RL-trained teacher, and characterize a calibration gap that SFT does not close - identifying both as directions for future work.

</details>


### [48] [Not Until the Evidence Says So: Teaching LLM Investigators When to Close a Case](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03190)
*Tingzhu Bi,Ping Wang,Meng Ma*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how large language models (LLMs) decide whether they have enough evidence to “close” an investigation case, introduces a dataset and evaluation protocol for this, and shows that targeted training greatly reduces unjustified, overconfident conclusions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In real-world investigations (accidents, defects, outages), analysts must decide not just what happened, but whether the currently available evidence is sufficient to reach a justified conclusion or whether the case should remain open. Off-the-shelf LLMs tend to be overconfident: they claim to know the cause even when evidence is missing, and measuring this behavior is tricky because naive baselines (like using the report source alone) already perform strongly. The paper is motivated by the need to (1) make LLMs’ closure decisions track actual evidence rather than guessing, and (2) design fair evaluations that distinguish true reasoning from exploiting dataset artifacts.

Method: The authors: (1) Formulate “case closure” as a decision-making problem where an LLM iteratively requests evidence from a case file, updates hypotheses, and either closes the case with a grounded conclusion or leaves it open and specifies missing information. (2) Construct Nautil, a dataset of 731 audited investigation cases across aviation, rail, maritime, chemical safety, vehicle defects, and production server incidents, including teacher trajectories, an OOD test set, and counterfactual evidence versions. (3) Define three evaluation axes: (a) closure accuracy, benchmarked against a strong source-only baseline and reported per source; (b) evidence dependence, by removing supporting evidence and checking whether closures decrease; and (c) conclusion and gap quality, via human-judged checklists of what the model claims and what missing evidence it names. (4) Fine-tune a 9B LLM on expert “teacher” trajectories to align its closure and explanation behavior with evidence, and then apply reinforcement learning that rewards only the binary closure decision to further improve accuracy.

Result: A naïve 9B model is highly overconfident: it overstates the available evidence in 97% of answers, and even a frontier model that correctly identifies the cause 84% of the time still overstates in 91% of cases and wrongly closes 17 of 41 officially “cause undetermined” cases. A simple baseline that predicts closure only from the case source achieves 83.0 balanced accuracy, revealing strong dataset bias. After fine-tuning on teacher trajectories, the 9B model’s behavior becomes more evidence-sensitive: removing supporting evidence reduces its closure rate by 26 percentage points relative to a matched control, overstatement drops from 97% to 35%, and fully correct, non-overstated conclusions rise from 3% to 43%. Applying RL focused solely on the closure decision further boosts balanced accuracy from 69.2 to 83.3 (matching the teacher) and within-source accuracy from 60.4 to 74.1, albeit with some loss in evidence dependence.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that standard LLMs are systematically overconfident when deciding whether to close investigation cases, frequently asserting causes without sufficient evidence. However, with a dedicated dataset (Nautil), structured evaluation metrics, and targeted fine-tuning plus RL, models can be trained to make substantially more evidence-grounded closure decisions and articulate what information is missing when they cannot conclude. There is a trade-off: optimizing closure accuracy via RL can reduce the tight coupling between closure decisions and explicit evidence, underscoring the need for balanced objectives when deploying LLMs as investigative assistants.

Abstract: Accident, defect and outage investigations end with a decision that ordinary question answering never faces: whether the evidence gathered so far is enough to close the case. We study this decision for LLM investigators, which request evidence from a case file, revise their hypotheses, and either close the case with a conclusion grounded in what they read or leave it open and name what is missing. This judgment does not come with capability: an untrained 9B model overstates its evidence in 97% of its answers, and a frontier model that identifies the right cause in 84% of cases still overstates in 91% and closes 17 of the 41 cases whose official finding is "cause undetermined". Measuring it is also non-trivial: the source of a case largely predicts its label, and a rule that reads only the source reaches 83.0 balanced accuracy on our test cases. We therefore evaluate closure with three tests: closure accuracy, reported against this rule and within each source; evidence dependence, which removes the grounds of a conclusion and checks whether the model stops closing; and conclusion and gap quality, a judged checklist of what the model asserts and what it says is missing. We build Nautil, 731 audited cases from aviation, rail, maritime, chemical-safety and vehicle-defect reports and production server incidents, with teacher trajectories, an out-of-distribution test set and counterfactual evidence versions. Fine-tuning a 9B model on these trajectories makes its closures follow the evidence: removing the grounds lowers its closure rate by 26 points relative to a matched control, overstatement falls from 97% to 35%, and correct, non-overstated conclusions rise from 3% to 43%. Reinforcement learning that rewards only the closure decision then raises balanced accuracy from 69.2 to 83.3, on par with the teacher, and within-source accuracy from 60.4 to 74.1, at some cost in evidence dependence.

</details>


### [49] [Source Preference in the Wild: How LLM Agents Favor Items by Source, and How to Reduce It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03195)
*Jonghyun Song,Haewon Park,Jeonghoon Shim,Woojung Song,Yohan Jo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how large language model (LLM) agents exhibit systematic preferences for content from certain sources when performing end-to-end search and decision-making across several domains, often favoring preferred sources even when they provide objectively worse options.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-based agents increasingly make decisions for users (e.g., selecting products, booking hotels, choosing papers to cite), their potential bias toward particular content sources can shape what users see and ultimately choose. Understanding whether, how, and why these agents prefer some sources over others is crucial for fairness, transparency, reliability, and for preventing distortions in information access and markets.

Method: The authors evaluate 12 different LLM agent models in an end-to-end search setting across three domains (e.g., shopping, travel, academic search). For each domain, they construct matched candidate items from different sources that satisfy user requirements to varying degrees, and control for ranking position. They then: (1) measure selection rates for items from different sources when requirements and positions are held constant; (2) analyze trade-offs between requirement satisfaction and source preference (e.g., choosing a worse item from a preferred source over a better item from a dispreferred one); (3) run interventions that hide, relabel, or manipulate source information to isolate its influence; and (4) design experiments to test mechanisms such as training-induced shortcuts (associating some sources with higher-quality items) and preconceptions triggered by missing information. They further test mitigations like supplying missing information or prompts that explicitly counter preconceptions.

Result: Across all three domains and 12 LLM agents, the models show consistent, systematic preferences for some sources and aversions to others, with substantial agreement across models on which sources are preferred. These source preferences can dominate objective quality: when a lower-quality item (fewer satisfied requirements) is from a preferred source and a higher-quality item from a dispreferred source, the worse-but-preferred item is selected roughly two-thirds of the time; the reverse scenario almost never occurs. Explicit source information by itself significantly affects choice: hiding source labels weakens the preference, and relabeling an item with a preferred source significantly boosts its selection rate. Experiments suggest two main mechanisms: reinforcement-style training that rewards picking better items can make source identity a shortcut signal for quality, and missing or incomplete item information encourages the model to fill gaps using prior beliefs about sources. Providing the missing details or adding prompts that challenge these priors reduces the observed source preference.

Conclusion: LLM agents exhibit strong and systematic source preferences that materially affect which options they choose for users, even when this conflicts with objective requirement satisfaction. These preferences appear to arise from both training dynamics (where certain sources become proxies for quality) and the model’s tendency to rely on prior beliefs when information is missing. Simple interventions—such as hiding or standardizing source information, enriching item descriptions, or explicitly prompting against stereotyped assumptions—can reduce source-driven biases, but they do not eliminate the underlying tendency. Designers of LLM-based search and recommendation agents should account for and mitigate source preference to ensure more fair, accurate, and user-aligned decisions.

Abstract: As LLM agents decide on users' behalf which product to buy, which hotel to book, or which paper to cite, a preference for items from certain sources (the sites or services they come from) shapes what users receive and which sources are selected. We study source preference in end-to-end search with 12 agent models across three domains. Comparing items from different sources that satisfy the same requirements at the same position, we find that each model prefers some sources and avoids others in every domain, largely agreeing on which. This preference can outweigh how well items satisfy the request: an item satisfying one requirement fewer is selected about two-thirds of the time when it comes from a preferred source and the better one from a dispreferred source, but almost never in the reverse case. The information identifying an item's source affects selection by itself: hiding it weakens the preference, and relabeling an item with a preferred source raises its selection rate. We test two routes to this preference: training that rewards better items can make a source a shortcut for requirement satisfaction, and missing information can trigger preconceptions about the source. Supplying missing information or a prompt countering these preconceptions reduces source preference.

</details>


### [50] [StanceEval 2026: The Second Stance Detection Shared Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03215)
*Rasha Albalawi,Nuha Albadi,Hamzah Luqman,Asma Yamani,Maram Kurdi,Saad Ezzini,Ahmed Ashraf,Maged Al-Shaibani,Nora Alturayeif*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: StanceEval 2026 is a shared task on Arabic stance detection in social media, focusing on cross-target and cross-domain generalization, where modern (often LLM-based) systems greatly outperform baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To advance automatic stance detection for Arabic social media, especially in realistic scenarios where systems must generalize to new but related targets or entirely unseen domains. Prior work often trained and tested on the same or very similar targets, which limits practical applicability. This shared task creates a standardized benchmark and comparison point for a wide variety of methods, emphasizing generalization rather than in-target performance.

Method: The organizers designed a shared task with two tracks and curated Arabic Twitter datasets with stance labels (Favor, Against, None) relative to different targets. Track 1 tests generalization from a training target (Women Empowerment) to a thematically related but distinct target (Women Driving). Track 2 tests cross-domain generalization to completely unseen targets (E-Cars and Trimester System). They defined an evaluation metric, F_avg2, as macro-averaged F1 over Favor and Against. They invited teams, provided training and test sets, and collected system outputs and descriptions. Participating teams used techniques such as fine-tuning pretrained Arabic language models, using prompt-based and retrieval-augmented LLMs, fine-tuning LLMs, and building hybrid cascaded systems.

Result: The shared task attracted 80 registered teams from 12 countries; 30 teams submitted runs, 21 were ranked in Track 1 and 13 in Track 2 after validation. Top systems achieved F_avg2 scores of 0.8994 on Track 1 and 0.9400 on Track 2, clearly surpassing strong baselines (0.7366 and 0.7475). This demonstrates major performance gains from modern LMs and LLM-based approaches on Arabic stance detection, including under cross-target and cross-domain conditions.

Conclusion: State-of-the-art models, particularly those based on large language models and fine-tuned pretrained encoders, can generalize remarkably well for Arabic stance detection, greatly outperforming baselines. Surprisingly, systems performed better on completely unseen targets than on thematically related ones, likely because of dataset-specific factors such as target polarization, class imbalance, dialect variation, and sarcasm. The task provides a benchmark and insights for future research on robust, generalizable stance detection in Arabic social media.

Abstract: StanceEval 2026 is the second edition of the StanceEval shared task series on stance detection in Arabic social media text. Stance detection aims to identify a writer's stance toward a given topic. Given a tweet and a target, participating systems must determine whether the writer's stance is Favor, Against, or None. This edition focuses on cross-target generalization across two distinct evaluation tracks: Track 1 evaluates thematically related cross-target transfer (testing on Women Driving, related to Women Empowerment from training data), while Track 2 evaluates cross-domain transfer to completely unseen targets (E-Cars and Trimester System). The shared task attracted 80 registered teams from 12 countries. During the evaluation phase, 30 unique teams submitted entries, with 21 teams officially ranked in Track 1 and 13 in Track 2 following validation filtering, and 20 teams submitting system-description papers. Participating teams employed diverse methodologies, including fine-tuned pretrained language models, prompt-based and retrieval-augmented large language models (LLMs), fine-tuned LLMs, and hybrid cascades. Top systems achieved impressive $F_{avg2}$ scores of 0.8994 on Track 1 and 0.9400 on Track 2, substantially outperforming the strongest baselines (0.7366 and 0.7475, respectively), where $F_{avg2}$ denotes the macro-averaged F1 score over the Favor and Against classes. Counterintuitively, performance on the unseen targets was higher than on the related target, a disparity could be driven by extreme target polarization, class imbalance, and dialectal or sarcastic nuance across topics.

</details>


### [51] [Collective Bias Mitigation via Model Routing and Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03240)
*Mingzhe Du,Luu Anh Tuan,Xiaobao Wu,Yichong Huang,Yue Liu,Dong Huang,Huijun Liu,Bin Ji,Jie M. Zhang,See-Kiong Ng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes Collective Bias Mitigation (CBM), a framework that reduces bias in large language models by coordinating multiple diverse LLMs instead of relying on a single model’s self-debiasing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used in high‑stakes domains (public health, finance, governance), where both accuracy and fairness are critical. Current LLMs often inherit or amplify biases from training data. Existing self‑debiasing methods depend on a single model recognizing and correcting its own biases, which is weak against deeply ingrained stereotypes. The authors aim to find a more robust, systematic way to mitigate bias by leveraging multiple models’ complementary strengths.

Method: They introduce Collective Bias Mitigation (CBM), a framework that orchestrates several diverse LLMs to reduce bias. CBM learns fine‑grained behavior patterns of individual models and enables knowledge sharing across them. The paper systematically designs and evaluates different multi‑model topologies, including “Debating” (models argue/critique each other’s outputs) and “Committee” (models form an ensemble whose combined decision is used). The framework is compared to standalone/self‑debiased single‑model baselines using quantitative bias metrics such as age‑bias scores.

Result: CBM significantly reduces measured bias compared with individual models and self‑debiasing baselines. For example, in a top‑7 model ensemble configuration, the Committee topology reduces the age bias score from 0.25 to 0.10. Both Debating and Committee structures achieve notable bias mitigation, demonstrating that coordinated multi‑LLM approaches outperform single‑LLM strategies.

Conclusion: Coordinating multiple diverse LLMs through the CBM framework leads to substantially fairer outputs than relying on one model’s self‑debiasing. The Committee topology, in particular, offers a good trade‑off between mitigation strength and computational cost. The study shows that careful selection and organization of heterogeneous LLMs is a promising direction for practical, scalable bias mitigation in real‑world applications.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in public health, finance, and governance, requiring both accuracy and societal value alignment. Despite recent advances, LLMs often perpetuate or amplify bias embedded in their training data, posing challenges to fairness. While self-debiasing encourages an LLM to identify and correct its own biases, relying on a single model's intrinsic knowledge may be insufficient to address deeply ingrained stereotypes. To address this limitation, we introduce Collective Bias Mitigation (CBM), a framework that alleviates bias by learning fine-grained model behavior and fostering knowledge sharing among diverse LLMs. This work is the first to systematically explore the effective selection and organization of distinct LLMs to cultivate fairer LLM responses. Experiments show CBM substantially outperforms standalone baselines (e.g., in the top-7 setting, Committee lowers the age bias score from 0.25 to 0.10). Our Debating and Committee topologies achieve substantial bias reduction, with the latter balancing mitigation effectiveness and inference cost, highlighting the potential of CBM for fairer LLMs.

</details>


### [52] [Shrome at Touché: Soft-Vote Ensembling and Counter-Causal Augmentation for Causality Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03268)
*Roham Zendehdel Nobari,Shayan Sooratgar*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents a system for detecting and interpreting both causal and counter-causal claims in news text, achieving state-of-the-art performance on the Countercausal News Corpus (CCNC).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing causality extraction systems often misinterpret counter-causal sentences whose wording looks causal (e.g., containing words like "caused" or "led to") but actually deny or negate causation. This leads to wrong polarity judgments and unreliable causal reasoning in news analysis. The paper aims to build a robust system that can detect causal statements, extract cause/effect spans, and distinguish between supporting, denying, and non-causal uses.

Method: The task is split into three subtasks, each with its own model: (1) Detection: a fine-tuned sentence-level classifier, augmented with a cross-task rule that uses extracted spans to filter out false positives. (2) Extraction: an ensemble of three RoBERTa-large sequence taggers with BILOU+CRF, combined by averaging token-level scores before CRF decoding instead of voting on final spans. (3) Polarity: a classifier trained with both gold data and synthetic counter-causal sentences generated by a large language model using nine prompt patterns adapted from prior work, with automatic structural filtering of the generated data.

Result: On the CCNC test set, the system achieves F1 0.869 for causal-sentence detection, macro-F1 0.817 for polarity classification, and a granularity-adjusted F1 0.728 for causal-span extraction in the organizers’ causal-only evaluation, the best extraction score among all participating systems including the baseline. Development data is used strictly for model selection and ablation studies.

Conclusion: Decomposing counter-causal news analysis into detection, extraction, and polarity subtasks, combined with ensemble sequence tagging and LLM-based data augmentation for scarce counter-causal patterns, yields strong performance and state-of-the-art extraction accuracy on the CCNC benchmark. The approach demonstrates that careful modeling of counter-causal language and cross-task interaction can substantially improve causal information extraction from news.

Abstract: Touché 2026 extends causality extraction to counter-causal claims: news sentences whose surface form appears causal but whose meaning denies the causation, as in "It is falsely believed that X caused Y." A system that relies on surface cues such as "caused" or "led to" will accept such a sentence as causal and give it the wrong polarity. On the Countercausal News Corpus (CCNC), the task has three subtasks: deciding whether a sentence is causal (detection), locating its cause and effect spans (extraction), and labeling its polarity as procausal, counter-causal, or uncausal. We build one model per subtask. Detection is a fine-tuned classifier with a single cross-task rule that uses the extracted spans to remove false positives. For extraction, we ensemble three RoBERTa-large BILOU+CRF taggers by averaging their token-level scores before decoding, rather than voting on the spans each tagger produces. For polarity, where labeled counter-causal examples are scarcest, we add training sentences generated by a large language model prompted with nine patterns of counter-causal expression adapted from Hagen et al., keeping only those that pass automatic structural checks. On the held-out CCNC test set, the system reaches F1 0.869 on detection and macro-F1 0.817 on polarity, and in the organizers' final causal-only evaluation of extraction it scores granularity-adjusted F1 0.728, the highest extraction score among all submissions including the organizers' baseline. The development split is used only for component selection and the ablations reported in the paper.

</details>


### [53] [To Jev or Not? Evaluating the Accuracy and Efficiency of Structured Decision Models for Hate-Speech Moderation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03324)
*Demetris Paschalides,George Pallis,Marios D. Dikaiakos*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates structured LLM-based decision models for hate-speech moderation, comparing them to commercial LLMs and other baselines, and finds they can be nearly as accurate at far lower cost, while explicit definitions and multi-step questioning do not consistently help.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online platforms must moderate vast amounts of content for hate speech, but definitions vary across datasets and communities. LLMs both exacerbate the problem by making it easier to generate harmful content and offer potential tools for moderation. There is a need to understand whether structured decision models that take natural-language definitions and output discrete labels can provide efficient, adaptable moderation without expensive task-specific training and with controllable cost and latency.

Method: The authors introduce HATEDECIDE, a benchmark-style evaluation framework. They configure six types of structured decision models (decision-model configurations) that accept natural-language criteria and choose among predefined labels. These are tested on four different hate-speech datasets. They systematically vary prompts: (1) with or without supplying the dataset’s specific definition of hate speech, and (2) with single-shot vs decomposed multi-question formats. They compare performance (macro-F1), latency, and cost against multiple baselines: specialized moderation models, zero-shot LLMs, commercial LLM-based moderation, and supervised models trained on each dataset. They also construct a focused diagnostic set of test cases to directly compare the best decision model with the best commercial LLM.

Result: Commercial LLM-based moderation only clearly beats all decision-model configurations on one of the four datasets. Providing the dataset’s hate-speech definition changes up to 28% of individual predictions but does not consistently improve overall classification metrics. Decomposing the moderation decision into multiple questions leads to significant performance gains in only about 20% of comparisons. On the diagnostic dataset, the best hosted decision model nearly matches the best commercial LLM (within 1.6 macro-F1 points) while reducing inference cost by around 97%.

Conclusion: Structured LLM-based decision models can achieve moderation performance close to commercial LLMs at a fraction of the cost, suggesting they are promising for scalable, inexpensive hate-speech moderation. However, simply supplying explicit definitions of hate speech or decomposing decisions into multiple questions does not reliably yield better classification, indicating that naively adding more structure or instructions is not a guaranteed path to improved moderation quality.

Abstract: The scale of online content makes hate-speech moderation challenging, while Large Language Models (LLMs) enable harmful material to be produced and adapted more easily. Moderation therefore requires efficient classifiers that can accommodate different definitions of hate speech. Recent structured decision models accept natural-language criteria and select among specified answers, raising the question of whether they can meet these requirements without task-specific training. We present HATEDECIDE, an evaluation of six decision-model configurations on four hate-speech datasets against specialized moderation, zero-shot, commercial, and supervised baselines. We examine whether supplying a dataset's definition, or decomposing it into multiple questions, improves classification, and we measure their latency and cost. We find that commercial LLMs significantly outperform all decision models on only one dataset. Supplying definitions changes up to 28\% of predictions without consistently improving classification, and decomposition significantly improves performance in only 20\% of the comparisons. On a diagnostic set of test cases, the best hosted decision model comes within 1.6 macro-F1 points of the best commercial LLM at approximately 97\% lower inference cost. These results identify opportunities for inexpensive moderation, while showing that explicit criteria and additional questions do not reliably improve classification.

</details>


### [54] [SyntaxBench: A Statistical Diagnostic Framework for Character-Level Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03329)
*Mohsen Larni,Sobhan Ebrahimi Azar,Pouyan Nahed,Kazem Taghva*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SyntaxBench is a diagnostic benchmark for testing and statistically analyzing character-level reasoning of large language models across several synthetic and naturalistic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of character-level reasoning in large language models mostly rely on isolated probes and simple aggregate accuracy, which miss how tokenization, input type, and reasoning modes affect performance in realistic scenarios where small syntactic errors matter.

Method: The authors build SyntaxBench, a benchmark with six tasks—five core tasks (character counting, letter containment, palindrome detection, edit distance, longest-string selection) using paired English vs. length-matched random strings, and a harder index_to_span substring extraction task over 200-500 word documents. They test eight open-weight models from 2B–32B parameters under 11 reasoning-mode configurations and evaluate with a rich statistical suite: exact and relaxed accuracy, Cohen’s kappa, McNemar tests with odds ratios, bootstrap confidence intervals, Kendall’s tau, class-conditional metrics, tokenization analysis, and multiple-comparison correction.

Result: (1) Tokenization has a strong effect on performance: random strings map to shorter tokens (1.892 vs. 3.169 characters per token), making characters more directly visible, and English character-counting accuracy degrades as words span more tokens. (2) Reasoning modes (e.g., with or without chain-of-thought style "thinking") do not universally help: some models like Gemma4-31B show little change on easy tasks, and others like Qwen3.6-27B actually perform worse with thinking on palindrome detection. (3) The index_to_span task is still very challenging, with the best four-shot exact-match accuracy only 6.75%.

Conclusion: Robust character-level evaluation of LLMs requires carefully controlled paired inputs, explicit consideration of tokenization effects, and analysis of reasoning modes, going beyond simple aggregate accuracy; SyntaxBench provides such a diagnostic framework and reveals current limitations, especially on harder substring extraction tasks.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used where small syntactic errors matter, yet character-level reasoning is still evaluated mostly through isolated probes and aggregate accuracy. We introduce SyntaxBench, a diagnostic benchmark and statistical evaluation framework for character-level reasoning. It contains five core tasks, character counting, letter containment, palindrome detection, edit distance, and longest-string selection, plus index_to_span, a harder substring-extraction stress test. The five core tasks use paired English and character-length-matched random-string inputs. index_to_span documents share a 200-500 word band and are not character-length matched. All six tasks use zero-, one-, and four-shot prompts.
  We evaluate eight open-weight models from 2B to 32B parameters across 11 reasoning-mode configurations. The framework reports exact-match and relaxed accuracy, Cohen's kappa, paired McNemar tests with odds ratios, bootstrap confidence intervals, Kendall's tau, class-conditional metrics, tokenization analysis, and multiple-comparison-corrected tests. Three findings stand out. First, tokenization shapes accuracy: random strings are more character-visible than English strings (1.892 vs. 3.169 characters per token), and character-counting accuracy falls as English words occupy more tokens.
