Second, reasoning mode is not uniformly helpful: Gemma4-31B is nearly unchanged across modes on the near-saturated tasks, while Qwen3.6-27B is worse with thinking on palindrome detection (0.952 non-thinking vs. 0.886 thinking at four-shot). Third, index_to_span remains largely unsolved; the best four-shot exact-match accuracy is 6.75%.
  Character-level evaluation needs controlled inputs, paired tests, and analyses of tokenization and reasoning mode rather than aggregate accuracy alone.

</details>


### [55] [CLIMB: Confidence-Guided Complementary Evidence for Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03421)
*Hang Gao,Wujiang Xu,Zhixing Zhang,Kai Mei,Jingyi Yang,Dimitris N. Metaxas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes CLIMB, a training-free, inference-time framework that improves multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) for knowledge-intensive visual question answering by selecting complementary evidence and refining answers only when confidence increases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal RAG systems rely on simple Top-K retrieval or reranking, which often yields redundant passages and offers little control over whether updated answers are truly supported by evidence. Knowledge-intensive visual QA tasks require better use of external textual evidence beyond the image and the model’s stored knowledge, as well as mechanisms to avoid unnecessary or poorly supported answer changes.

Method: The authors introduce CLIMB, a framework that operates at inference time without retraining the retriever or MLLM. CLIMB first builds a compact evidence pool via an MMR-style objective that trades off relevance to the query with low redundancy among passages. Then, within this fixed pool, it iteratively refines the answer using an R/E/C critic that scores passages on relevance, evidence specificity, and cross-modal alignment. An evidence-grounded confidence estimator decides whether to accept an updated answer, allowing refinement to stop when confidence no longer improves.

Result: On the Encyclopedic-VQA and InfoSeek benchmarks, CLIMB outperforms existing retrieval-augmented multimodal baselines in accuracy for knowledge-intensive visual QA. Ablation studies show that each core component—complementary evidence pooling, critic-based scoring, and iterative confidence-controlled refinement—contributes measurable gains, confirming their importance to the overall performance.

Conclusion: CLIMB offers an effective, training-free enhancement to multimodal RAG for knowledge-intensive visual question answering. By selecting complementary evidence and enforcing confidence-controlled refinement, it improves answer quality and efficiency without altering the underlying retriever or MLLM, and its components are empirically validated through ablations.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have shown strong visual reasoning abilities, but knowledge-intensive visual question answering often requires external textual evidence beyond the image and the model's parametric knowledge. Existing multimodal RAG systems commonly rely on Top-$K$ retrieval or reranking, which may return redundant passages and provide limited control over whether an answer update is sufficiently supported by the retrieved evidence. We propose \textit{CLIMB}, a training-free inference-time framework for multimodal RAG. CLIMB first constructs a compact complementary evidence pool using an MMR-style objective that balances query relevance and passage-level redundancy. It then performs confidence-controlled refinement within this fixed pool: an R/E/C critic scores passages by relevance, evidence specificity, and cross-modal alignment, while an evidence-grounded confidence estimator accepts an updated answer only when the estimated confidence increases. This design provides a simple stopping criterion and reduces unnecessary refinement without modifying the underlying retriever or MLLM. Experiments on Encyclopedic-VQA and InfoSeek show that CLIMB consistently improves over retrieval-augmented multimodal baselines. Ablations further indicate that complementary pooling, critic-based scoring, and iterative confidence-controlled refinement each contribute to the final performance.

</details>


### [56] [Single-Pass Uncertainty Heads for Claim-Level Hallucination Detection in Persian Medical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03482)
*Mehrdad Ghassabi,Pedram Rostami,Hamidreza Baradaran Kashani,Sadra Hakim,Audrina Ebrahimi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper adapts an LLM Uncertainty Head framework to detect hallucinations in Persian medical language models efficiently, achieving solid single-pass claim-level uncertainty estimates without repeated sampling or retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical LLMs must minimize hallucinations because incorrect outputs can directly harm patients, but common detection methods like repeated sampling are computationally expensive, and existing uncertainty modules are not easily transferable across model architectures or languages. There is also a lack of tools and datasets specifically for Persian medical models, especially at the fine-grained claim level.

Method: The authors adapt the LLM Uncertainty Head (LUH) framework to two Persian medical LLM backbones (Gaokerena-V and Gaokerena-R) built on Aya-Expanse-8B. They first measure response variability via five-run consistency on a 168-question Iranian medical entrance exam. Then they build two Persian claim-level hallucination datasets, each with 1,600 responses per backbone and automatically generated labels. Lightweight uncertainty heads are trained on frozen backbone features, specifically attention maps and token probabilities, to predict hallucination likelihood per claim in a single forward pass, without modifying the base model or using retrieval/re-sampling at inference.

Result: Gaokerena-V shows lower five-run answer consistency than Aya-Expanse-8B on the exam benchmark, while Gaokerena-R is comparable. The claim-level uncertainty heads achieve PR-AUCs of 0.4820 and 0.4652, which are 2.30x and 2.66x higher than random baselines, and ROC-AUCs of 0.7852 and 0.7810 on held-out test sets. The method works in a single inference pass and does not require expensive sampling or external retrieval.

Conclusion: Single-pass claim-level uncertainty estimation via lightweight heads on frozen LLM features is feasible for Persian medical models and gives substantially better-than-random hallucination detection without added inference cost from sampling or retrieval. However, this is an initial study with small test splits and automatically generated labels, so more extensive, human-validated datasets and broader evaluations are needed to fully validate and generalize the approach.

Abstract: Hallucination detection is particularly important for medical language models, but repeated-sampling approaches are expensive and existing uncertainty-head resources do not directly transfer to a new backbone and language. We adapt the LLM Uncertainty Head (LUH) framework to Aya-Expanse-8B-based Persian medical models, using Gaokerena-V and Gaokerena-R as two previously developed backbones. We first examine response variability on a 168-question Iranian medical entrance examination and observe substantially lower five-run consistency for Gaokerena-V than for Aya-Expanse-8B, whereas Gaokerena-R is comparable to Aya-Expanse-8B. We then construct two paired claim-level hallucination datasets directly in Persian, containing 1,600 responses for each backbone, and train lightweight claim-level heads on frozen backbone attention maps and token probabilities. On held-out test splits, the heads obtain PR-AUCs of 0.4820 and 0.4652, corresponding to 2.30 and 2.66 times their respective random baselines, and ROC-AUCs of 0.7852 and 0.7810. The heads require neither retrieval nor repeated sampling at inference time. These results provide an initial study of single-pass claim-level uncertainty estimation for Persian medical language models; the test splits are small and the labels are automatically generated.

</details>


### [57] [Learning from Repaired Reasoning: Root-Cause-Guided On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03515)
*Chenglei Shen,Haoyang Yao,Weijie Yu,Song Jin,Xiao Zhang,Jun Xu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose RC-OPD, a distillation method that fixes a model’s own reasoning errors step-by-step and uses those repairs as supervision, improving reasoning performance and avoiding common pitfalls of on-policy self-distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing on-policy self-distillation techniques supervise a student model with reference solutions. This creates a reasoning mismatch: the guidance explains how to solve the problem correctly, but not why the student’s own reasoning failed. As a result, the student may copy final answers while retaining flawed reasoning steps. Additionally, applying the same reference hindsight along the whole trajectory can overconstrain reasoning, causing a distillation trap that interferes with learning flexible but valid solution paths. The authors are motivated to design a distillation approach that directly targets and repairs the student’s actual reasoning errors while preserving and reinforcing its valid intermediate steps.

Method: They introduce Root-Cause-Guided On-Policy Distillation (RC-OPD). For each failed reasoning attempt generated by the student model, RC-OPD: (1) finds the earliest substantive reasoning error (the root cause of failure); (2) constructs a local correction to that step; and (3) treats the corrected intermediate result as an anchor representing a valid prefix of reasoning. An iterative diagnosis–repair–continuation loop lets the student continue its reasoning from the corrected anchor, and the system keeps locating and repairing further errors within a limited repair budget. When this chain of repairs eventually yields a correct final answer, the process creates two kinds of supervision: (a) root-cause-guided distillation, which uses explicit failure diagnoses and corrective goals to supervise the erroneous segments; and (b) anchor-guided distillation, which supplies reasoning trajectories that lead from the original prefix to the repaired intermediate states, thereby reinforcing the valid parts of the student’s reasoning.

Result: Across multiple benchmarks and different model scales, RC-OPD improves reasoning performance compared to standard on-policy self-distillation and other baselines. The experiments show that the method reduces reasoning mismatch (the gap between guidance and the student’s actual errors) and alleviates the distillation trap, leading to higher accuracy and more robust reasoning behaviors. Detailed analyses validate that supervising error segments with repair-based guidance and reinforcing valid prefixes are both critical contributors to the gains.

Conclusion: RC-OPD demonstrates that guiding distillation using repairs of the student’s own reasoning—rather than generic reference solutions—can more effectively correct reasoning errors while preserving useful intermediate reasoning. By explicitly diagnosing root-cause errors, locally repairing them, and iteratively continuing from corrected anchors, the method mitigates reasoning mismatch and distillation traps in on-policy self-distillation. This results in substantial and scalable improvements in reasoning-heavy tasks, suggesting a promising direction for training more reliable reasoning models.

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) uses reference solutions as privileged hindsight to supervise student-generated reasoning trajectories. However, reference-based guidance may explain a correct solution without addressing why the student's own reasoning fails. This reasoning mismatch between the guidance provided and the correction needed can encourage the student to borrow correct conclusions while leaving its reasoning errors unresolved. Moreover, applying the same hindsight throughout the trajectory risks a distillation trap, where unnecessary constraints on valid reasoning compete with correction of substantive errors. To address these issues, we propose Root-Cause-Guided On-Policy Distillation (RC-OPD), which uses repairs of the student's own reasoning to provide guidance that addresses its specific errors while building on valid progress. For each failed attempt, RC-OPD locates the earliest substantive error, develops a local correction, and uses the corrected intermediate result as an anchor for the valid prefix. An iterative diagnosis--repair--continuation process tests the repairs through student continuation, identifying further errors within a fixed repair budget. For repair chains that reach a correct answer, root--cause--guided distillation uses failure diagnoses and corrective goals to supervise the erroneous segments, while anchor-guided distillation supports the corresponding valid prefixes with reasoning chains leading to the repaired intermediate results. We evaluate RC-OPD across multiple datasets and model scales. Extensive experiments and analyses show that it mitigates reasoning mismatch and the distillation trap, yielding substantial performance gains.

</details>


### [58] [Structured Composition of Verifiable Atomic Insights for Table-to-Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03525)
*Teng Lin,Xinyu Liu,Nan Tang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ComInsight, a framework that generates long analytical reports from tables by composing many small, verifiable data insights into coherent evidence chains, improving factuality and coverage over prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing table-to-report systems either rely on sequential data agents or direct LLM generation. Both tend to lock onto early, local patterns and thus miss broader, cross-table, or multi-dimensional insights—a phenomenon described as exploration bias. There is also a need for reports whose claims are fully traceable and verifiable via the underlying data and queries. The authors aim to overcome these limitations by redesigning how insights are discovered and composed.

Method: The authors reformulate insight discovery as composing atomic evidences. They (1) define an atomic insight as the smallest executable analytical unit that fits predefined analysis patterns; (2) systematically enumerate all such atomic insights from the database schema and content; (3) build a multi-relational insight graph where nodes are verified data facts and edges represent logical, temporal, or hierarchical relations; and (4) apply composition operators that merge atomic nodes into higher-order composite conclusions. Each composite insight is linked to executable SQL and detailed provenance.

Result: On three benchmarks—InsightBench, DDR-Bench, and T2R-Bench—ComInsight outperforms strong baselines on factual correctness, novelty of insights, and structural completeness of generated reports, indicating better coverage and reliability in table-to-report generation.

Conclusion: ComInsight demonstrates that structuring table-to-report generation as compositional reasoning over atomic, verifiable insights leads to more accurate, novel, and structurally complete reports. By grounding every composite conclusion in executable queries and explicit provenance, it offers a reliable, efficient, and explainable approach to automated report generation from relational tables.

Abstract: Table-to-report generation refers to the task of automatically generating article-level analyt- ical reports from relational tables and is an essential capability for automated data science and decision support. Its central challenge lies in systematically discovering verifiable com- posite insights across tables, attributes, and analytical perspectives, and organizing them into coherent, complete, and traceable evidence chains. Existing methods primarily rely on sequential, reactive data agents or direct Large Language Model(LLM) generation. They suffer from exploration bias: early local observations constrain subsequent actions, causing models to focus prematurely on local analyzes and miss cross-table or cross-dimensional evidence. We propose ComInsight, which reformulates insight discovery as the composition of atomic evidences. We first define an atomic insight as the smallest executable analytical unit conforming to a predefined analysis pattern and enumerate all valid atomic insights from database schema and content. These atoms are then organized into a multi-relational insight graph, where nodes represent verified data facts and edges encode logical, temporal, or hierarchical relations. Finally, a set of composition operators systematically fuses atomic nodes into higher-order composite conclusions. Every composite output is accompanied by executable SQL and fine-grained provenance, ensuring full verifiability. Across three benchmarks InsightBench, DDR-Bench, and T2R-Bench, ComInsight consistently outperforms strong baselines in factual correctness, novelty, and structural completeness. We believe ComInsight offers a reliable, efficient, and explainable path toward table-to-report generation.

</details>


### [59] [Author Representation Strategies for Zero-Shot Authorship Attribution: A Comparative Study of LLM-Based and Embedding-Based Approaches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03531)
*Nudrat Habib,Tosin Adewumi,Sana Sabah Al-Azzawi,Marcus Liwicki,Elisa Barney*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how different ways of representing authors’ writing styles affect zero-shot authorship attribution, finding that explicit author representations greatly help and that a new two-stage style-embedding method works best.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot authorship attribution is hard because the system must recognize subtle stylistic cues without any task-specific training data. Prior work underexplores how to represent authors in such settings, especially using modern LLMs and style embeddings. The paper aims to clarify which author representations are most effective and practical in zero-shot conditions.

Method: The authors compare four strategies for zero-shot authorship attribution: (1) a label-only prompting baseline with just author names, (2) prompts augmented with representative writing samples for each author, (3) prompts augmented with LLM-generated descriptions of each author’s style, and (4) an embedding-based method (LISA) that uses neural style embeddings and cosine similarity. They study prompt design, then propose a two-stage LISA framework: first reduce the candidate author set, then select effective embedding dimensions for attribution. They evaluate all methods on authorship attribution tasks without task-specific training.

Result: Label-only zero-shot prompting performs poorly for authorship attribution. Adding explicit author representations (samples, style descriptions, or style embeddings) consistently boosts performance. The two-stage LISA embedding framework achieves the best overall attribution accuracy. LLM-generated style descriptions offer a compact, text-based representation but sacrifice some performance compared to LISA.

Conclusion: Effective author representation is crucial for zero-shot authorship attribution. Simple label-only prompts are inadequate, while richer representations—especially the proposed two-stage LISA style-embedding framework—substantially improve performance. LLM-generated descriptions are more compact but less accurate than embeddings. Overall, current open-source LLMs alone are not sufficient for robust, zero-shot authorship attribution unless enhanced by better representation learning methods.

Abstract: Authorship Attribution (AA) requires capturing fine-grained stylistic characteristics, making it particularly challenging in zero-shot (ZS) settings where no task-specific supervision is available. In this work, we investigate the effect of author representations on ZS AA by evaluating a label-only prompting baseline together with three author representation strategies: representative writing samples, LLM-generated descriptions, and style embeddings (LISA). The first three approaches perform attribution using LLM prompting, while the embedding-based approach uses style embeddings with cosine similarity. We investigate the influence of prompt design and propose a two-stage embedding-based attribution framework that combines candidate space reduction with embedding-dimension selection. The results show that label-only ZS AA is ineffective, while incorporating author-specific representations consistently improves attribution performance. Among the evaluated approaches, the proposed two-stage LISA framework achieves the strongest overall performance, whereas LLM-generated style descriptions provide a substantially more compact representation of author style at the cost of some attribution performance. These findings demonstrate the importance of author representation in ZS AA, while indicating that current open-source LLMs remain insufficient for robust attribution without more effective representation learning.

</details>


### [60] [Writerslogic at PAN 2026: Process over Content for Robust Detection under Domain Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03565)
*David L. Condrey*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents the Writerslogic systems for three PAN@CLEF 2026 shared tasks and unifies them with a theory about which stylistic features remain robust under domain shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and systematically exploit which stylometric and behavioral features of text remain reliable when the domain, topic, or generator changes between training and test, and to use this understanding to build stronger systems for authorship- and generation-related tasks.

Method: They propose a framework based on support overlap between train and test distributions, defining a taxonomy of features (domain-anchored, domain-portable, domain-invariant). Using this, they engineer feature sets and system architectures for three tasks: (1) Reasoning Trajectory Detection with domain-shifted math vs non-math data, using agreement between models like Opus and Sonnet and a query-refusal decomposition; (2) Voight-Kampff Generative AI Detection via a calibrated ensemble of DeBERTa-v2, LightGBM on 44 stylometric features, and SVM on n-gram TF-IDF with stacking and isotonic calibration; (3) Multi-Author Writing Style Analysis using spectral clustering on character n-gram similarity graphs, normalized compression distance for boundaries, and SmolLM-135M perplexity for neural change-point detection.

Result: On Reasoning Trajectory Detection, they achieved 1st place in source detection (0.85 macro F1) and 3rd in safety classification (0.66 macro F1) despite strong domain shift. On Voight-Kampff, their best ensemble reached 0.891 overall with well-balanced sub-metrics (0.853–0.902). For Multi-Author Writing Style Analysis, no official scores are available due to a platform issue, so they only provide the method and expected behavior. Empirically, domain-portable and domain-invariant features like vocabulary fingerprints, compression measures, and character n-grams proved robust, while generator-specific cues failed under shift.

Conclusion: Feature robustness under domain shift is determined more by train–test support overlap than by effect size in training, which motivates preferring domain-portable/invariant features tied to generation processes over domain-anchored, generator-specific signals. This framework both explains cross-domain performance patterns in stylometric tasks and guides the design of competitive systems for reasoning trajectory detection, AI-generated text detection, and multi-author style analysis.

Abstract: We describe the Writerslogic systems for three PAN at CLEF 2026 shared tasks (Reasoning Trajectory Detection, Voight-Kampff Generative AI Detection, and Multi-Author Writing Style Analysis), unified by a shared analytical framework: feature robustness under distribution shift is governed by support overlap between training and test distributions, not by training-set effect size. This yields a taxonomy (domain-anchored, domain-portable, domain-invariant) that explains why generator-specific features die under domain shift while vocabulary fingerprints (hapax ratio, Yule's K, Heaps' exponent), compression measures, and character n-grams survive. On Reasoning Trajectory Detection, where training was entirely mathematics and 84 percent of test was unseen domains, the framework guided system design to 1st place in source detection (0.85 macro F1 via Opus-Sonnet agreement) and 3rd place in safety classification (0.66 macro F1 via query-refusal decomposition). For Voight-Kampff, we built a calibrated ensemble of DeBERTa-v2 (ONNX), multi-seed LightGBM with 44 domain-portable stylometric features, and SVM on n-gram TF-IDF, combined via learned stacking with isotonic calibration; the best configuration achieved 0.891 on the PAN 2026 test set with balanced sub-metrics (0.853 to 0.902 across all evaluation dimensions). For Multi-Author Writing Style Analysis, we describe a system fusing spectral clustering over character n-gram similarity graphs, normalized compression distance for local boundary detection, and SmolLM-135M perplexity for neural change-point detection; a platform mix-up meant our run never reached the official evaluation, so we report the design and its a priori predictions. Across all three tasks, features measuring generation process properties are designed to outperform features measuring generated content properties under domain shift.

</details>


### [61] [Writerslogic at the CLEF 2026 SimpleText Track: Multi-Candidate LLM Simplification and Stacked Complexity Spotting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03567)
*David L. Condrey*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents the Writerslogic team’s systems for the CLEF 2026 SimpleText shared task, achieving top or near-top performance on both text simplification and hallucination/overgeneration detection in biomedical texts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automatic text simplification and reliable detection of hallucinated or ungrounded content are critical for making complex biomedical information accessible and trustworthy, especially in settings like Cochrane systematic reviews. Existing systems often struggle to balance clarity, faithfulness to the source, and robustness across languages. The shared task provides a standardized benchmark to compare different approaches to sentence/document simplification and to identifying unsupported content.

Method: For Task 1 (text simplification), the authors use GPT-4o-mini to generate five candidate simplifications per sentence at different sampling temperatures and then apply a reference-free scoring heuristic to select the best one. The heuristic jointly rewards shorter outputs (compression), retention of important source words, use of vocabulary typical of Cochrane Plain Language Summaries, and lexical simplicity. A Claude Sonnet 4-based submission is used for sentence-level simplification evaluation. For Task 2 (complexity/hallucination spotting), they fine-tune a DeBERTa-v3-large model in an NLI (natural language inference) setting on 350K labeled (source, sentence) pairs. The model reads the most relevant source sentence as the premise and the candidate as the hypothesis and learns to classify whether the candidate is grounded or hallucinated, including both binary identification and multi-class error categorization. They also experiment with ensembles for improved F1.

Result: On Task 1.1 (sentence-level simplification), their Claude Sonnet 4 system achieves SARI 47.43 and BLEU 14.21, ranking first among sentence-level systems and third overall when including document-level systems. On Task 2.1 (binary overgeneration identification), the fine-tuned DeBERTa system attains a document-level macro F1 of 0.8081, with an ensemble variant reaching 0.8085, making it the top system within the identification track and second overall behind AIIR Lab. On Task 2.2 (multi-class error classification), their best system obtains 0.804 accuracy, again placing second among unique teams, slightly behind AIIR Lab’s 0.827. All evaluations are conducted on English and multilingual biomedical texts from Cochrane reviews.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a multi-candidate LLM-based generation strategy combined with a task-specific, reference-free scoring heuristic is highly effective for sentence-level biomedical text simplification, achieving state-of-the-art SARI scores in the shared task. For hallucination and overgeneration detection, reframing the problem as NLI and fine-tuning a strong encoder model (DeBERTa-v3-large) yields robust, competitive performance in both binary detection and fine-grained error categorization. These results demonstrate that carefully engineered pipelines using existing large language models and NLI-based detectors can provide accurate, trustworthy simplification of biomedical content across languages, making complex evidence more accessible while minimizing unsupported claims.

Abstract: We describe the Writerslogic team's participation in the CLEF 2026 SimpleText shared task, addressing Task 1 (text simplification) and Task 2 (complexity spotting). For Task 1, we develop a multi-candidate generation pipeline using GPT-4o-mini that produces five simplification candidates per sentence at varying temperatures, then selects the best candidate using a reference-free scoring heuristic that rewards compression, source word retention, Cochrane Plain Language Summary vocabulary usage, and lexical simplicity. On Task 1.1 (sentence-level simplification), our Claude Sonnet 4 submission achieves SARI 47.43 and BLEU 14.21, the top-ranked sentence-level system (3rd on the combined Task 1 leaderboard, behind two document-level submissions). For Task 2, we fine-tune a DeBERTa-v3-large NLI model on 350K labeled (source, sentence) pairs, framing hallucination detection as natural language inference. The model reads the most relevant source sentence as premise and the candidate as hypothesis, directly learning to distinguish grounded from hallucinated content. On Task 2.1 (binary overgeneration identification), our fine-tuned DeBERTa system achieves 0.8081 document-level macro F1 (0.8085 in our best ensemble), the top-ranked entry within the identification track and 2nd among teams overall, behind AIIR Lab (0.8197). On Task 2.2 (multi-class error classification), our best submission reaches 0.804 multiclass accuracy, ranking 2nd among unique teams behind AIIR Lab (0.827). We evaluate both tasks on English and multilingual biomedical text from Cochrane systematic reviews.

</details>


### [62] [FALCON: A Model and Dataset Agnostic Framework for Synthetic Data Generation for NL2SQL Pairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03625)
*Darian Lee,Shannon Rumsey,Jack St. Clair,Xinyi Tang,Aditya Bansal,Yuanming Shi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FALCON is a framework for generating realistic, ambiguity-aware NL-to-SQL training data using compact open models, producing complex SQL and rich natural language that better prepares models for real-world database querying.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current synthetic NL-to-SQL data generation often yields oversimplified, unambiguous queries, which do not reflect the ambiguity and structural complexity of real-world natural language database access. There is a need for a low-cost, locally runnable way to create challenging, realistic training data that captures both SQL complexity and linguistic richness while remaining model- and database-agnostic.

Method: FALCON combines reserved-word SQL seeding to ensure structurally complex SQL queries, persona-based prompting to induce diverse and ambiguity-rich natural language questions, and an alignment-based filtering stage that discards genuinely incorrect examples while retaining complex but valid ones. It uses compact open-source language models to generate and refine NL-SQL pairs and produces difficulty-stratified data similar in complexity to strong real-world benchmarks.

Result: Human evaluation shows high and consistent quality of the generated NL-SQL pairs across different model sizes. The generated dataset surpasses existing benchmarks in SQL structural complexity and in the richness of the associated natural language. In experiments, models trained on FALCON data increasingly outperform baselines as query complexity grows. When small amounts of standard benchmark data are mixed into training, models regain strong performance on simpler queries without losing their advantage on complex ones.

Conclusion: FALCON successfully produces realistic, ambiguity-aware, and structurally complex NL-to-SQL training data using compact open models, improving model performance on complex queries and maintaining competitiveness on simple ones when combined with benchmark data. Its model- and database-agnostic, locally deployable design makes it a practical solution for organizations needing high-complexity NL-to-SQL training data without reliance on external APIs.

Abstract: Relational databases are among the most widely deployed forms of structured knowledge, and natural language access to them requires grounding language onto schema entities and relations while handling the ambiguity inherent in how people phrase requests. Existing synthetic NL-to-SQL data generation methods largely ignore this ambiguity and produce oversimplified queries that fail to prepare models for the complexity of real-world structured knowledge access. We present FALCON, a framework that generates realistic, ambiguity-aware NL-to-SQL data matching the complexity of challenging real-world benchmarks, at low cost using compact open models. Our approach combines reserved-word SQL seeding and persona-based prompting to generate structurally complex queries, while alignment-based filtering preserves difficulty by distinguishing genuinely incorrect examples from complex but valid queries. Human evaluation confirms consistent high quality across model sizes, and our generated data exceeds existing benchmarks in both SQL complexity and natural language richness. Difficulty-stratified analysis shows models trained on FALCON data increasingly outperform baseline-trained models as query complexity increases, validating our pipeline's success in generating challenging training data. When combined with a small proportion of existing benchmark data, mixed training recovers performance on simpler queries while preserving these advantages on complex ones. The model- and database-agnostic design enables organizations to generate high-complexity NL-to-SQL training data locally without external APIs.

</details>


### [63] [Language Models that Play Chess and Explain Their Moves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03695)
*Adithya Bhaskar,Jeffrey Cheng,Danqi Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Introduces Queen, a 4B-parameter chess-language model that reaches strong Grandmaster strength while producing fluent, coherent natural-language explanations of its moves, by combining a silent chess engine encoder with an instruction-tuned language model and an iterative explanation-distillation procedure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chess engines are superhuman at playing but provide no human-understandable explanations, while language models can explain but are weak at actual chess. There is a need for a system that can both play strongly and provide trustworthy, high-quality natural-language reasoning, and for a general recipe to leverage powerful but silent domain experts (e.g., engines, robots) through language.

Method: They build an encoder–decoder model where a strong but non-explanatory chess engine serves as a silent expert encoder and an instruction-tuned language model acts as the decoder. Cross-attention lets the LM read the engine’s internal representations. They train this via a QA-style curriculum so the LM learns chess concepts from the encoder. On top of this, they apply an iterative distillation process inspired by the Bellman update: for each position, the model analyzes positions following its top candidate moves, generates detailed explanations for those successors, and then aggregates them into an improved explanation of the current position. This improved explanation is distilled back into the model, and the procedure is repeated for multiple iterations to jointly improve playing strength and explanation quality.

Result: After seven iterations of this training and distillation loop, the Queen model gains over 900 Elo rating points, from 1782 to 2697, reaching solid Grandmaster-level strength. It also beats contemporary frontier models on both gameplay strength and puzzle-solving accuracy, despite having roughly three orders of magnitude fewer parameters than those large models. Independent LM-based evaluation indicates that its natural-language explanations are fluent and nearly as coherent as those produced by a strong proprietary model (GPT-5.6-Sol (high)).

Conclusion: Combining a silent expert encoder with an instruction-tuned language model via cross-attention, and refining explanations through an iterative, Bellman-like distillation process, can yield compact models that are simultaneously strong reasoners in a domain and capable natural-language explainers. The success of Queen in chess suggests this framework is broadly applicable to other domains where powerful non-linguistic experts exist, such as games, robotics, and computer interfaces, offering a general recipe for building explainable, domain-specialized language agents.

Abstract: Modern chess engines are silent experts: they play at a superhuman level, but do not offer explanations for their play. On the other hand, language models (LMs) can generate plausible-sounding explanations, but their weak playing strength limits the utility of their explanations. We introduce Queen, a 4B-parameter chess-language model that can explain its moves and plans while playing at the level of a typical Grandmaster. Our novel framework enables domain-specific reasoning through complementary components: an encoder-decoder architecture and an iterative distillation algorithm. This architecture integrates a silent expert chess encoder with an instruction-tuned LM through cross-attention, which we train via a question-answering curriculum to extract chess concepts from the encoder's representations. Building on this domain-adapted model, we iteratively improve its explanations with a natural-language analog of the Bellman update: the model analyzes the positions after its top candidate moves and consolidates them into an explanation of the current position, which is then distilled back into the model. Over seven iterations, our model gains over 900 Elo points (1782 to 2697), substantially surpassing all frontier models on both playing strength and puzzle accuracy, despite containing three orders of magnitude fewer parameters. Furthermore, LM-based evaluations show that our explanations are fluent and approach GPT-5.6-Sol (high) in coherence. The generality of our architecture and training procedure suggests a recipe for applying language models to domains where silent expert encoders are available, like games, robotics, and computer use.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [64] [MintFlow: Minimal Trajectory Intervention for Constrained Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02260)
*Yesom Park,Kelvin Kan,Qifan Chen,Thomas Flynn,Hayden Schaeffer. Xihaier Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MintFlow is a training-free method to enforce constraints on flow matching generative models via minimal interventions on their trajectories, preserving the original data distribution while satisfying constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many downstream applications of flow matching generative models require outputs that satisfy constraints like measurements or physical laws. Existing constrained sampling methods often enforce constraints at the cost of significantly shifting samples away from the pretrained data distribution, degrading generative quality. There is a need for a principled way to impose constraints that minimally disturbs the original model behavior.

Method: MintFlow treats constraint enforcement as a minimal perturbation problem on the intermediate state of a pretrained flow trajectory. It searches for the smallest change to a latent state such that evolving this state under the unchanged pretrained flow field yields samples satisfying the desired constraints. Using an adjoint formulation, the authors derive a closed-form expression for the optimal perturbation, avoiding iterative optimization. Additionally, MintFlow adaptively chooses the intervention time along the trajectory to trade off the magnitude of the perturbation against its subsequent amplification by the remaining flow evolution.

Result: Across diverse tasks in generative vision and physical system modeling, MintFlow matches or improves upon state-of-the-art constrained sampling methods in terms of constraint satisfaction, while keeping generated samples closer to the original pretrained generative distribution. This demonstrates improved fidelity to the base model with competitive constraint enforcement.

Conclusion: By framing constraint enforcement as a minimal intervention on flow trajectories and solving it analytically via an adjoint approach, MintFlow provides an efficient, training-free way to sample from flow matching models under constraints. It preserves the pretrained data distribution better than existing methods while still achieving strong constraint satisfaction, making it broadly useful for constrained generative modeling tasks.

Abstract: Flow matching models excel at generative modeling, and many downstream applications require their samples to satisfy prescribed constraints, such as observed measurements and physical laws. However, existing constrained samplers often face a trade-off: \textit{enforcing constraints can substantially displace samples from the pretrained data distribution}. To address this trade-off, we introduce \textbf{MintFlow}, a training-free constrained sampling framework that formulates constraint enforcement as a minimal intervention on the pretrained flow trajectory. MintFlow seeks the minimal perturbation of an intermediate flow state such that its subsequent evolution under the pretrained flow field satisfies the target constraint. By minimally perturbing the flow state while keeping the pretrained flow field unchanged, MintFlow enforces the constraint while minimizing unnecessary deviation from the pretrained distribution. An adjoint formulation yields a closed-form expression for this perturbation, eliminating expensive iterative optimization. Furthermore, MintFlow adaptively selects the intervention time to balance the required perturbation magnitude with its amplification by the remaining flow. Across a range of tasks in generative vision and physical system modeling, MintFlow achieves competitive constraint satisfaction while preserving the pretrained generative distribution substantially better than state-of-the-art constrained methods.

</details>


### [65] [Fast Models, Slow Evidence: A Paired and Self-Audited Evaluation of System-1 Decision Models for LLM Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02267)
*Jiawei Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper benchmarks two System-1 decision models (one open, one hosted) across multiple agent decision tasks, finding the hosted model usually more accurate and exposing several evaluation mistakes that inflated earlier deployment claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents need to make many small, typed decisions (tool choice, model routing, safety checks, relevance judgments). Using full LLM calls for each decision is costly and slow, so specialized lightweight System-1 models are attractive, but their actual reliability and the robustness of their evaluation is unclear. The paper aims to rigorously assess such models on realistic agent decision points and to audit previous evaluation pipelines for errors and confounds.

Method: The authors construct 11 concrete agent decision tasks from 18 public datasets, yielding 7,283 base cases plus 6,640 robustness variants (e.g., option order reversals, many/near-duplicate candidates, channel variants). They then perform a paired evaluation of two System-1 models—Laya (open weights) and Jev (hosted)—ensuring byte-identical inputs, paired tests, and cross-hardware/day reproducibility. They analyze per-task accuracy, robustness to perturbations (like option reordering and number/similarity of candidates), and conduct an audit of their own evaluation pipeline to identify and quantify analysis and design errors that affected original deployment claims.

Result: Jev significantly outperforms Laya on 9 of 11 decision points, with accuracy advantages of roughly 11–46 percentage points. Both models perform no better than chance on zero-shot model routing, and they are comparable on RAG relevance gating. Laya is highly sensitive to option ordering (changing 30% of answers when order is reversed) and fails badly with many or very similar candidates (only 31% accuracy at 50-nearest-neighbour tool selection) compared to Jev’s strong performance (98% when a unique correct tool exists). The pipeline audit reveals multiple issues: a pre-screen cost was omitted, dramatically reducing claimed cost savings (from 23.9% to 4.3%); gate accuracy was misreported as end-to-end quality (58% vs. actual 98%); thresholds were tuned in-sample, leading to higher miss rates on held-out data (up to 17% vs a 5% target); and a supposed “channel effect” on prompt injection false positives disappears when evaluated on channel-native content. Two other hypothesized confounds were investigated and found not to affect the main conclusions.

Conclusion: System-1 decision models can substantially improve cost and latency for agent decisions, but their reliability varies widely, and naïve evaluations can be seriously misleading. The hosted Jev model is generally stronger and more robust than the open Laya, especially in multi-candidate and order-perturbed settings, while both struggle with zero-shot model routing. Careful, reproducible evaluation and thorough auditing of analysis pipelines are crucial: small analysis mistakes and design confounds can meaningfully distort deployment claims and overstate cost savings or quality. The authors release all cases, outputs, and code to support transparent benchmarking and further work on robust System-1 decision models for agents.

Abstract: Agent harnesses make many small, typed decisions per task: which model to call, which tool to use, whether retrieved text is relevant, whether an input carries an injection. System-1 decision models answer such questions in a single forward pass with class probabilities, promising large cost and latency savings over LLM calls. We present a paired evaluation of an open-weight (Laya) and a hosted (Jev) System-1 model on 11 agent decision points built from 18 public sources: 7,283 base cases plus 6,640 robustness variants, with byte-identical inputs, paired tests, and cross-hardware and cross-day reproducibility checks. Jev is significantly more accurate on 9 of 11 decision points (+10.8 to +46.0 pp). Neither model beats chance on zero-shot model routing, and they tie on RAG relevance gating. Laya changes 30% of its answers when the option order is reversed and degrades sharply with many or similar candidates (31% at 50 nearest-neighbour tools, vs. 98% for Jev on items with a unique correct tool). We also audit our own pipeline. Three analysis errors and one design confound distorted headline deployment claims: an omitted pre-screen cost (reported 23.9% saving, actual 4.3%), gate accuracy reported as end-to-end quality (58% vs. 98%), in-sample thresholds (5% target, up to 17% held-out misses), and a "channel effect" on injection false positives that vanishes with channel-native content. Two other suspected confounds did not change the conclusions. All cases, raw outputs and analysis code are available at https://github.com/David-DL-Space/sys1-eval.

</details>


### [66] [The AI Risk Observatory: What Can We Learn from AI Disclosures in Annual Reports About Societal Resilience?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02281)
*Bart Jaworski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether large language models (LLMs) applied to thousands of UK companies’ annual reports can extract useful signals about how firms disclose AI-related risks, adoption, and harms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Societal resilience to AI disruptions requires systematic, scalable evidence on how organizations perceive and disclose AI risks and adoption. Existing approaches are often small-scale, manual, or non-reproducible. The authors aim to determine if annual reports, processed with LLM-based classifiers, can serve as a large-scale, reliable data source for understanding corporate AI responses, thereby informing regulators, policymakers, and researchers.

Method: The authors assemble a corpus of 9,821 annual reports from 1,362 UK listed companies (2020–2025, partial 2026). They build a reproducible two-stage LLM-based classification pipeline. First, they validate the classifiers against 474 human-annotated passages, evaluating recall and label agreement. Then they apply the pipeline across the full corpus to identify and categorize disclosures about AI risk, AI adoption, vendor mentions, and harms. They also construct a substantiveness classifier to distinguish between superficial and substantive AI risk disclosures and analyze patterns by time, sector (especially Critical National Infrastructure), and market segment (AIM vs Main Market).

Result: (1) AI-related disclosure increases sharply over time: AI risk mentions grow from 2.8% of reports in 2020 to 41.2% in 2025, while AI adoption disclosure rises from 13.8% to 45.2%. Vendor mentions are highly concentrated among a few providers, led by Microsoft. (2) There are large cross-sector and market differences: AIM-listed firms disclose AI risk much less frequently than Main Market firms, and sectors such as Energy and Data Infrastructure lag in AI risk disclosure relative to others. (3) Explicit harm disclosures are extremely rare, appearing in only seven reports across the entire dataset. (4) The substantiveness classifier shows that most AI risk disclosures are superficial; although 41.2% of 2025 reports mention AI risk, only 4.3% contain content classified as substantively discussing AI risk.

Conclusion: LLM-based analysis of annual reports can yield scalable, informative signals about how companies discuss AI, revealing rapid growth in AI risk and adoption disclosure, stark differences across sectors and markets, and a near-absence of reported harms. However, the quality of AI risk disclosure is generally low, with only a small fraction of reports providing substantive discussion. These findings suggest that while corporate awareness and reporting of AI are increasing, current disclosure practices fall short of providing detailed, actionable information necessary for societal resilience, and that regulators and stakeholders may need to encourage or mandate more substantive AI risk reporting.

Abstract: Societal resilience research relies on access to useful and actionable data, which motivates our main research question: Can annual reports, processed at scale with LLMs, provide a useful signal about how companies disclose their response to AI? We test this by applying a reproducible two-stage classification pipeline to 9,821 annual reports from 1,362 UK listed companies (2020-2025, with partial 2026 data). We first validate the method against 474 human-annotated passages, finding high recall and moderate label-level agreement. We then report three empirical patterns: (i) between 2020 and 2025, the share of reports mentioning AI risk rose from 2.8% to 41.2%, while AI adoption disclosure also rose, from 13.8% to 45.2%, and named vendor mentions cluster around a small set of major providers led by Microsoft; (ii) disclosure varies substantially by Critical National Infrastructure sector and market segment: AIM reports disclose AI risk at far lower rates than Main Market reports, and sectors such as Energy and Data Infrastructure lag behind the rest in AI risk disclosure; and (iii) harm disclosures are near-absent (seven reports across the entire corpus). We develop a substantiveness classification to assess the quality of the disclosure and find that most AI risk disclosure is not substantive: in 2025, 41.2% of all reports mention AI as a risk, but only 4.3% contain AI risk disclosure we classify as substantive.

</details>


### [67] [Keep It CALM: Analyzing the Limits of Global Unsafety in Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02300)
*NaHyeon Park,Minhyun Lee,Hyunjung Shim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose CALM, a training-free, prompt-local safeguard for text-to-image models that outperforms global unsafe-subspace removal by being more selective and preserving benign content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing training-free safety methods rely on a single global “unsafe direction” or shared toxic subspace in the model’s representation space. This assumes unsafe content is geometrically coherent across diverse prompts. The authors suspect this is unrealistic: a compact unsafe subspace may miss many heterogeneous unsafe cases, whereas enlarging it harms benign, safety-adjacent prompts. They want a more precise safeguard that handles varied unsafe semantics without degrading normal generations.

Method: They conduct a controlled geometric analysis of representation spaces used by text-to-image models to test the assumption that a single global unsafe subspace can capture diverse unsafe semantics. Observing a coverage–selectivity trade-off, they design CALM (Counterfactual Adaptive Local Modulation), a training-free mechanism that: (1) uses matched unsafe–benign anchor pairs to define counterfactual directions per unsafe category, (2) dynamically routes each input prompt to the relevant unsafe categories, (3) minimally edits only the token representations that violate safety constraints, nudging them toward safe counterparts, and (4) suppresses positive unsafe residual components in the representation. All edits are local, prompt- and token-specific rather than global.

Result: Their experiments across broad benchmarks show that CALM improves suppression of unsafe content compared to global unsafe-subspace removal methods, while better preserving the quality and semantics of benign generations. Quantitatively, CALM yields higher safety scores and smaller degradation on benign prompts, demonstrating a more favorable safety–utility trade-off.

Conclusion: The global-unsafety assumption—that a single reusable unsafe direction or global toxic subspace can safely filter all prompts—is flawed because it induces a fundamental trade-off: tight subspaces lack coverage, and broad ones harm benign prompts. Local, counterfactual, prompt-specific modulation as in CALM offers a more selective and effective alternative, achieving stronger safety with less impact on benign content, all without retraining the underlying text-to-image model.

Abstract: Training-free safeguards for text-to-image generation often rely on a reusable safety signal, such as an unsafe direction or global toxic subspace, applied broadly across prompts. We provide a controlled geometric analysis of this global-unsafety assumption and reveal a consistent coverage-selectivity trade-off: compact unsafe subspaces fail to cover heterogeneous unsafe semantics, whereas broader aggregation increasingly distorts safety-adjacent benign prompts. Motivated by this finding, we propose CALM (Counterfactual Adaptive Local Modulation), a training-free safeguard that replaces uniform global removal with prompt-local counterfactual correction. Using matched unsafe-benign anchors, CALM routes each prompt to active unsafe categories, minimally edits only violating token representations toward the safe side, and suppresses positively aligned unsafe residual components. Across broad evaluation, CALM significantly improves unsafe content suppression while preserving benign utility, demonstrating that local counterfactual correction provides a more selective alternative to global unsafe signal removal.

</details>


### [68] [Choosing Before Acting: Comparative Value Estimation for Long-Horizon Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02330)
*Yu Li,Zheng Zhang,Xin Liu,Shengtian Yang,Guangfeng Cai,Lei Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose CITA, a method that learns to estimate the long-term value of candidate next tool calls in LLM agents using comparative supervision, improving tool-use performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM tool-use agents for complex, long-horizon tasks struggle with credit assignment: final outcome rewards are too delayed and sparse, while step-level rewards require expensive human/LLM judgment or extra rollouts. Moreover, agents should evaluate the long-horizon impact of a next tool invocation, but logs only show what was actually executed, not alternatives. This creates a gap in training agents to choose the best next tool under a given context.

Method: They introduce Comparative Inference for Tool-use Agents (CITA). CITA trains a Comparative Inference Model (CIM) that takes a context and alternative potential tool invocations and predicts which is better for ultimate task success. Training uses paired comparative signals derived from: (1) observed behavior from logged tool trajectories, (2) scalable supervision from a Bayesian tool-graph simulator that can infer likely downstream consequences, and (3) semantic comparisons produced by an LLM that judges which tool choice seems better. These sources are combined to create supervision over pairs of candidate invocations so CIM learns relative long-horizon value estimates.

Result: On three tool-use benchmarks and across multiple backbone LLMs, CITA improves both Tool F1 (accuracy/quality of tool invocation sequences) and overall task success compared to baselines. Analysis shows that the learned CIM provides accurate step-level value estimates when comparing candidate tool choices, indicating that it captures meaningful long-horizon evaluations.

Conclusion: Comparative, context-conditioned value estimation over alternative next tool invocations is an effective way to improve long-horizon tool use in LLM agents. By leveraging simulated Bayesian supervision and LLM-based semantic comparisons, CITA can train a CIM that guides better tool selection at each step, leading to higher task success and more accurate tool use without requiring extensive human step-level annotation or costly rollouts.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) rely on long-horizon tool invocation sequences for complex tasks, where each invocation can alter the task state and condition subsequent decisions. In long-horizon tool use, final-outcome rewards provide weak credit assignment over long interaction traces. Step-level rewards can offer more targeted feedback, but obtaining reliable step supervision often requires human or LLM judgment, or additional rollouts to estimate the downstream effect of an intermediate decision. In this paper, we argue that effective tool-use agents should estimate the long-horizon value of a possible next tool invocation before executing it. This objective requires comparative supervision over alternative invocations under the same context, while logged trajectories only contain the invocation that was actually taken. Therefore, we propose Comparative Inference for Tool-use Agents (CITA). CITA trains a Comparative Inference Model (CIM) from paired signals that combine observed tool behavior, scalable supervision from a Bayesian tool-graph simulator, and semantic judgments from LLM-based comparison. The resulting CIM learns to estimate how likely a possible next tool invocation is to support final task success under the current context. Across three tool-use benchmarks and multiple backbone LLMs, CITA consistently improves Tool F1 and task success. Additional analysis shows that CIM learns accurate step-level value estimates for comparative tool choices.

</details>


### [69] [World Editing: Intervening on Executable Worlds at Increasing Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02331)
*Max Ku,Nok-Kan Law,Yu-Chien Tang,Shih-Ying Yeh,Ping Nie,Andy Zheng,Tat Hei Lai,Fei-Yueh Chen,Nikko Yu,Wei-Chieh Sun,Suzy Huang,Chiao-Wei Hsu,Chih-Chuan Huang,Chak-Wing Mak,Ho Yin Sam Ng,Edisy Kin Wai Chan,Min-Hung Chen,Ho Kei Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces and benchmarks the new task of editing existing executable game worlds, showing that strong coding agents can often make working edits but struggle with deeper behavioral and visual consistency requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work focuses on generating or interacting with simulated worlds, but not on precisely editing already-built, executable environments while preserving what should remain unchanged. As interactive world models become more capable, there is a need to systematically define and evaluate this ‘world editing’ ability, especially in realistic, industry-grade game settings.

Method: The authors formalize world editing as making interventions on an existing world under preservation constraints, and define intervention depth to characterize how entangled an edit is with world entities, dynamics, and systems. They instantiate this in the context of professional game modding for Minecraft and Terraria by building IGMWorld and IGMBench: a benchmark of 110 editing tasks with over 1.1K executable criteria. Tasks are categorized by intervention type (property, entity, dynamics, system) and are automatically evaluated via deterministic executability checks, behavioral tests, preservation checks, and visual inspections. Frontier coding agents are then used to attempt these tasks, and performance is measured both at task level and per-criterion level, including visual pass rates.

Result: On IGMBench, the best-performing coding-agent setup successfully completes 78.2% of tasks under a strict task-level metric, and 94.8% of individual criteria. Performance degrades as intervention depth increases, even when controlling for the number of criteria. Most unsuccessful edits still compile and load, indicating that failures are primarily in satisfying the intended behavioral changes, not in basic executability. All evaluated agents perform poorly on visual consistency, achieving under 50% joint visual pass rate.

Conclusion: World editing is empirically distinct from world generation and interaction, demanding precise, coupled changes to entities, dynamics, and systems while preserving other properties. Executable games like Minecraft and Terraria provide a viable, rigorous testbed for studying this capability. Current frontier coding agents show strong but imperfect world-editing skills, with significant weaknesses at higher intervention depths and in visual consistency, highlighting room for targeted research on controllable, reliable world modification.

Abstract: Interactive world models are increasingly capable of generating environments and acting within them, yet deliberately editing an existing executable world remains underexplored. We formulate world editing as intervening on an existing world while preserving properties that should remain unchanged, and introduce intervention depth as an axis describing how strongly an edit couples world entities, dynamics, and systems. We instantiate this capability through industry-grade game modding and introduce IGMWorld, together with IGMBench, a benchmark of 110 tasks and over 1.1K executable state and behavioral criteria across Minecraft and Terraria. The tasks span property, entity, dynamics, and system interventions and are evaluated through deterministic executability, behavioral, preservation, and visual checks. Frontier coding agents already exhibit substantial world-editing capability: the strongest configuration solves 78.2% of tasks under a strict task-level criterion, while criterion-level performance reaches 94.8%. Reliability generally decreases with intervention depth, and this pattern persists even among tasks with similar numbers of evaluation criteria. Most failed edits still build and load successfully, suggesting that the main difficulty is making the edited world behave as requested. Visual consistency remains a separate weakness, with all evaluated configurations below 50% joint visual pass rate. These results show that world editing is a distinct capability from world generation and interaction, and that executable games provide a practical testbed for studying it.

</details>


### [70] [A Multi Method Importance and Performance Efficiency Analysis of Topological Metrics for Natural Visibility Graph Based Cyber Attack Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02342)
*Ali Melih Kanca,Ilker Turker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that using only the three most important Natural Visibility Graph (NVG) metrics yields better cyber-attack classification performance and 96% lower runtime than using all 21 metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NVG-based analysis of network traffic uses many topological metrics, but not all of them are equally useful for detecting cyber-attacks. Calculating a large set of metrics increases computational cost, which is problematic for efficient or real-time intrusion detection. The authors want to know whether they can reduce the number of metrics while preserving or improving detection performance.

Method: The authors compute 21 NVG-derived topological metrics from network traffic and assess their importance for cyber-attack classification using four methods: SHAP, grouped Permutation Importance, Boruta, and Recursive Feature Elimination (RFE). They combine the four importance rankings into a Consensus Ranking. Based on this ranking, they define feature subsets (Full21, Top15, Top10, Top7, Top5, Top3) and evaluate each with a CNN classifier on the CICIDS2018 dataset using stratified 5-fold cross-validation, comparing classification metrics and runtime.

Result: The three most important metrics, according to the consensus ranking, are avg_clustering_coeff_median, avg_clustering_coeff_std, and avg_clustering_coeff_mean. The subset with only these three metrics (Top3) achieves the best observed mean performance: 97.148% accuracy, 97.055% weighted F1 score, and MCC of 0.9675, outperforming the full 21-metric set, which attains 95.999% accuracy, 95.521% weighted F1, and MCC of 0.9549. Additionally, Top3 reduces total runtime from 14,961.39 s to 589.22 s, a 96.06% reduction.

Conclusion: A small, carefully selected subset of NVG metrics, guided by a consensus feature-importance analysis, can yield equal or better cyber-attack classification performance than a larger full metric set, while drastically reducing computational cost. The study suggests that NVG-based intrusion detection systems can be made both more accurate and more efficient by focusing on a compact metric representation dominated by clustering coefficient statistics.

Abstract: Natural Visibility Graph (NVG) based analysis characterizes network traffic through topological descriptors reflecting different structural properties. However, not all descriptors contribute equally to cyber-attack classification, and extracting a large metric set can increase computational cost. This study evaluates 21 NVG derived topological metrics and investigates whether a compact subset can preserve classification capability while improving computational efficiency. Four importance analysis methods SHAP, grouped Permutation Importance, Boruta, and Recursive Feature Elimination (RFE) are integrated through a Consensus Ranking strategy. Based on this ranking, Full21, Top15, Top10, Top7, Top5, and Top3 configurations are evaluated using the CICIDS2018 dataset, a CNN classifier, and stratified 5 fold cross validation. The three highest ranked metrics are avg_clustering_coeff_median, avg_clustering_coeff_std, and avg_clustering_coeff_mean. Top3 achieved the highest observed mean performance, with 97.148% accuracy, 97.055% weighted F1 score, and an MCC of 0.9675, compared with 95.999%, 95.521%, and 0.9549 for Full21, respectively. It also reduced total runtime from 14,961.39 s to 589.22 s (96.06%). These results indicate that importance guided metric reduction can provide a compact NVG representation with higher observed mean predictive performance and substantially lower computational cost under the evaluated setting.

</details>


### [71] [DeReAct: Decomposed Reasoning and Acting for Reliable AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02351)
*Ajay Vohra,Tao Chen,Neeti Narayan,Caron Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DeReAct is a modular ReAct-style agent architecture that adds external gating components to validate actions and certify task completion, improving performance especially for weaker base models while yielding better-grounded trajectories for stronger models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ReAct agents use a single LLM to both propose actions and decide when to stop, tightly coupling execution and completion decisions. This makes it hard to separately control which actions are allowed and when a task is truly solved, leading to error propagation, premature or unsupported completions, and weaker guarantees about grounding and constraint satisfaction.

Method: The authors design DeReAct, which decouples the monolithic ReAct policy into: (1) a Brain that proposes actions; (2) a Critic that reviews and authorizes or rejects proposed actions before they are executed; and (3) a Context Manager that reconstructs an environment-supported state representation and decides whether the task is complete based on evidence. They evaluate this modular architecture on GAIA and SWE-bench Verified with several LLMs of varying strength, and perform trajectory-level and ablation analyses to understand when external gating helps.

Result: On GAIA and SWE-bench Verified, DeReAct yields substantial Pass@1 improvements for weaker Brain models, with 6.5–7.0 point gains for Qwen3-Coder-480B and 4.2–5.2 point gains for Claude Sonnet 4.5. As the Brain model becomes stronger (e.g., Claude Opus 4.5), raw Pass@1 gains diminish and become comparable to the ReAct baseline, but DeReAct’s trajectories exhibit better evidence completeness and adherence to constraints. Ablations show that the benefits of external gating depend on both the prevalence of the addressed failure modes and the capability of the gating policies themselves.

Conclusion: Modularizing ReAct agents via external gating components (Critic and Context Manager) improves reliability and performance for weaker LLM agents and maintains or improves grounding and constraint satisfaction for stronger ones. This suggests that separating action authorization and completion control from the main policy can mitigate common failure modes like premature termination and unsupported completions, and that such modular architectures scale gracefully as underlying model capability increases.

Abstract: ReAct-based agents typically rely on a single LLM policy to propose actions, interact with the environment, and decide when a task is complete. This coupling makes action authorization and completion control difficult to enforce independently, allowing errors to propagate and unsupported completion claims to terminate execution. We introduce DeReAct, a modular agent architecture that externalizes two gating policies: a Critic that validates proposed actions before execution, and a Context Manager that reconstructs an environment-supported \textsc{State} and certifies task completion.
  Across GAIA and SWE-bench Verified, DeReAct improves Pass@1 most for weaker Brain models, with gains of 6.5--7.0 points for Qwen3-Coder-480B and 4.2--5.2 points for Claude Sonnet~4.5; gains diminish as Brain capability increases. Trajectory and ablation analyses show that external gating is effective when targeted failures are sufficiently prevalent and the gating policy is itself sufficient. With Claude Opus~4.5, Pass@1 remains comparable to ReAct, while DeReAct produces more evidence-complete and constraint-satisfying trajectories, indicating that completion control can trade earlier termination for stronger grounding. Overall, DeReAct improves weaker agents while retaining grounding benefits as models strengthen.

</details>


### [72] [Traversing the Satisfaction-Diversity Frontier in Text-to-Image Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02372)
*Kevin Zhai,Siva Rajesh Kasa,Soumya Roy,Sumit Negi,Mubarak Shah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes SatisDive, a training-free, inference-time method for text-to-image generation that guarantees both a minimum reward (preference alignment) per image and a minimum diversity for the batch, achieving a better balance between satisfaction and diversity than prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-to-image methods struggle to jointly ensure that each generated image is well aligned with user preferences (high reward) and that the set of images is sufficiently diverse. Current approaches either optimize reward and diversity separately or merge them into a single scalar objective, allowing high diversity to mask low rewards for some images. This leads to batches where some images are unsatisfactory even if the overall score is high. The paper aims to provide a principled way to guarantee per-image satisfaction while preserving batch diversity.

Method: They reframe generation as a satisficing problem with two constraints: (1) a reward floor that every candidate image must satisfy and (2) a diversity cutoff that the batch must satisfy. They show that varying the reward floor trades off worst-case reward against diversity, tracing out a Pareto frontier. They then introduce SatisDive, a training-free inference-time algorithm that uses a batch-relative reward cutoff. During generation, candidates below the cutoff are pushed to improve their reward (preference alignment), while those above the cutoff are encouraged to diversify relative to each other. This procedure navigates the satisfaction–diversity frontier without retraining the base model or reward model.

Result: On the Pick-a-Pic benchmark, using DreamSim as a diversity/quality measure, SatisDive outperforms FK steering in terms of worst-candidate reward at matched DreamSim levels. With FLUX.1-dev as the base model and HPSv3 as the reward model, SatisDive improves worst-candidate reward by up to 0.43 over FK steering. With SANA-1.6B as the base model and ImageReward as the reward, the improvement reaches up to 0.70. Across overlapping ranges of DreamSim scores, the satisfaction–diversity curves obtained by SatisDive Pareto-dominate those of FK steering, indicating a strictly better trade-off between per-image reward and batch diversity.

Conclusion: Formulating text-to-image generation as a satisficing problem with explicit reward and diversity thresholds yields a more principled and effective way to produce image batches that are both individually satisfactory and collectively diverse. The proposed SatisDive method, which operates at inference time without additional training, consistently improves the worst-candidate reward for a given diversity level and Pareto-dominates a strong baseline (FK steering). This demonstrates that carefully balancing per-image reward optimization with diversity promotion can significantly enhance the usefulness of generated image sets.

Abstract: Text-to-image generation enables users to explore several images generated from the same prompt. For these generated images to be useful, each one must reflect the user's preferences, measured by a learned reward, and differ visually from the others to maintain diversity. Existing methods are limited: they either address reward and diversity separately or combine them in one aggregate score, enabling high diversity to offset low rewards. In this paper, we address these limitations by formulating generation as satisficing: every image (candidate) must satisfy a reward floor and the batch of images must satisfy a diversity cutoff. The reward floor controls the balance between worst-candidate reward and batch diversity; we show that varying this floor defines a Pareto frontier. To traverse this frontier, we introduce SatisDive, a training-free inference-time method. SatisDive uses a batch-relative reward cutoff to distinguish lower- from higher-reward candidates, emphasizing reward improvement for candidates below the cutoff and diversity among candidates above it. On Pick-a-Pic, at matched DreamSim, SatisDive improves worst-candidate reward over FK steering by up to 0.43 with FLUX.1-dev as the base model and HPSv3 as the reward, and by up to 0.70 with SANA-1.6B as the base model and ImageReward as the reward. More broadly, across their overlapping DreamSim ranges, SatisDive's satisfaction-diversity curve Pareto-dominates FK steering's curve in each setting.

</details>


### [73] [THPL: A Vision-to-Language Decision Support Framework for Rainbow Trout Feeding Management in RAS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02378)
*Meng Liang,Guanbo Feng,Haozhuang Chi,Shilong Zhao,Zhixin Xiong,Yuhang He,Wenfeng Han,Tianhao Zhao,Zhihong Ma,Ying Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework (THPL) for automated, interpretable feeding decisions in recirculating aquaculture systems that grounds an LLM in fish movement trajectories and expert rules to optimize precision feeding for rainbow trout.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Precision feeding in RAS is essential to reduce costs and improve fish welfare, but current systems cannot reliably translate raw behavioral signals of fish into cognitively aligned, interpretable feeding decisions understood by managers. There is a gap between low-level movement data and high-level expert reasoning, resulting in suboptimal automation and limited trust in AI recommendations.

Method: The authors design THPL, a generative feeding decision pipeline. (1) They use Fishsort to extract fish trajectories and define an Activity Coefficient (AC) to quantify feeding intensity. (2) They introduce a Hierarchical Behavior Encoder (HBE) that uses Temporal Transformers for individual temporal patterns and Set Transformers for group dynamics, producing two types of tokens: explicit physical tokens (continuous spatiotemporal features) and implicit soft tokens (higher-level behavior abstractions). (3) These tokens are combined with environmental data, metadata, and expert rules to fine-tune a large language model using LoRA. (4) They further train the model with counterfactual multimodal Direct Preference Optimization (mDPO), using preference pairs and counterfactual scenarios to enhance causal reasoning and reduce biases in generated feeding decisions.
