Result: Empirically, the Activity Coefficient strongly correlates with expert-labeled feeding intensity (Spearman ρ = 0.925, p < 0.001), validating it as a behavioral proxy. Ablation studies show decision accuracy jumps from 33.33% with a text-only LLM to 93.33% when dual-evidence spatiotemporal tokens are included, demonstrating the importance of physical grounding. Applying counterfactual mDPO on top of LoRA fine-tuning further lifts decision accuracy from 93.33% to 96.67%, substantially improves language quality metrics (METEOR from 58.10% to 85.30%), slightly lowers Self-BLEU-2 (58.79% to 52.88%, indicating less templated outputs), and increases Distinct-3 (6.68% to 7.81%, indicating more diverse responses).

Conclusion: Integrating continuous kinematic data with LLM-based reasoning yields a more accurate, interpretable, and causally grounded decision support tool for precision aquaculture feeding. The THPL framework shows that dual-evidence trajectory tokens can bridge the gap between fish behavior and expert management logic, and that counterfactual mDPO can reduce templating and actuation biases while enhancing safety. This paradigm may generalize to other cyber-physical and animal management systems where grounding LLMs in real-world dynamics is crucial.

Abstract: In Recirculating Aquaculture Systems (RAS), precision feeding is critical for minimizing costs and improving fish welfare. However, existing methods lack cognitive alignment between fish behaviors and management knowledge, impeding translation into executable, interpretable feeding decisions. To address this, we propose THPL, a generative feeding decision framework tailored for rainbow trout (Oncorhynchus mykiss) in RAS. First, Fishsort extracts trajectories to establish an Activity Coefficient (AC) quantifying feeding intensity. Second, a Hierarchical Behavior Encoder (HBE) models individual temporal progression and collective dynamics using Temporal and Set Transformers, transforming trajectory tensors into dual-evidence representations of explicit physical and implicit soft tokens. Finally, these tokens are integrated with environmental parameters, metadata, and expert rules to fine-tune an LLM via LoRA, followed by counterfactual multimodal Direct Preference Optimization (mDPO) to reinforce causal reasoning. Results show that AC exhibits a statistically significant monotonic positive correlation with expert-annotated feeding intensity (Spearman $ρ= 0.925$, $p < 0.001$). Ablations indicate that decision accuracy improves from 33.33% (text-only baseline) to 93.33% with dual-evidence tokens, confirming that continuous spatiotemporal tokens provide necessary physical grounding for LLMs. Compared with standard LoRA, counterfactual mDPO elevates decision accuracy from 93.33% to 96.67%, advances METEOR from 58.10% to 85.30%, reduces Self-BLEU-2 from 58.79% to 52.88%, and increases Distinct-3 from 6.68% to 7.81%, suppressing templating and actuation biases while reinforcing causal consistency and operational safety. Overall, by integrating continuous kinematics with LLM reasoning, this study provides a novel decision support paradigm for precision aquaculture.

</details>


### [74] [FlashSinkhorn 2: Block-Sparse Entropic Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02395)
*Felix X. -F. Ye,Yu Chin Fabian Lim,Naigang Wang,Davis Wertheimer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces FlashSinkhorn 2 (FS2), a GPU-based, two-stage streaming solver that efficiently handles extremely large entropic optimal transport problems without storing the full cost kernel, achieving state-of-the-art scale and accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing streaming GPU solvers for entropic optimal transport, like FlashSinkhorn, avoid storing the dense kernel but still have to evaluate all n×m pairwise interactions each Sinkhorn iteration. This becomes computationally prohibitive for very large discrete problems, especially when aiming for strict accuracy on modern GPUs. The authors seek a method that scales to hundreds of millions of points while meeting prescribed marginal residual tolerances, all on a single GPU and within practical runtimes.

Method: They propose FlashSinkhorn 2 (FS2), tailored to squared-Euclidean costs on low-dimensional point clouds, using a two-stage approach. (1) A coarse stage clusters points into cells, solves EOT on cell centroids, and then lifts the resulting dual potentials back to individual points. A sampled marginal consistency check determines whether the lifted solution is adequate; if not, the solver continues iterating on centroids, replacing most per-point updates. (2) A block-sparse fine stage corrects residual errors that centroid-level updates cannot fix. Points are organized into Morton-ordered spatial blocks, enabling screening of negligible interactions and efficient fused tensor-core kernels. A mass-based threshold guarantees that omitted tiles have a bounded impact on every row and column of the transport plan.

Result: On synthetic benchmarks, FS2 meets the target marginal residual on all 32 tested problems, outperforming GeomLoss multiscale which reaches the target on only 10 of them. On a single NVIDIA A100 GPU, FS2 successfully solves an EOT problem between two measures of size 1.34×10^8 particles each, from a cosmological N-body simulation, with entropic regularization on the order of the mean interparticle distance. It achieves an all-particle marginal residual below 0.01 in under 2.5 hours, demonstrating unprecedented scale and speed for this accuracy level.

Conclusion: FS2 demonstrates that very large-scale entropic optimal transport problems, with hundreds of millions of points, can be solved accurately within hours on a single modern GPU by combining a centroid-based coarse stage with a structured block-sparse fine stage. The screening and tensor-core–friendly Morton ordering make the method both memory- and compute-efficient. The authors claim state-of-the-art problem size and accuracy for discrete EOT in this time budget and provide an open-source implementation to facilitate reproducibility and adoption.

Abstract: Streaming GPU solvers for entropic optimal transport (EOT), such as FlashSinkhorn, avoid storing the dense kernel but still evaluate all $n\times m$ point pairs in every Sinkhorn iteration. We present \textbf{FlashSinkhorn~2} (FS2), a solver for squared-Euclidean cost on low-dimensional point clouds that solves large discrete EOT problems to a prescribed marginal residual on a single GPU by coupling two stages. A coarse stage solves on cell centroids, lifts the potentials to every point and, when a sampled marginal check rejects the lift, continues on the centroids, replacing most point-level updates. A block-sparse fine stage then removes the centroid error that coarse updates cannot. Its Morton-ordered blocks support screening and fused tensor-core execution, and a threshold set by the block masses bounds each omitted tile's contribution to every row and column. On synthetic benchmarks, FS2 reaches the target residual on all 32 problems and GeomLoss multiscale on 10. On one A100, FS2 solves discrete EOT between two $1.34\times10^8$-particle measures from a cosmological $N$-body simulation, at an entropic blur equal to the mean interparticle distance, to an all-particle marginal residual below 0.01 in under 2.5 hours. To our knowledge, it is the largest discrete EOT problem solved to this accuracy within hours. For reproducibility, we release an open-source implementation at https://github.com/ot-triton-lab/flash-sinkhorn

</details>


### [75] [When Terminal-Agent Training Stalls: Demystifying Data Generation and Verification Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02405)
*Xi Qin,Isabel Kurth,Xin Cui,Elin Park,Alexander Schaefer,Yaad Oren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes reliability issues when using powerful language models as meta-agents to generate RL tasks and verifiers, showing that runnable benchmarks can still be unfaithful and that task solvability is highly model-specific.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As researchers increasingly use frontier LLMs to auto-generate RL environments, tasks, and test suites, they often assume that a working Docker image and tests imply a valid, faithful benchmark. However, hidden issues like invalid tasks, brittle evaluation harnesses, and misaligned reward signals can lead to misleading performance measurements and poor downstream agent behavior. The paper is motivated by the need to systematically identify and correct these reliability gaps in meta-agent-generated benchmarks.

Method: The authors build a meta-agent pipeline in which a strong LLM (Claude Opus) generates terminal tasks and corresponding verifiers for RL training. They then systematically probe the pipeline for three failure types: benchmark invalidity, harness brittleness, and reward misalignment. They experiment with prompt redesign and extended context to improve baseline task solvability and then stress-test the system by adding harder tasks without changing the training configuration, measuring performance of a 9B RL-trained model using pass@2 within a 20-step interaction budget.

Result: Redesigning prompts and extending context increased baseline task solvability by 5.6x, and a 9B model achieved up to 81.3% mean pass@2 within 20 steps on tasks generated by Claude Opus. However, when harder tasks were introduced while keeping the training setup fixed, the same model’s mean pass@2 dropped sharply to 20.6%. This large performance decrease, despite identical training configuration, indicates that the range of tasks a model can reliably solve (its 'solvability band') is tightly coupled to the model’s capabilities and to properties of the meta-generated benchmark.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that simply having runnable Docker images and passing test suites is insufficient to guarantee a faithful, reliable benchmark for training terminal agents with meta-generated tasks. Robust meta-agent pipelines must explicitly calibrate the solvability band to the target model, conduct thorough verifier and harness audits, and systematically account for infrastructure-related errors. These considerations should be treated as primary evaluation criteria rather than as after-the-fact diagnostics when interpreting RL performance on LLM-generated tasks.

Abstract: Using a frontier model like Claude Opus as a meta-agent to generate terminal tasks and verifiers for RL training is increasingly common. Yet a runnable Docker image and executable test suite do not guarantee a faithful end-to-end pipeline for terminal agent training. We present a meta-agent pipeline motivated by this gap, diagnosing three classes of failure: benchmark invalidity, harness brittleness, and reward misalignment. Prompt redesign and context extension raise baseline solvability 5.6 times, but a 9B model saturates at 81.3% mean pass@2 within 20 steps on Claude Opus-generated tasks. Adding hard tasks reduces mean pass@2 to 20.6% without changing the training configuration, a strong evidence that the solvability band is model-specific. These findings demonstrate that meta-agent reliability requires solvability-band calibration, verifier audits, and infrastructure error accounting as first-class evaluation criteria, not post-hoc diagnost.

</details>


### [76] [Reinforcement Learning Techniques for the Optimization of Target Polarization in Nuclear Physics Scattering Experiments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02452)
*Armen Kasparian,Torri Jeske,Monibor Rahman,Chris Keith,James Maxwell,Thomas Britton,Malachi Schram,David Lawrence*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They use data-driven surrogate models and reinforcement learning to automatically tune microwave frequency in dynamically polarized targets, doubling performance over human operators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Operating dynamically polarized targets in nuclear physics experiments requires continuous, expert-driven manual retuning of microwave frequency due to radiation damage and changing material properties. This process is inefficient, hard to scale, and depends heavily on operator expertise. The authors want an automated, reliable control strategy that can adapt to changing conditions, transfer across multiple target samples, and explicitly handle uncertainty and distributional shift.

Method: They build surrogate models that map microwave frequency, beam current, and accumulated radiation dose to target polarization using operational data from the APOLLO cryogenic target system. They compare multilayer perceptrons (MLPs) and Gaussian process regression (GPR), assessing prediction accuracy, uncertainty calibration, and ability to detect out-of-distribution inputs. To scale across multiple target samples, they develop a Gaussian process approximation and wrap the surrogate into a standardized simulation environment. On top of this simulator, they train a reinforcement learning agent with a lower-confidence-bound-based reward, which explicitly trades off expected performance against model uncertainty, to control microwave frequency automatically.

Result: Gaussian process models yield well-calibrated uncertainty estimates and can reliably flag inputs outside the training distribution, whereas MLPs handle distributional shift poorly and lack meaningful uncertainty estimates. The GP-based surrogate can be approximated to support learning and control over multiple target samples and integrated into a reusable simulation environment. An RL agent trained in this framework learns a control policy that, when evaluated against historical operator actions, nearly doubles the performance (in terms of target polarization) relative to human operators.

Conclusion: Data-driven surrogate modeling combined with uncertainty-aware reinforcement learning can effectively automate the tuning of dynamically polarized targets, reducing reliance on expert manual control. Gaussian process-based surrogates are preferable to MLPs in this setting due to their calibrated uncertainties and robustness to distributional shift. Embedding such surrogates into a standardized environment enables scalable training of RL agents, and the proposed lower-confidence-bound reward yields policies that significantly outperform human operators while accounting for model uncertainty.

Abstract: The operation of dynamically polarized targets in nuclear physics experiments relies on continuous tuning of the microwave frequency to compensate for radiation damage and evolving material properties, a task that is traditionally performed through manual trial-and-error by expert operators. This work presents a data-driven control framework that combines surrogate modeling with reinforcement learning to optimize the target polarization. Using operational data from the APOLLO cryogenic target system, we train and evaluate multilayer perceptron and Gaussian process regression models to predict polarization as a function of microwave frequency, beam current, and accumulated radiation dose. We show that Gaussian process-based models provide calibrated uncertainty estimates and reliably identify regions outside the training distribution, while MLPs exhibit limited sensitivity to distributional shift. To enable learning and control across multiple target samples, we introduce a Gaussian process approximation and embed the surrogate model within a standardized simulation environment. A reinforcement learning agent is trained using a lower-confidence-bound reward formulation that balances performance maximization against uncertainty. We are able to show an almost 2x improvement on the operators actions utilizing our RL agent.

</details>


### [77] [Tropical Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02478)
*Arip Asadulaev,Aladin Djuhera,Karim Salta,Holger Boche,Fakhri Karray,Martin Takac*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Tropical Reinforcement Learning, which replaces probability summation with maximization to better support compositional reasoning in large language models, and demonstrates performance gains on several agentic tasks using the TROPIC algorithm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical reinforcement learning for large language models maximizes expected return by summing probabilities of successful trajectories. This approach only tracks how often the policy succeeds, not which specific solutions work, and the normalization constraint means reinforcing one solution can suppress others that are not known to be wrong. This is especially problematic for compositional reasoning, where correct solutions must be assembled from partial reasoning steps that rarely co-occur in a single rollout. The authors seek an alternative formulation that preserves and recombines useful partial solutions instead of forgetting them.

Method: The authors propose changing the algebra underlying reinforcement learning from standard addition to maximization over alternative solutions, yielding the tropical semiring. In this Tropical Reinforcement Learning framework, the value of a state is defined as the log-probability of its most likely verified solution, along with an explicit trajectory (path) that can be stored and replayed. This permits composition of solutions by joining the best prefixes and suffixes that meet at a shared state, even when they originate from different rollouts. To realize this idea, the authors develop TROPIC, a training algorithm tailored to deterministic, resettable environments with verifiable outcomes, applied to large language model agents.

Result: On four agentic benchmark tasks—Sokoban, Countdown, FrozenLake, and WebShop—the proposed TROPIC algorithm outperforms strong on-policy reinforcement learning baselines by as much as 16 percentage points in task success metrics, showing that the tropical formulation can concretely improve performance.

Conclusion: By replacing the additive probability aggregation in reinforcement learning with a max-based tropical semiring, the proposed Tropical Reinforcement Learning framework enables explicit tracking and recomposition of verified solution paths, which better supports compositional reasoning in language-model agents. The empirical gains of TROPIC across several tasks indicate that revisiting the algebraic foundations of reinforcement learning, rather than only improving estimators, can yield substantial benefits for complex reasoning and planning problems.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for large language models typically maximizes expected return, adding up the probabilities of all successful trajectories. However, the classical sum formulation can only report how often the model policy succeeds, not which solution actually worked, and because probabilities sum to one, reinforcing one solution can make the model forget another that was never shown to be wrong. This makes expected return a poor fit for compositional reasoning, where a solution must be assembled from reasoning steps that the model produces in separate, often failed, attempts but rarely produces together. To address this, we propose Tropical Reinforcement Learning, which rests on a simple change of algebra: instead of adding the probabilities of alternative solutions, we take their maximum, which yields the tropical semiring. The value of a state then becomes the log-probability of its most likely verified solution, together with an explicit path that can be replayed and reused. This enables true composition, since the best prefix and the best suffix meeting at a shared state can be joined even when they come from different rollouts. To put this into practice, we introduce TROPIC, a training algorithm for deterministic, resettable environments with verifiable outcomes. On four agentic tasks (Sokoban, Countdown, FrozenLake, WebShop), TROPIC outperforms the strongest on-policy baselines by up to 16 percentage points. Changing the algebra of reinforcement learning, not just its estimators, can thus substantially improve compositional reasoning in language models

</details>


### [78] [MEA: A Reward-Driven Multi-Agent System for Faithful Model Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02480)
*Yuyang Cheng,Raghav Kaushik Ravi,Srivarshinee Sridhar,Sriparna Saha,Akash Ghosh,Chirag Agarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MEA, a multi-agent system that automatically selects and runs explanation tools, then converts their outputs into faithful natural language explanations across tabular, text, and vision ML models, outperforming existing explainability methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ML models are widely used in high-stakes domains but are opaque to practitioners. Existing post-hoc explanation tools are hard for non-experts to use and interpret, and large language models tend to produce unfaithful, unsupported explanations. There is a need for an interface that lowers the expertise barrier and yields faithful, natural-language explanations across modalities and explanation types.

Method: The authors design MEA, a multi-agent framework with two core agents: (1) a Proposer that, given a user’s question and data modality, selects and configures appropriate explanation tools; and (2) an Actor that is trained end-to-end to generate natural language explanations grounded in the tools’ outputs and optimized for faithfulness. They create diverse explanation question types (feature attribution, counterfactuals, spurious feature detection), each coupled with perturbation-based faithfulness metrics. They then optimize the Actor using faithfulness rewards, plus a modality-adaptive penalty, and evaluate on six datasets spanning tabular, text, and vision, comparing against post-hoc explainers, agentic systems, and closed-source baselines.

Result: The study shows that off-the-shelf frontier LLMs often yield unfaithful explanations. MEA, after reward-based optimization, significantly improves faithfulness over the untrained backbone: +28% for tabular, +21% for text, and +34% for vision tasks. It consistently outperforms traditional post-hoc explainers, other agentic approaches, and closed-source baselines across six datasets and multiple question types.

Conclusion: MEA demonstrates that specialized AI agents can act as a scalable, modality-agnostic interface for ML explainability, automatically orchestrating tools and producing faithful, natural-language explanations. This suggests a new direction for XAI, moving beyond fixed, single-purpose explanation methods toward agent-based systems that adapt to user questions, data modalities, and faithfulness objectives.

Abstract: Recent years have seen the employment of a plethora of machine learning (ML) models in high-stakes domains, but they remain largely opaque to the practitioners who act on their predictions. While post-hoc explanation methods offer a lens into this model behavior, wielding them effectively demands expertise most domain experts lack: navigating high-dimensional outputs, selecting the best explanations, and synthesizing evidence across disparate tools. To this end, we present MEA, a multi-agent framework that removes the explanation knowledge barrier entirely: a Proposer agent selects and configures explanation tools based on the question and modality, while an Actor agent is optimized end-to-end against faithfulness, transforming the outputs into natural language explanations grounded in model behavior across tabular, text, and vision modalities. Further, we introduce diverse question types spanning feature attribution, counterfactual reasoning, and spurious feature detection, each paired with a perturbation-based faithfulness metric. We find that frontier LLMs systematically produce unfaithful explanations. By optimizing against faithfulness rewards augmented with a modality-adaptive penalty, MEA consistently outperforms post hoc explainers, agentic, and closed-source baselines across six datasets, with reward-driven optimization yielding faithfulness gains of +28% (tabular), +21% (text), and +34% (vision) over the untrained backbone. More broadly, our findings suggest that AI agents themselves can serve as a scalable, adaptable interface to ML explainability, opening a path toward natural-language explainability that generalizes beyond the fixed, single-purpose tools that have long defined the field.

</details>


### [79] [What Does a Token Cost? A Mixture-of-Agents Measurement of Sufficient Per-Token Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02491)
*Zhixu Du,Weijia Han,Hai Helen Li,Yiran Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how much computation each token truly needs during generation in large language models and shows that only a small fraction of tokens requires most of the compute, using a Mixture-of-Agents setup to measure per-token sufficient compute and improve routing and drafting efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs use the same computational budget for every generated token, even though many tokens are easy to predict and might not need a large model. Existing efficiency methods like speculative decoding and model routing assume there is redundancy in computation but lack a principled, empirical measure of how much compute each token actually requires. The authors are motivated to quantify token-level compute needs and expose structure that can be exploited to reduce latency and FLOPs without hurting accuracy.

Method: The authors construct a Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) panel consisting of 15 language models of varying sizes from three model families (Qwen, OLMo, and R1-distilled). For a given reference sequence, each agent tries to reproduce the next token, conditioned on the ground-truth previous tokens. For each position, they identify the smallest model in the panel that correctly predicts the reference token; its inference cost is defined as that token’s sufficient compute, which serves as an upper bound on required compute. They then analyze the distribution of sufficient compute across tokens on several benchmarks and build a token-level “MoA-map” of compute difficulty. Using this map, they design and evaluate model routing and drafting strategies and compare them against baselines like confidence-based routing and fixed-window drafting.

Result: Empirically, they find that a relatively small 0.5B-parameter model correctly reproduces 92–95% of tokens across three core benchmarks, indicating that most tokens are easy. However, the most difficult 10% of tokens account for 64–80% of total estimated FLOPs across the tested model families, demonstrating a highly skewed compute distribution. When applying the MoA-derived difficulty map to model routing on all 500 MATH-500 problems, they reduce projected latency from 7.59 seconds to 5.12 seconds while slightly increasing accuracy compared to the best confidence-based routing baseline. For drafting, their MoA-guided strategy uses 32.6% fewer draft tokens and achieves about 20% lower projected latency than a fixed-window drafting scheme at comparable accuracy.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that token-level compute requirements in LLMs are highly heterogeneous: a small fraction of tokens demands most of the computational budget. Their Mixture-of-Agents framework provides a practical way to estimate per-token sufficient compute and exposes structure that existing uniform-compute decoding overlooks. By leveraging this structure through MoA-guided routing and drafting, they show meaningful latency and compute savings without harming accuracy, and they argue there is further headroom for more sophisticated controllers that adaptively allocate compute per token based on inferred difficulty.

Abstract: Large language models spend the same amount of computation on every token they generate, regardless of how difficult each token is to produce. Methods such as speculative decoding and model routing are built on the premise that much of this computation is unnecessary, yet the computation an individual token actually requires has not been measured. We measure it through a Mixture-of-Agents (MoA) lens: a panel of fifteen language models of increasing capacity, drawn from three families, in which every agent attempts to reproduce a reference sequence token by token, conditioned on the correct preceding tokens. We define the inference cost of the smallest agent that succeeds as the token's sufficient compute, which upper-bounds what the token requires. On three core benchmarks, a 0.5B agent reproduces 92--95\% of reference tokens. Across Qwen, OLMo, and R1-distilled panels, the most expensive 10\% account for 64--80\% of estimated FLOPs. On all 500 MATH-500 problems, the MoA-derived map helps model routing reduce projected latency from 7.59 to 5.12 seconds while slightly improving accuracy, relative to the best confidence-routing baseline. The MoA-map helps drafting use 32.6\% fewer draft tokens and approximately 20\% lower projected latency than fixed-window drafting at similar accuracy. These comparisons reveal remaining allocation headroom, motivating controllers that exploit sufficient-compute structure.

</details>


### [80] [Right Order, Wrong Scale: Auditing LLM Judges for Occupational AI Measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02492)
*Harry Lyu,Neil Thompson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper audits how well large language model (LLM) “judges” match human workers when deciding if AI-generated workplace outputs are acceptable, using a new benchmark built from a large worker survey. It finds that even when LLM judges rank responses similarly, they can be badly miscalibrated on what fraction of outputs are acceptable for real occupations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as automatic judges of AI outputs in work settings (e.g., code review, writing feedback, task evaluation). Many evaluations focus on how well these judges agree with humans on relative rankings of answers, but organizations actually care about acceptance decisions and aggregate statistics across tasks and occupations (e.g., what share of outputs meet job standards). The authors want to know whether current LLM judges are reliable for these acceptance and measurement purposes, not just for ranking quality.

Method: The authors construct O*NET-BENCH, an evaluation suite based on 45,796 existing worker ratings from O*NET-like surveys, yielding 4,501 test ratings for audit. They test 33 different LLM-judge configurations spanning six model families, including prompt-only and fine-tuned judges, and compare them to a simple response-only TF-IDF baseline trained on the data. They compute tie-aware pair accuracy for response ranking, assess estimated acceptance rates versus worker-provided acceptance levels, and analyze agreement at task and occupation aggregates. They also compare pointwise scoring with a bundled few-shot/listwise judging protocol within a fine-tuned model lineage, perform cross-validated calibration of judge scores, and evaluate how well calibrated scores explain individual worker-rating variance and support prediction-assisted estimation under different label budgets.

Result: Many LLM-judge configurations achieve relatively high ranking agreement (tie-aware pair accuracy ≥ 0.60), and a straightforward TF-IDF baseline nearly matches the best judges on ordering. However, the judges’ implied acceptance rates range extremely widely (3.0%–97.9%) and diverge sharply from occupation-matched workers’ acceptance rate of 61.1%. Changing the judging protocol from pointwise to bundled few-shot/listwise improves ordering metrics but actually worsens agreement with worker means at task and occupation levels; this degradation is confirmed on a held-out split with pre-specified criteria. Cross-validated calibration can largely remove average bias in acceptance levels, but even calibrated scores explain at most 8.5% of the variance in individual worker ratings. Using these predictions to assist estimation only yields modest precision gains at the studied label budgets.

Conclusion: Good ranking performance of LLM judges does not guarantee that they provide accurate acceptance decisions or occupational-level statistics. Simple baselines can rival sophisticated judges on ordering, while judges can be severely miscalibrated in terms of acceptance rates and aggregates. Protocol changes that improve ranking may actually harm alignment with worker judgments at the levels practitioners care about. Although calibration can correct mean bias, it does not recover fine-grained agreement with individual workers and only slightly improves estimation efficiency. Therefore, LLM judges intended for workplace or occupational measurement must be validated explicitly against the acceptance rates and aggregate quantities they will be used to estimate, rather than being trusted based on ranking metrics alone.

Abstract: LLM judges are increasingly used to assess whether AI outputs meet workplace requirements, but agreement on response rankings does not establish agreement on acceptance rates or occupational aggregates. We introduce O*NET-BENCH, an audit suite derived from an existing survey of 45,796 worker ratings, and evaluate 33 pre-existing judge configurations across six model families on 4,501 test ratings. Twenty-five configurations achieve tie-aware pair accuracy of at least 0.60, although a train-fitted response-only TF-IDF baseline nearly matches the strongest judge. Despite this ordering agreement, judges estimate that 3.0%-97.9% of responses are acceptable, compared with 61.1% for occupation-matched workers. In one fine-tuned lineage, changing from pointwise scoring to a bundled few-shot/listwise protocol improves response ordering while reducing agreement with worker means at the task and occupation levels; this reversal replicates on a task- and worker-disjoint validation split under prespecified criteria. Cross-validated calibration largely removes mean bias, but calibrated scores explain at most 8.5% of individual worker-rating variance. Prediction-assisted estimation yields at most small precision gains at the studied label budgets. These results show that ranking agreement alone is insufficient for occupational measurement. Judges should be validated against the acceptance rates and aggregates their scores will be used to estimate.

</details>


### [81] ["I just assumed that it would translate": examining MT risk awareness among healthcare staff with abbreviations as a use case](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02496)
*Eleanor Taylor-Stilgoe,Félix do Carmo,Constantin Orăsan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates how UK healthcare staff use Google Translate for translating medical abbreviations and the associated patient-safety risks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Healthcare staff in the UK increasingly use machine translation, especially Google Translate, to communicate with patients who have limited English proficiency. While this is meant to support care, relying on MT in high‑stakes clinical contexts may be dangerous. Existing work mostly looks at interpersonal conversations or patient information leaflets, not at the translation of patient medical records. Additionally, medical abbreviations are already known to be risky even within a single language, sometimes leading to severe patient harm. The authors therefore want to explore how translating such abbreviations with MT could compound these risks and how aware staff are of them.

Method: The authors selected medical abbreviations as a focused use case because of their known safety implications. They extracted contextualised examples in French and Spanish from authoritative clinical corpora and translated them into English using Google Translate. These MT outputs were then shown to 21 UK healthcare staff members from varied roles and specialties during semi‑structured interviews. The interview data and participants’ reactions were analysed qualitatively and comparatively (cross‑analysis) to identify themes around risk perception, understanding, and use of MT.

Result: The study produced qualitative data on how healthcare staff interpret Google Translate’s renderings of medical abbreviations, how much they trust or question MT output, and what kinds of potential errors or misunderstandings they notice or overlook. Through cross‑analysis, the authors identified recurring patterns and themes regarding staff awareness of MT risks, the perceived reliability of MT for abbreviations, and the kinds of safety issues that could arise when MT is used on medical records.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that using general‑purpose MT systems such as Google Translate to translate medical abbreviations in clinical documentation poses non‑trivial patient‑safety risks, exacerbating problems already known from monolingual abbreviation use. Staff awareness of these specific risks appears limited or uneven. The findings suggest a need for clearer institutional guidance, training on MT limitations, and caution—if not outright restriction—when using MT for translating medical records and abbreviations in healthcare settings.

Abstract: In the UK, public healthcare staff report turning to machine translation (MT) - predominantly Google Translate (GT) - to communicate with patients across language barriers. Though intended to support their duty of care, potentially uninformed reliance on MT in such contexts could have serious consequences for patient safety. Research nonetheless remains limited on staff awareness of the possible risks posed by higher-stakes MT use in general and with patient medical records in particular, most existing literature instead examining its use in interpersonal situations or with patient-oriented documentation. Moreover, medical abbreviations are well-documented as increasing patient risk even monolingually, with outcomes from their misuse and/or misinterpretation ranging from temporary harm to the death of the patient. Abbreviations were therefore selected as a use case for identifying the potential risks posed by their translation with MT. Contextualised French and Spanish data examples drawn from authoritative clinical corpora and translated via GT were presented during semi-structured interviews to 21 healthcare staff participants in diverse roles and specialties. The results were then subject to qualitative analysis and cross-analysis.

</details>


### [82] [HXAI: Hierarchical Privacy-Preserving Explainable AI in Distributed Energy Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02504)
*Poushali Sengupta,Sabita Maharjan,Frank Eliassen,Yan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces HXAI, a hierarchical privacy-preserving explainable AI framework for grid-level energy demand management that keeps appliance-level details local while providing useful aggregated explanations to grid operators under differential privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern power grids face challenges balancing supply and demand due to intermittent renewable generation and stochastic household consumption. Grid operators need fine-grained, explainable insights into consumption patterns to manage peaks and design tariffs, but such transparency risks exposing sensitive household information. Existing XAI can leak privacy, while standard privacy techniques degrade explanation usefulness, creating a trade-off the paper aims to overcome.

Method: Proposes HXAI, a hierarchical XAI framework with two main components: (1) a local model running within each household’s secure environment that generates fine-grained, appliance-level explanations; (2) a zonal model that aggregates these local explanations for grid-level analysis. The system enforces privacy using flexible privacy-budget management under differential privacy, explicitly controlling cumulative privacy exposure under repeated queries. The key design choice is to preserve the semantic structure of explanations rather than strictly minimizing numerical error.

Result: On both simulated and real-world energy datasets, HXAI yields explanations that are informative for zonal load management and tariff design while preventing the transmission of appliance-level consumption data to grid operators. The framework maintains decision-relevant information at the grid level and successfully limits cumulative privacy loss despite repeated operator queries, outperforming naive differentially private approaches that focus mainly on numerical accuracy.

Conclusion: HXAI demonstrates that hierarchical, locally-computed explanations combined with carefully managed differential privacy can provide grid operators with meaningful, decision-relevant insights without compromising household privacy. Preserving the semantic structure of explanations is more important than minimizing numerical error for XAI under privacy constraints, offering a practical path to jointly achieving explainability and privacy in energy management systems.

Abstract: Balancing electricity demand and supply is increasingly difficult due to the inherent intermittency of renewable power generation and the stochastic power consumption. Grid operators require fine-grained, decision-relevant insights into household energy consumption to manage peak loads and design responsive tariffs, but increased transparency at this level raises significant privacy concerns. Traditional methods for explainable AI (XAI) can reveal sensitive information, while standard privacy techniques often reduce the usefulness of explanations. To address this issue, we introduce HXAI, a hierarchical framework that preserves privacy while enabling reasonable explainable analysis for grid-level demand management. HXAI consists of two main components: (1) a local model that generates fine-grained explanations within a secure, private environment, and (2) a zonal model that aggregates these explanations to support grid-level analysis while enforcing privacy through flexible privacy-budget management. We explicitly limit cumulative privacy exposure under repeated operator queries and show that the proposed framework preserves decision-relevant information without compromising household privacy. Experiments on both simulated and real-world energy datasets demonstrate that HXAI provides useful insights for zonal load management while ensuring that appliance-level consumption remains local and is never transmitted to grid operators. Our results show that preserving the semantic structure of explanations, rather than minimizing numerical error, is the key to XAI under differential privacy. This framework provides a way to achieve both privacy and explainability in energy management.

</details>


### [83] [World Action Modeling with Progressive Visual Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02508)
*Fei Zhang,Zhaochong An,Duncan Frost,Yikai Wang,Pengfei Liu,Ya Zhang,Michal Drozdzal,Amir Bar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ProWAM is a progressive world action model that predicts a sequence of sparse visual sub-goals plus actions, enabling efficient, robust long-horizon robotic control without dense video rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world action models for robot control either generate dense long-horizon video rollouts, which is computationally inefficient and struggles with long tasks, or predict only a single future frame, which lacks explicit guidance on how to progress toward the final goal. There is a need for a scalable method that can handle long-horizon prediction, leverage large-scale passive video data, and provide explicit visual structure to guide action generation in complex, out-of-distribution settings.

Method: ProWAM introduces a progressive world action model that outputs both actions and an ordered sequence of sparse visual sub-goals conditioned on an initial observation and instruction. A video backbone predicts these visual sub-goals, which serve as waypoints to anchor the action generation process throughout the task. The sub-goal predictor can be trained on large-scale, action-free videos, allowing visual planning to be largely decoupled from the action policy. For efficiency, ProWAM runs the heavy video backbone once to cache sub-goal features, and then performs lightweight action denoising for replanning, avoiding iterative dense video generation.

Result: On simulation benchmarks, ProWAM achieves state-of-the-art performance: 85.8% success on LIBERO-Plus and 75.7% on randomized RoboTwin, with relative gains up to +35.9% over the strongest baseline. On RoboCasa365, it obtains 48.1% success overall and 18.2% on the difficult Composite-Unseen split, ranking 4th. In zero-shot real-world experiments in novel scenes, ProWAM attains 70.0% success, surpassing the strongest baseline by an absolute +15.0% (from 55.0% to 70.0%), which is a +27.3% relative improvement.

Conclusion: Progress-indexed visual foresight via sparse sub-goals provides an effective inductive bias for closed-loop robotic control. By separating visual planning from action generation and using a single-pass video backbone with cached sub-goal features, ProWAM scales to long-horizon tasks, improves out-of-distribution robustness, and achieves strong performance in both simulation and zero-shot real-world settings.

Abstract: World action models (WAMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for robotic control by jointly predicting future visual dynamics and actions from an initial observation and instruction. However, existing WAMs struggle with long-horizon prediction, as generating dense video rollouts is highly inefficient. Some recent WAMs address this by predicting a single future frame without generating the full video, but this approach neglects how to progress toward the goal. We present ProWAM, a progressive world action model that jointly predicts actions and an ordered sequence of sparse visual sub-goals, providing explicit visual guidance to anchor action generation throughout task execution. This design scales naturally, as sub-goal prediction can be learned from large-scale action-free videos, allowing the video backbone to offload complex visual planning from the action policy. For efficient action generation, ProWAM executes a single video-backbone forward pass to cache sparse sub-goal features, eliminating iterative full-video generation and requiring only lightweight action denoising during replanning. Across extensive evaluations, ProWAM achieves superior out-of-distribution robustness. On simulation benchmarks, it sets new state-of-the-art results on LIBERO-Plus (85.8%) and randomized RoboTwin (75.7%), outperforming the strongest baseline with relative gains of up to +35.9%. On RoboCasa365, ProWAM achieves a 48.1% success rate and 18.2% on the challenging Composite-Unseen split, ranking 4th overall. Crucially, in zero-shot real-world experiments, ProWAM achieves 70.0% success, outperforming the strongest baseline by +15.0 (from 55.0% to 70.0%, a +27.3% relative gain) in novel scenes. These results demonstrate the value of progress-indexed visual foresight for closed-loop control. Our program is in https://sii-ferenas.github.io/ProWAM-page.

</details>


### [84] [On-Premises Multi-Course RAG Tutoring for Business Education: Hardware-Software Trade-offs in a Campus AI Tutor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02510)
*Sidney Shapiro,Joshua Lindemann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper describes CourseChat, an on-premises, multi-course RAG-based AI tutor for business education, focused on secure deployment, model selection under latency constraints, and engineering tradeoffs rather than learning outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Campus AI tutors must keep course content and student data on institutional infrastructure while ensuring that generated answers are grounded strictly in assigned materials. Existing cloud-based or single-course solutions do not fully satisfy these security, privacy, and multi-course management needs, especially in undergraduate business education settings and LMS integration contexts like Moodle.

Method: The authors built CourseChat, an on-premises RAG system deployed behind a campus web gateway and integrated with Moodle, supporting multiple isolated courses keyed by course reference numbers. The architecture uses twin-edge AI hosts running FastAPI, a local vector database, and local LLMs served via Ollama. They conducted two generation-model bake-offs to compare models under a classroom-speed constraint, ran a fixed-evidence source-fidelity evaluation, and performed audits of conversations and quizzes. They also iteratively modified the software to improve follow-up topic handling and created a bank of 435 prebuilt questions across 65 modules.

Result: Larger LLMs failed to meet the required classroom response-time threshold, while a 12B and a 7B model passed, and an 8B model worked well enough to be retained as the production model. A mixture-of-experts candidate improved some answer corrections but introduced new factual and continuity errors, so it was not adopted. Architectural and software changes enhanced follow-up topic resolution without expanding beyond course scope, and the prebuilt question bank allowed practice decoupled from live generation. The evaluation showed that multiple factors—model choice, evidence selection, serving stack, and product design—interact importantly in shaping system performance.

Conclusion: CourseChat demonstrates that a multi-course, on-premises RAG tutor can be deployed within campus infrastructure with acceptable latency and controlled grounding in course materials, but optimal model size and architecture are context dependent. The work argues that selecting models, curating evidence, ensuring serving compatibility, and designing the user experience should be treated as a single joint engineering problem. However, the current results do not show pedagogical effectiveness or learning gains; additional work is needed to evaluate faculty satisfaction, system behavior under peak load, and wider adoption through public-gateway access.

Abstract: Campus AI tutors based on retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) must ground answers in assigned course materials while keeping textbooks and student dialogue on institutional infrastructure. We present CourseChat, an on-premises, multi-course RAG tutor for undergraduate business education, deployed behind a campus web gateway and intended for use embedded in Moodle. Six isolated course offerings, each keyed by its own course reference number (CRN), share twin-edge AI hosts running a FastAPI service, a local vector database, and a local large language model (LLM) served by Ollama. We report two generation-model bake-off rounds, a separate fixed-evidence source-fidelity comparison, and conversation and quiz audits. Several larger models failed the classroom speed gate, but a 12B model and a 7B alternative passed. A separate mixture-of-experts candidate improved some corrections while introducing new factual and continuity errors. We therefore retain the 8B production model pending a demonstrated overall improvement, rather than claiming that 8B is universally optimal. Software changes improved follow-up topic resolution while preserving course scope; 435 prebuilt questions across 65 modules decouple practice from live generation. The results support treating model choice, evidence selection, serving compatibility, and product design as a joint engineering decision. They do not establish learning gains: faculty ratings, peak-load capacity, and complete public-gateway acceptance remain separate evaluation needs.

</details>


### [85] [Hypothesis-guided discovery of cognitive algorithms via program refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02523)
*Huiwen Alex Yang,Mark K. Ho,Bill D. Thompson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hybrid system where human-written probabilistic cognitive models are iteratively refined by LLM agents to better match behavioral data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cognitive science aims to recover the cognitive algorithms underlying human behavior, but existing approaches have trade-offs. Traditional cognitive modeling methods are interpretable and let researchers inject domain knowledge, yet they are labor-intensive, not very flexible, and struggle to scale to complex algorithmic reasoning. Newer LLM-based approaches can automatically generate models and scale better, but they largely sideline human expertise, give less structural control, and have mostly been demonstrated on simpler tasks rather than full algorithm recovery. There is a need for a method that combines human-guided interpretability and structural guarantees with LLM-driven flexibility and search power.

Method: The authors frame cognitive algorithm discovery as a program refinement problem. Researchers first specify cognitive models as probabilistic programs encoding hypothesized algorithms. A set of LLM agents then: (1) analyze mismatches between model predictions and observed human behavior; (2) propose code-level edits and extensions subject to constraints set by the researcher (to preserve interpretability and theoretical commitments); and (3) check that the modified program still respects desired structural properties and model class assumptions. Each revised program is passed to a probabilistic inference engine that infers latent variables and computes data likelihood, enabling quantitative assessment of model fit. This loop—diagnose, propose edits, verify, and refit—is iterated over a space of possible cognitive algorithm refinements.

Result: Applied to data from a problem‑solving paradigm designed to elicit diverse cognitive strategies, the pipeline produces refined probabilistic programs that systematically better account for participants’ behavior than the original hand-crafted models. Across runs, the system converges on a small set of recurring code-level innovations—shared modifications that repeatedly improve likelihood—that correspond to psychologically interpretable variations in strategy and information use. These recurrent changes help explain meaningful individual and condition-level variability in how people solve the task.

Conclusion: A hybrid human–LLM program refinement framework can serve as a practical tool for cognitive algorithm discovery. By embedding human-designed probabilistic programs inside an LLM-driven refinement and evaluation loop, the approach retains interpretability and theoretical control while gaining flexibility and scalability. The empirical results show that such a system can reliably improve model fit and uncover a compact set of psychologically meaningful algorithmic innovations, suggesting a promising direction for future cognitive modeling workflows that combine domain expertise with automated model search.

Abstract: Developing cognitive models of algorithmic reasoning from behavioral data is a central problem in cognitive science that challenges current methods. Traditional approaches to cognitive modeling are interpretable and benefit from human expertise, but lack flexibility and scalability. Emerging techniques using large language models (LLMs) for de novo generation of cognitive models are scalable and flexible, but lack a role for human expertise and have mostly been applied to simpler tasks than algorithm recovery. We propose a hybrid system that treats discovery of cognitive algorithms as a program refinement problem. Human-created cognitive models are expressed as probabilistic programs and provided to a system of LLM agents with a mandate to: identify mismatches between model and behavior; propose code-level modifications within researcher-specified constraints; and verify structural fidelity. Revisions propagate to a probabilistic inference module that performs inference for latent variables and data likelihood computations. We evaluate the pipeline on human behavior in a problem-solving paradigm that exposes a variety of cognitive algorithms. Revised models consistently improve model fit relative to ancestral models and reveal a small set of recurring innovations that capture meaningful behavioral variability in this task.

</details>


### [86] [Learning What to Investigate Next: Meta-Reasoning for Long-Horizon Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02525)
*Ankur Samanta,Yonathan Efroni,Paul Sajda,Kaveh Hassani,Anirudh Goyal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MIRA, a hierarchical meta-reasoning framework that separates deciding *what research step to do next* from *how to execute it*, enabling better long-horizon autonomous research and more focused reinforcement learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon autonomous research agents must plan sequences of investigations where key decisions are sparse and their impact may only be visible many steps later. Standard token-level or step-level RL struggles with credit assignment and efficient use of additional compute in such long traces, especially in domains like theorem proving or neural architecture search. There is a need for an architecture that explicitly represents and learns the high-level meta-reasoning policy that directs research, while handling execution separately and enabling better value estimation over partial progress.

Method: They propose MIRA, a hierarchical architecture with: (1) an outer-loop meta-reasoner that reads a persistent research record, curates context, and issues a high-level work order for the next investigation (or terminates the episode); (2) an inner-loop executor, freshly instantiated for each work order, that carries out the investigation, effectively embedding execution between meta-decisions. They then exploit the natural decision boundaries at each work order to train a generative critic that predicts expected remaining return from these partial states, rather than at token level. They further perform cross-environment pretraining of this critic to obtain a transferable prior over partial-progress values. Building on this, they introduce MIRA-AC, a generative actor-critic where a single generative model jointly (a) forecasts remaining return and (b) proposes the next work order, thus eliminating a separate critic. Policy optimization is focused only on the sparse meta-reasoning decisions, and they use the model’s own proxy hill-climbing signals as training feedback in several autoresearch environments.

Result: MIRA, even without policy training, improves long-horizon inference and uses extra compute more effectively in both theorem proving and neural architecture search tasks. The generative critic trained at meta-decision boundaries outperforms token-level value estimators in forecasting remaining return. Cross-environment pretraining further enhances both forecasting accuracy and adaptation, yielding a transferable value prior that can be reused across domains. With MIRA-AC, they achieve efficient long-horizon RL by restricting optimization to meta-reasoning actions, and training on proxy hill-climbing signals improves performance across four different autoresearch environments. The learned actor and value initialization transfer successfully between environments.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling meta-reasoning as a separable, learnable policy over high-level research decisions enables more effective long-horizon autonomous research. By structurally separating research allocation from execution, exploiting decision boundaries for generative value prediction, and using a unified generative actor-critic (MIRA-AC), the approach improves long-horizon inference, credit assignment, and sample efficiency across multiple autoresearch domains. Meta-reasoning policies can thus be learned and transferred as priors for directing complex, multi-step research processes without needing to directly optimize over entire long execution traces.

Abstract: Long-horizon research agents must decide both how to investigate and what to investigate next as evidence accumulates. This is hard to learn because such decisions are sparse in long execution traces, and their consequences may emerge several investigations later. We introduce Meta-reasoning for Iterative Research Agents (MIRA), a hierarchical architecture separating research allocation from execution. An outer-loop meta-reasoner curates context from a persistent research record, then writes a work order for the next investigation or ends the episode. A fresh inner-loop executor carries out each work order, making execution part of the transition between meta-reasoning actions. Without policy training, MIRA improves long-horizon inference and allocates additional compute more effectively in theorem proving and open-ended neural-architecture research. Its decision boundaries also provide natural units for credit assignment. At each boundary, we train a generative critic to forecast expected remaining return from partial states, outperforming token-level alternatives. Cross-environment pretraining improves forecasting and adaptation, yielding a transferable prior for valuing partial progress. We use this prior to initialize MIRA-AC, a generative actor-critic jointly trained to forecast remaining return and choose the next investigation, without a separate critic model. MIRA-AC concentrates policy optimization on meta-reasoning decisions, enabling efficient long-horizon reinforcement learning without directly optimizing the longer execution traces they initiate. Training MIRA-AC on the model's own proxy hill-climbing signals improves gold performance across four autoresearch environments; the actor transfers with cross-environment value initialization. Together, these results show that meta-reasoning can be learned as an explicit policy for directing long-horizon autonomous research.

</details>


### [87] [How To Train Your World Model: Fine-tuning vs RAG for LM-based World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02542)
*Dhananjay Ashok,Shantanu Agarwal,Vivek Datla,Jonathan May,Alfy Samuel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper compares fine-tuned language-model world models with retrieval-augmented ones across multiple text-based environments, finds complementary strengths, identifies retrieval errors and improvements, and proposes a hybrid parametric+retrieval world model that performs best overall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models let agents plan by simulating environment dynamics, and in text-based settings, they are often implemented by fine-tuned language models. Meanwhile, retrieval-augmented generation has been very successful elsewhere, but its role in world modelling is not well understood. The authors are motivated to rigorously compare fine-tuning vs. retrieval-based world models, understand their strengths and weaknesses, diagnose issues in retrieval, and see whether combining them can yield more robust, data-efficient agents.

Method: They run a systematic evaluation over five varied text-based environments (embodied tasks, web navigation, social scenarios), constructing world models via (1) fine-tuning LMs on interaction experience and (2) RAG that retrieves past transitions from an experience buffer. They measure downstream agent rewards under different data scales and exploration diversity. They further analyze RAG-based models by introducing a counterfactual intervention procedure to estimate retrieval error rates (how often suboptimal transitions are retrieved), experiment with different query reformulation and hierarchical retrieval strategies, and finally design a hybrid world model that uses a parametric LM for core dynamics and an actively updated memory store for retrieval when needed.

Result: Fine-tuned world models usually lead to higher agent rewards, winning in 15 of 20 environment–setting combinations. Both fine-tuned and RAG-based WMs improve with more and more diverse data, but RAG is more data-efficient at smaller scales, while fine-tuning benefits more from large-scale experience. Their counterfactual analysis shows current retrievers often fetch suboptimal transitions. Hierarchical query reformulation improves retrieval quality over standard RAG pipelines. The final hybrid world model that merges parametric dynamics with selective retrieval consistently outperforms pure fine-tuned or pure RAG-based models across different environments and LM backbones.

Conclusion: Fine-tuning and RAG represent complementary ways of building LM-based world models: fine-tuning excels with ample data, while RAG is more data-efficient but hampered by retrieval errors. By diagnosing and improving retrieval and then combining parametric and non-parametric components into a hybrid WM, agents can achieve more robust and generally superior performance. The work suggests that future world modelling systems should not rely solely on either fine-tuning or retrieval, but instead integrate both with careful retrieval design and active memory management.

Abstract: World models (WMs) simulate the transition dynamics of environments, enabling agents to plan over the consequences of their actions. In text-based environments, fine-tuning a Language Model (LM) to serve as a WM has emerged as a dominant paradigm. However, despite the widespread success of non-parametric approaches such as Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), retrieval for LM-based world modelling remains underexplored. We conduct a systematic evaluation across five diverse environments spanning embodied, web navigation and social settings, comparing fine-tuning and RAG-based approaches for LM-based world modelling. Our study reveals that fine-tuning often outperforms RAG, with fine-tuned WMs enabling agents to obtain higher rewards on 15/20 settings. While both construction paradigms benefit from additional and more diverse exploration, RAG-based approaches prove more data-efficient, and fine-tuning approaches disproportionately benefit from scaling the amount of experience collected. With a focus on RAG-based WMs, we devise a procedure that uses counterfactual intervention to estimate the error rate of the retrieval stage, and show that retrievers consistently surface suboptimal transitions from the experience buffer. Hoping to address this failing, we study a variety of query reformulation strategies, demonstrating that a hierarchical approach outperforms the traditional retrieval pipeline. Finally, we compose our findings into a hybrid world modelling system that parametrically captures core environment dynamics, while learning to rely on retrieval from an actively maintained memory store. Our hybrid system consistently outperforms other methods across multiple environments and models, showcasing the robustness of the approach and the applicability of our findings.

</details>


### [88] [How to Have a Sensitive Debate: An Instance-Optimal Protocol for AI Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02557)
*Jiawei Li,Zhiyang Xun,Lijie Chen,Jonah Brown-Cohen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an improved AI debate protocol that offers stronger theoretical guarantees for using humans to reliably judge complex problems via debates between powerful AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As advanced AI systems surpass human experts on complex tasks, it becomes difficult for humans to reliably supervise and evaluate their outputs. AI debate is a candidate solution, where two AIs argue opposite sides and a human judges the winner, but existing protocols only provide average-case guarantees and weaker equilibrium properties. There is a need for oversight protocols with stronger, worst-case correctness guarantees and better incentive alignment for honest behavior.

Method: The authors work in the framework of computational and query complexity, modeling AI debate as a protocol that decomposes complex problems into simpler subproblems that humans can judge. They focus on problems that admit sufficiently stable decompositions. They design a new debate protocol and analyze it using tools from query complexity, particularly the notion of fractional block sensitivity, to characterize how reliably complex problems can be broken into human-judgable pieces. They then prove formal guarantees about correctness, equilibrium properties, and lower bounds for any black-box supervision scheme over this problem class.

Result: The new protocol achieves correctness in a worst-case sense for all problems with sufficiently stable decompositions, strengthening previous average-case results. It ensures that being honest and correct is a dominant-strategy equilibrium for both debaters, rather than requiring a Stackelberg (leader-follower) structure. Additionally, they prove black-box lower bounds that show instance-wise optimality: no other protocol that only queries human judgments as a black box can do better on any instance in this problem class.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that for a broad class of problems with stable decompositions, AI debate can be structured to yield strong theoretical oversight guarantees: worst-case correctness, dominant-strategy incentives for honesty, and instance-wise optimality under black-box human supervision. By connecting stable decompositions to fractional block sensitivity, the work both refines the theory of AI debate and sets limits on how much any such black-box oversight protocol can be improved.

Abstract: As powerful AI systems reach and sometimes surpass the abilities of human experts across a range of cognitively demanding tasks, the problem of accurate oversight and supervision of these systems has become increasingly urgent. One promising approach is AI debate, which seeks to leverage a debate between two powerful AIs to break complex questions down into simpler claims that can be easily judged directly. Theoretical work on debate has formalized this intuition in the language of computational complexity theory, where the goal is to design protocols (i.e., rules of the debate game) that provide rigorous guarantees on correctness for judging solutions to complex problems with limited supervision. Specifically, the current best protocol has been shown to work for all problems that have sufficiently stable decompositions into subproblems. In this paper, we design a new protocol for this same class of problems that improves on the prior work in several ways. First, correctness holds in a worst-case rather than an average-case sense. Second, being honest and correct is a dominant-strategy equilibrium for both debaters, rather than a Stackelberg equilibrium. Finally, we prove black-box lower bounds, showing that our new protocol is instance-wise optimal. That is, no protocol for this class of problems can outperform ours while making only black-box queries to human judgments. We obtain these results by relating the notion of stable problem decompositions to the concept of fractional block sensitivity from query complexity.

</details>


### [89] [Mitigating Social Sycophancy via Pluralistic Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02568)
*Stephane Hatgis-Kessell,Myra Cheng,Xiaoxuan Hou,Qian Hu,Rahul Gupta,Natasha Jaques,Emma Brunskill*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Pluralistic Preference Optimization (PlurPO), a training method that reduces social sycophancy in language models by having them simulate multiple stakeholders in interpersonal conflicts and optimize for responses acceptable to all, using only model-generated signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models are widely used for personal and relationship advice but tend to be overly affirming (socially sycophantic), which can encourage harmful overconfidence and poor conflict resolution. Existing anti-sycophancy methods mostly target factual questions with clear ground truth, or use weak prompting/post-training for social advice, leaving a gap in effectively handling nuanced interpersonal scenarios where no single ground truth exists.

Method: The authors propose Pluralistic Preference Optimization (PlurPO). For inputs describing interpersonal conflicts or advice-seeking, the LM first identifies and simulates all relevant stakeholders affected by the user’s behavior. It then generates candidate responses and uses its own internal evaluations of how acceptable each response is from each stakeholder’s perspective to construct preference data. The model is then trained (preference-optimized) to favor responses that are acceptable across stakeholders, without relying on external ground-truth labels.

Result: Across four datasets and four model families, PlurPO significantly reduces social sycophancy compared to prior methods. In harmful-intent scenarios where endorsement should be low, PlurPO cuts endorsement rates by 89% on average. In general advice settings, it narrows the gap between model and human endorsement rates by more than half (from 17.8% to 8.0% on average). Preference data built with an 8B model also transfers effectively to a larger 32B model, improving its behavior as well.

Conclusion: Social sycophancy in LMs stems partly from over-centering on the user’s perspective and neglecting other stakeholders. By leveraging the LM’s own ability to simulate multiple perspectives and optimizing for responses acceptable to all, PlurPO can substantially mitigate social sycophancy without ground-truth labels, and its learned preferences transfer across model scales.

Abstract: Personal advice, including relationship advice, now ranks among the most common uses of generative AI. But language models (LMs) exhibit sycophancy: they affirm users much more often than humans do, which can make people overconfident and less willing to repair their relationships after a conflict. Prior work on mitigating sycophancy has focused on factual settings where a response can be checked against a ground truth answer, while mitigations for social sycophancy (e.g., personal advice, where there is no ground truth) have relied on simple prompting and post-training methods with limited effectiveness. Our insight is that social sycophancy occurs in part because LMs overly center on the user and fail to consider the perspectives of other stakeholders impacted by the user's behavior. To address this problem we propose Pluralistic Preference Optimization (PlurPO): given inputs describing interpersonal conflicts, the LM identifies and simulates the relevant stakeholders, and is then trained to prefer and generate responses acceptable to all stakeholders. PlurPO uses only signals the model produces about its own outputs, without ground-truth labels. PlurPO substantially reduces social sycophancy across four datasets and four model families compared to prior methods. For example, on statements of intent to cause harm, where the users' actions should not be endorsed, PlurPO reduces the endorsement rate by 89% on average across four models. On general advice questions, where the target is to match the endorsement rate of human responses, it closes the gap by more than half, from 17.8% to 8.0% on average. The preference dataset constructed by PlurPO for an 8B model also effectively transfers to mitigating sycophancy in a larger (32B) model. Our results indicate that social sycophancy can be reduced by leveraging a model's own capabilities to simulate a plurality of relevant perspectives.

</details>


### [90] [Answering clinicians' questions over trial evidence tables with verifiable, feedback-driven language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02576)
*Manan Roy Choudhury,Suparno Roy Chowdhury,Swastik Sahoo,Muhammad Ali Khan,Kaneez Zahra Rubab Khakwani,Mohamad Bassam Sonbol,Irbaz Bin Riaz,Vivek Gupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents FD-SCoPE, a language-model-based framework that lets clinicians ask complex, auditable questions over clinical trial evidence tables, including questions about attributes not explicitly stored, and shows strong performance and improvability via expert feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinicians rely on systematic reviews and evidence tables to understand clinical trial results, but current tools only allow rigid database-style queries on pre-defined fields. Many clinically meaningful questions involve derived or harmonised attributes—such as drug target classes or harmonised endpoints—that are not explicitly encoded in these tables. There is a need for a system that supports natural, flexible querying over both recorded and derived attributes while remaining transparent and auditable, so that clinicians can trust and inspect how answers are produced.

Method: The authors design FD-SCoPE, a framework that uses language models to translate clinician-style questions into executable queries over an evidence table, infer or derive missing attributes (e.g., target class, harmonised endpoints) from trial descriptions, and produce answers accompanied by the underlying query, selected trials, and derivation rules. The system incorporates mechanisms for expert feedback: clinicians can correct derivations or selections, and these corrections are used to update and improve the model. FD-SCoPE is evaluated on an oncology evidence table comprising 159 immune checkpoint inhibitor trial records and a suite of 140 representative clinician-style tasks, as well as larger sets of simulated questions to assess generalisation and learning from feedback.

Result: On 140 clinician-style tasks over the oncology trial table, FD-SCoPE successfully completed all tasks, achieving performance in the range of 90.7–97.9% (depending on the task type or metric). For questions requiring derived attributes, it retrieved 99.3% of relevant trial records with a positive predictive value of 89.8%. Its derived-attribute answering performance (F1 score of 77.7%) exceeded that of four alternative approaches, which achieved F1 scores between 64.8% and 73.4%. When the system was given 299 expert-like corrections, simulated from reference answers, its F1 score on a separate set of 1,201 unseen questions increased from 77.9% to 84.9%, demonstrating that expert feedback can substantially improve performance.

Conclusion: FD-SCoPE demonstrates that coupling language models with executable database queries, explicit derivation rules, and expert feedback enables clinicians to pose natural, flexible questions over clinical trial evidence while preserving auditability and transparency. The system can accurately handle both directly recorded and derived attributes, outperforming alternative methods, and can improve further through iterative expert corrections. This approach suggests a viable path toward trustworthy, interactive, and continuously learning tools for accessing and reasoning over clinical trial evidence in practice.

Abstract: Systematic reviews condense clinical trials into evidence tables, yet clinicians can interrogate these tables only through database queries, and many questions concern attributes that the table does not record, such as a drug's target class or a harmonised endpoint. Here we introduce FD-SCoPE, a language-model framework that answers both kinds of question, exposes the query, the selected trials and the derivation rule behind every answer, and learns from expert corrections. On an oncology evidence table of 159 immune checkpoint inhibitor trial records, FD-SCoPE completed all 140 clinician-style tasks (alternatives, 90.7-97.9%). For questions needing derived attributes it retrieved 99.3% of relevant trial records at a positive predictive value of 89.8% and outperformed four alternative approaches (derived-value F1 77.7% versus 64.8-73.4%).
