Corrections on 299 questions, simulated from reference answers, raised F1 on 1,201 unseen questions from 77.9% to 84.9%. Language models coupled with executable queries, verified programs and expert feedback can give clinicians auditable access to trial evidence.

</details>


### [91] [Labels Override Definitions in Jev-Style Typed Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02586)
*Seyedarmin Azizi,Erfan Baghaei Potraghloo,Massoud Pedram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies whether typed decision models and classifier-style LMs actually follow option *definitions* or just the short *labels*, and finds that many models are heavily biased toward labels due to how prompts are rendered.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Typed decision models are widely used for routing, moderation, and triage, where each option has a label and a natural-language definition describing the rule to follow. Practitioners often assume the model bases its decision on these definitions. However, in practice, models may instead key off the short labels, especially when used as classifiers via label-string scoring. This mismatch can silently break safety, routing, or policy logic, so the authors want to understand whether models follow definitions or labels, and where any bias originates.

Method: The authors examine four open-weight typed decision models (built on a Qwen2.5 backbone), three ways of reading answers from that backbone, eleven standard classification tasks, and a new synthetic routing benchmark called PolicyBench where the governing rule appears only in option definitions. They probe the models under several ablations: deleting all definitions, renaming labels (e.g., to A/B), and introducing contradictions between labels and definitions. Crucially, they compare two prompt rendering styles: one that formats options as "{label}: {definition}" (laya) versus another that provides only definitions (von). Without changing weights, they swap rendering styles across systems to test causality. They also design a simple two-call test for practitioners to detect label-vs-definition reliance and evaluate four mitigation strategies.

Result: For most models and tasks, predictions follow the option labels rather than the written definitions, an effect termed option-label bias. Removing all definitions barely changes accuracy (e.g., laya-td: 0.8559 vs 0.8487), even though definitions alone can achieve strong performance (0.7971). Renaming options to neutral labels like A and B significantly improves accuracy (+0.1511 with tight confidence bounds). One system (von) initially shows no label bias, but when its prompt rendering is changed to include labels as "{label}: {definition}", its performance collapses (accuracy from 0.8511 to 0.2281 when labels contradict definitions). Conversely, making laya render only definitions makes its checkpoints invariant, eliminating the label-driven behavior, all without retraining.

Conclusion: The primary source of option-label bias in typed decision models is not the constrained decision head architecture, as previously suspected, but the prompt rendering format that entangles labels with definitions. When options are rendered as "label: definition", models tend to overfit to the label token(s) and ignore the longer rule text. Using definition-only rendering largely removes this bias. The authors provide a simple two-call diagnostic test so practitioners can determine whether their models rely on labels or definitions, and they quantify the impact of several mitigation techniques, guiding safer and more reliable use of typed decision models in routing, moderation, and policy applications.

Abstract: A typed decision model answers a fixed question about an input by returning a probability for each of several caller-defined options. Each option carries a short label and a written definition, which is where a developer states the rule the model should apply. Jev introduced this interface for routing, moderation and triage, open implementations followed, and the same operation occurs whenever a language model is used as a classifier by scoring label strings. We study the open implementations, whose weights we can inspect and patch, and ask whether the probability follows the definitions or the labels. A preference for the label we call option-label bias. Across four open-weight typed decision models, three ways of reading an answer from a Qwen2.5 backbone, eleven classification tasks and PolicyBench, a synthetic routing suite we introduce in which the rule appears only in the definitions, the answer is mostly the labels. Deleting every definition leaves accuracy unchanged (laya-td: 0.8559 against 0.8487), although those definitions support 0.7971 on their own, and renaming the options to A and B raises accuracy by +0.1511 [+0.1377, +0.1646]. One system, von, is unaffected, and the two code bases differ in one expression: laya writes each option as "{label}: {definition}", while von writes only the definition. Changing that expression in both directions, with no weight changed, makes all three laya checkpoints exactly invariant (+0.0000 [+0.0000, +0.0000]) and creates the effect in von, whose accuracy falls from 0.8511 to 0.2281 when a label contradicts its definition. Earlier work attributed this failure to the constrained decision head these models use in place of a text decoder; our results locate it in the prompt rendering. We give a two-call test that tells a practitioner which case applies to their model, and measure what four mitigations are worth.

</details>


### [92] [Open-Endedness Bench: Measuring Epistemic Process from Agent Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02588)
*Chengyang Shi,Xianglin Ji,Jintao Huang,Jicheng Wang,Yifeng He,Jiachen Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes OEB, a method to evaluate how well autonomous agents conduct open-ended research by analyzing their execution logs instead of their final scores or reference answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations judge agents mainly by outcome scores or comparison to reference answers, which fails in open-ended research tasks where no ground truth or clear optimum exists. This makes it hard to know whether claimed discoveries are actually supported by experiments and whether agents are engaging in sound scientific reasoning rather than reward hacking or lucky guesses. The authors want a way to assess the epistemic quality of an agent’s research process itself.

Method: They introduce OEB (Open-Endedness Bench), a benchmark-agnostic methodology that uses only the agent’s execution record (logs of actions and written outputs) and ignores outcome scores and reference answers. OEB compiles these logs into an epistemic event graph whose nodes are propositions and actions, with edges linking what the agent claims to the experiments it runs to test those claims. Each node is grounded in exact log excerpts verified automatically. Using a single core principle—that only executed actions can provide supporting or refuting evidence—OEB analyzes this graph to score four competence dimensions (evidence use, experiment design, hypothesis revision, and avoidance of reward hacking) based largely on the fraction of available opportunities where the agent behaved like a sound researcher. It also derives six subjective persona traits that summarize the agent’s research style.

Result: Applying OEB to 119 existing runs across 12 tasks from three benchmarks (LLM post-training, chip design, and a training-speed record), the authors find that only 16–29% of improvements claimed by agents match the logged experimental evidence. They observe that in 9 out of 10 tasks, the best-performing run explores more new ideas in its second half than the worst run, indicating more persistent experimentation. The inferred research personas show strong model-specific signatures: for every trait, which model was used explains much more variance in trait scores across runs than which task was used (median 43% vs 7%).

Conclusion: OEB provides a systematic, benchmark-agnostic way to evaluate agents on how they conduct open-ended research rather than just on final performance. By grounding every proposition in execution logs and demanding experimental evidence for claims, it exposes overclaimed improvements and reveals differences in epistemic competence and research style across models and runs. This suggests that future work on research agents should explicitly target and measure these epistemic behaviors, not only outcome metrics.

Abstract: Agents are increasingly given open-ended research tasks: discovering an empirical law from self-designed experiments, improving a heuristic whose optimum nobody knows, or beating a standing record. Their execution logs record every step of this research, yet the runs are still judged by their outcome score. That score alone does not establish whether an agent's claims follow from executed experiments, and a reference answer may be unavailable. We evaluate the agent's epistemic process: how it forms hypotheses, tests them, and revises them in response to evidence. We introduce OEB (Open-Endedness Bench), a benchmark-agnostic methodology that reads only the agent's execution record and never a reference answer or an outcome score. OEB compiles the record into a unified epistemic event graph whose edges connect the propositions the agent states to the executed actions that test them; each node carries an exact excerpt that code verifies against the record. One principle governs scoring: prose can state a proposition, but only evidence returned by an executed action can support or refute it, so OEB checks what the agent writes against what it actually ran. From the graph, OEB scores four competence axes (evidence, experiment, revision, and no reward hacking), mostly as the share of opportunities for sound research that the agent took, and profiles six subjective persona traits that describe the agent's research habits. We score 119 existing runs over 12 tasks from three benchmarks: LLM post-training, chip design, and a training-speed record. Against logged results, only 16-29% of the improvements agents claim are real. On 9 of 10 tasks, the best run tries more new ideas in its second half than the worst run. The persona readings follow the model: for every trait, the model that ran explains more of its variance across runs than the task (a median of 43% against 7%).

</details>


### [93] [TasteBench: Multimodal Benchmark for Sensory Prediction, from Molecules to Sustainable Foods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02599)
*Anna T. Thomas,Sohum Patnaik,Caroline Cotto,Benjamin Sanchez-Lengeling*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TasteBench is a benchmark and competition for predicting human sensory perception of taste for sustainable, plant-based proteins, providing datasets, reliability analyses, and baseline multimodal models for food-level ranking and molecular-level taste prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing sustainable, plant-based proteins is slowed by the lack of fast, computational proxies for human taste evaluation. Unlike drug and materials discovery, where computational tools like docking or DFT accelerate screening, food science still largely relies on slow, expensive human sensory panels. The authors aim to create a standardized, data-driven benchmark that captures human taste judgments and their variability, enabling machine learning models to be trained and fairly evaluated as potential proxies for human sensory evaluation.

Method: The authors construct TasteBench, a multimodal benchmark and associated privacy-preserving competition. They compile a large-scale food-level dataset: 21,000+ human sensory evaluations of 215 plant-based foods spanning 24 product categories. These evaluations are converted into 935 within-category ranking pairs indicating which food tastes more like its animal-based target. In parallel, they build a molecular-level taste classification dataset of 15,000 flavor molecules. They rigorously characterize the reliability and noise in the human ground truth by estimating inter-rater agreement (Krippendorff’s alpha) and split-half reliability of aggregated panel rankings. They then implement and evaluate baseline models across four input modalities on the ranking task, comparing model performance to both individual panelists and the panel-aggregated reliability ceiling.

Result: Ground-truth analysis shows that individual panelists have low agreement (Krippendorff’s α = 0.077), but aggregated panel rankings achieve a split-half reliability ceiling of 0.825, setting an upper bound for realistic ML performance. On the same food pairs judged by panelists, the best baseline model reaches 0.661 pairwise accuracy, which is comparable to the median individual panelist accuracy of 0.650 and below the reliability ceiling. When evaluated over all within-category pairs, the best model attains 0.683 pairwise accuracy. These results demonstrate that ML models can approach, but not yet surpass, typical human performance on this benchmark and highlight remaining headroom relative to the reliability ceiling.

Conclusion: TasteBench establishes a standardized, rigorously characterized benchmark and competition for sensory prediction relevant to sustainable protein discovery. It shows that while current multimodal models can match the accuracy of a median human panelist on food-level taste similarity rankings, there is still significant room to improve toward the reliability ceiling of aggregated panels. By providing datasets, reliability analyses, and baseline models for both food-level ranking and molecular-level taste classification, TasteBench offers the necessary infrastructure to systematically measure and accelerate progress in computational screening for plant-based food design.

Abstract: Sustainable protein discovery lacks the fast computational proxies, analogous to molecular docking or density functional theory, that accelerate drug and materials discovery. Evaluating whether a novel food tastes like its animal-based target requires expensive human sensory panels, bottlenecking the design-build-test loop. We introduce TasteBench, a multimodal benchmark and privacy-preserving competition for sensory prediction, spanning two tasks: a food-level ranking task built on 21K+ human evaluations across 215 plant-based foods in 24 product categories, yielding 935 within-category ranking pairs, and a supporting molecular-level taste classification task over 15K flavor molecules. To enable rigorous interpretation of model performance, we characterize the ground truth: inter-rater agreement among panelists is low (Krippendorff's $α= .077$), and the split-half reliability ceiling of panel-aggregated rankings is .825, establishing the range within which ML systems on this benchmark should be assessed. We evaluate baselines across four input modalities; on the same pairs panelists rated, the best model achieves .661 pairwise accuracy, competitive with the median individual panelist (.650), and .683 across all within-category pairs. TasteBench provides the evaluation infrastructure and baselines for measuring progress on computational screening for sustainable protein discovery.

</details>


### [94] [Time Series Forecasting Benchmarks Need Scenario-Grounded Stress Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02608)
*Yuyang Zhao,Lian Xu,Hao Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper argues that current time series forecasting benchmarks are too narrow and proposes a scenario-grounded stress-testing framework that evaluates models under realistic, structured failure conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series forecasts are used in critical domains, but evaluation focuses almost exclusively on average predictive accuracy under simplistic assumptions (e.g., Gaussian noise, random masking, or small adversarial perturbations). This misses real-world failure modes such as regime shifts, broken dependencies, sensor faults, and structured events that change system dynamics, especially problematic with large TSF foundation models trained on opaque data.

Method: The authors conceptually propose a new evaluation paradigm called scenario-grounded stress testing. Each test case should bundle: (1) standard historical inputs and future targets, (2) a semantic scenario describing the real-world situation, (3) an explicit failure operator that defines how and why the data or system is perturbed, and (4) a measurable difficulty level. This structure aims to simulate realistic, interpretable stress conditions rather than i.i.d. noise.

Result: As this is an abstract and likely a position or methodology paper, it does not report empirical numerical results; instead, it presents an argument and framework that reveal how current TSF benchmarks and robustness tests fail to capture important semantic, causal, and system-level failures, and shows that the proposed structured scenarios yield more informative evaluations of when and why models fail.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that TSF evaluation must move beyond i.i.d. perturbation-based robustness and pure held-out error, toward scenario-grounded stress testing. This makes evaluations more interpretable, attributable, and aligned with deployment needs, allowing practitioners to understand not just which model is most accurate, but under which conditions it fails and the causes of those failures.

Abstract: Time series forecasting (TSF) increasingly drives decisions in transportation, energy, finance, healthcare, and infrastructure, yet current evaluation remains overly narrow: standard benchmarks reward low held-out error, while robustness studies typically reduce failure to Gaussian noise, random masking, or bounded adversarial perturbations. This obscures the real failure modes of deployed forecasting systems. Input-side anomalies are not merely noisier inputs: they often reflect structured events that alter temporal dynamics, break cross-variable dependencies, induce regime shifts, or propagate from faulty sensors to downstream decisions. These semantic, causal, and system-level failures cannot be faithfully captured by i.i.d. perturbations alone. The rise of TSF foundation models makes this evaluation gap more urgent, as unauditable pretraining corpora make held-out generalization increasingly unreliable. We therefore advocate scenario-grounded stress testing. Each test instance should include historical inputs and future targets, together with a semantic scenario, an explicit failure operator, and a measurable difficulty level. This shift makes evaluation interpretable, attributable, and deployment-relevant and friendly, enabling the community to ask not only which model is accurate, but under what conditions it fails and why.

</details>


### [95] [VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02616)
*Zekai Wang,Yingqiang Ge,Zekun Wang,Hai Wang,Yuhui Xu,Joshua Frandsen,Shancong Fu,Ashia C. Wilson,Chandan K. Reddy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces VERSE, a verified self-evolving optimizer that not only improves an LLM agent’s prompts, tools, and workflow but also iteratively improves its own optimization process using execution-based verification, leading to better performance on SWE-rebench and out-of-distribution coding tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent optimizers typically focus on evolving the agent’s harness (prompts, tools, workflow) while keeping the optimizer’s own tools and procedures fixed. Preliminary experiments show that allowing the optimizer to self-evolve can fail without reliable verification but can yield strong gains when execution-based verification is present. This motivates designing an optimizer that can systematically improve both the target agent and its own optimization mechanisms under verified feedback, to address complex software engineering benchmarks and generalize across languages and tasks.

Method: The authors study optimizer self-evolution across multiple executors and observe how optimizers autonomously construct tools for failure analysis, verification, audits, and workflow control. Building on these insights, they propose VERSE, a Verified Self-Evolving optimizer. VERSE provides facilities to test draft edits, replay failures, and perturb suspected steps before committing changes, while tracking fixes and regressions over optimization rounds. Using this structured feedback loop, VERSE jointly revises the executor harness (prompts, tools, hooks, workflow) and its own prompts, skills, tools, and notes, with model weights kept fixed. They evaluate VERSE under a shared protocol with disjoint train/validation/test tasks on SWE-rebench and additional out-of-distribution tasks across five programming languages, comparing against four baseline harness optimizers.

Result: Optimizer self-evolution without execution-based verification does not improve performance, but with verification it can achieve the strongest results in controlled studies. VERSE, which incorporates verified self-evolution, consistently improves all four evaluated harness optimizers on held-out SWE-rebench tasks and on newer out-of-distribution tasks in five languages. The best VERSE-selected harness achieves 42.3% and 37.7% accuracy on two evaluation settings, compared to 39.2% and 29.3% for the strongest baselines, demonstrating significant gains in code-generation task success rates without changing model weights.

Conclusion: Verified self-evolution—where the optimizer improves both the agent harness and its own optimization process using execution-based verification—can significantly enhance LLM agent performance. VERSE operationalizes this idea by enabling systematic testing, failure replay, and regression tracking, leading to better harnesses and optimization behaviors while keeping model weights fixed. The empirical gains over strong baselines on SWE-rebench and diverse multilingual tasks suggest that future LLM agent systems should treat the optimizer’s own tools and workflows as first-class objects for iterative improvement, provided robust verification mechanisms are available.

Abstract: Harness evolution improves an LLM agent's prompts, tools, and workflow, while the optimizer's own tools and procedures often remain fixed. We study whether an optimizer can improve another agent more effectively by also improving how it diagnoses failures, develops edits, and tests their effects. Two observations guide our design. In a controlled study, optimizer self-evolution fails to improve performance without execution-based verification, but achieves the best result of that study when verification is available. Across five executors, self-evolving optimizers build their own tools for failure analysis, verification, training audits, and workflow control. Motivated by these findings, we introduce VERSE, a Verified Self-Evolving optimizer for agent harnesses. VERSE lets the optimizer test draft edits, replay failures, and perturb suspected steps before submission, while tracking fixes and regressions across rounds. Using this feedback, the optimizer revises both the executor harness and its own prompts, skills, tools, hooks, and notes, while the weights of the optimizer and executor models stay fixed. Under a shared protocol with disjoint training, validation, and test tasks, VERSE improves all four evaluated harness optimizers on held-out SWE-rebench tasks and newer out-of-distribution tasks in five languages. Its best validation-selected harness reaches 42.3% and 37.7% accuracy, respectively, against 39.2% and 29.3% for the strongest baselines. Code is available at https://github.com/wzekai/VERSE.

</details>


### [96] [CuBEs: Culturally-Situated Behavioral Evaluations and the Limitations of Culture-Blind LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02622)
*Hoda Ayad,Tanu Mitra,Abhishek Mukherji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CuBEs, a framework for culturally-situated behavioral evaluations of LLMs, showing that model behaviors and biases vary significantly across cultural contexts and that standard, culture-agnostic tests miss important risks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing behavioral evaluations of LLMs examine issues like sycophancy, self-preference, and over-confidence but largely ignore the user’s cultural context, even though LLMs are deployed globally. This lack of cultural situatedness makes current evaluations less generalizable and may hide important risks or biases that only appear in specific cultural settings.

Method: The authors design CuBEs (Culturally-situated Behavior Evaluations), an automated evaluation pipeline that explicitly injects cultural context into behavioral test scenarios and into the evaluation process. They then construct a human-labeled dataset spanning 12 distinct cultures to capture nuanced interpretations of model behaviors. Using this setup, they run comparative evaluations of 13 open- and closed-source LLMs under culturally varied scenarios and measure how often certain behaviors (e.g., bias, sycophancy) occur and how they differ across cultures.

Result: The dataset shows substantial cross-cultural variation in how behaviors manifest and are understood, and it reveals that uniform, culture-agnostic judgments miss these nuances. When applying CuBEs to 13 LLMs, the authors observe that adding cultural context to scenarios significantly changes the presence and form of behaviors. For instance, political bias evaluations that in Western settings mostly reflect axes like the American conservative–progressive divide instead surface completely different salient dimensions in non-Western contexts, such as religious or colonial issues.

Conclusion: Culturally-agnostic behavioral evaluations are insufficient for assessing LLM risks in global deployments because they miss important culture-specific behaviors and biases. Culturally-situated evaluations like CuBEs are necessary to reveal these differences, to more accurately characterize model behavior across diverse user groups, and to inform safer, more equitable deployment of LLMs worldwide.

Abstract: Evaluating the occurrence and triggers of large language model (LLM) behaviors - such as sycophancy, self-preference, or over-confidence - is critical for predicting real-world model deployment risks. However, existing situated behavioral evaluations typically ignore cultural context, limiting their generalizability across an increasingly global user base. To address this gap, we propose CuBEs - Culturally-situated Behavior Evaluations that probe for response patterns across diverse user cultures. We first extend an automated testing pipeline to inject cultural context into behavioral test scenarios and subsequent evaluation. We assess the cultural adaptability of this pipeline by building a human-labeled dataset that captures nuanced dimensions of behavior understanding across 12 distinct cultures. Our dataset reveals significant cross-cultural variations that one-size-fits all judgments fail to capture. Through evaluating 13 open- and closed-source LLMs, we find that introducing cultural situatedness in the evaluation scenario creates significant variation in the presence of a behavior. For example, while our baseline experiments testing for political bias capture localized Western political dimensions like the American conservative-progressive divide, non-Western culturally situated evaluations surface entirely different axes of bias such as religious and colonial political issues. Our findings demonstrate that standard, culturally-agnostic evaluations fail to capture these shifts, highlighting the necessity of culturally situated behavioral testing for global deployments.

</details>


### [97] [Lost in the Request: How Communication Variation Disrupts Retrieval and Action in Email Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02627)
*Feng Chen,Ritam Dutt,Atnaz Taheri,Alex Williams*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how robust email AI assistants are to differences in how users phrase the same request, showing that stylistic and dialect changes can significantly degrade performance even when the underlying task is identical.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks usually test each task with a single, canonical phrasing, so they largely ignore whether systems do equal work when users phrase the same request in different styles or dialects. This is problematic for real-world deployment, where users naturally vary in communication style and language variety. The authors want to systematically measure this form of robustness for email assistants.

Method: The authors construct validated paraphrase variants of user email requests along five communication-style dimensions (including indirectness, formality, verbosity, etc.) and four rule-based English dialect conditions. They then evaluate three systems: (1) a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline using a lexical email retriever, and (2) two different tool-using agent setups. For each variant, they hold constant the requested information, available evidence, and expected outcome, and measure how performance changes solely due to request style or dialect. They further analyze where in the pipeline the failures arise—retrieval vs. reasoning vs. action selection.

Result: Indirect requests reduce performance across all three benchmarks; formal requests reduce performance on the two agentic benchmarks; and verbose requests particularly harm the lexical retriever by making the relevant email harder to find. Indirect and dialectal variants still degrade performance even when the correct email is successfully retrieved. In the agentic setups, indirect and formal styles primarily lead agents to omit necessary actions rather than generate more hallucinated or unsupported actions.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that current email assistants are not robust to stylistic and dialectal variation in user requests, even when the underlying task and available evidence are unchanged. A single successful response to a canonical phrasing is therefore insufficient to claim robustness. Evaluations should explicitly vary how requests are expressed and should separately measure whether agents actually complete the requested work, not just whether one formulation is handled correctly.

Abstract: An email assistant should not complete less work simply because a user phrases the same request differently. Yet most benchmarks test each task with only one canonical request, leaving this form of robustness largely unmeasured. We test whether email assistants remain reliable when the requested information, available evidence, and expected outcome stay fixed, but the communication style or English variety changes. We construct validated variants along five communication-style axes and four rule-based dialect conditions, and evaluate them on three benchmarks: a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline and two tool-using agents. Indirect requests reduce performance on all three benchmarks, while formal requests reduce performance on both agentic benchmarks. Examining the systems more closely shows that these failures have different causes. Verbose requests mainly hurt a lexical retriever by making the relevant email harder to find. By contrast, indirect and dialect variants remain harmful even when the relevant email is retrieved. In the agentic setting, indirect and formal requests mainly cause the agents to omit required actions, not to take more unsupported actions. These results show that a successful response is not enough to establish robustness: evaluations should vary how requests are expressed and separately measure whether agents complete the requested work.

</details>


### [98] [Designing the Future of User Feedback for Generative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02631)
*Alisa Frik,Julia Bernd,Amitis Karami,Mohammad Tahaei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to design good post-deployment user feedback mechanisms for generative AI systems, benchmarking current industry practices, identifying problems, and proposing and testing a better feedback tool.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-deployment user feedback is crucial for monitoring and improving generative AI systems in a scalable, cost-effective way, and is increasingly required by regulations and guidelines. However, there is little concrete guidance on how to design feedback mechanisms that are both usable for everyday users and provide actionable, structured data for product teams. This gap between regulatory/industry expectations and practical design knowledge motivates the work.

Method: The authors conducted a multi-phase, collaborative study between academic researchers and eBay. First, they performed a benchmark evaluation of existing industry feedback mechanisms for GenAI features, analyzing discoverability, terminology, user value, and other UX aspects. Using these insights, they derived best-practice design recommendations. They then designed a prototype feedback-collection tool embodying these recommendations, and empirically tested it to evaluate both user experience (efficiency, flexibility, positivity of feedback process) and usefulness for product teams (richness and usability of the collected data).

Result: The benchmark revealed common shortcomings in current industry practices, including poor discoverability of feedback channels, confusing or unclear terminology, and little attention to making feedback valuable and engaging for users. The newly designed prototype tool, grounded in the derived best practices, was able to offer an efficient and flexible feedback experience for users while producing richer, more structured performance and problem data that is easier for product teams to use. (Details such as quantitative improvements are not reported in the abstract.)

Conclusion: Effective post-deployment feedback mechanisms for generative AI systems require deliberate UX and data-design choices, not just generic comment boxes. By identifying common pitfalls in current implementations and demonstrating a prototype that better serves both users and product teams, the paper provides practical best-practice recommendations. This work supports regulatory and industry aims for ongoing user engagement and offers a more systematic approach to collecting actionable feedback in deployed GenAI products.

Abstract: Post-deployment feedback from users can be a cost-effective, scalable, and representative means to monitor and improve generative AI systems and features. When implemented effectively, giving such feedback can increase users' engagement with and trust in GenAI systems. Government regulations and industry guidelines call for post-deployment user engagement, but there is little guidance on designing mechanisms that are usable for consumers and provide actionable input for product teams. We conducted a multi-phase study as a collaboration between academic researchers and eBay. Our benchmark evaluation of current industry approaches identified common issues including lack of discoverability, unclear terminology, and inattention to user value. Based on these findings, we developed best-practice recommendations and designed and tested a prototype feedback-collection tool. The tool aimed to provide users with an efficient, flexible, and positive feedback-giving experience, and provide product teams with rich data on performance and potential problems in a usable format.

</details>


### [99] [Batched Speech Decisions Without Decoding: Single-Token Supervision Lets a Frozen LLM Hear Beyond the Transcript](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02638)
*Jie Jin,Ziyin Ma,Min Yin,Jinyu Chen,Haigang Song,Zhikun Pang,Xiaowen Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DuplexJev is a fast, full-duplex spoken-LLM system that directly maps ASR encoder states to LLM decisions without decoding, enabling very low-latency, multi-task voice understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-duplex voice agents must make many small, rapid decisions (e.g., in-dialog choices, user-state inferences) but current systems rely on slow autoregressive decoding of language models. There is a need for a much faster, low-latency approach that can still leverage strong LLMs while also using information in the speech signal (like speaker traits and emotion), which conventional text-only distillation cannot provide.

Method: The authors introduce DuplexJev, which takes hidden states from an ASR encoder and passes them through a small trainable connector module into a frozen LLM. Instead of generating text, the LLM is queried as a classifier: each micro-decision is expressed as an answer token, and the system reads out the single-token probability distribution over the candidate options, avoiding autoregressive decoding. They explore different connector designs (last-layer and cross-attention connectors) and train with cross-entropy on the correct answer token, while keeping standard distillation for content-only tasks. The architecture is modular, allowing different ASR encoders and LLMs to be swapped in. They also implement a batched inference pipeline for efficient, full-duplex serving and construct a bilingual spoken-QA dataset for evaluation.

Result: On spoken QA, a simple last-layer connector achieves performance close to transcript-based QA (90% vs 91%), demonstrating that working directly from speech encoder states nearly matches text. A cross-attention connector, which better integrates speech information, significantly improves paralinguistic tasks: gender and emotion recognition accuracy rise to 90% from 55% and 28%, respectively. This richer connector causes only a minor drop (1 point) in spoken QA performance (83% to 82%). An 8-GPU node can process around 80 decisions across eight utterances in about 0.1 seconds, confirming high throughput and low latency.

Conclusion: DuplexJev shows that small connector modules can effectively bridge ASR encoders and frozen LLMs, enabling fast, decoding-free, full-duplex spoken agents that jointly reason over content and vocal traits. Single-token answer training is an efficient alternative to standard transcript distillation, and the modular design allows mixing and matching encoders and LLMs. The released weights, training recipe, serving pipeline, and bilingual spoken-QA data aim to support further research and deployment of low-latency, multi-task voice agents.

Abstract: Full-duplex voice agents make many small, closed decisions, which current systems answer by slow autoregressive decoding. We propose DuplexJev, which feeds ASR-encoder hidden states through a small connector into a frozen LLM and reads each question as a single-token distribution over its options. Nothing is decoded, and an 8-GPU node answers 80 decisions about eight utterances in about 0.1 s. With a last-layer connector, spoken QA stays close to reading the transcript (90% vs. 91%). DuplexJev also hears the speaker: gender and emotion accuracy both reach 90% (from 55% and 28%) with a cross-attention connector, whose spoken QA drops by only 1 point (83% to 82%). We train decisions with cross-entropy on the read-out answer token, instead of the usual transcript distillation, whose teacher never hears the voice, and keep distillation for content. Encoders and LLMs are interchangeable; we release weights, training recipe, a batched-inference pipeline for full-duplex serving and a bilingual spoken-QA set.

</details>


### [100] [Coherence-Driven Belief Formation and Population Dynamics of Contagion in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02654)
*Tathagata Banerjee,Nima Moghaddas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically measures how language-model-based agents adopt beliefs from peers and shows their behavior follows complex contagion dynamics, with belief adoption depending on a coherence-like dimension that captures claim plausibility, source reliability, and agent disposition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most models of social contagion assume how individuals adopt beliefs and then derive population-level behavior theoretically, but lack direct empirical measurement for AI agents. With the rise of LLM-based agents interacting socially, there is a need to quantify how such agents adopt beliefs from peers and whether their dynamics resemble known complex contagion patterns from human social systems.

Method: The authors set up populations of language model agents that exchange claims along network structures. They empirically measure the probability that an agent adopts a claim as a function of how many peers endorse it, varying claim plausibility, perceived source reliability, and agent disposition. They fit adoption kernels, analyze their shapes, and study system-level dynamics on different network topologies (e.g., clustered vs. random), examining cascade behavior, contagion windows, and hysteresis in consensus states.

Result: They find that belief adoption in LLM agents follows a sigmoidal adoption kernel, characteristic of complex contagion, rather than simple contagion. The adoption threshold shifts systematically with claim plausibility, source reliability, and agent disposition, and these three factors can be effectively captured by a single latent dimension interpreted as the coherence of the new belief with the agent’s prior beliefs. At the collective level, they observe greater spread on clustered than on random networks, a bifurcating cascade window, and hysteretic, self-sustaining consensus dynamics.

Conclusion: LLM agents exhibit complex-contagion-like belief adoption both at the individual and collective level. A single coherence-like dimension explains how plausibility, source reliability, and prior disposition jointly shape adoption thresholds. Networked populations of such agents can lock into self-sustaining consensus states that are much harder to overturn than to create, highlighting important implications for the stability and manipulability of beliefs in multi-agent AI systems.

Abstract: Models of social contagion usually assume how individuals adopt beliefs and derive population behavior from it. We instead empirically measure belief adoption in language model agents, quantifying the probability an agent adopts a claim given how many peers endorse it. We find this adoption kernel to be sigmoid, a characteristic of complex contagion, with a threshold that is sensitive to three sources: the claim's plausibility, the source's reliability, and the agent's disposition. These three dimensions are well approximated by a single effective dimension which we propose can be understood as the coherence of the incoming belief with the LLM agent's prior beliefs. Further, we observe a characteristic of complex contagion in the collective dynamics of belief adoption in a system of AI agents: further spread on clustered than random networks. These systems also exhibit a bifurcating cascade window, and self-sustaining hysteretic consensus which lead to consensus being far harder to remove than to establish.

</details>


### [101] [A GHOST in Long-Horizon Agents: Governance Hazard from Overlooked Safety Constraints across Turns](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02664)
*XinPeng Shen,Lan Zhang,Yixiao Huang,Haoran Cheng,Jiewei Lai,Leilei Chen,Haoxiang Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies and analyzes a new safety failure mode for long-horizon AI agents, where agents forget or overlook safety constraints stated many turns earlier and thus perform unsafe actions; it then proposes a two-layer defense (STAR-Guard) that largely eliminates this issue in experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI agents assist humans over long, multi-step interactions, they accumulate long histories of instructions and constraints. Existing safety work largely focuses on single-turn prompts or adversarial settings, while overlooking that even under benign, cooperative interactions, an agent can forget or ignore safety constraints given many turns earlier, thereby causing potentially irreversible harm. The authors are motivated to characterize and measure this long-horizon safety risk, understand its theoretical inevitability under certain conditions, and design a principled defense mechanism that can be practically deployed.

Method: The authors: (1) define a new failure mode, GHOST (Governance Hazard from Overlooked Safety Constraints across Turns), where an agent violates earlier safety constraints under non-adversarial interactions; (2) empirically evaluate the occurrence of GHOST events for a long-horizon agent based on GPT-5.5, finding an 11.5% rate under their benchmark; (3) provide a theoretical analysis showing that when the residual conditional probability of violating constraints along each safe interaction prefix is bounded below by a non-summable sequence, the process will almost surely eventually enter a hazard region; and (4) propose STAR-Guard, a two-layer defense combining (i) historical semantic restoration of safety constraints to surface applicable constraints back to the agent at decision time, and (ii) a deterministic pre-execution audit that filters remaining unsafe actions before they are executed in the environment.

Result: Empirically, the authors observe that GHOST failures occur with nontrivial frequency (11.5%) in GPT-5.5-based long-horizon agents, even though the interactions are benign and not adversarially crafted. Their theoretical result shows that if the per-step residual violation hazard does not decrease fast enough (i.e., its lower bound forms a non-summable sequence), the agent will almost surely eventually enter a hazardous state. When they deploy STAR-Guard—combining constraint restoration with deterministic action auditing—they report that GHOST events no longer occur in their GPT-5.5 experimental setup.

Conclusion: Long-horizon AI agents face a distinct and underexplored safety challenge: they can overlook or forget previously stated safety constraints and thereby execute dangerous actions, even in benign multi-turn interactions. This GHOST failure mode is empirically significant and theoretically hard to avoid if residual per-step hazard persists. However, a structured, two-layer defense like STAR-Guard—restoring historical safety constraints and enforcing deterministic pre-execution audits—can effectively eliminate such failures in tested settings, suggesting a practical path toward safer long-horizon agent deployment.

Abstract: Long-horizon agents are now playing an increasingly significant role in assisting humans with complex problem-solving. However, it is exactly their extended interaction history that introduces an underexplored execution-safety concern. Under benign interaction conditions, an agent may execute an action that violates a safety constraint specified many turns earlier. We term this failure mode Governance Hazard from Overlooked Safety Constraints across Turns (GHOST), which may cause irreversible damage. Our experiments reveal that GHOST events are not isolated cases: this failure mode, occurring precisely under benign interaction conditions, yields an occurrence rate of 11.5% on GPT-5.5. Furthermore, we theoretically show that if the residual conditional violation hazard along each safe prefix is bounded below by a non-summable sequence, the execution enters the hazard region almost surely. Leveraging this theoretical insight, we further propose STAR-Guard, a two-layer defense coupling historical semantic safety constraint restoration with pre-execution audit. STAR-Guard restores applicable safety constraints to reduce unsafe proposals, while its deterministic audit layer prevents residual violations from reaching the environment. Consistent with this two-layer design, we observe no GHOST events in our experiments under the GPT-5.5 setup.

</details>


### [102] [Spend Teacher Tokens Where They Matter: Success-Referenced On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02678)
*Xiang Chen,Futao Su,Kong Wang,Jiayi Chen,TanLin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a cheaper way to do on‑policy distillation by only asking the teacher to supervise carefully chosen student rollouts, while keeping almost the same reasoning performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard on‑policy distillation requires dense, token‑level teacher supervision on every student rollout, which is computationally expensive because it consumes many teacher input tokens. The authors want to reduce this teacher cost while preserving the benefits of on‑policy distillation for reasoning tasks.

Method: They introduce Success-Referenced On-Policy Distillation (SR-OPD), which selectively allocates teacher supervision. For each prompt, they identify cases where the student generates both successful and failed rollouts. The successful rollout becomes a reference trajectory in hidden-state space. They then prioritize failed rollouts whose hidden states diverge strongly and persistently from that successful reference, while incorporating an estimate of teacher-input cost into the selection. Teacher token budget is thus concentrated on prompts with mixed outcomes and on the most informative failures relative to a successful example.

Result: Across three different teacher–student model pairs and six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, SR-OPD achieves nearly the same reasoning performance as vanilla OPD but requires only about 3.46–5.02% of the teacher input tokens in a single-pass training setting. In additional controlled experiments, when both methods are constrained to the same 5% teacher-token budget, SR-OPD’s design choices—focusing on prompts with both successful and failed rollouts and using successful rollouts to guide which failures to supervise—are empirically validated as beneficial.

Conclusion: The student’s own successful rollouts can act as effective references for deciding where to spend limited teacher supervision. By exploiting this, SR-OPD dramatically reduces teacher-token usage compared with standard on-policy distillation while maintaining comparable reasoning performance, showing that careful supervision allocation is a powerful lever for efficient distillation.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) combines student-generated rollouts with dense token-level supervision from a teacher, but providing such supervision for every rollout requires substantial teacher computation. We introduce Success-Referenced On-Policy Distillation (SR-OPD), which reduces this cost by selecting which prompts and rollouts receive teacher supervision. When the student produces both successful and failed rollouts for the same prompt, a successful rollout can serve as a natural reference for selecting failed rollouts. SR-OPD therefore focuses on such prompts and prioritizes failed rollouts whose hidden-state trajectories show sustained divergence from a successful reference, while accounting for estimated teacher-input cost. Across three teacher-student pairs and six mathematical reasoning benchmarks, SR-OPD uses only 3.46-5.02% of the teacher-input tokens required by Vanilla OPD in the one-pass setting while maintaining comparable reasoning performance. Under a controlled setting matched to 5% of Vanilla OPD's teacher-input budget, further experiments support both key design choices: focusing supervision on prompts with both successful and failed rollouts, and using successful rollouts to guide failure selection. These results indicate that a student's own successful behavior can serve as a useful reference for allocating teacher supervision under a fixed teacher-input budget.

</details>


### [103] [DataWeave: Deploying Human-LLM Analytics for Exploratory Structured Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02679)
*Raquib Bin Yousuf,Harith Laxman,Vitaliy Shkremetko,Eunice Son,Shambhavi Verma,Brian O'Leary,Venketesh Subramony,Sylvain Nazef,Jacquelyn Elias,Ron Coddington,Chris Contakes,Michael Riley,Naren Ramakrishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces DataWeave, a system that uses LLMs as interactive partners to help journalists explore large, complex structured datasets more reliably and efficiently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data journalists need to quickly uncover trends and accountability stories from large, complex public datasets, but current workflows are slow, fragile, and demand substantial technical and domain expertise. Existing "natural language to SQL" LLM approaches fail in realistic newsroom settings due to schema drift, misunderstood domain semantics, and hidden assumptions, creating risks for high‑stakes public-interest reporting.

Method: The authors design and implement DataWeave, a system that integrates conversational interaction with schema grounding, explicit analytical planning, and generation of executable queries. LLMs are positioned as collaborative agents whose intermediate outputs can be inspected and corrected rather than as autonomous answer providers. They conduct a case study with professional journalists using DataWeave on the IPEDS dataset, iteratively refining the architecture and workflow based on deployment experience.

Result: In the case study, professional journalists were able to conduct exploratory analyses of the IPEDS dataset more effectively, with the system helping surface and correct schema mismatches, domain-meaning errors, and other common LLM failure modes. Iterative deployment led to an evolved architecture that better supports real newsroom analytical practices.

Conclusion: Treating LLMs as inspectable, steerable collaborators—supported by schema grounding and explicit analytical planning—can make exploratory analysis of complex structured data more trustworthy and usable for data journalists. The paper extracts general design principles and deployment lessons for building human‑LLM systems that fit high‑stakes, real‑world data analysis workflows.

Abstract: Data journalism, the practice of using data analysis to surface newsworthy stories, depends increasingly on the ability of reporters and investigative journalists to uncover trends, disparities, and accountability narratives. In practice, exploring large structured datasets remains slow and brittle: journalists must navigate hundreds of variables across many datasets over years, understand data coding conventions, and write non-trivial analysis code while hypotheses evolve. Although LLMs are often touted as "ask in English, get SQL/answers," real newsroom workflows expose recurring failures, e.g., schema mismatches and drift, misread domain semantics and units, and silent assumptions. We present DataWeave, a system that addresses these needs by combining conversational interaction, schema grounding, analytical planning, and executable query generation to support exploratory analysis over structured data. Rather than treating LLMs as autonomous answer engines, DataWeave frames them as interactive partners whose outputs can be inspected, corrected, and steered as hypotheses shift. We present a case study with professional journalists using our system to analyze the U.S. Department of Education's Integrated Postsecondary Education Data System (IPEDS), a high-stakes public dataset with substantial domain semantics and frequent schema updates. We also report how deployment experience and iterative refinement shaped the current DataWeave architecture and its analytical workflow. Our findings distill design principles and deployment lessons for trustworthy human-LLM collaboration in structured data analysis.

</details>


### [104] [Large language models exhibit unreliable updating of clinical judgment as patient evidence evolves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02684)
*Min Zeng,Rui Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tests how well large language models update their clinical risk estimates over time as patient data change and shows that they often update unreliably, with systematic biases and pathologies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are being considered for clinical reasoning tasks, which are inherently longitudinal: clinicians repeatedly revise risk estimates as new patient data arrive. While many benchmarks test single-timepoint performance, little is known about whether LLMs can appropriately revise prior judgments when evidence changes over time. This paper aims to fill that gap by rigorously evaluating longitudinal belief updating and identifying failure modes that might undermine safe clinical deployment.

Method: The authors use matched intensive-care trajectories from electronic health records as longitudinal test cases. At each timepoint, LLMs are asked to estimate clinical risk endpoints and are either given or not given their preceding judgments. They compare prediction errors with and without conditioning on prior assessments across diverse LLMs and for at least two endpoints. They perform controlled interventions: (1) holding prior judgment fixed while varying current respiratory evidence to compare responses to worsening vs improving states, and (2) holding current evidence fixed while experimentally manipulating the prior risk given to the model. They also test prompt-based mitigation strategies and propose an Evidence-Validated Longitudinal Update (EVLU) procedure that only counts revisions supported by evidence and quantifies a reliability-coverage trade-off.

Result: Conditioning on a model’s own preceding judgment generally worsened performance when the risk estimate changed, rather than improving it, and this pattern held across models and endpoints. Intervention (1) showed an asymmetry: models reacted more strongly to worsening than to equivalently improving respiratory evidence, and this bias persisted even after normalizing for remaining performance headroom, especially at medium and high evidence strengths. Intervention (2) showed that changing the stated prior risk from 10% to 90% shifted the model’s risk estimates by an average of 26.2 percentage points despite identical current evidence, confirming that prior beliefs causally influence current predictions. Prompting strategies failed to restore consistently reliable updating. The proposed EVLU framework reduced the number of accepted revisions but the remaining ones were more trustworthy, demonstrating a trade-off between how often models revise and how reliable those revisions are.

Conclusion: Longitudinal belief updating—how a model revises its risk estimates as evidence accumulates—is a distinct and critical aspect of LLM reliability that is not captured by static benchmarks. Current LLMs display systematic updating failures, including asymmetric sensitivity to negative vs positive evidence and undue anchoring on prior beliefs, and these are not easily fixed with simple prompting. The EVLU approach offers a way to filter for more trustworthy updates at the cost of coverage. Overall, the work warns against uncritical use of LLMs for evolving clinical decisions and motivates new evaluation and design efforts focused specifically on temporal belief revision.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly explored for clinical reasoning, but whether they appropriately revise judgments as patient evidence evolves remains unclear. We evaluated longitudinal belief updating using matched intensive-care trajectories from electronic health records. Across diverse LLMs, conditioning on a preceding judgment more often increased than reduced prediction error when estimates changed, replicated for a second endpoint. Controlled interventions revealed two failure modes. First, with preceding assessment fixed, models responded more strongly to worsening than matched improving respiratory evidence; this asymmetry persisted after headroom normalization at moderate and strong evidence levels. Second, with current evidence fixed, increasing prior risk from 10% to 90% shifted estimates by 26.2 percentage points, demonstrating causal influence of prior model beliefs. Prompting did not restore reliable updating. Evidence-Validated Longitudinal Update (EVLU) identified fewer, more reliable revisions, revealing a reliability-coverage trade-off. These findings establish longitudinal belief updating as a distinct dimension of LLM reliability.

</details>


### [105] [Decoupling Memory from Context: Structured Memory for Token-Efficient Test-Time Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02687)
*Yehya Farhat,Michael Desmond,Anastasios Kyrillidis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes GraphMemory, a graph-based memory system for large language model agents that optimizes and retrieves only relevant context, achieving similar performance to baselines while using far fewer tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs used in enterprise, science, and medicine must integrate domain-specific knowledge and adapt from experience without expensive weight updates. Existing context engineering and memory methods either require manual trial-and-error or naively grow the context, leading to high token costs, context-window limits, and degraded performance. The paper aims to provide a principled, efficient way to update and use memory over time.

Method: The authors first present a unified formulation of context optimization, interpreting agent memory updates as optimization steps over the model’s context. Based on this view, they design GraphMemory, a lightweight graph-based memory where nodes encode reusable strategies or pieces of knowledge and edges capture relations among them. As the agent interacts, the graph accumulates, refines, and connects strategies. For each new query, GraphMemory retrieves only a bounded, relevant subgraph to construct the context, enabling online adaptation while keeping retrieval size constant as the interaction history grows.

Result: In experiments, GraphMemory matches or closely approaches the downstream task performance of stronger baseline memory systems while using substantially fewer tokens for memory construction—approximately 81–85% fewer than the baselines. The bounded retrieval mechanism prevents context from growing with the number of interactions, preserving efficiency.

Conclusion: Viewing memory updates as context optimization provides a framework for principled memory design in LLM agents. Within this framework, GraphMemory offers an efficient, scalable memory system that organizes knowledge as a graph and retrieves only relevant subgraphs, allowing continual online adaptation without unbounded context growth and with significantly reduced token costs compared to existing approaches.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in enterprise, scientific, and medical applications, where agents must incorporate domain-specific knowledge and adapt from experience. Context engineering offers a practical alternative to weight updates by improving model behavior through instructions, strategies, and evidence supplied at inference time. However, adapting context online typically requires a costly trial-and-error process, while queries are often processed independently, preventing useful experience from carrying forward. Memory systems address this limitation by retaining information across interactions, but approaches that continually append information to a shared context face increasing token costs, context-window limits, and performance degradation as the context expands. We introduce a unified formulation of context optimization and show that an agent memory system update can be interpreted as an optimization update procedure over the model's context. This perspective attempts to provide a principled framework for studying memory design and its efficiency. We then propose GraphMemory, a lightweight graph-based memory that accumulates, refines, organizes, and connects reusable strategies. For each query, GraphMemory retrieves only the relevant subgraph, enabling online context adaptation without exposing the model to the entire memory. Under bounded retrieval, the amount of retrieved memory remains constant as the number of processed examples grows. Experiments show that GraphMemory achieves competitive downstream performance while using approximately 81-85% fewer memory-construction tokens than our baselines.

</details>


### [106] [Learning to Revise Reasoning with Segment-wise On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02703)
*Yuxiang Zhang,Ding Cao,Shuting Cui,Lei Wang,Weijieying Ren,Tianxiang Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes Segment-wise On-Policy Distillation (Seg-OPD), a method that improves large language model reasoning by teaching models to revise intermediate reasoning segments using teacher redrafts, leading to better downstream reasoning performance than standard token-wise on-policy distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional on-policy distillation supervises a student model token-by-token on its own rollouts, but it does not explicitly teach how to revise flawed intermediate reasoning steps. When the student generates a bad reasoning prefix, the teacher’s token-wise supervision conditioned on that prefix can entrench poor reasoning patterns instead of correcting them. The authors are motivated to create a distillation scheme that explicitly supports revising and repairing intermediate reasoning segments to improve subsequent reasoning quality.

Method: The authors introduce Segment-wise On-Policy Distillation (Seg-OPD). The procedure: (1) run the student model on tasks to obtain its reasoning trace; (2) segment this reasoning and select specific student segments to revise using an uncertainty-based selection metric; (3) have a teacher model generate ‘redrafts’ for these selected segments—coherent alternative reasoning chunks designed to improve subsequent reasoning; (4) train the student with a preference-style objective to favor teacher redrafts over their original segments, while also retaining dense token-wise supervision from standard OPD. They also perform controlled interventions where selected student segments are replaced with teacher redrafts to verify that these edits causally improve downstream reasoning accuracy.

Result: In controlled experiments, replacing student reasoning segments with teacher redrafts is shown to reliably increase the accuracy of the remaining reasoning and final answers. Models trained with Seg-OPD achieve higher rates of successful reasoning revisions than baselines. Across mathematical reasoning and competitive programming benchmarks, Seg-OPD yields consistent gains in reasoning accuracy, with an average relative improvement of 5.22% compared to state-of-the-art baselines, and this holds across multiple model sizes and architectures.

Conclusion: Segment-wise revision-based distillation is more effective than pure token-wise on-policy distillation for teaching reasoning. By learning to prefer teacher redrafted segments and explicitly training revision behavior, large language models become better at repairing intermediate reasoning and achieve higher final task accuracy. Seg-OPD offers a general framework for integrating targeted reasoning edits into the distillation process and can be applied broadly to reasoning-intensive domains such as math and programming.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) improves large language model reasoning by training students on their own rollouts with dense token-wise supervision from the teacher. However, token-wise OPD does not explicitly provide a coherent alternative reasoning step showing how the student's step could be revised to improve subsequent reasoning. Furthermore, this paradigm can become less effective when the student produces a degenerate reasoning prefix, as subsequent teacher supervision remains conditioned on that prefix and may reinforce poor reasoning patterns. In this work, we focus on learning reasoning revision with segment-wise OPD to rework intermediate reasoning steps and better support subsequent reasoning. Through controlled reasoning interventions, we find that replacing student segments with teacher redrafts improves subsequent reasoning accuracy. Therefore, we address the problem of turning teacher redrafts into explicit supervision for learning to revise reasoning. We propose Segment-wise On-Policy Distillation (Seg-OPD), which selects student segments based on an uncertainty metric and obtains corresponding teacher redrafts. Seg-OPD trains the student to prefer teacher redrafts over their paired student segments while retaining dense token-wise OPD supervision. Extensive experiments on mathematical reasoning and competitive programming tasks show that Seg-OPD-trained students achieve higher revision success rates than baselines. Seg-OPD consistently outperforms the compared state-of-the-art baselines in reasoning accuracy with an average relative improvement of 5.22% across diverse models and tasks. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Seg-OPD.

</details>


### [107] [Label-Efficient Time Series Classification at Scale: A Dual-Stream OSSE-LSTM with Counterfactual Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02704)
*Nguyen Ho,Bach Tung Tran,Trung Ky Nguyen,Zhenchang Xia,Bolong Zheng,Long Van Ho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tackles few-shot time series classification with a dual-stream deep model and a new explainability method, achieving strong and stable accuracy across varying label counts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series are widely generated but costly to label, so the main bottleneck is the number of labeled examples per class rather than raw data volume. Practitioners need to know how few labels can still support a usable classifier, especially in scenarios where the set of classes is known but labels are scarce.

Method: The authors introduce Dual-Stream OSSE-LSTM, an episodic metric-learning framework. One stream is an Omni-Scale CNN with Squeeze-and-Excitation recalibration for multi-scale motif extraction without per-dataset kernel tuning. The other is a Bidirectional LSTM to capture global temporal context. The streams are independently normalized and fused into a prototype-based embedding space. They also propose Counterfactual Integrated Gradients (C-IG), which explains decisions by attributing the margin between target and competing class prototypes, and uses these attribution maps as soft masks to refine prototypes at test time without changing the encoder.

Result: On 19 univariate UCR time-series datasets, OSSE-LSTM achieves the highest average accuracy and the most per-dataset wins for every tested number of labeled examples per class. Its accuracy stays in a narrow band (96.36–96.72%) across support sizes, and even its weakest configuration (93.99%) outperforms the best configuration of all baselines at any label count.

Conclusion: Dual-Stream OSSE-LSTM is an effective few-shot time series classifier that maintains high and stable performance across different label regimes. The combination of multi-scale CNN features, global LSTM context, metric-learning with prototypes, and the C-IG explainability/refinement mechanism provides both strong accuracy and interpretable decisions when only a few labeled examples per class are available.

Abstract: Time series are produced continuously at enormous scale by industrial equipment, wearables, power grids, and clinical monitors, yet annotation remains manual, expensive, and expert-dependent. The binding constraint in large-scale time series analytics is therefore not data volume but label volume, and the question facing a practitioner is concrete: how many examples per class must be labeled before a classifier becomes usable? We study this question directly, in a regime where the label space is fixed and known in advance and the decision rule must be constructed from only K labeled examples per class. We propose Dual-Stream OSSE-LSTM, an episodic metric-learning framework that pairs an Omni-Scale CNN with Squeeze-and-Excitation recalibration, for multi-scale motif extraction without per-dataset kernel tuning, with a Bidirectional LSTM for global temporal context. The two streams are independently normalized and fused into a prototype-oriented embedding. Because decisions taken from a few labels must also be explainable, we introduce Counterfactual Integrated Gradients (C-IG), which attributes the prototype margin between target and opposing classes rather than an isolated classifier logit, and reuses the resulting maps as soft masks for test-time prototype refinement without updating the encoder. On 19 univariate UCR datasets, OSSE-LSTM attains the highest average accuracy and per-dataset win count at every support size, and its accuracy remains within a 0.36-point band (96.36-96.72%) across that range. Its weakest configuration still exceeding the best result any compared baseline achieves at any K (93.99%).

</details>


### [108] [Ego2World: Compiling Egocentric Cooking Videos into Executable Worlds for Belief-State Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02715)
*Qinchuan Cheng,Zhantao Gong,Pengzhan Sun,Angela Yao,Shijie Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Ego2World, a benchmark that converts annotated egocentric cooking videos into executable planning environments for evaluating agents’ actions and planning under partial observability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing egocentric video datasets show human activity but do not provide a way to execute and automatically evaluate an agent’s chosen actions and plans, especially under partial observation and with persistent world state and belief. There is a need for a reusable, controlled testbed to study how planning and memory strategies influence task execution quality and observation demands.

Method: They build a compiler that transforms annotated cooking activities into symbolic, executable planning environments with explicit action rules, persistent world states, agent-belief states, and task conditions. The benchmark supports executing agents’ proposed actions, checking outcomes, and distinguishing between interrupted runs, partial attainment, and completed-but-unsuccessful executions. They evaluate six planners on 105 tasks and conduct a paired study with Qwen-Plus to measure the effect of persistent belief on action validity, visual-query frequency, and completion.

Result: Across six planners and 105 tasks, many accepted operations fail to achieve task goals, and the execution traces clearly categorize outcomes (interrupted, partial, complete without goal). In the Qwen-Plus study, maintaining persistent belief boosts action validity by 4.15 percentage points, cuts visual-query attempts by 90.27%, increases token use, but shows no measurable improvement in task completion.

Conclusion: Ego2World acts as a reusable benchmark and testbed that links egocentric human activity data to the systematic development and evaluation of interactive agents. By separating world state from agent belief, it enables controlled studies of planning and memory choices and their impact on execution quality, observation demands, and task attainment in partially observed environments.

Abstract: Egocentric videos capture how people carry out everyday activities, yet testing an agent requires evaluating the consequences of actions it chooses itself. We introduce Ego2World, a benchmark that turns annotated cooking activities into executable planning environments under partial observation. Its compiler links source steps and objects to symbolic action rules, persistent world states, and explicit task conditions, so researchers can execute an agent's proposed actions and check their outcomes. World state and agent belief are maintained separately, enabling controlled studies of planning and information reuse across continuing tasks. Evaluating six planners on 105 tasks shows that accepted operations often leave task goals unmet. Execution traces and condition checks distinguish interrupted runs, partial attainment, and completed execution without goal attainment. In a separate paired Qwen-Plus study, persistent belief improves action validity by 4.15 percentage points and reduces visual-query attempts by 90.27%, with higher token use and no detected completion gain. Ego2World provides a reusable testbed for tracing how planning and memory choices affect execution, observation demand, and task attainment, connecting recorded human activity to the development and evaluation of interactive agents.

</details>


### [109] [On the Chain-of-Thought Monitorability of Looped Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02741)
*Han Wang,Ishwar B Balappanawar,Huan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well we can monitor chain-of-thought reasoning in looped language models (LoopLMs), finding that increased loop depth sometimes reduces monitorability under stress, but looped architectures are not inherently less monitorable than standard transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought monitoring is important for safety and oversight of language models, but most work assumes standard transformer architectures. Looped language models reuse layers to gain effective depth without increasing parameters, yet it is unknown whether this extra recurrent computation makes their internal reasoning harder to monitor. The paper aims to fill this gap for oversight and safety evaluations.

Method: The authors systematically study CoT monitorability in LoopLMs in two main experimental setups: (1) varying loop depth within a single LoopLM family to isolate the effect of additional recurrent passes through shared layers; and (2) comparing LoopLMs against standard (non-looped) transformers that are carefully matched in terms of parameter count, number of transformer layers, or effective depth. They evaluate on eight MonitorBench tasks in both standard and stress-test regimes, and analyze performance, verification pass rates, token lengths, and qualitative CoT behavior, particularly for Logic/Science/Engineering Cue Answer tasks.

Result: They find task-dependent reductions in chain-of-thought monitorability for deeper-loop models, especially under stress tests on certain Logic/Science/Engineering Cue Answer tasks. Other tasks show weaker or different patterns. These reductions cannot be fully explained by obvious factors like task difficulty, verification pass rate, or length of the generated reasoning. Qualitative inspection indicates that models with more loop depth sometimes change how they use or attribute given cues. In cross-model comparisons, LoopLMs are not consistently less monitorable than non-looped models matched on size or effective depth.

Conclusion: Deeper loop depth can negatively affect chain-of-thought monitorability on some tasks under stress conditions, but this effect is not universal and does not appear to stem simply from the looped architecture itself. Looped transformers, when matched on size or depth to standard models, are not intrinsically less monitorable, suggesting that architecture choice alone is not a decisive factor for CoT monitorability; instead, monitorability depends on specific tasks, stress conditions, and how the model leverages additional recurrent computation.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring provides a promising approach for detecting undesirable model behavior.
