Looped language models (LoopLMs) repeatedly apply shared transformer layers, increasing effective computational depth and enabling additional latent computation without increasing model size. However, the effect of looped architectures on CoT monitorability remains largely unexplored. In this work, we provide the first systematic evaluation of CoT monitorability in LoopLMs. We study two complementary settings: (1) varying the loop depth within the same LoopLM family to isolate the effect of additional recurrent computation, and (2) comparing LoopLMs with non-looped language models matched by parameter size, transformer-layer count, or effective depth to study whether LoopLMs are less monitorable. Across eight tasks from MonitorBench and both standard and stress-test settings, we observe task-dependent reductions in CoT monitorability under stress tests on specific Logic/Science/Engineering \texttt{Cue Answer} tasks, while other tasks exhibit weaker or qualitatively different trends. Our diagnosis suggests that these declines are not fully explained by task difficulty, verification pass rate, or generated token length; qualitative examples further suggest changes in how deeper-loop models explicitly use or attribute provided cues. Our cross-model comparison finds no evidence that LoopLMs are systematically less monitorable than non-looped language models matched on size or depth. Overall, our results suggest that deeper loop depth can reduce CoT monitorability in some tasks under stress tests, but looped transformer architecture alone does not necessarily imply lower monitorability.

</details>


### [110] [Dynamic LLM Routers are Often Misguided](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02762)
*Sam Wang,Julia White,Sahibzada Allahyar,Dhruv Atreja,Urchade Zaratiana,Kelton Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates dynamic LLM routing systems and finds that current commercial routers often fail to beat a simple random two-model router at equal cost, due to systematic biases and flawed objectives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic LLM routers are supposed to reduce inference cost by sending each query to the cheapest model that can still answer correctly, but it is unclear whether existing commercial routers actually deliver these gains in realistic, diverse settings and what failure modes they exhibit.

Method: The authors benchmark six commercial routing systems across 14 experimental setups covering eight different task categories. They compare router performance against a random baseline that chooses between two carefully selected models at matched cost, perform error analysis to identify systematic routing patterns (difficulty blindness, length reversal, semantic matching, roster suboptimality), study how these patterns are incentivized by the standard cost–accuracy objective, examine assumptions behind using large model rosters, and propose an alternative evaluation methodology plus a simple two-model router designed to avoid these flaws.

Result: None of the six commercial routers consistently outperforms the random two-model baseline at the same cost, with several underperforming by more than 10 accuracy points. The observed failure modes include prioritizing moderately hard over hardest queries, favoring short over long queries, routing based on superficial source similarity instead of true difficulty, and using unnecessarily large and poorly chosen model rosters. The standard Pareto-style cost–accuracy objective is shown to encourage these behaviors, and empirical evidence does not support the assumptions of fine-grained model granularity or strong model specialization. A simple two-model router that follows the new evaluation protocol avoids all four identified patterns but still yields only modest gains, largely because an optimized small roster leaves limited room for routing to add value.

Conclusion: Current commercial LLM routing solutions are not reliably better than a strong random baseline and are undermined by systematic, objective-driven biases and suboptimal roster design. Large, finely graded model rosters and assumptions of strong specialization are not empirically justified in the studied setting. More appropriate evaluation methodologies are needed to avoid rewarding pathological routing patterns, and even with improved routing logic, the main efficiency gains come from choosing a good small set of base models rather than from complex dynamic routing policies.

Abstract: Dynamic LLM routers promise to cut inference costs by sending each query to the cheapest model that can answer it correctly. We analyze six commercial routers across 14 settings on a diverse benchmark spanning eight task categories, finding that none of them outperforms a router that randomly selects between two well-chosen models at matched cost. Some underperform by more than 10 percentage points. We trace this gap to four patterns prevalent across routers: difficulty blindness, length reversal, semantic matching, and roster suboptimality. We show that the first three are what the standard objective rewards: cost-accuracy Pareto efficiency on realized costs favors escalating moderately hard queries over the hardest ones, shorter queries over longer ones, and routing by a query's source over its difficulty. We also argue that the two assumptions that would justify large rosters, model granularity and model specialization, do not hold empirically. We propose an alternative evaluation methodology that does not reward these patterns, and as a proof of concept, we design a simple two-model router that avoids all four. Nevertheless, its gain over random routing is limited, because a well-chosen roster leaves little to route.

</details>


### [111] [Law And Order: Tax Law Autoformalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02792)
*Sophia Simeng Han,Yoshiki Takashima,Anjiang Wei,Zhaoyu Li,Michael Genesereth*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents Law&Order, a neuro-symbolic framework that automatically converts tax law forms and instructions into correct executable programs, achieving perfect accuracy on held-out tax returns by combining LLM synthesis with symbolic verification and repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As legal systems move into software, there is a growing need to automatically convert complex legal texts—like tax forms and instructions—into precise, executable symbolic representations. Existing approaches relying mainly on large language models struggle to achieve the high accuracy and verifiability required in legal and financial contexts. The authors are motivated by the challenge of scaling legal autoformalization while ensuring both structural alignment with legal documents and denotational correctness of the resulting computations.

Method: The authors introduce Law&Order, a neuro-symbolic pipeline that formalizes tax forms into executable programs. The method enforces two correspondences: (1) structural correspondence, mapping legal elements (e.g., cells, schedules) to symbolic program components; and (2) denotational correspondence, ensuring that each symbolic component computes exactly what the legal text specifies. They use a large language model to synthesize candidate symbolic programs from tax forms and instructions, then perform cell-level verification using human-written OpenTaxSolver returns as test cases. When discrepancies are found, the system performs iterative, localized error repair guided by verification failures, resynthesizing only the problematic parts until all cells pass. The final programs are then evaluated on a separate held-out dataset (TaxCalcBench) that is never used in synthesis or repair.

Result: Using the Law&Order framework, the authors achieve 100% accuracy at both cell and form levels on 51 held-out TaxCalcBench tax returns. In contrast, the best standalone large language model reaches only 66% accuracy on the same task. This shows that integrating LLM-based program synthesis with rigorous symbolic verification and targeted error repair dramatically improves reliability and correctness in autoformalizing tax law.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that combining neuro-symbolic techniques—specifically, LLM-driven synthesis with fine-grained verification and repair—provides a scalable and verifiable approach to legal autoformalization. Law&Order demonstrates that high-stakes legal computations, such as tax forms, can be automatically translated into accurate symbolic programs, outperforming pure LLM baselines. The approach suggests a broader path forward for building trustworthy, large-scale legal reasoning systems by tightly coupling language models with formal methods.

Abstract: Legal systems are increasingly implemented through software, yet scalable methods for translating legal texts into accurate symbolic representations remain underdeveloped. We study this problem through tax law, where forms and filing instructions define large computational structures involving arithmetic, branching, recursion, and tabular reasoning. We propose Law&Order, a neuro-symbolic framework for automatically formalizing tax forms and instructions into executable symbolic programs. Our approach establishes two forms of correspondence between law and logic: structural correspondence, which aligns legal and symbolic components such as cells and schedules, and denotational correspondence, which requires symbolic components to implement the computations specified by their legal counterparts. We combine large language model synthesis with cell-level verification and iterative localized error repair using human-written OpenTaxSolver tax returns. We then evaluate the resulting formalizations on independently authored, held-out TaxCalcBench returns, that are never exposed during generation or repair. Although the most advanced LLM achieves only 66% accuracy, Law&Order achieves 100% cell-level and form-level accuracy on 51 held-out returns, demonstrating the effectiveness of combining LLM-based synthesis with symbolic verification for scalable and verifiable large-scale legal autoformalization compared with using an LLM alone.

</details>


### [112] [PAPER2LLM++: Continual Self-Evolution of LLMs from Research Papers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02793)
*Hongji Pu,Yilun Zhao,Wenpeng Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PAPER2LLM++ is a framework that continually improves LLMs by reading research papers about their failures and automatically turning those findings into targeted updates, while preserving previously learned improvements and general capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM development doesn’t automatically leverage the rapidly growing research literature that analyzes model failures, their causes, and fixes. Insights from these papers usually require human engineers to manually design datasets, training objectives, or patches. As a result, model evolution lags behind human understanding, and each new weakness must be corrected via a costly ad‑hoc process. The authors aim to close this gap by creating a mechanism through which LLMs can directly and continually learn from research papers about their own limitations.

Method: The authors propose PAPER2LLM++, a continual self-evolution framework built around research papers as a stream of supervision. For each new paper: (1) the system parses the paper and extracts concrete, evidence-backed findings about specific LLM limitations or behaviors; (2) it checks whether these limitations still appear in the current model by running tests or evaluations inspired by the paper; (3) if the limitation persists, the system automatically converts the paper’s findings into candidate learning signals (e.g., training examples, constraints, or objectives); and (4) a try–evaluate–commit loop runs controlled updates and accepts them only when they fix the targeted behavior without causing substantial forgetting of past improvements or harming general capabilities. This process repeats sequentially over a stream of papers describing different failures.

Result: In experiments where the model is exposed to a sequence of papers that each document particular LLM failures, models updated with PAPER2LLM++ progressively reduce the corresponding failure modes. Importantly, the framework allows the model to retain earlier improvements even as new updates are applied, and it avoids major degradation in broad capabilities. Empirical results show that the system can stably integrate multiple paper-derived updates over time, demonstrating effective continual self-evolution guided by research literature.

Conclusion: PAPER2LLM++ demonstrates that research papers can be treated not only as static knowledge sources but also as dynamic supervision signals that drive continual improvement of LLMs. By automatically detecting whether documented failures still apply and selectively committing updates that help without harming previous gains, the framework closes part of the loop between human analysis and model training. This points toward a future where models continuously learn from the evolving research literature about their own shortcomings and fixes, making LLM development more scalable and aligned with ongoing human discovery.

Abstract: Research on LLMs continually uncovers model limitations, their causes, and potential solutions. Yet these human discoveries remain largely disconnected from model evolution: an LLM does not automatically learn from new research about its own failures. We introduce PAPER2LLM++, a framework for continual self-evolution of LLMs from research papers. Rather than treating papers merely as knowledge to retrieve, PAPER2LLM++ uses the growing literature as a stream of evidence and supervision for model improvement. For each incoming paper, it extracts evidence-grounded findings, tests whether the reported limitation persists in the current model, and, when needed, converts the findings into candidate learning signals. A try-evaluate-commit procedure integrates an update only when it improves the targeted behavior without substantially forgetting prior improvements or degrading general capabilities. Across a sequential stream of research-discovered LLM failures, we show that models can progressively incorporate new findings while retaining earlier gains. PAPER2LLM++ thus takes a step toward closing the loop between human discovery and model evolution, enabling models to continually learn from research about their own limitations and improvements.

</details>


### [113] [Modeling Shared and Individual Structure for Cross-Subject Continuous Affect Regression from EEG-fNIRS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02796)
*Xuan Wang,Bing Wang,Shuai Chang,Hao Yuan,Xinbo Qi,Xinyue Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a zero-shot, cross-subject method to continuously estimate valence and arousal from EEG-fNIRS, using a shared stimulus-driven trajectory plus subject-specific scaling derived from an unlabeled EEG alpha-synchrony marker, and shows it clearly outperforms strong subject-independent baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most continuous affect estimation models from physiological signals assume subject-dependent training, which is unrealistic for practical deployment where models must generalize to unseen individuals without labeled data. There is a need for methods that work in a zero-shot, subject-independent regime and can calibrate affect intensity per person using only unlabeled physiological recordings, especially on raw, fine-grained rating scales.

Method: They use a synchronized EEG-fNIRS dataset where multiple subjects watch the same videos. The affect trajectory is decomposed into (1) a shared, stimulus-driven trajectory common to all viewers and (2) a subject-specific scaling around the scale midpoint. The subject scaling (gain) is inferred from a label-free EEG marker—alpha-band cross-channel synchrony—without using that subject’s labels. The method includes learning the shared trajectory from labeled training subjects and then calibrating each test subject by mapping their alpha synchrony to an individual gain. They rigorously validate this calibration via correlation analyses, comparison with non-linear mapping alternatives, repeated leave-4-out ablations of pipeline components, and a ceiling analysis for per-subject scaling.

Result: On held-out subjects, the approach achieves an MAE of 25.96 / 22.80 over two evaluation batches (valence 21.94 / 19.6, arousal 29.98 / 26.0) on the raw [1,255] scale, substantially outperforming EEGNet and ASAC-Net baselines (MAE 60.6 and 55.0) on the same subject-independent split. None of the many alternative architectures, feature sets, or prediction targets tested were able to surpass the simple alpha-synchrony-based calibration marker, yielding a systematic negative result for those variants.

Conclusion: A simple, interpretable per-subject calibration based on alpha-band EEG synchrony is sufficient to achieve strong zero-shot cross-subject performance in continuous valence-arousal prediction, clearly outperforming existing subject-independent baselines. The results suggest that decomposing affect trajectories into shared stimulus structure plus minimal subject-specific scaling is effective, and that more complex architectures or features may not offer additional benefit beyond this alpha-synchrony marker for this task and dataset.

Abstract: Continuous, second-by-second valence-arousal estimation from physiological signals is typically studied in a subject-dependent setting, where the model sees labeled data from the same person it is later evaluated on. We study the harder zero-shot cross-subject variant on a synchronized EEG-fNIRS dataset: predict raw-scale ([1, 255]) valence and arousal trajectories for subjects whose labels the model never observes, given only their unlabeled EEG/fNIRS recordings while watching the same video stimuli as a disjoint set of training subjects. We decompose the affect trajectory into a structure shared across subjects who watch the same stimuli and an individual structure estimated for each test subject from a label-free EEG marker (alpha-band cross-channel synchrony), which rescales the shared trajectory around the scale midpoint. We validate the per-subject calibration mechanism on four independent axes: leave-one-subject-out correlation between the marker and each subject's true optimal gain, a functional-form comparison against non-linear alternatives, a repeated leave-4-out component ablation isolating each part of the pipeline's contribution, and a ceiling analysis bounding the remaining headroom for per-subject scaling. On held-out subjects, the model reaches an overall MAE of 25.96 / 22.80 across two evaluation batches (valence 21.94 / 19.6, arousal 29.98 / 26.0), well below EEGNet and ASAC-Net baselines reported for the same subject-independent split (raw scale score 60.6 and 55.0 respectively). We further report a systematic negative-result search across model architectures, feature representations, and prediction targets that found no signal able to improve on the single alpha-synchrony marker.

</details>


### [114] [BitNest: Bit-Nested Speculative Decoding for Memory-Efficient LLM Inference Acceleration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02800)
*Chence Yang,Ningxi Cheng,Arash Akbari,Qitao Tan,Qingchan Zhu,Ci Zhang,Changdi Yang,Yanzhi Wang,Wei Niu,Jinhui Wang,Jin Lu,Geng Yuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BitNest is a speculative decoding framework that packs a low-precision draft model and a higher-precision target model into one shared weight and KV-cache representation, achieving high acceptance rates and significant decoding speedups without extra memory overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speculative decoding speeds up autoregressive generation but typically needs a separate draft model or extra weight representation, which is costly on memory-limited devices. Self-speculative methods reduce but don’t eliminate the trade-off between model quality, draft quality, and storage. There is a need for a method that keeps speculative speedups while using essentially the same memory footprint as a single model, suitable for edge-friendly LLMs and long contexts.

Method: BitNest introduces a bit-nested speculative decoding scheme where a strong low-precision base model is built first, and the higher-precision target model is obtained by adding residual refinements on top of it. Both draft and target share one physical weight representation instead of duplicating parameters. The same progressive-precision idea is extended to the KV cache so that both low- and high-precision states for long-context inference are stored compactly. During decoding, the low-precision base proposes multiple tokens, which are then verified/refined by the higher-precision representation reconstructed from the shared weights.

Result: On multiple 7B–8B LLMs designed for edge deployment and across varied workloads, BitNest attains an average speculative acceptance rate of 95.2% while keeping performance close to that of the full higher-precision model. It yields 1.48–1.61× end-to-end speedup over standard FP16 autoregressive decoding. When evaluated on LLaMA models, where various self-speculative baselines exist, BitNest matches or outperforms them in decoding speedup.

Conclusion: Embedding a low-precision draft model within a higher-precision target via bit-nested weights enables speculative decoding with almost no additional memory footprint, high acceptance rates, and substantial speedups. The progressive-precision extension to the KV cache further supports efficient long-context inference, making BitNest a competitive and memory-efficient self-speculative decoding framework for edge-scale LLMs.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by using a lightweight draft to propose multiple tokens for parallel verification. However, existing methods often require an additional draft model or weight representation, introducing non-negligible memory overhead on resource-constrained devices. Self-speculative approaches reduce this overhead, yet still face trade-offs between draft quality, target quality, and storage efficiency. We propose BitNest, a bit-nested speculative decoding framework that embeds a low-precision draft directly into the higher-precision target representation. Instead of deriving a draft from a predefined target, BitNest first constructs a strong low-precision base and then recovers the higher-precision target through residual refinement, enabling both models to share a single physical weight representation. BitNest further extends this progressive-precision design to the KV cache for long-context inference. Across multiple 7B--8B edge-friendly LLMs and diverse workloads, BitNest achieves an average speculative acceptance rate of 95.2% while closely preserving higher-precision model quality, and delivers 1.48--1.61x end-to-end speedup over FP16 autoregressive decoding. On the LLaMA models supported by all representative self-speculative baselines, BitNest also achieves consistently competitive or higher decoding speedup.

</details>


### [115] [VIGOR: Zero-Shot Visual Generalization via Latent-Space Consistency in Model-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02801)
*Mingyu Park,Samyeul Noh,Hyun Myung,Donghwan Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces VIGOR, a model-based RL framework that achieves zero-shot robustness to unseen visual distractions via enforcing latent-space consistency, while preserving sample efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model-based RL is sample-efficient but brittle to unseen visual changes (e.g., backgrounds, lighting, camera shifts). Unlike model-free RL where encoder errors affect only single steps, model-based methods roll out in latent space and thus amplify encoder errors over long horizons. There is a need for a method that preserves the sample-efficiency benefits of MBRL while achieving robust, zero-shot visual generalization to out-of-distribution observations.

Method: VIGOR (Visual Generalization via latent-space consIstency in mOdel-based RL) augments a standard latent MBRL backbone with three coupled components: (1) Asymmetric weak-to-strong augmentation: each batch forms paired views where one uses weak visual augmentation and the other applies weak+strong augmentation, creating latent representations with controlled perturbations. (2) Dynamics-level consistency: the world model is trained so that its latent transition predictions are invariant to these augmentations, enforced via direct regression between predicted latent sequences from different views. This encourages the learned dynamics to ignore visual nuisances. (3) Encoder-level stabilization: additional objectives or regularization prevent the encoder from drifting due to cross-augmentation supervision, ensuring that the representation remains stable and suitable for planning. Together, these mechanisms enforce latent-space consistency across visual perturbations during training.

Result: On DeepMind Control Suite and Robosuite with visual distractions, VIGOR outperforms both model-free and model-based state-of-the-art baselines, improving the second-best method by 3.4% on DMC and 43.6% on Robosuite. Ablation studies show that replacing the default visual augmentations with other, qualitatively different perturbation families maintains strong generalization, indicating that the robustness stems from the latent consistency framework rather than from a specific augmentation heuristic.

Conclusion: Enforcing augmentation-invariant latent dynamics and stabilizing the encoder within a model-based RL framework yields strong zero-shot robustness to unseen visual distractions without sacrificing sample efficiency. Latent-space consistency emerges as the key principle: as long as the method aligns latent rollouts across perturbed views, the specific augmentation choice is less critical. VIGOR thus narrows the robustness gap between MBRL and model-free methods while retaining the advantages of planning in learned dynamics.

Abstract: Model-based reinforcement learning (MBRL) achieves strong sample efficiency by planning within learned latent dynamics, yet its performance degrades substantially under unseen visual distractions such as background variations, lighting changes, or camera shifts. Unlike model-free RL, where encoder perturbations affect only single-step predictions, MBRL suffers from a two-level vulnerability: visual distractions first push encoder outputs out of distribution, and these errors then compound through recursive latent rollouts over the planning horizon. We propose visual generalization via latent-space consistency in model-based RL (VIGOR), a framework that enables zero-shot generalization to unseen visual distractions while retaining the sample efficiency of its MBRL backbone. VIGOR integrates three interdependent components: (i) asymmetric weak-to-strong augmentation, which pairs weak-only and weak-to-strong latent views within a single batch; (ii) dynamics-level consistency, which enforces augmentation-invariant transition predictions through direct latent regression; and (iii) encoder-level stabilization, which prevents encoder drift under the cross-augmentation supervision imposed by dynamics-level consistency. Evaluations on the DeepMind Control Suite (DMC) and Robosuite show that VIGOR outperforms state-of-the-art model-free and model-based baselines, surpassing the second-best baseline by 3.4% on DMC and 43.6% on Robosuite. Ablations further show that VIGOR's robustness is augmentation-agnostic: replacing the default augmentation with alternatives from distinct perturbation families preserves strong generalization, confirming that latent-space consistency, not the augmentation choice, drives robustness.

</details>


### [116] [ROUTEAUDIT: Interaction-Aware Identification for Budgeted Multi-Verifier Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02808)
*Miaobo Hu,Shuhao Hu,Xiaobo Guo,Xin Wang,Bokun Wang,Tianshu Fu,Daren Zha,Jun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ROUTEAUDIT, a framework for fairly comparing adaptive multi-verifier routing policies by holding a detailed “contract” fixed and only varying the policy, yielding rigorous, policy-independent attributions and confidence intervals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of adaptive multi-verifier systems (e.g., cascades or learned routing over multiple verifiers/models) often compare endpoint quality vs. cost under different experimental setups. However, as policies change, so do verifier catalogs, availability, accounting rules, filtering, and scoring. This entangles policy effects with infrastructure and measurement changes, making quality–cost gaps hard to interpret causally. The authors want a principled identification framework that isolates the causal effect of routing policies under a shared, explicit contract about what is fixed and what is allowed to vary.

Method: The authors formalize verifier routing as a contract-conditioned identification problem. They define a “contract” that fixes request support, the verifier catalog, realized availability, resource accounting, online filtration, and post-trace scoring, so that comparisons vary only in the routing policy coordinate. ROUTEAUDIT then adds: (1) a contract lattice, which averages coordinate increments over all admissible bridge orders to attribute performance differences and to quantify path sensitivity; (2) a policy-independent response tape, which records sequences of observations so that paired comparisons remain valid even when adaptive policies see different observations; and (3) incomplete-matching bounds that, at the request level, use whichever potential outcome is observed to construct sharp finite-population confidence intervals. The protocol enforces that paid observations and ledger events are committed before joining with the evaluation oracle, and it outputs an attribution certificate summarizing the comparison for each route pair.

Result: Using two held-out raw-tail caches, the framework shows that a matched static SF+SA configuration performs equivalently to a cascade policy; the apparent gains over a full static baseline (0.1797 and 0.1250) are attributed entirely to changes in the verifier set rather than adaptivity. On 1,319 held-out tasks, learned and RLVR (reinforcement-learning-based verifier routing) policies achieve qualities of 0.9522 and 0.9553 versus 0.9484 for matched static. The RLVR–static quality difference is estimated as +0.0068, with a tight request-paired interval [0.0015, 0.0122] and a training-seed-by-request hierarchical interval [0.0006, 0.0131]. Controlled attribution recovery experiments show very small route mean absolute error (0.0011) and endpoint reconstruction error (0.0004), supporting the correctness of the attribution. Additional factorial, bridge-order, and stochastic-provider experiments probe the behavior of the attribution and the robustness of the certificate interface; RLVR serves as a stress test for learned policies under the fixed identification contract.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that ROUTEAUDIT provides a rigorous, contract-based identification and attribution framework for comparing adaptive multi-verifier routing policies. By fixing a detailed contract and using constructs like the contract lattice, response tape, and sharp finite-population bounds, it can separate true policy effects from confounding changes in infrastructure, availability, or scoring. Empirical studies show that some apparent gains from cascades are actually due to verifier-set changes rather than adaptivity, while learned routing policies like RLVR do provide measurable, precisely quantified improvements over matched static baselines. The small reconstruction errors and systematic sensitivity analyses indicate that the attribution certificates are accurate and robust, making ROUTEAUDIT a reliable tool for auditing and stress-testing verifier routing systems.

Abstract: Adaptive multi-verifier systems are commonly compared through endpoint quality-cost gaps, even when the verifier catalog, availability, accounting, information filtration, or scorer changes with the policy. We formulate verifier routing as a contract-conditioned identification problem. The contract records request support, verifier catalog, realized availability, resource accounting, online filtration, and post-trace scoring; a matched route contrast changes only the policy coordinate. ROUTEAUDIT adds three measurable objects to this contract. A contract lattice averages coordinate increments over every admissible bridge order and reports the resulting attribution together with its path sensitivity. A policy-independent response tape identifies paired sequential contrasts when adaptive policies reveal different observations. For incomplete matching, request-level bounds use whichever potential outcome remains observed and give a sharp finite-population interval. The protocol commits paid observations and ledger events before the oracle join and returns an attribution certificate for each comparison. On two held-out raw-tail caches, matched static SF+SA equals the cascade, assigning the apparent gains of 0.1797 and 0.1250 over full static to the verifier-set edge. On 1,319 held-out task requests, the learned and RLVR studies report quality 0.9522 and 0.9553 versus 0.9484 for matched static; the RLVR-static paired difference is +0.0068 with a request-paired interval $[0.0015,0.0122]$ and a training-seed-by-request hierarchical interval $[0.0006,0.0131]$. Controlled attribution recovery yields route mean absolute error 0.0011 and endpoint reconstruction error 0.0004. Factorial, bridge-order, and stochastic-provider studies evaluate the certificate interface; RLVR supplies a learned-policy stress test under the same identification contract.

</details>


### [117] [iS-KV: Online Low-Rank KV Cache Compression via Block-Incremental SVD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02815)
*Yiren Zhao,Guanghui Song,Tianrui Qin,Kejiang Ye,Cheng-zhong Xu,Xitong Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces iS-KV, an online low-rank KV-cache compression method that supports long chain-of-thought reasoning with much lower memory while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive LLM decoding for long chain-of-thought reasoning makes the KV cache grow linearly with sequence length, leading to large memory usage. Existing solutions mostly evict tokens, but this can permanently remove information that might be needed later in the reasoning process. The authors seek a compression scheme that limits memory while still preserving access to the entire history.

Method: They analyze SVD-based low-rank compression and identify that naively updating the low-rank basis online causes a drift between stored historical coordinates and the evolving basis. Building on this, they propose iS-KV: an online low-rank KV-cache compression algorithm that (1) keeps a recent window of tokens in exact form, (2) incrementally folds older key/value states into a bounded-rank low-rank representation, and (3) synchronizes historical coordinates whenever the basis is updated, ensuring consistency between basis and stored coefficients during decoding.

Result: On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B, iS-KV obtains 82.6% accuracy with a 4.06× reduction in persistent KV-cache size, nearly matching the original 83.6% accuracy. On Qwen3-8B, it achieves 89.2% accuracy at 5.64× compression. Under the same memory constraints, iS-KV outperforms token-eviction compression baselines across evaluations.

Conclusion: Online low-rank KV-cache compression with coordinated basis and coefficient updates (iS-KV) can significantly reduce memory usage in long-horizon reasoning while maintaining accuracy. Properly managing basis evolution and historical coordinate synchronization avoids representation drift and makes low-rank compression more effective than token eviction under comparable memory budgets.

Abstract: Long chain-of-thought reasoning substantially increases KV-cache memory during autoregressive decoding, as every generated token introduces new key and value states and causes the cache to grow linearly with decoding length. Existing KV-cache compression methods typically control this growth through token eviction, but irreversible deletion can remove historical states that later reasoning may need to revisit. SVD-based low-rank compression provides an alternative by retaining all positions with a more compact representation. However, extending it from a fixed prompt cache to online decoding is non-trivial. Through our investigation, we find that if the basis is updated for new tokens while old tokens keep their coordinates in the old basis, the stored history drifts substantially. Based on this observation, we propose iS-KV, an online low-rank KV-cache compression method for long-horizon reasoning. iS-KV keeps a recent window exact while incrementally folding older states into bounded-rank representations. As the low-rank basis evolves, it synchronizes historical coordinates with the updated basis to maintain representation consistency. On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B, iS-KV achieves 82.6% accuracy at 4.06-fold persistent-KV compression, close to the original model's 83.6%. On Qwen3-8B, it achieves 89.2% accuracy at 5.64-fold compression. Under matched memory budgets, iS-KV consistently outperforms token-eviction baselines.

</details>


### [118] [MetaRubric: Learning to Reward for Rubric-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02824)
*Yuxuan Fan,Jaehong Yoon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies and fixes a key failure mode in rubric-based RL for LLMs—rubrics giving credit without evidence—by introducing MetaRubric, which ties scores to explicit evidence and iteratively adapts the rubric and weights using counterfactual prompts, yielding large accuracy gains on medical QA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rubric-based reinforcement learning lets models get partial credit on complex, open-ended tasks, which is valuable when simple scalar rewards are hard to define. However, current rubric judges can mistakenly award high scores to responses that do not actually contain the required information or actions, a problem the authors call Vacuous Credit. This misaligned credit signal can even make objectively worse answers appear better to the learning algorithm, undermining policy optimization. The paper is motivated by the need for a more faithful, evidence-grounded rubric framework that provides reliable learning signals and works especially well in sensitive, high-stakes domains like medical question answering.

Method: The authors propose MetaRubric, a two-level procedure combining evidence-aware policy optimization with iterative rubric adaptation. First, for each task, they construct counterfactual prompts by altering one key fact in the original prompt. During policy optimization, a response only receives credit for a rubric criterion when it contains explicit, sufficient evidence that the criterion is satisfied, thus preventing rubric judges from scoring based on surface features alone. To refine the rubric, after each optimization stage they use the model’s current responses to both original and counterfactual prompts to revise rubric criteria so that each criterion’s meaning is preserved but better tied to factual evidence under each prompt’s facts. They also adapt criterion weights at stage boundaries, increasing emphasis on criteria where the policy is weak and decreasing emphasis where it already performs well or where criteria are noisy, thereby improving the robustness of the learning signal.

Result: Applying MetaRubric across several model backbones, the authors observe substantial performance gains on medical question answering tasks. On PubMedQA, MetaRubric improves accuracy by 6.00 to 20.40 percentage points compared to a baseline that uses a static rubric judge with GRPO. They also report further improvements on the HealthBench-Hard benchmark and on two multimodal medical benchmarks, suggesting that the method generalizes across architectures and input modalities. These results indicate that enforcing evidence-aware scoring and iteratively adapting rubric criteria and weights can markedly enhance the effectiveness of rubric-based reinforcement learning.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that Vacuous Credit is a significant and previously under-recognized failure mode in rubric-based reinforcement learning, capable of distorting policy gradients and harming performance. MetaRubric addresses this by enforcing evidence-conditioned scoring and by jointly adapting both rubric criteria and their weights using model responses and counterfactual prompts. The demonstrated gains on multiple medical QA benchmarks show that aligning rubrics with explicit evidence can substantially improve learning, especially in complex, high-stakes domains. The authors imply that rubric design should be treated as an adaptive, data-driven process rather than a fixed, one-shot specification, and that similar meta-rubric strategies could benefit other open-ended tasks beyond medical QA.

Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning extends reward-driven optimization to open-ended tasks by assigning partial credit to individual response requirements. However, rubric judges can assign a high criterion score even when the information or action it requires is absent from the response, a failure mode we term Vacuous Credit. Such awards persist after the required information is removed and can reverse the sign of a response's GRPO advantage. To address this problem, we introduce MetaRubric, which alternates evidence-aware policy optimization with response-guided rubric adaptation. We construct counterfactual counterparts by changing one task-relevant fact in each prompt. During policy optimization, credit is assigned only when the response contains sufficient evidence to satisfy the required rubric criterion. After each policy-optimization stage, current policy responses guide revisions to original and counterfactual criteria while preserving the meaning of the original prompt's initial rubric as interpreted under each prompt's facts. We also adapt criterion weights at stage boundaries to better address observed policy errors. Across multiple backbones, MetaRubric improves PubMedQA accuracy by 6.00--20.40 percentage points over static-judge GRPO, with further gains on HealthBench-Hard and two multimodal medical benchmarks.

</details>


### [119] [Scaling Trajectories for Complex Tasks through Recursive Self-Rewrite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02826)
*Zongxia Li,Yucheng Shi,Zhongzhi Li,Junyao Yang,Ruhan Wang,Chengsong Huang,Fuxiao Liu,Haitao Mi,Jordan Boyd-Graber,LeoweiLiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Recursive Self-Rewrite (RSR), a framework where a single base model generates, rewrites, and replays successful task-solving trajectories collected under different, specialized interaction harnesses, transforming them into training data usable under a more general, deployment-like harness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many complex tasks, such as long-horizon terminal or software tasks, are easier for models to solve when aided by specialized harnesses (e.g., tools, extra feedback, or special environments). However, these harnesses are often not available at deployment time, so direct reliance on them limits real-world usefulness. The authors want a way to leverage the rich supervision signals from harness-assisted successes to improve a model that ultimately must operate under a more restrictive, general harness, without leaking verifier information or overfitting to the harness specifics.

Method: They propose Recursive Self-Rewrite (RSR), applied to a base model (Qwen-3.8-27B). First, the model attempts many terminal tasks under multiple specialized harnesses and records successful trajectories. A planner component extracts high-level procedures from these trajectories, turning them into structured runbooks that describe how to solve the tasks. A critic checks these runbooks for verifier leakage or direct copying of solutions and iteratively guides revisions to make them more general and safe, recursively refining the procedures. An executor then runs these approved runbooks in fresh, clean sandboxes under a general harness to generate new, harness-agnostic training trajectories. This pipeline turns a smaller set of successful harness-assisted trajectories into a much larger set of rewritten trajectories suitable for supervised finetuning of the same base model.

Result: On roughly 3,000 self-curated terminal tasks, using three different harnesses together solves 759 tasks, which is 34.3% more than the best single harness alone. From 2,001 original successful trajectories, RSR generates 11,094 rewritten trajectories for SFT. Models trained on this rewritten data beat both the original base model and standard direct trajectory SFT. Measured by pass@3, performance on Terminal-Bench 2 improves from 57.0% to 74.2%, on Terminal-Bench 4 from 1.5% to 9.1%, on a self-curated Terminal-Bench Hard from 39.0% to 63.0%, and on a Software Terminal-Bench from 3.0% to 6.0%. Process reward on Long-Horizon Terminal-Bench rises from 0.21 to 0.29.

Conclusion: Harness-assisted experiences can be systematically transformed into general, reusable capabilities for a single model by extracting, sanitizing, and replaying solution procedures under a general harness. Recursive Self-Rewrite enables a base model to self-curate and expand its successful trajectories, producing higher-quality training data than raw harness logs and yielding substantial gains on multiple challenging terminal benchmarks. This suggests a promising path to leverage rich but non-deployable supervision settings to improve models meant to operate under more realistic constraints.

Abstract: Successful trajectories on difficult tasks provide valuable supervision for model improvement, but specialized harnesses introduce interventions that may be unavailable during deployment. We propose Recursive Self-Rewrite (RSR), a framework that uses one base model, Qwen-3.8-27B, to discover successful solutions under diverse harnesses and reconstruct them as training trajectories under a general harness. A planner extracts procedures into runbooks, a critic screens for verifier and solution leakage and guides recursive revision, and an executor follows qualified runbooks in fresh sandboxes. Across approximately 3K self-curated terminal tasks, three harnesses jointly solve 759 tasks, 34.3% more than the strongest individual harness in the recorded pool. RSR expands 2,001 successful source trajectories into 11,094 rewritten trajectories for supervised finetuning. Training on these trajectories outperforms both the base model and direct trajectory SFT. Compared with the base model, pass@3 increases from 57.0% to 74.2% on Terminal-Bench 2, from 1.5% to 9.1% on Terminal-Bench 4, from 39.0% to 63.0% on our self-curated Terminal-Bench Hard, and from 3.0% to 6.0% on our Software Terminal-Bench. Process reward on Long-Horizon Terminal-Bench rises from 0.21 to 0.29. These results show how diverse harness-assisted experiences can be reconstructed into reusable capabilities for a model operating under a general harness.

</details>


### [120] [MLCommons Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02827)
*Carsten Maple,Cagatay Yucel,Isaac Holeman,Chris Knotz,Peter Mattson,James Goel,Jonathan Petit,Sean McGregor,James Ezick,Abhishek Kumar,Alicia Parrish,Murali Emani,Kashyap Iyer,Faiza Khan Khattak,Washington Mbonu,Daniel Machlab,Eileen Long,Shaona Ghosh,Jibin Varghese,Roman Lutz,Andrew Gruen,Bennett Hillenbrand,Prabal Gupta,Mohammed Serrhini,Dhivya Nagasubramanian,Aakash Gupta,Jun,Lu,Kurt Bollacker,Chang Liu,Jonathan Petit,Cong Chen,Jean-Philippe Monteuuis,Brent Miller,Apurv Verma,Roman Eng,Armstrong Foundjem,Mohammed Serrhini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces MLCommons Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0, a standardized, end-to-end benchmark for measuring how robust large language models are to single-turn text jailbreak attacks, using human and automated evaluation to quantify safety degradation under adversarial prompts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As large language models become widely deployed, they are protected by safety mechanisms that can often be bypassed with carefully crafted jailbreak prompts. There is no widely accepted, rigorous, and reproducible way to compare how different models withstand such attacks or to understand where and how they fail. The authors are motivated to create a standardized benchmark and methodology to systematically evaluate and compare models’ robustness against jailbreaks across multiple hazard types and attack strategies.

Method: The authors design an end-to-end benchmarking pipeline called MLCommons Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0. It starts with criteria-driven selection of both target systems (eight open-weight models) and jailbreak attacks, using the MLCommons Jailbreak Taxonomy. They build a dataset of 264 seed prompts spanning eleven hazard categories. For each prompt, they run both baseline (non-adversarial) and adversarial (jailbreak) queries. Model responses are evaluated using the AILuminate Assessment Standard v1.4, combining human annotation with calibrated automated evaluators. They define a metric called Resilience Gap, which measures the change in safety performance (unsafe-response rate) between baseline and jailbreak conditions. They also analyze evaluator reliability and characterize measurement error sources inside the pipeline.

Result: Across all systems and attacks, the benchmark finds that the unsafe-response rate rises from 11.08% in baseline conditions to 18.65% under jailbreak conditions, corresponding to an average Resilience Gap of 7.57%. Systems that are more accessible (easier to use or integrate) tend to exhibit larger safety degradation. The effectiveness of jailbreak attacks varies widely across different attack types and hazard categories, showing that some combinations are much more successful at eliciting unsafe outputs than others. The study also surfaces non-trivial evaluator reliability issues and quantifies measurement variability within the pipeline.

Conclusion: The benchmark demonstrates that jailbreaks measurably and substantially degrade the safety performance of current open-weight language models, with meaningful variation across models, attack categories, and hazard types. MLCommons Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0 offers a reproducible, extensible methodology and metric (Resilience Gap) for comparative jailbreak evaluation. The authors position this benchmark as a foundation that can be expanded to cover additional systems, more diverse attacks, hazard categories, and improved evaluation methods, thereby facilitating more systematic progress in AI safety and robustness against jailbreaks.

Abstract: Modern AI systems are designed to refuse hazardous requests. A jailbreak is a prompt crafted to bypass those safeguards and elicit outputs that the system would normally refuse to provide. The MLCommons Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0 provides an end-to-end methodology for evaluating the robustness of large language models to single-turn, text-based jailbreak attacks. It combines criteria-driven system and attack selection, paired baseline and adversarial evaluation, human annotation, automated evaluator calibration, scoring, grading, and risk-calibrated disclosure within a single benchmarking pipeline. The benchmark evaluates eight open-weight systems using 264 seed prompts spanning eleven hazard categories and representative attacks drawn from the MLCommons Jailbreak Taxonomy. Responses are assessed using the AILuminate Assessment Standard v1.4, and robustness is measured through the Resilience Gap: the change in safety performance between baseline and adversarial conditions. Across all evaluated systems and attacks, the unsafe-response rate increased from 11.08% under baseline conditions to 18.65% under jailbreak conditions, producing an average Resilience Gap of 7.57%. Accessible systems showed a larger mean gap, while attack effectiveness varied substantially across attack categories and hazards. The benchmark also examines evaluator reliability and sources of measurement error. Beyond reporting results, Jailbreak Benchmark v1.0 establishes a reproducible methodological foundation for comparative jailbreak evaluation and for future expansion across systems, attacks, hazards, and evaluation methods.

</details>


### [121] [FSPO: Policy-Consistent Risk and Pareto-Feasible Control for Budgeted LLM RL Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02828)
*Miaobo Hu,Shuhao Hu,Xiaobo Guo,Xin Wang,Bokun Wang,Daren Zha,Jun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FSPO is a feedback-state controller framework for budgeted LLM RL post-training that jointly handles risk modeling, calibration after selective actions, and multi-resource feasibility, improving accuracy, calibration, and safety metrics over strong adaptive baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing adaptive reinforcement-learning post-training methods for LLMs adjust many training hyperparameters online (e.g., temperature, group size, KL regularization, verifier allocation, update budgets), but they suffer from three unresolved issues: (1) future-risk models trained on past behavior may not reflect the risk profile under the controller that will actually be deployed, especially when the deployment policy differs from the logging policy; (2) risk or confidence scores calibrated on logged state-action data can become miscalibrated after the system selectively chooses actions (i.e., off-policy or selective prediction setting); and (3) treating resource constraints independently per-resource cannot guarantee that there exists a feasible continuation over multiple resources simultaneously, leading to potential budget violations or unsafe trajectories. A unified solution is needed to produce policy-consistent risk estimates, maintain calibration under selective decisions, and certify multi-resource feasibility during LLM RL post-training.

Method: The paper proposes FSPO, a feedback-state controller for budgeted LLM RL post-training that integrates three key components. First, it learns a policy-consistent risk-to-go model and a long-horizon utility model, where the Bellman target for the risk model is computed using the same frozen controller that will be used for future decisions, thus aligning risk prediction with the deployed policy. Second, it introduces decision-conditioned trajectory calibration (DCTC), which calibrates risk estimates using cross-fitted trajectories generated by actions that provisional controllers would actually select, correcting for distribution shifts induced by selective action choice. Third, it defines a Pareto resource continuation certificate (PRCC), which admits an action only if there exists a non-dominated cumulative reservation of multiple resources that remains feasible over the remaining horizon, ensuring that multi-resource constraints are jointly satisfiable. These elements are combined in a feedback control loop that adaptively adjusts LLM RL training actuators (e.g., temperature, group size, clipping, KL, verifier allocation, update budgets) under a budgeted setting and are evaluated under matched GRPO-style resource envelopes and ablation studies.

Result: Under a matched GRPO resource envelope, FSPO achieves 66.11% held-out accuracy and 59.43% out-of-distribution (OOD) accuracy, outperforming the strongest adaptive baseline (PB2) which attains 64.47% and 57.03%, respectively. Across three paired training seeds, FSPO yields gains of +2.42 and +3.19 percentage points over a contextual bandit baseline on held-out and OOD evaluations. In scenarios with high mismatch between behavior policy and deployment controller, the policy-consistent risk modeling reduces expected calibration error (ECE) on selected decisions from 0.108 to 0.053. DCTC further improves calibration by reducing ECE from 0.039 to 0.022 at matched acceptance rate. PRCC eliminates false-feasible admissions in a test with an 18-action catalog, reducing such errors from 0.197 to 0.000. When all three components are enabled together, trajectory failure rate in a factorial ablation drops from 0.181 to 0.083, indicating substantially improved safety and constraint satisfaction.

Conclusion: FSPO provides a unified feedback-state control approach for budgeted LLM RL post-training that simultaneously addresses policy-consistent risk estimation, post-selection calibration, and multi-resource feasibility. By aligning the risk-to-go model with the frozen deployment controller, calibrating on decision-conditioned trajectories, and enforcing a Pareto-style continuation certificate over multiple resources, FSPO achieves better accuracy, calibration, and safety compared to strong adaptive baselines such as PB2 and contextual bandits, while strictly respecting resource budgets. The results suggest that principled control of risk and resources during LLM RL post-training can deliver more robust and reliable models, especially under behavior-deployment mismatch and complex multi-resource constraints.

Abstract: Adaptive LLM reinforcement-learning post-training changes multiple training actuators online, including rollout temperature, group size, clipping, KL regularization, verifier allocation, and update budget. Three coupled issues remain unresolved. A future-risk model trained from behavior trajectories need not estimate the risk induced by the controller that will be deployed; a score calibrated on logged state-action pairs can become miscalibrated after selective action choice; and independent per-resource minimum costs do not in general certify a feasible multi-resource continuation. We introduce FSPO, a feedback-state controller for budgeted LLM RL post-training that addresses these issues jointly. FSPO learns a policy-consistent risk-to-go model whose Bellman target follows the same frozen controller used for future decisions, together with a long-horizon utility model. Decision-conditioned trajectory calibration (DCTC) calibrates risk on cross-fitted trajectories generated by actions selected by provisional controllers. A Pareto resource continuation certificate (PRCC) admits an action only when a non-dominated cumulative reservation remains feasible over the residual horizon. Under a matched GRPO resource envelope, FSPO reaches 66.11% held-out and 59.43% OOD accuracy, compared with 64.47% and 57.03% for PB2, the strongest evaluated adaptive baseline. Three paired training seeds give gains of +2.42 and +3.19 percentage points over the contextual bandit on held-out and OOD evaluation. Under high behavior-deployment mismatch, policy-consistent risk lowers selected-decision ECE from 0.108 to 0.053; DCTC lowers it from 0.039 to 0.022 at matched acceptance; PRCC removes false-feasible admissions on an 18-action catalog ($0.197\rightarrow0.000$); and enabling all three components reduces trajectory failure from 0.181 to 0.083 in a factorial ablation.

</details>


### [122] [AMBER: Multi-View Adaptive Budget Allocation for Listwise Vision-Language Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02831)
*Wenteng Chen,Jiachen Zhu,Rong Shan,Tianyi Xu,Yuxiang Chen,Congmin Zheng,Teng Wang,Junjie Wu,Weiwen Liu,Changwang Zhang,Weinan Zhang,Jun Wang,Jianghao Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces AMBER, an adaptive, budgeted reranking framework that uses VLMs more efficiently for multimodal retrieval by modeling fragmented listwise scores as Elo-based local tournaments and dynamically allocating limited VLM calls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models work well as listwise rerankers for multimodal retrieval but are computationally expensive, so practical systems can only use them on small subsets and with fixed, often wasteful multi-call schedules. There is a need for a principled way to use these limited VLM calls more effectively, allocating them to the most informative candidate comparisons and queries while still approximating a strong global ranking.

Method: AMBER models each partial listwise VLM output as a local tournament and maintains a global ranking using continuous Elo rating updates. It operates under a fixed budget of VLM calls and uses this Elo-based state to: (1) adaptively construct new candidate views by focusing on items with high score ambiguity, and (2) schedule which user/query to evaluate next based on maximizing expected information gain, justified via a submodular information-theoretic criterion. The authors show that Elo updates align with stochastic gradient ascent on the Bradley–Terry log-likelihood, providing a probabilistic grounding for the method.

Result: On three multimodal retrieval benchmarks—CIRR, CIRCO, and PhotoBench—AMBER outperforms other multi-call VLM reranking strategies when constrained to comparable VLM-call budgets, and it also maintains strong effectiveness when budgets are tighter, indicating better efficiency–effectiveness trade-offs.

Conclusion: Adaptive, information-theoretic allocation of expensive VLM reranking calls via an Elo-based global ranking state can significantly improve multimodal retrieval performance under realistic compute budgets, outperforming fixed multi-call schedules. AMBER provides both a practical reranking framework and a theoretically grounded connection between Elo updates and Bradley–Terry models.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are powerful listwise rerankers for multimodal retrieval, but high inference costs restrict them to evaluating small local candidate views. Existing multi-call strategies rely on fixed schedules, wasting expensive VLM calls on uninformative candidate pairs and easy queries. To address this, we propose Adaptive Multi-view Budgeted Elo Reranking (AMBER), an online, budgeted multi-view reranking framework that dynamically optimizes global resource allocation. AMBER treats fragmented listwise VLM outputs as local tournaments, using continuous Elo updates to maintain a lightweight global ranking state. Building on this, it allocates computation at two levels: dynamically constructing candidate views with high score ambiguity, and scheduling queries to maximize expected information gain. We show that each Elo update corresponds to a stochastic gradient ascent step on the Bradley-Terry log-likelihood, and provide a submodular information-theoretic motivation for the query-level allocation strategy. Experiments on CIRR, CIRCO, and PhotoBench demonstrate that AMBER achieves the strongest overall performance among the compared multi-call VLM reranking methods under comparable VLM-call budgets, while remaining effective in lower-budget settings. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/wnlfc/AMBER.

</details>


### [123] [DNAlign: Dynamic Null-Space Safe Alignment for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02844)
*Jisheng Dang,Yushuo Zhao,Dewei Liu,Junfeng Fang,Bimei Wang,Tiantian Rao,Hong Peng,Bin Hu,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DNAlign is a lightweight safety alignment framework for LLMs that uses control-theoretic perturbations restricted to a harmful-related subspace, reducing harmful outputs while preserving fluency and factual utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-aligning LLMs often either requires expensive retraining or harms the model’s general capabilities, exposing a trade-off between safety and utility. The paper aims to design an alignment method that is computationally efficient, preserves core knowledge and fluency, and can be practically deployed across different LLMs.

Method: The authors model an LLM as a dynamic system and apply control-theoretic optimization to inject small, controllable perturbations into the model’s hidden states during generation. They introduce a null-space projection module that confines these perturbations to a harmful-related subspace identified from neutral hidden states, so benign knowledge remains largely untouched. A value function, trained on human preference data for safety, adaptively optimizes the control signals to guide the model toward safe responses. The framework is evaluated on multiple LLM backbones and compared against existing alignment methods.

Result: Across several LLM architectures, DNAlign reduces harmful outputs while maintaining fluency, coherence, factual accuracy, and output diversity. It outperforms previous alignment baselines on combined safety–utility metrics, with lower computational overhead than full fine-tuning approaches.

Conclusion: DNAlign offers an effective and practical approach to LLM safety alignment by steering internal activations with control-theoretic perturbations constrained to a harmful-related subspace. This design mitigates the safety–utility trade-off, achieving better safety without notable degradation in general performance, and is suitable for deployment across diverse LLM backbones.

Abstract: Ensuring the safe and reliable deployment of large language models (LLMs) remains a fundamental challenge. Existing safety alignment approaches either incur high computational cost or unintentionally disrupt the model's core knowledge, leading to degraded fluency and factual accuracy on benign tasks. This reveals a persistent trade-off between safety and utility. We propose DNAlign, a lightweight alignment framework that integrates control-theoretic optimization with null-space projection. By treating the LLM as a dynamic system, the proposed framework introduces controllable perturbations to steer generation toward safe behavior. A key component is the projection module, which restricts these perturbations to the harmful-related subspace derived from neutral hidden states, thereby preserving general knowledge and response quality. A value function trained on human preference data adaptively optimizes the control signals to align with human safety preferences. Extensive evaluations across multiple LLM backbones demonstrate that our framework consistently reduces harmful outputs while maintaining fluency, coherence, and factual utility. It achieves superior overall performance compared to prior alignment baselines without sacrificing generation diversity. These results indicate that the proposed framework provides an effective and practically deployable solution for safe LLM alignment. Code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/DNAlign.

</details>


### [124] [Bounded Reachability & Jailbreak Detection via Contraction-Constrained State Space Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02853)
*Omanshu Thapliyal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper studies formal robustness of state space model (SSM)-based safety heads for harmful-input detection and finds a simple spectral condition that enables exact certification via interval bound propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety heads are used to detect harmful prompts before a language model generates responses, but current work focuses on empirical detection performance without guarantees. The authors want to understand when such safety heads can be *formally certified* robust to small perturbations in the embedding space (e.g., slight paraphrases or adversarial tweaks), providing worst-case guarantees rather than just average-case accuracy.

Method: They model the safety head as a linear time-invariant State Space Model classifier and analyze robustness under bounded perturbations in the input embedding space. Using interval bound propagation (IBP), they derive conditions for the reachable output interval to remain bounded and allow certification. They identify the key requirement as an \(\ell_\infty\)-norm contraction condition on the state transition matrix (\(\|A\|_\infty < 1\)). They enforce this condition during training via a hinge penalty regularizer and empirically study the effect on robustness and certification on toxic comment and jailbreak detection benchmarks. They also compare S4 safety heads to a simple logistic regression on mean-pooled embeddings.

Result: Theoretically, they prove that when the state transition matrix satisfies the contraction condition (\(\|A\|_\infty < 1\)), IBP gives exact certification for linear time-invariant SSM classifiers: the reachable output interval has a bounded steady-state width, enabling robust classification guarantees within a perturbation radius. If the condition fails, the interval width grows exponentially with sequence length, making practical certification impossible. Empirically, imposing a contraction penalty increases the certified robust fraction on toxic comment data from 41% to 59%, with a sharp phase transition at \(\|A\|_\infty = 1\) consistent with the theory. A contraction-regularized S4 head for jailbreak detection generalizes zero-shot to AdvBench and HarmBench with high detection rates (0.994 and 0.988). A simple logistic regression on mean-pooled Mamba-130M embeddings performs as well or better on detection metrics, implying harmful intent is already linearly separable in the embedding space.

Conclusion: Robust certification of SSM-based safety heads hinges on a simple contraction condition on the state transition matrix, under which IBP becomes exact and practical certification is possible. Enforcing this condition in training significantly boosts the fraction of inputs that can be certified robust, and empirical results validate a sharp theoretical phase transition at the contraction boundary. While an SSM (S4) head does not outperform a simple linear probe in discriminatory power—since harmful intent is linearly separable in embeddings—its main value lies in offering formal robustness guarantees that such probes lack.

Abstract: Safety heads are lightweight classifiers attached to pretrained language models for flagging harmful inputs before generation. Their empirical detection performance has been studied, but their formal robustness properties remain largely unexplored. We ask when a State Space Model (SSM)-based safety head can be certified to produce the same prediction for all inputs within a bounded embedding-space perturbation. We prove that the answer turns on a single condition: the $l_\infty$ norm of the state transition matrix must satisfy $\norm{A}_\infty<1$ (the \emph{contraction condition}), which enables exact interval bound propagation (IBP) certification for linear time-invariant classifiers. When the contraction condition holds, the reachable output interval has bounded steady-state width and examples can be certified as robustly classified. When it fails, the interval grows exponentially with sequence length and certification is impossible at any practical perturbation radius. We enforce contraction with a hinge penalty and show on toxic comment data that certified fraction improves from 41\% to 59\%, with a sharp empirical phase transition at $\norm{A}_\infty=1$ matching the theory. Applying a contraction-regularized S4 head to jailbreak detection on JailbreakBench, we achieve a zero-shot transfer to AdvBench (DR=0.994) and HarmBench (DR=0.988). A logistic regression on mean-pooled Mamba-130M embeddings matches or exceeds the S4 head on every detection metric, confirming that harmful intent is already linearly separable in the embedding space. The S4 safety head's contribution is not superior discrimination but the formal certification that no probe-based approach provides.

</details>


### [125] [Harness-Aware Distillation for Small Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02858)
*Moonseok Choi,Taehong Moon,Giung Nam,Juho Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose Harness-Aware Distillation (HAD), a way to distill large language model agents that explicitly accounts for the agent’s surrounding software harness, improving long-horizon agent performance over standard on-policy distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In agentic LLM systems, much of the behavior is governed by a harness that manages tools, context, and feedback. When distilling a large teacher agent into a smaller student, the harness remains unchanged, so the student mainly needs to learn what the teacher contributes beyond what the harness already provides. Standard distillation ignores this separation and simply imitates teacher outputs given the full input (including harness state), which can be inefficient and may cause the student to conflate harness-controlled behavior with model-controlled behavior. The authors want a distillation procedure that is better aligned with the actual deployment setting of agents using a fixed harness, to improve robustness, reduce unproductive loops, and avoid requiring task rewards or success labels.

Method: They introduce Harness-Aware Distillation (HAD), which augments on-policy distillation with two key components that explicitly factor in harness information. First, they define an action preference signal by contrasting the same teacher’s actions when it has access to harness information versus when it does not, applied after the student has generated its own reasoning, thus highlighting what the teacher adds beyond the harness. Second, they apply a validity check that discards preference pairs where the preferred teacher action conflicts with the harness’s recorded state, ensuring that the supervision respects the harness’s ground truth. Training then encourages the student to prefer actions that align with the teacher’s harness-aware behavior while filtering out invalid guidance, without relying on task rewards, explicit success labels, or future trajectory information.

Result: On multiple long-horizon language agent benchmarks and with different model sizes, HAD consistently outperforms standard on-policy distillation baselines under the same fixed harness configuration. Empirically, agents trained with HAD enter fewer unproductive or repetitive action loops and more frequently recover from earlier mistakes during task execution. The learned students appear to internalize the parts of feedback that are beneficial to encode in the model weights while continuing to rely on the harness for reading and using state information.

Conclusion: Separating the roles of the harness and the language model during distillation leads to more effective and robust student agents. HAD enables a student to learn precisely the teacher-specific behaviors that are not already handled by the harness, using only teacher trajectories and harness records, without extra reward or label engineering. This harness-aware perspective yields better performance on complex, long-horizon tasks and suggests a broader design principle: treat the harness and the model as distinct components during training so that the model learns adaptive use of external feedback while offloading pure state tracking to the surrounding system.

Abstract: Language model agents are deployed with a harness, the software around the model that manages its context, tools, and feedback. When such an agent is distilled into a smaller one, the harness stays in place, so the student mainly needs the teacher-specific abilities that the harness cannot provide, such as acting correctly on harness information. Standard distillation, however, imitates the teacher's full outputs and treats the harness as part of the input. We propose Harness-Aware Distillation (HAD), which focuses distillation on what the teacher adds beyond the harness. HAD complements on-policy distillation with two components: an action preference that contrasts the same teacher's actions with and without the harness information, scored after the student's own reasoning, and a validity check that drops preference pairs whose preferred action contradicts the harness records. We show that the contrast gives the student information that imitating the teacher alone cannot provide, and HAD needs no task rewards, success labels, or future information. Across multiple long-horizon agent benchmarks and models, HAD outperforms on-policy distillation baselines with the same fixed harness. Our analysis shows that HAD enters fewer unproductive loops and recovers from errors more often than the baselines, and suggests that it adaptively keeps learnable feedback in its weights while reading state information from the harness.

</details>


### [126] [TACD: Distilling Efficient Text-to-Motion Models via Terminal Amplification Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02867)
*Wei-Jin Huang,Yuan-Ming Li,Kun-Yu Lin,Wang Luo,Yinlin Zhu,Yue Yu,Shenghao Ye,Junbin Yuan,Fa-Ting Hong,Qing Zhang,Wei-Shi Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes TACD, a new distillation method that significantly speeds up text-to-motion generation while preserving or improving motion quality and text alignment, without needing real motion data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current text-to-motion models rely on many denoising steps and large architectures, leading to slow inference and high memory usage, which hinders deployment. Existing distillation methods for acceleration can degrade motion quality, especially in few-step generation, due to instability in how teacher supervision is applied along the denoising trajectory.

Method: They introduce Terminal-Amplification-Controlled Distillation (TACD), an on-policy flow/distillation scheme. The student generates its own denoising trajectory and is supervised using the teacher’s clean-motion predictions along this trajectory. They analyze a failure mode where velocity matching with a fixed supervision grid overemphasizes errors near the terminal denoising step. TACD ties the final teacher query (the denoising endpoint supervision) to the student’s actual step size so that the effective loss weights in clean-motion space are bounded, avoiding amplification of terminal errors, without changing the student’s inference procedure. They apply TACD to both flow-based HY-Motion and diffusion-based teachers, with compact student architectures and fewer steps.

Result: On HumanML3D and KIT-ML, TACD improves few-step motion generation quality. For an eight-step HY-Motion student, TACD reduces FID by 58% compared to distillation without the proposed bound. For diffusion teachers, an endpoint-matching TACD variant yields four-step students with lower FID and equal or better text-motion retrieval than their 50-step teachers on HumanML3D. On HY-Motion and Kimodo benchmarks, eight-step TACD students with smaller components obtain 7.7–11.9x end-to-end speedups and 3.8–6.7x reductions in peak GPU memory relative to their teachers.

Conclusion: TACD is an effective on-policy distillation framework for compressing text-to-motion models into compact, few-step students without access to real motion data. By controlling terminal amplification in the loss, it stabilizes supervision along the denoising trajectory, enabling students that are both much faster and more memory-efficient while matching or surpassing their teachers’ motion quality and text alignment.

Abstract: Recent text-to-motion models have improved motion quality and instruction following, yet many-step denoising and large model components make deployment slow and memory-intensive.
