We present Terminal-Amplification-Controlled Distillation (TACD), an on-policy approach for training efficient motion generators from text prompts and pretrained teachers, without real-motion training data. Building on segmented on-policy flow distillation, we supervise clean-motion predictions along student-generated trajectories. We identify a failure mode in which velocity matching on a fixed supervision grid repeatedly overweights errors near the denoising endpoint, degrading few-step generation. TACD ties the latest teacher query to the student's step size, bounding the effective loss weights in clean-motion space without changing inference. Experiments on HumanML3D and KIT-ML demonstrate improved few-step generation, including a 58% reduction in eight-step HY-Motion student FID relative to distillation without this bound. For diffusion teachers, the endpoint-matching form of TACD yields four-step students with lower FID and matched or improved text-motion retrieval relative to their 50-step teachers on HumanML3D. On HY-Motion and Kimodo, eight-step students with compact components achieve 7.7-11.9x end-to-end speedups and reduce peak GPU memory by 3.8-6.7x relative to their teachers. Project page: https://vkgo.github.io/TACD/

</details>


### [127] [PsyEvo: A Personalized Counseling Agent That Self-Evolves at Test Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02885)
*Yuting Yan,Shihao Xu,Junhao Yu,Mingcong Zuo,Lu Chen,Nan Xiang,Haiyang Geng,Dongjie Tao,Minghao Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes PsyEvo, a framework that allows large language model–based counselors to adapt in real time to individual clients and improve their counseling policies using online feedback, outperforming ablations in simulated evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a global shortage of trained mental health practitioners, leaving many people without adequate psychological care. LLM-based counseling systems could help scale access, but existing approaches mostly rely on offline training and cannot effectively personalize to each client or learn continuously from ongoing interactions. The authors are motivated to build a framework that supports client-specific adaptation and continual improvement of counseling strategies during test-time use, while remaining data-efficient and robust.

Method: The authors design PsyEvo, an online-learning framework built on top of an LLM counselor with three tightly coupled components: (1) Hierarchical Bayesian Skill Policy (HBSP), which models counseling interventions as discrete skills and maintains a client-specific posterior over which skills to use, updating this posterior after each session based on feedback; (2) Inter-session Listwise Preference Optimization (LiPO), which refines how selected skills are linguistically realized by training a shared response adapter using listwise (i.e., across multiple candidates) preference evidence aggregated across clients; and (3) State-conditioned Ordinal Credit Assignment (SOCA), which generates candidate responses, compares them for self-consistency and quality, and converts these comparisons into ordinal preference signals and trajectory-level credit that feed HBSP and LiPO. The framework is evaluated in a simulated-client setting with shared online cohort adaptation, and component ablations are run to isolate contributions.

Result: In experiments with simulated clients using the PsychEval benchmark, PsyEvo achieves an Overall score of 7.684, outperforming all tested variants that remove or modify individual components across three matched runs. When any of the key components (HBSP, LiPO, or SOCA) is removed, the mean overall score drops by approximately 0.138–0.171 under the shared adaptation setting, indicating that each contributes meaningfully to performance. The framework also demonstrates stable learning behavior in the online setting where data are collected incrementally across a client cohort.

Conclusion: PsyEvo shows that large language model–based counseling systems can be effectively adapted online at test time to individual clients and can continually improve their response policies using feedback from therapeutic interactions. By combining a hierarchical Bayesian personalization layer, cross-client preference-based optimization, and a state-aware credit assignment mechanism, the framework achieves better simulated counseling quality than ablated variants. The results suggest that structured online adaptation is a promising path for scalable, personalized, and higher-quality LLM counseling support, and the released code enables further research and extension.

Abstract: Mental health disorders affect a substantial proportion of the global population, yet a persistent shortage of trained practitioners leaves the majority without adequate care. Large language model (LLM)-based counselors present a promising direction for delivering scalable conversational psychological support. Offline model training alone leaves limited room to adapt to individual clients or to learn from ongoing therapeutic interaction at test time. We introduce PsyEvo, an LLM-based counseling framework that enables both client-specific personalization and response-policy improvement at test time through three components: Hierarchical Bayesian Skill Policy (HBSP) personalizes what intervention to apply by maintaining a per-client skill posterior updated from session feedback; Inter-session Listwise Preference Optimization (LiPO) improves how the selected skill is expressed by updating a shared response adapter from cross-client preference evidence; and State-conditioned Ordinal Credit Assignment (SOCA) supplies candidate preferences and trajectory credit to the two components through consistency-checked comparisons and ordinal projection. In simulated-client evaluation with shared online cohort adaptation, PsyEvo obtains 7.684 Overall on PsychEval and exceeds every component variant in each of three matched runs. Removing individual components lowers mean overall score by 0.138--0.171 under the shared configuration, supporting conditional contributions within the complete scaffold. Our code is available at https://github.com/Lingxi-mental-health/PsyEvo

</details>


### [128] [Interpreting at Write Time: A Policy Ablation for Multi-Goal Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02897)
*Albert Sadowski,Jarosław A. Chudziak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how AI assistants should summarise long interaction histories when they must serve multiple user goals simultaneously, comparing different summary-writing policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-running assistants cannot store full interaction histories and must rely on summaries. But summarising is inherently goal-dependent: different user goals value different parts of the past, and one-shot summarisation decisions are made before it is known which goal will query the memory. The authors want to understand what a memory should optimise for when it must support several standing goals at once.

Method: The authors formalise three summarisation policies: (1) goal-agnostic (neutral) summaries, (2) a single summary optimised jointly for all goals, and (3) separate per-goal summaries that are later read together. They evaluate these policies across multiple models and synthetic event streams, keeping the reading/query mechanism fixed so that only the summarisation (write step) differs. They then compare overlap between summaries, and measure relevance, completeness, and accuracy of retrieved information for different goals.

Result: Summaries optimised for different goals share surprisingly little content: per-goal summaries overlap each other less than a summary overlaps a rewrite of itself. Per-goal summaries perform best across relevance, completeness, and accuracy metrics. The single all-goal summary performs worst, even compared to a neutral summary written with much less token budget.

Conclusion: Choosing what to store in memory should be explicitly conditioned on the intended goal at write time. Interpreting and selecting history with respect to a specific goal substantially improves downstream usefulness, but this benefit is highly goal-specific. Attempts to create a single summary that serves all goals not only fail to capture diverse needs but can underperform simple, goal-agnostic baselines.

Abstract: A long-running assistant cannot keep everything it has seen, so it summarises. Summarising is not neutral: what is kept is chosen against some notion of what the record is for, and that choice is made once, before anyone knows which of the user's standing goals will ask. Goals rarely disagree about what happened. They disagree about which parts of it were worth the space. Once the history is too long to re-read, the summary replaces the stream, and whatever it left out is gone. We ask what a memory should summarise for when it serves several standing goals at once. Three policies answer differently: summarise with no goal in view, write one summary covering every goal, or write one summary per goal and read them together. We compare them across several models and event streams, holding the read step fixed so that only the write differs. The goals do pull apart: summaries written for different goals overlap each other less than a summary overlaps a rewrite of itself. Per-goal summaries win on relevance, completeness and accuracy, and the all-goal summary loses even to the neutral one written at a fraction of its budget. Interpreting at write pays off, but only for the goal that later asks.

</details>


### [129] [LUMOS: Tracing Parametric Knowledge from Training Data to Behavioral Outputs in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02902)
*Seoyeon Ye,Gayoung Kim,Jiyoung Hong,Sookyung Kim,Hyunsoo Cho*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They present LUMOS, a framework that links what an LLM was actually trained on to how it behaves, showing gaps between internal encoding, behavioral use, and self-reflection on knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most current evaluations of LLMs infer what models "know" solely from outputs, without knowing what was in the training data. This makes it impossible to distinguish genuine generalization from memorization or to make evidence-based claims about parametric knowledge. With fully transparent training data now available for some models, the authors want to move from speculation to verifiable, data-grounded analysis of LLM knowledge.

Method: They build LUMOS, a diagnostic framework that traces knowledge from explicit training-data exposure, through internal representations, to final outputs using the OLMo 2 model whose training corpus is fully known. They: (1) determine which facts are present or absent in the training data; (2) measure how well these facts are encoded in the model’s internal representations (separability of rare facts); (3) measure how often the model can behaviorally express those facts; and (4) test self-reflection by asking the model to judge its own answers, including under chain-of-thought prompting.

Result: They find that rare facts present in training are internally well encoded (84% separability) but are expressed correctly in outputs much less often (54%), though this retrieval gap shrinks with model scale. When self-reflecting, the model is well calibrated on seen/trained content (83%) but falls to chance-level performance (49%) on facts it has not seen. Chain-of-thought prompting does not fix this; instead it raises apparent confidence without improving calibration.

Conclusion: Including explicit training-data exposure as an axis in LLM evaluation changes purely output-based, speculative diagnoses into testable, verifiable claims. The authors argue that such data-grounded analysis—linking exposure, internal encoding, behavior, and self-reflection—should become standard practice for assessing knowledge in LLMs.

Abstract: Current analyses of LLMs' parametric knowledge are largely output-centric, drawing conclusions about what a model knows without verifying what it was actually trained on. This leaves fundamental questions, such as whether a correct response reflects genuine generalization or rote memorization, grounded in speculation rather than evidence. To resolve these ambiguities, we introduce LUMOS, a diagnostic framework that traces knowledge along the causal chain from training-data exposure to behavioral output, leveraging OLMo 2 with its fully transparent training corpus. By grounding analysis in verified exposure, we reveal that models internally encode rare facts with high separability (84%) yet fail to express them behaviorally (54%), though this retrieval gap narrows with scale. Furthermore, when models are asked to self-reflect on their own answers, they perform reliably on trained content (83%) but drop to random-baseline levels (49%) on unseen content. This collapse persists even under chain-of-thought prompting, which inflates confidence signals rather than improving calibration. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that incorporating the training-data axis into LLM evaluation transforms speculative diagnoses into verifiable claims, and we advocate that this axis should be a standard component of knowledge assessment in LLMs.

</details>


### [130] [Continual Graph Memory for Mathematical Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02945)
*Junyi Zhang,Jinxi Yu,Eric Hanchen Jiang,Jiachen Lu,Zhi Zhang,Xinjie He,Hyunsik Chae,Ethan Ji,Alexander K Taylor,Vigyan Sahai,Yiwen Kou,Kai-Wei Chang,Raghu Meka,Nanyun Peng,Amit Sahai,Terence Tao,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Ansatz, a mathematical research agent that uses a continual graph-based memory to organize and reuse intermediate proof search results, enabling it to solve and make progress on challenging open mathematical problems autonomously.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier mathematical research problems require long-horizon, large-scale proof searches carried out by many agents in parallel. These searches generate huge numbers of intermediate results (facts, partial proofs, counterexamples, failed attempts). Current systems struggle to organize these artifacts over time or reuse the knowledge they contain across different problems, limiting efficiency and the ability to sustain very long research trajectories. The paper is motivated by the need for a principled, persistent research memory that can structure these explorations and leverage past work for future problems.

Method: The authors design Ansatz, centered on a Continual Graph Memory that stores and organizes all intermediate products of mathematical exploration. This memory is a unified graph where nodes represent facts, plans, counterexamples, and other artifacts, and edges encode explicit relationships such as logical dependence or refinement. On top of this structure, they implement: (1) dependency-aware retrieval that fetches precisely the local context needed for a current reasoning step; (2) an evidence-sensitive curator that updates the current research frontier and distills lessons from both successful and failed attempts; and (3) scoped recall mechanisms that bring back earlier statements and negative results for local re-proving instead of blindly reusing them. They evaluate Ansatz on the First Proof Second Batch tasks and perform component ablations to study the contributions of each system element.

Result: Ansatz successfully solves all ten tasks in the First Proof Second Batch benchmark, showing that it can sustain and resume long-horizon mathematical proof search. The component studies indicate that the structured graph memory, dependency-aware retrieval, and curation/recall mechanisms each contribute meaningfully to performance. Beyond the benchmark, Ansatz autonomously discovers proofs of the Jamison caterpillar conjecture and three Erdős problems (289, 348, 488), and achieves partial progress on several additional open problems, demonstrating both robustness and generalization of its research capabilities.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a continual, graph-structured research memory, coupled with targeted retrieval and cautious reuse of prior work, enables AI agents to conduct extended mathematical investigations and tackle nontrivial open problems without human intervention. This architecture addresses key limitations in existing proof-search agents related to organizing and reusing intermediate results. The authors suggest that Ansatz is a step toward scalable, memory-rich mathematical research systems and that similar memory-based designs could be important for long-horizon scientific discovery more broadly.

Abstract: Using frontier agent harnesses to tackle mathematical research problems has emerged as an effective means of advancing mathematics. However, solving frontier problems in mathematics may require a massive number of agents working in parallel for extended periods to construct proofs, thereby generating an enormous volume of intermediate proof results. Organizing these intermediate results throughout a long-horizon proof-search process and reusing knowledge gained from prior explorations remain major challenges. We present Ansatz, a mathematical research agent built around Continual Graph Memory, a graph-based, evolvable, cross-problem mathematical research memory system that explicitly organizes the entire proof search process and reuses information from exploration trajectories of previous problems. Specifically, we develop a unified graph memory that represents all intermediate exploration results, including facts, plans, and counterexamples, together with edges that explicitly represent the relationships among them; dependency-aware retrieval supplies precisely targeted local context; an evidence-sensitive curator updates the research frontier and distills lessons from prior attempts; and scoped recall surfaces earlier statements and negative findings for local re-proving rather than uncritical reuse. Experiments cover runs across all ten First Proof Second Batch problems, together with four component studies. Ansatz reports closure on all ten research tasks, demonstrating its ability to sustain and resume long-horizon mathematical search. Beyond these problems, Ansatz also produces solutions to the Jamison caterpillar conjecture and Erdős Problems 289, 348, and 488 without human intervention, and makes partial progress on several open problems, illustrating its strong ability to solve open mathematical research problems.

</details>


### [131] [Frequency Is Not Sensitivity Identifying Safety-Sensitive Experts in Sparse MoE LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02910)
*Md Nurul Absar Siddiky,Liuwan Zhu,Yingfei Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that in sparse Mixture-of-Experts language models, selectively suppressing experts chosen by router-gradient sensitivity (rather than by how often they are activated) more effectively weakens safety refusal behavior without harming generation quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models route tokens to a small subset of experts, and prior security/safety work has assumed that the most frequently activated experts are the most important for behaviors like refusal. However, frequency reflects how often an expert is used, not how much it influences model loss or specific behaviors. The authors want a more principled, influence-based way to identify which experts are responsible for safety behaviors, because suppressing such experts can constitute a powerful safety or security attack without retraining.

Method: The authors introduce and evaluate router-gradient sensitivity as a signal: the sensitivity of sequence loss to the router gate weights that select each expert. They compute this for prompts and rank experts accordingly. Using five different MoE architectures, they compare expert ranking by activation frequency versus router-gradient sensitivity on 500 benign and 500 malicious prompts. They then suppress top-ranked experts under two types of budgets: (1) equal numbers of experts suppressed and (2) equal proportions of routing traffic on malicious prompts (1%-5%). They assess safety degradation by measuring how many of 100 held-out malicious prompts the model refuses after expert suppression, comparing gradient-based, activation-based, and random expert removal. They also run a control where they match suppressed expert counts per layer and conduit an exploratory cross-model analysis of how differences in malicious vs. benign expert concentration relate to gradient effects.

Result: Under both budget settings and across five MoE architectures, selecting experts for suppression using router-gradient sensitivity leads to a larger reduction in refusals than selecting by activation frequency in 24 out of 25 tested conditions, and it always beats the mean of ten random-selection trials. The strongest effect appears in the OLMoE model, where refusals on the malicious set drop from 34 to 9 out of 100 prompts (a 73.53% relative reduction) without obvious degradation in generation quality, implying real compliance instead of model failure. Even when they strictly equalize the number of suppressed experts per layer, the gradient-based method still outperforms activation in 23 of 25 conditions, with two ties. The cross-model analysis finds that larger differences in expert concentration between malicious and benign prompts correlate strongly with stronger peak gradient effects (Spearman rho = 0.90, p = 0.083, n = 5).

Conclusion: Router-gradient sensitivity is a more effective and targeted signal than activation frequency for identifying experts that drive safety refusal behavior in sparse MoE language models. Suppressing a small, gradient-selected subset of experts can substantially weaken refusals on malicious prompts without visibly harming general generation, revealing a non-retraining pathway for security-relevant manipulation of model safety. The observed correlation between malicious–benign expert concentration gaps and gradient effects suggests that safety-relevant influence is concentrated in particular experts that can be located using gradient information under realistic attack budgets.

Abstract: Suppressing a small set of routed experts can weaken the safety behavior of a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language model without retraining. Which experts to suppress is therefore a security question, and the usual answer is activation frequency, but frequency measures use, not influence. We test an alternative: router-gradient sensitivity, the sensitivity of the sequence loss to the gate weights that select an expert. Across five MoE architectures, we rank experts by each signal on 500 benign and 500 malicious prompts and measure refusal on 100 held-out malicious prompts under two budgets: equal expert counts and equal nominal malicious routing traffic (1%-5%). Under each of the two budgets, router-gradient selection reduces refusals more than activation in 24 of 25 conditions, and more than a ten-trial random mean in all 25. The largest effect is in OLMoE, where refusals fall from 34 to 9 of 100 prompts (73.53% relative) with no degraded outputs, indicating substantive compliance rather than broken generation. After matching expert counts in every layer, gradient selection still produces greater refusal reduction than activation in 23 of 25 conditions, with two ties. An exploratory cross-model analysis links larger malicious-versus-benign concentration gaps to greater peak gradient effects (rho = 0.90; exact two-sided p = 0.083, n = 5). Together, the results support gradient selection under the tested budgets.

</details>


### [132] [Personalized Automatic Speech Recognition for a Dysarthric and Tracheostomic Speaker using Artificial Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03017)
*David Nadrchal,Monorama Swain,Florian Schmid,Gerhard Widmer,Paul Primus*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Personalized ASR for a Czech speaker with severe dysarthria using a new dataset and multi-stage fine-tuning of Whisper, cutting character errors by half.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: People with a permanent tracheal stoma and severe dysarthria often produce speech that is unintelligible to standard ASR systems and even to untrained human listeners, limiting their communication autonomy. There is a lack of public, realistic datasets and tailored ASR methods for such extreme speech impairments, especially for less-resourced languages like Czech.

Method: The authors collect 33 hours of annotated speech from a single Czech speaker with a permanent tracheal stoma, using a specially designed "artificial conversation" protocol to elicit engaging, realistic dialogues. They build a personalized ASR system by taking Whisper Base and applying a multi-stage training pipeline: (1) fine-tune on standard Czech speech, (2) fine-tune on acoustically simulated tracheostomic speech, and (3) fine-tune on the target speaker's own data. They then evaluate the resulting system in three near real-time usage scenarios: scripted conversations, question answering, and spontaneous dialogue.

Result: The personalized ASR system achieves a 50% relative reduction in Character Error Rate compared to the unadapted Whisper Base baseline. Its recognition accuracy for isolated utterances exceeds the average accuracy of the speaker's human assistants when recognizing the same acoustic material.

Conclusion: With appropriate data collection and staged fine-tuning, even highly atypical and severely impaired speech can be recognized by ASR systems at practically useful accuracy levels. The new dataset and pipeline show that helpful ASR is feasible for a Czech speaker with severe dysarthria, and the positive user feedback underscores its real-world value.

Abstract: This work presents an automatic speech recognition (ASR) system personalized for a Czech speaker with a permanent tracheal stoma and severe dysarthria rendering their speech unintelligible to untrained listeners. We release a public dataset containing 33 annotated hours of the speaker's speech, collected using a novel "artificial conversation" protocol designed for high engagement and dialogue realism. We propose a multi-stage training pipeline based on Whisper Base: fine-tuning on standard Czech speech, acoustically simulated tracheostomic speech, and the speaker's data. We evaluate the system across three near real-time scenarios: scripted conversations, question answering, and spontaneous dialogue, achieving a 50\% relative reduction in Character Error Rate compared to Whisper Base baseline and surpassing the average recognition accuracy of their assistants in acoustic recognition of isolated utterances. We demonstrate that even for severely impeded speech, a helpful ASR is achievable, as evidenced by the quantitative results and the feedback from the speaker.

</details>


### [133] [HASTE: Evolving Agent Harnesses Against Emerging Attacks Using Sparse Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02920)
*Xiqiao Xiong,Moxin Li,Zhixin Ma,Ouxiang Li,Wenjie Wang,Fuli Feng,Xiangnan He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces HASTE, a multi-agent framework that automatically evolves safety harnesses for AI agents using sparse threat evidence, reducing attack success rates while maintaining utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent safety harnesses rely heavily on manual updates and tuning to block new attack techniques. However, attacks on AI agents evolve quickly, and often the only available information about new threats is sparse—short descriptions or a few example attacks from reports or preprints. This mismatch creates a lag in defenses and limits the scalability and responsiveness of harness-based safety mechanisms. The authors aim to automate and accelerate the evolution of safety harnesses using limited, real-world threat evidence.

Method: The authors propose HASTE, a multi-agent adversarial framework. It has two key components: (1) a safety-specification generation agent that, given sparse threat evidence and evaluation feedback, synthesizes and iteratively refines safety specifications that describe what behaviors should be disallowed or constrained; and (2) an attack-case generation agent that generates new adversarial test cases to probe the current harness for remaining vulnerabilities. The system operates in a feedback loop: the current harness is updated according to the latest safety specifications, then exposed to generated attack cases; the observed failures and successes are fed back to both agents so they can improve future specifications and attacks. This process runs across different backbone models, attack types, and forms of evidence.

Result: Across diverse backbone models, attack types, and types of sparse threat evidence, HASTE substantially lowers the attack success rate relative to baseline harnesses that are manually constructed or less adaptive. At the same time, it maintains or largely preserves performance on benign tasks, indicating that the automatically evolved harnesses do not excessively restrict normal agent functionality. Empirical evaluations demonstrate consistent gains in robustness and show that HASTE can generalize safety improvements beyond the specific attacks present in the initial sparse evidence.

Conclusion: HASTE provides an effective and scalable way to evolve agent safety harnesses from limited threat information. By coupling safety-specification generation with adversarial attack-case generation in a closed feedback loop, the framework can anticipate and counter emerging attacks beyond the originally observed evidence, leading to more robust and adaptable defenses while preserving benign-task utility. This suggests that automated, data-efficient harness evolution is a promising direction for keeping pace with rapidly evolving agent threats.

Abstract: Agent harnesses play a critical role in defenses by enforcing safety constraints to prevent unsafe actions. However, rapidly emerging attacks outpace manual harness adaptation, motivating automated harness evolution. Yet the signals available for harness evolution are often sparse, such as brief descriptions or a few attack examples in threat reports and preprints. To address this limitation, we introduce HASTE, a multi-agent framework that evolves agent harnesses from sparse threat evidence through an adversarial interplay between safety-specification generation and attack-case generation. Safety specifications guide harness updates toward addressing identified safety vulnerabilities, while attack cases probe for remaining safety vulnerabilities after each update. By feeding evaluation outcomes back into both processes, HASTE enables harness evolution against emerging attacks beyond the initially observed evidence. Experimental results across multiple backbone models, attack types, and evidence forms show that HASTE consistently reduces attack success rates while preserving benign-task utility. The code is available at https://github.com/xxiqiao/HASTE.

</details>


### [134] [Verifiable, Articulable, and Tacit Components of Preference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03025)
*Alexander Spangher,Sheldon Huang,Andreas Haupt,Noah D. Goodman,Diyi Yang,Daniel E. Ho,Sanmi Koyejo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CreativePreferences, a large dataset and modeling framework to study how much of human preference—especially about creative quality—is tacit (not easily articulated or verified) and measures systematic gaps between tacit preferences and explicit rubrics or verifiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many important human judgments (e.g., what makes a story gripping or a proof elegant) are guided by tacit knowledge, yet modern AI alignment and training methods rely heavily on explicit rubrics, constitutions, and verifiers (RLAIF, RLVR). This creates a potential mismatch: AI systems may optimize for what is easy to articulate or verify rather than what people actually prefer. The authors want to quantify and understand this mismatch—articulability and verifiability gaps—across diverse domains, including ones usually seen as fully objective like math or software engineering.

Method: The authors build CreativePreferences, a large-scale preference dataset with 2.8M texts and 317M human comparison judgments across seven creative domains and 42 benchmark tasks. They train three main types of models on this data: (V) executable/verifier-like programs and metrics, (A) rubric-based models using explicit articulated criteria from rubric banks, and (VAT) densely trained models that directly fit the full preference data. They then define and measure: (a) articulability gaps (difference between VAT and VA) and (b) verifiability gaps (difference between VAT and V). To rigorously estimate these gaps, they introduce a measurement procedure that (1) searches for articulable/verifiable metrics, (2) detects spurious correlates, and (3) uses a capture-recapture-inspired method to estimate the contribution of undiscovered metrics and tight bounds on the gaps.

Result: They find robust, nontrivial articulability and verifiability gaps across all seven domains, including domains traditionally assumed to be fully verifiable, such as mathematics and software engineering, and domains focused on truth claims or novelty like news, patents, and peer review. Peer review and creative writing exhibit the largest articulability gaps. The measured gaps grow as more people are involved in making the judgments, consistent with theories of collective tacit knowledge. Empirically, full preference-trained models (VAT) align better with human aggregate preferences than models constrained by articulated rubrics or verifiers, and they often disagree with the explicit criteria. Furthermore, they observe an effect analogous to Goodhart’s law: when a tacit preference dimension is articulated into metrics, optimizing the articulated metric tends to shift preferences away from the original tacit dimension.

Conclusion: Tacit knowledge forms a substantial, measurable component of human preferences, even in domains considered objective or verifiable. Explicit rubrics and automated verifiers systematically fail to capture this component, generating articulability and verifiability gaps that grow with collective judgment. Models trained directly on rich preference data can better match human judgments than rubric- or verifier-based approaches, but articulated criteria can distort what is optimized. The authors recommend: distinguishing when prompting and explicit rubrics are appropriate versus when judgments should remain with humans; refining learning mechanisms to better capture tacit aspects of preferences; and treating Goodhart-like shifts as a central concern in alignment and evaluation design.

Abstract: What makes a short story gripping; a news article newsworthy; or a math proof elegant? These constructs resist articulation or verification; their meaning is at least partially tacit. However, modern AI models are improved primarily via articulated constitutions, rubrics and verifiers (i.e. in RLAIF and RLVR); tacit components of preferences are typically understudied. We introduce a large, labeled preference dataset CreativePreferences, containing 2.8M texts labeled by 317M human preference judgments across 7 creative domains, with 42 benchmark tasks. We model these labels with executable programs, rubric banks and densely trained models (V, A and VAT, respectively). We observe robust articulability gaps, VAT-VA; and verifiability gaps, VAT-V; we estimate upper and lower bounds for each gap with a novel measurement approach that discovers articulable and verifiable metrics, identifies spurious variables and estimates the value of undiscovered metrics using capture-recapture. These gaps occur across all domains, even in domains traditionally treated as fully verifiable: correctness-centered domains (i.e. mathematics and software engineering) and claim- and novelty-centric domains (i.e. news, patents, peer review). The size of the gap varies based on domain (e.g. peer review and creative writing have the largest articulability gaps) and widens as more people take part in the judgment, consistent with Collins' collective tacit knowledge. We show two consequences: (1) on human generations, the full model more closely matches human preferences, often in disagreement with articulated criteria, and (2) in an analogy to Goodhart's law, articulating preference shifts it away from the tacit dimension. Articulability and verifiability gaps are consequential; we give recommendations on when tasks can be prompted; how learning mechanisms might improve; and when to leave judgments with humans.

</details>


### [135] [Positive-Unlabeled Learning for Agent Safety False Alarm Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02925)
*Xichen Yan,Chongyang Gao,Kezhen Chen,Guangyi Zhang,Jiaqi Wu,Lixu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a PU-learning-based ranking framework to better identify false alarms produced by safety monitors for language-model agents without requiring labeled alarm data, significantly improving precision-recall performance and the efficiency of human review.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety monitors for language-model agents tend to be conservative, triggering many alarms to avoid missing harmful behavior. This creates a large number of false alarms, which wastes human review effort and undermines trust in the monitor. Existing monitor scores mix true and false alarms, so choosing a good cutoff still needs extensive manual checking. In realistic settings, only some non-alarmed trajectories are manually confirmed safe, while alarms themselves are unlabeled, turning the problem of auditing false alarms into a positive-unlabeled ranking task. However, standard PU methods fail because the observed positives (accepted safe references) are biased: they differ systematically from the hidden positives the user cares about (safe but flagged alarms), due to the monitor’s own selection behavior.

Method: The paper introduces a three-part framework tailored to monitor-induced selection bias in positive-unlabeled ranking. First, Trust-aware PU Supervision adapts the distribution of known safe references toward the alarm domain and reduces the risk that potential false alarms are pushed too strongly toward the negative class, mitigating representation mismatch. Second, Reliability-gated Rank Distillation trains a student ranking model by aggregating and distilling orderings from multiple PU reference models, but only where those models agree sufficiently, to obtain more reliable supervision from noisy PU signals. Third, Consensus-guided Structural Refinement further improves the ranking by incorporating structural information: hierarchical relationships among safe references, relational structure among alarms themselves, and the predicted consensus confidence over references, refining the student model’s ordering of alarms. The framework is applied without changing the underlying safety monitor and relies solely on unlabeled alarms plus a small set of safe, non-alarmed trajectories.

Result: On multiple mainstream LLM safety monitors, the proposed method achieves a macro area under the precision-recall curve (AUPRC) of 0.6444, outperforming eight positive-unlabeled baselines by 5.27 to 16.98 absolute percentage points. Against PULDA, the strongest baseline, the method recovers 33.3% more false alarms under a 5% human review budget, indicating a substantially more efficient prioritization of likely-false alarms for manual inspection.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that carefully addressing monitor-induced selection bias in PU ranking can greatly improve the identification of false alarms in safety monitoring for language-model agents, using only a limited set of verified-safe trajectories and unlabeled alarms. Their two-stage PU supervision and distillation framework, enhanced with consensus-guided structural refinement, delivers significantly better ranking quality without altering the underlying monitor. This allows practitioners to review more genuine false alarms under the same budget, improving both the efficiency and reliability of safety auditing in tool-using LLM systems.

Abstract: Safety monitors help safeguard language-model agents interacting with external tools and environments, but conservative monitoring can generate many false alarms, consuming extensive review resources and weakening trust in alerts. Because false and genuine alarms often remain interleaved in native monitor scores, obtaining a reliable cutoff still requires substantial manual verification. In practice, a small set of verified-safe non-alarmed trajectories may be available while alarms remain unlabeled, naturally casting false-alarm auditing as a positive-unlabeled (PU) ranking problem. The key challenge is monitor-induced selection, since observed safe references are accepted by the monitor, while the hidden safe alarms of interest are precisely those it incorrectly flags, making the observed positives poorly representative of the positives to be recovered. To address this challenge, we propose a two-stage framework in which Trust-aware PU Supervision adapts safe references toward the alarm domain and protects plausible false alarms from excessive negative pressure, while Reliability-gated Rank Distillation consolidates consistent ordering preferences from multiple PU reference models into a single student. Consensus-guided Structural Refinement then improves the student ranking using hierarchical safe-reference support, alarm relations, and predicted reference consensus. The framework requires no alarm safety labels for fitting and leaves the underlying monitor unchanged. Across mainstream safety monitors, our method achieves a macro AUPRC of $0.6444$, outperforming eight evaluated PU baselines by 5.27--16.98 absolute percentage points; compared with PULDA, the strongest evaluated PU baseline, it recovers 33.3% more false alarms at a 5% review budget.

</details>


### [136] [Peer Influence across Heterogeneous AI Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03095)
*Frida Nøhr Laustsen,Marie Haahr Petersen,Victoria Popa,Ariel Flint,Romualdo Pastor-Satorras,Andrea Baronchelli,Luca Maria Aiello*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how and when one AI language model persuades another to change its answer during disagreements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As multi-agent AI systems increasingly use multiple language models together, their final decisions depend on how models influence each other in interaction. Yet we lack a clear understanding of which models tend to persuade others, which tend to be persuaded, and whether known single-model metrics like scale or confidence can predict these dynamics. This gap makes it hard to design safe, reliable multi-agent systems.

Method: The authors formalize persuasion as the probabilistic change in an agent’s decision after seeing a dissenting peer’s answer and explanation. They run controlled pairwise interactions between seven open-weight language models of different families and sizes on three language understanding tasks. For each model pair, they measure how frequently a receiver model changes its initial answer after one exchange, and analyze the relationship between persuasion patterns and factors like model size, standalone decision consistency, and expressed certainty. They also decompose persuasion into properties of the speaker (persuasiveness) and the listener (susceptibility).

Result: They find that persuasion between models is strong: when models initially disagree, the receiver often abandons its original answer after one interaction. However, persuasion dynamics are not well predicted by model scale, standalone certainty, or isolated performance. Some models that are highly consistent when acting alone are very easily persuaded in interaction. Smaller models can be as persuasive as larger ones and can resist larger models’ influence to a similar degree. The magnitude of decision shifts is driven more by how susceptible the listening model is than by the inherent persuasiveness of the speaking model. Persuasion patterns turn out to be highly pair-specific, with heterogeneity between models sometimes increasing and sometimes decreasing overall persuasion. In some pairings, a small model can reliably overturn the decisions of a much larger one.

Conclusion: Persuasion in multi-model interactions is strong, asymmetrical, and highly dependent on the specific model pairing. Traditional single-model attributes—such as size, confidence, and isolated consistency—do not reliably predict which model will persuade the other. Instead, listener susceptibility is a key driver of decision change. Consequently, the behavior and reliability of multi-agent AI systems cannot be inferred from component models in isolation; they must be empirically evaluated in the exact combinations and interaction patterns in which they are deployed.

Abstract: When two AI agents disagree, who persuades whom? As multi-agent systems increasingly combine language models of different families and sizes, the answer can determine which judgments survive interaction. Measuring persuasion as the probabilistic shift in an agent's decision after a single exchange with a dissenting peer, we test seven open-weight models across three language understanding tasks. We find that persuasion is strong: when models disagree, receivers often abandon their initial judgment after seeing a peer's answer and explanation. Surprisingly, however, neither standalone certainty nor model scale reliably predicts persuasion dynamics. Models producing almost perfectly consistent decisions in isolation can be among the most susceptible to persuasion, and small models can match larger ones as persuaders and resist their influence just as effectively. Furthermore, we show that the size of the shift depends more on the susceptibility of the listener than on the persuasiveness of the speaker. Persuasion patterns are therefore specific to each model pairing, with heterogeneity amplifying persuasion in some combinations and suppressing it in others, allowing a dissenting agent running a small model to overturn the judgments of a much larger one. These findings show that the behavior of interacting models cannot be inferred from their individual properties but must be evaluated in the combinations in which they will operate.

</details>


### [137] [When to Compile a Computer-Use Agent? Measuring Payback and Making Compilation Decisions for Token Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02932)
*Yulong Ming,Jie Xu,Zihan Wu,Xiaohua Jia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PACE decides when to compile repeatedly executed GUI tasks into programs to save token costs, while rigorously controlling worst-case cost relative to using only an agent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent-based GUI operation is token-expensive when the same procedures are repeatedly executed. Naively compiling these into programs can backfire because compilation attempts are costly, may require repair, and can fail; additionally, future reuse and GUI drift are uncertain, so it is unclear when compilation will pay for itself.

Method: The authors introduce PACE, which consists of (1) a measurement protocol that logs the costs of successful and failed compilation attempts and compares agent versus compiled-program execution on matched inputs to estimate per-use savings and payback counts; and (2) an online compilation algorithm that uses these empirical estimates plus observed task-arrival patterns and compilation outcomes to decide whether to execute via agent or attempt compilation. The algorithm enforces a cumulative budget, checking execution and compilation charges against it, and is analyzed under explicit action-cost assumptions.

Result: Theoretically, the algorithm guarantees that after each task arrival, the total cost incurred is at most (1+ε) times the cost of always using the agent. Empirically, for tasks where compilation succeeds, the measured payback (ignoring the source agent runs) typically occurs within 2–16 uses. In simulations based on recorded task arrivals, PACE reduces token costs by an average of 17.3% compared with ReAct, 24.9% compared with an AutoRPA-style baseline, and 17.3% compared with a ToolPro-style baseline when ε=0.25.

Conclusion: PACE provides a principled, data-driven way to decide when to compile GUI procedures into programs, achieving substantial token savings while keeping worst-case cost close to that of using an agent alone. Its measurement protocol and online decision rule show that safe, payback-aware compilation is feasible even under uncertain compilation success and task reuse.

Abstract: Compiling GUI procedures that agents execute repeatedly into programs can reduce their token costs. However, measuring payback and deciding when to compile have two challenges. First, compilation costs are uncertain because attempts can require repair and still fail to produce a usable program. Second, future reuse is unknown because tasks may stop arriving or GUI drift may stop the program from working. To address these challenges, we propose PACE (Payback-Aware Compilation from Experience), a system with a measurement protocol and an online compilation algorithm. The measurement protocol records successful and failed compilation costs, and compares agent and program execution costs on matched task inputs to estimate per-use savings and payback counts. Using these measurements, the online algorithm compares estimated future savings with compilation costs, including failed attempts, based on past task arrivals and compilation outcomes. It checks execution and compilation charges against a cumulative budget determined by observed task arrivals before allowing either action. Under stated action-cost assumptions, total cost after each arrival is at most $1+ε$ times the cost of running every task with the agent. For successful compilation attempts, estimated payback counts excluding source agent runs are 2-16 uses. In simulations using recorded task arrivals, PACE reduces token costs by 17.3% compared with ReAct, 24.9% with the AutoRPA adaptation, and 17.3% with the ToolPro adaptation on average ($ε=0.25$).

</details>


### [138] [Gains and Collapse in On-Policy Distillation:A Reinforcement Learning Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03185)
*Han Cui,Jianhao Yan,Yun Luo,Hongbo Zhang,Zhizhang Fu,Yue Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes why on-policy distillation (OPD) sometimes improves language models and sometimes causes long, repetitive, collapsed outputs, explaining it through an implicit reinforcement learning lens and proposing mitigations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OPD is widely used for language model post-training and often yields strong performance, but it can also lead to pathological behaviors like excessively long and repetitive generations. The field lacks a clear theoretical and empirical understanding of when and why OPD helps versus harms, which is critical for safely scaling post-training methods.

Method: The authors reinterpret OPD as a form of reinforcement learning where the teacher acts as an implicit reward model that evaluates student rollouts, even for behaviors the teacher itself rarely produces. They conduct empirical studies to test how OPD changes student behavior and capabilities, analyze when the implicit reward aligns or misaligns with actual quality, and examine conditions under which reward hacking occurs. They then design and test mitigation strategies such as masking unhealthy responses during training and initializing from supervised fine-tuning (SFT).

Result: They find that OPD mainly changes the sampling distribution of the student rather than expanding its underlying capabilities: when the teacher’s implicit reward is reliable, correct responses become easier to sample. However, when the teacher’s preferences are misaligned with actual quality, OPD incentivizes and amplifies overlong, repetitive rollouts—a form of reward hacking—despite the teacher rarely generating such outputs itself. Experiments show that masking low-quality or unhealthy responses and starting from an SFT-initialized model significantly reduce collapse and reward hacking behaviors.

Conclusion: OPD should be understood primarily as amplifying behaviors that the teacher’s implicit feedback favors, not as directly transferring the teacher’s generative abilities. The key determinant of success is how reliably the teacher evaluates student outputs. By improving the reliability of this implicit reward (e.g., via masking bad rollouts and good initialization), practitioners can gain OPD’s performance benefits while mitigating collapse into degenerate, repetitive text.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has become an important approach to language model post-training. However, despite its performance gains, OPD can also collapse into excessively long and repetitive generation, and the mechanism underlying these divergent outcomes remains poorly understood. We explain these outcomes through a reinforcement learning perspective: the teacher implicitly rewards student behaviors, even those it rarely exhibits itself. From this perspective, our experiments show that OPD improves performance without expanding the student's capabilities. When the implicit reward model is reliable, OPD makes correct responses easier to sample. In contrast, when the preference misaligns with quality, reward hacking happens: the implicit reward model amplifies overlong, repetitive student rollouts, even though it rarely generates such text itself. Guided by this diagnosis, we find that masking unhealthy responses during training and using SFT initialization can each effectively mitigate the collapse. Together, these findings show that OPD amplifies student behaviors favored by the teacher's implicit feedback, shifting the focus from how well the teacher generates to how reliably it evaluates student rollouts. Our code is available at https://github.com/HancCui/opd_hacking.

</details>


### [139] [KV$^2$: A Self-Refining KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03198)
*Johannes Wesch,Danni Liu,Jan Niehues*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes KV^2, a query-agnostic KV-cache compression method that drastically reduces memory cost for long-context LMs without reprocessing the full context, achieving much better quality-cost tradeoffs than prior methods at tight cache budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context language models require storing large key-value (KV) caches, whose memory footprint becomes a bottleneck, especially in reusable settings where a single long prefill is followed by many queries. Existing query-agnostic compression either uses cheap but inaccurate heuristics or expensive full-context reconstruction for accurate scoring. There is a need for a method that preserves important context while keeping compression inexpensive and scalable.

Method: KV^2 performs query-agnostic KV-cache compression via selective reconstruction. It first uses a lightweight proxy scoring mechanism to quickly estimate the importance of in-context tokens and select an informative subset. Then it reprocesses only this subset of tokens to compute refined, final eviction scores for the KV cache. This avoids reconstructing the entire context while still basing decisions on higher-fidelity signals for critical tokens.

Result: On benchmarks RULER, Needle-in-a-Haystack, and LongBench, KV^2 consistently outperforms prior KV-cache compression baselines, with advantages that grow as the compression budget becomes tighter. On RULER 16K at a 2% KV-cache budget, it improves average score by more than 40 percentage points over the next-best baseline. On LongBench, it achieves the best average performance across 2%-10% cache budgets while also reducing compression-time runtime and peak memory relative to full-context reconstruction approaches.

Conclusion: Reusable KV-cache compression for long-context models can be done effectively without reprocessing the entire prompt. By combining a cheap proxy scorer with selective reconstruction of only the most informative tokens, KV^2 achieves superior accuracy-memory tradeoffs, especially at very low KV-cache budgets, while also being more efficient in runtime and peak memory than full-context reconstruction methods.

Abstract: The memory footprint of the key-value (KV) cache constrains the practical use of long-context models, and it dominates cost when one prefilled context must later serve many different queries. In this reusable setting, query-agnostic compression trades cost against quality: lightweight estimators are cheap but less accurate, whereas full-context reconstruction scoring is more accurate yet reprocesses the entire prompt. We introduce KV$^2$, a query-agnostic KV-cache compression method based on selective reconstruction. KV$^2$ first uses a lightweight proxy scorer to identify informative in-context tokens, then reprocesses only this subset to compute final eviction scores. On RULER, Needle-in-a-Haystack, and LongBench, KV$^2$'s margin over baselines widens as the budget tightens: on RULER 16K at a 2% KV-cache budget it improves the average score over the next-best baseline by more than 40 percentage points, and on LongBench it attains the highest average across 2%-10% budgets at lower compression-stage runtime and peak memory than full-context reconstruction. Reusable KV-cache compression thus does not require reprocessing the full context. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/KVsquared-0B97.

</details>


### [140] [Reasoning with Evidence, Not Merely Rationales: Verifiable Preference Proofs for LLM-Based Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02968)
*Yu Hou,Nathaniel Kang,Pengkai Wang,Hua Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes PROVE-REC, a two-pass framework that makes LLM-based recommendations verifiable by ensuring that stated preference rationales are both grounded in historical evidence and influential on the final ranking, achieving better accuracy and more faithful explanations than strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based recommenders can infer user preferences and generate natural-language rationales, but those rationales often do not truly correspond to the evidence used or meaningfully affect the final recommendation. This creates a grounding-influence gap: explanations may be weakly supported by history or may not drive the ranking, undermining trust, interpretability, and reliability. The authors are motivated to create a recommendation framework where the reasoning path from interaction history to preference claims to final recommendation is explicit, testable, and actually used by the model.

Method: They introduce PROVE-REC, a general two-pass framework. In Pass A, the model processes the user’s full pre-target interaction history and compresses it into a structured "preference proof" that contains both positive and avoidance preference claims, each explicitly linked to selected evidence entries in the history. In Pass B, a separate prediction stage generates the next-item recommendation using only this proof and the referenced evidence, so the model cannot shortcut around the reasoning path. To verify that claims are grounded in evidence, they compare the effect of masking the selected evidence entries against masking matched control entries. To verify that the proof influences the recommendation, they delete individual preference claims and measure the drop in the target item’s ranking margin. They also add a ranking-preservation objective so that compressing the history into proofs does not degrade recommendation quality. The framework is evaluated through comprehensive experiments and ablations on multiple real-world datasets.

Result: Across diverse real-world datasets, PROVE-REC consistently outperforms strong sequential, generative, and LLM-augmented recommendation baselines, with gains up to 7.45% on standard metrics. Ablation studies show that both the two-pass architecture and the verification-based objectives are key contributors to performance: removing them degrades accuracy and faithfulness. Empirically, the system’s generated preference claims are better supported by specific historical interactions and have demonstrably higher causal influence on the recommended ranking, while still preserving or improving overall ranking quality compared to baselines.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that PROVE-REC successfully closes much of the grounding-influence gap in LLM-based recommendation. By structuring recommendation as a verifiable reasoning process—history → evidence-linked preference proof → recommendation—the framework produces explanations that are both faithful to user history and genuinely influential on the final ranking. The method improves recommendation accuracy over strong alternatives and offers a principled way to test and enforce explanation faithfulness, suggesting a promising direction for more trustworthy LLM-driven recommender systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can infer user preferences from interaction histories and reviews, yet the rationales they generate may not reflect the information actually used for recommendation. A preference claim may be weakly supported by its selected evidence, or may have little effect on the final ranking. We refer to these two failures as the grounding-influence gap. We introduce PROVE-REC, a general framework for verifiable preference reasoning in LLM-based recommendation. Pass A converts the complete pre-target history into a compact preference proof consisting of positive and avoidance claims linked to selected evidence entries. Pass B predicts the next item using only the proof and its selected evidence, preventing the recommender from bypassing the reasoning path. To verify evidence-to-proof grounding, we compare the effect of masking selected evidence with masking a comparable control entry. To verify proof-to-recommendation influence, we remove a preference claim and measure the resulting decrease in the target item's ranking margin. A ranking-preservation objective further retains useful information from the complete history. Comprehensive experiments on wide-ranging real-world datasets demonstrate that PROVE-REC consistently outperforms strong sequential, generative, and LLM-enhanced baselines, with improvements of up to 7.45%. Controlled ablations confirm the effectiveness of the two-pass architecture and verification objectives. Moreover, PROVE-REC produces claims that are more strongly grounded in historical evidence and more influential to recommendation while preserving ranking quality.

</details>


### [141] [Multilingual GSM-Symbolic: What determines capability transfer across languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03367)
*Kenneth Enevoldsen,Riley Herchert,Sofie Mosegaard,Dan Saattrup Smart,Simon Enni,Isaac Chung,Sofie Bruun,Ayush Sunil Munot,Max Müller-Eberstein,Adnan El-Assadi,Elisa Bassignana,Gianluca Barmina,Hafsteinn Einarsson,Iben Nyholm Debess,Linda Freienthal,Lukas Galke Poech,Mike Zhang,Nicolas Legrand,Vladimir Salnikov,Yevhen Kostiuk,Zafar Hussain,Sagandeep Kaur,Agnes Toftgård,Marie Mattson,Kristoffer Nielbo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Multilingual GSM-Symbolic, a large symbolic math dataset across 15 languages, and uses it to quantify how model size, language resources, reasoning ability, and typological distance jointly determine cross-lingual capability transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of cross-lingual transfer are limited: datasets across languages are incomparable, prone to saturation, and rarely allow joint analysis of multiple determinants of transfer. This makes it hard to predict how capabilities acquired in one language carry over to others, and forces exhaustive, expensive evaluations across language pairs instead of targeting the true bottlenecks for low-resource languages.

Method: The authors build Multilingual GSM-Symbolic, a multilingual math reasoning benchmark with 30,000 item-matched question–answer pairs across 15 languages. Problems are generated from symbolic templates, allowing millions of controlled variations and reducing overfitting. Using this dataset, they perform a joint statistical analysis (with standardized effect sizes β) to estimate how model size, language resource level, reasoning capability, and typological distance each contribute to performance and interact, and they construct predictive models of performance on unseen language–model combinations.

Result: They find that the largest positive determinants of cross-lingual capability are model size (β = 1.77), language resource level (β = 0.77), and reasoning capability (β = 0.67), while typological distance harms transfer (β = −0.25). Joint estimation reveals tradeoffs such as a 32B model in a low-resource language like Marathi performing similarly to a 10B model in English. Model size and reasoning especially help close the gap between low- and high-resource languages (interaction β ≈ −0.27 and −0.20), but have limited effect when languages are typologically distant. Their framework explains 92% of between-language variance and 23% of model-by-language variance, predicting performance on unseen languages within 6 percentage points (r = 0.96), further reducible to 4.19pp when adding a small number of language-specific template measurements.

Conclusion: A principled, template-based multilingual math dataset enables rigorous, joint analysis of cross-lingual transfer factors. Model size, language resources, and reasoning ability are the dominant levers for improving transfer, especially for low-resource but typologically similar languages, whereas typological distance remains a stubborn barrier. Their predictive framework can estimate performance on new languages with modest measurement, reducing the need for large downstream datasets and guiding developers on where to invest effort to improve multilingual models.

Abstract: We understand little about how capabilities acquired in one language carry over to another, or what governs this transfer: evaluations rely on incomparable, saturation-prone datasets and rarely examine its determinants jointly. Identifying what predicts transfer would let us avoid exhaustive evaluation across all language pairs and let developers target the factors that limit performance in low-resource languages. To evaluate cross-lingual capability transfer, we introduce Multilingual GSM-Symbolic, an extensible multilingual mathematical dataset covering 30,000 item-matched question-answer pairs and spanning 15 languages. It utilises symbolic templates to prevent overfitting and ensure generalisation by allowing generation of millions of high-quality variations from a single sample. Using Multilingual GSM-Symbolic, we quantify the largest determinants of capability as model size ($β= 1.77$), language resource level ($β= 0.77$), reasoning ($β= 0.67$) and typological distance ($β= -0.25$). This joint estimation allows these determinants to be expressed in terms of one another: a 32B model evaluated in Marathi performs like a 10B model in English. Our findings have important implications for model developers, showing that model size and reasoning narrow the performance gap between low- and high-resource languages ($β= -0.27$ and $β= -0.20$, respectively), while similar levers have little or no effect on typologically distant languages.
  Overall, our analysis framework explains 92% of between-language variation, but only 23% of the model-by-language variation, and predicts a model's performance on an unseen language within 6.0pp (r=.96). Incorporating measurements from just 10 templates in the target language reduces this to 4.19pp, enabling reasonable estimates of performance with little or no downstream dataset.

</details>


### [142] [CreateScore: Domain-Theory-Informed Bayesian Routing for LLM-Based CV Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02972)
*Rupsa Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CreateScore uses a Bayesian network to route CV evaluation decisions between a small and a large LLM to save cost, but its uncertainty signal fails to reliably flag small-model errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating CVs against detailed rubrics with powerful LLMs is expensive if every candidate and every criterion must be scored by a large model. There is a need for a principled, auditable way to use cheaper models most of the time while escalating only difficult or ambiguous decisions to a more capable, costly LLM, without building a fully autonomous hiring system.

Method: The authors design CreateScore, a hand-crafted, causally motivated Bayesian network with Dirichlet-multinomial conditional probability tables that maps evidence from CVs to posterior uncertainty over rubric criteria. For each criterion, if posterior uncertainty is below a calibrated threshold, a local 8B-parameter LLM’s decision is accepted; otherwise, the case is escalated to a 120B-parameter reference model. The escalation threshold is tuned on a training fold to target resolving about 70% of decisions locally, then fixed. They test this routing policy on 200 synthetic data-science CVs scored on five criteria and compare token usage, agreement rates, and the ability of the uncertainty scores to distinguish correct vs incorrect 8B-model decisions. They further analyze an earlier flawed evaluation where truncated reasoning-model outputs were inadvertently replaced with local labels.

Result: On the held-out test set of 61 synthetic CVs (305 criterion-level decisions), 77.7% of decisions were resolved by the 8B model, slightly exceeding the 70% target. Relative to always using the 120B model, this routing cut token consumption by 65.2%. Exact agreement with the 120B reference scores improved from 32.8% when using the 8B model alone to 42.6% under routing, although at n=61 this improvement was not statistically distinguishable from chance (95% CI 31.0–55.1%). The Bayesian-network-derived uncertainty did not successfully identify where the 8B model would be wrong: disagreement rates were similar for escalated (16.2%) and locally resolved (19.4%) decisions, and the AUROC for using uncertainty to predict 8B/reference disagreement was 0.47 (95% CI 0.39–0.56), effectively no better than random. They also discovered and describe an evaluation artifact in an earlier version, where truncated outputs from a reasoning model were silently substituted with local labels, invalidating those previous results.

Conclusion: CreateScore demonstrates that a transparent Bayesian routing layer can substantially reduce LLM evaluation costs and modestly improve agreement with a strong reference model, but its current uncertainty metric does not reliably pinpoint the small model’s mistakes. The system is therefore useful as an auditable cost-reduction mechanism, not yet as an effective targeted error detector and certainly not as a standalone hiring solution. The work also underscores the importance of robust safeguards in cascade and routing evaluations to prevent subtle data and logging errors, such as truncated outputs being replaced by fallback labels, from corrupting results.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can support rubric-based screening of CVs, but applying a high-capability model to every candidate and criterion is costly. We present CreateScore, a domain-theory-informed Bayesian network for criterion-level LLM routing. A hand-specified directed acyclic graph with Dirichlet-multinomial conditional probability tables converts CV evidence into posterior uncertainty; low-uncertainty decisions are resolved by a local 8B model and uncertain ones are escalated to a 120B reference model. The graph is causally motivated, but the system performs standard Bayesian conditioning, not causal inference. The escalation threshold is calibrated on a training fold (target: 70% resolved locally) and then frozen. On 200 synthetic Data Science CVs (139 training and 61 test candidates, five criteria), 77.7% of criterion decisions were resolved locally (237 of 305). Relative to a reference condition in which the 120B model adjudicated every criterion, routed escalation reduced token use by 65.2% and raised exact score agreement from 32.8% (8B alone) to 42.6% (95% CI 31.0-55.1%); at n = 61 the gain was not statistically distinguishable. The uncertainty signal did not, however, identify the decisions on which the 8B model erred: disagreement with the reference was 16.2% among escalated and 19.4% among locally resolved decisions (AUROC 0.47, 95% CI 0.39-0.56), no better than random selection. We also document how an earlier evaluation was invalidated when truncated reasoning-model outputs were silently replaced by local labels, and we recommend safeguards for cascade evaluation. CreateScore is supported as an auditable cost-reduction mechanism, not yet as a targeted error detector, and is not an autonomous hiring system.

</details>


### [143] [Benchmarking Candidate Coverage in Typed Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03387)
*Jiawen Lu,Tongtong Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a benchmark to test whether typed decision models correctly handle cases where the correct answer is missing from the provided options, and compares two models, Laya and Jev, across several datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of typed decision models focus on accuracy given a complete, correct set of answer options, which does not reveal whether models can recognize when the correct answer is absent or whether they improperly reject valid options. There is a need for a controlled way to measure candidate coverage and rejection behavior separately from overall classification accuracy.

Method: The authors design a paired candidate-coverage benchmark protocol using AG News, DBpedia, Emotion, and TREC datasets. For each model, they freeze the input texts and requests and create present/absent candidate pairs and name variants that preserve descriptions, members, and ordering. They run 300 calibration and 589 test texts, obtaining 23,932 predictions per model for Laya and Jev. They analyze native rejection behavior, apply calibration-only none-score thresholds, and perform competence-conditioned analysis, probability-precision sensitivity checks, and interface audits.

Result: In TREC with five naturally named candidates, Laya natively detects 97.2% of missing-answer cases but falsely rejects 69.7% of valid present controls, while Jev detects 24.8% of missing-answer cases with 0% false rejections. After applying none-score thresholds based only on calibration data, Laya's detection/false-rejection rates change to 33.9%/3.7%, and Jev's to 45.0%/1.8%. On DBpedia, Jev achieves a high coverage-score AUROC that supports a better operating point, whereas both models exhibit weak accuracy when all true labels are present for the Emotion dataset.

Conclusion: The benchmark shows that classification accuracy, score ranking, and rejection behavior are distinct properties that must be evaluated separately for typed decision models. The initial results highlight substantial differences in native and calibrated rejection policies between Laya and Jev, and reveal dataset-specific strengths and weaknesses. However, this benchmark is descriptive and restricted to controlled label-omission setups; it does not address broader out-of-scope generalization, provide causal explanations, or introduce new rejection algorithms.

Abstract: Typed decision models return choices or distributions over answer options supplied at request time. Accuracy with complete options does not establish whether a model recognizes that a reference answer is missing or avoids rejecting valid candidates. We present a paired candidate-coverage benchmark protocol and an initial evaluation of Laya and Jev across AG News, DBpedia, Emotion, and TREC. The models receive identical frozen texts and requests: 300 calibration and 589 test texts yield 23,932 predictions per model. Present/absent pairs match ordinary candidate count, and name variants preserve descriptions, members, and order. Native rejection behavior differs sharply: at five TREC candidates with natural names, Laya detects 97.2% of missing-answer cases but falsely rejects 69.7% of present controls; Jev's rates are 24.8% and 0.0%. Calibration-only none-score thresholds change these rates to 33.9%/3.7% and 45.0%/1.8%, respectively. On DBpedia, Jev's high coverage-score AUROC supports a stronger operating point, whereas both models have weak complete-set accuracy on Emotion. Competence-conditioned analysis, probability-precision sensitivity, and interface audits show why classification, score ranking, and rejection policies need separate measurement. This initial benchmark is descriptive and limited to reference-label omission; it does not establish natural out-of-scope generalization, causal mechanisms, or a new rejection method.

</details>


### [144] [Reliable Self-Evolution with Imperfect Proxy Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02975)
*Kangjun Noh,Soyu Kim,Kyungwoo Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CISE, a method that uses conformal prediction to provide statistically calibrated reward intervals in LLM-based self-evolving search, reducing false positives in low-cost proxy evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based self-evolving search can accelerate scientific discovery, but in domains where high-fidelity evaluation is expensive, systems must use cheap proxy rewards that can mistakenly assign high scores to infeasible candidates. These false positives not only corrupt the final set of proposed solutions but also mislead the evolutionary process in subsequent iterations. There is a need for a principled way to quantify uncertainty and calibrate rewards so that only reliably feasible candidates are selected, especially when validation budgets are limited.

Method: The authors propose Conformal Interval-Driven Self-Evolution (CISE). CISE builds candidate-specific reward intervals via conditional conformal inference combined with iteration-wise online density-ratio estimation to handle covariate shift between iterations. Instead of using point proxy rewards, CISE computes conservative interval-based rewards and uses them as feedback in the evolutionary loop. It only returns candidates whose entire predicted property intervals lie inside the predefined feasible regions. The framework is accompanied by theoretical fixed-iteration coverage guarantees under assumptions of independence and covariate shift.

Result: On three self-evolving search tasks in materials science, CISE is compared against baseline self-evolving search methods that rely on uncalibrated proxy rewards. CISE returns fewer candidates overall, but every candidate it returns is a true positive when checked with expensive, high-fidelity evaluation.
