Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) for learning path recommendation (LPR) faces two coupled obstacles. First, the policy must commit to a sequence of L concepts without intermediate feedback, producing a combinatorial search space that grows super-exponentially with L and provides reward only at the final step. Second, expert learning paths would be the natural cure for sparse-reward RL, but they do not exist in educational data, because student logs record what learners did, not what they should have done. We address both obstacles by importing a recipe from simulator-based demonstration learning in robotics: the knowledge tracing simulator is used both to synthesize per-learner expert demonstrations through evolutionary search and to train a deployment-free policy that distills these demonstrations into a feed-forward learner. Our framework, EVOL, instantiates this pipeline with an asymmetric actor-critic where the actor commits to deployment-realistic blind planning while the critic exploits the privileged simulator state during training. Across three datasets (ASSIST15, Junyi, and EdNet; 39-189 concepts) and path lengths L = 5, 10, and 20, EVOL surpasses 8 baselines spanning heuristic, sequential, RL, graph-enhanced RL, and LLM-enhanced methods. We further compare three imitation strategies (BC, AWR, and DAPG) and show that final performance is governed by the quality of evolutionary experts rather than by the particular imitation objective.

</details>


### [162] [JOVE: Joint Execution and Verification for Resource-Aware LLM Task Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03296)
*Haoran Zhang,Dongjun Kim,Seohyeon Cha,Kevin S Chan,Ananthram Swami,Gustavo De Veciana,Haris Vikalo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces JOVE, an online framework that dynamically assigns subtasks in decomposed reasoning queries to heterogeneous LLMs and selectively verifies intermediate outputs to optimize accuracy, cost, and latency under uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex reasoning tasks can be decomposed and parallelized across multiple LLMs, enabling lower latency and the use of smaller, cheaper models. However, it is unclear in advance which LLM is best for each subtask, and correctness of intermediate outputs is unknown without verification. Verification is expensive and slow, so the system must strategically decide which outputs to verify and which models to use, taking into account limited budgets, latency constraints, and unknown, stochastic LLM performance. Existing approaches lack a principled way to jointly learn model quality and optimize allocations in this setting.

Method: The authors model complex queries as directed acyclic task graphs with subtasks that can be dispatched to different LLMs. They introduce JOVE, which for each incoming query solves a mixed-integer linear program (MILP) to jointly choose (1) which LLM executes each subtask and (2) which intermediate outputs are sent for paid verification, respecting a latency constraint and long-term budget. JOVE treats LLM qualities (accuracy), costs, and runtimes as initially unknown stochastic quantities. It uses online learning to update task-dependent estimates of LLM quality based on verification outcomes, and augments the objective with an information-gain bonus so that allocations trade off immediate task performance with the long-term value of learning better quality estimates. The system operates fully online, adapting its decisions as more verification feedback is gathered, and the authors analyze its performance using regret bounds.

Result: Theoretically, the authors prove that under reasonable assumptions about the stochastic behavior of LLM services and verification, JOVE achieves sublinear regret in learning LLM qualities, meaning it asymptotically approaches the performance of an oracle with perfect quality knowledge. Empirically, they evaluate JOVE on four reasoning benchmarks, comparing against standard inference baselines such as fixed model selection or naïve allocation strategies. JOVE attains similar or better accuracy while reducing both average monetary cost and latency by at least a factor of 3.17 across these benchmarks.

Conclusion: JOVE provides a principled framework for orchestrating heterogeneous LLMs on decomposed reasoning tasks while selectively using paid verification. By jointly optimizing model assignment and verification decisions and incorporating online learning with an information-gain bonus, JOVE can learn model qualities over time and approach oracle-level allocations. Experiments demonstrate that this yields substantial savings in cost and latency without sacrificing accuracy, suggesting that intelligent orchestration and verification can make complex reasoning with LLM ensembles far more efficient in practice.

Abstract: Complex reasoning queries can be decomposed into directed acyclic task graphs and distributed across heterogeneous LLMs, reducing latency through parallelism and enabling smaller models to solve complex tasks. In practice, however, the suitability of an LLM for a given subtask may be a priori unknown, and execution alone does not reveal output correctness. We propose JOVE, an online framework that jointly assigns executor LLMs and selects intermediate outputs for paid verification. Verification runs asynchronously and is used to improve future allocations, so the system must balance spending on execution now against learning for later. We study how to optimize this trade-off under a long-term budget and a per-query latency constraint, with stochastic, initially unknown LLM service quality, invocation costs, and execution times. JOVE makes execution and verification decisions by solving a sequence of per-query mixed-integer linear programs. Online learning updates task-dependent estimates of LLM quality based on verification feedback, while an information-gain bonus incorporates the value of learning into allocation decisions. Under a natural set of assumptions, we establish sublinear quality-learning regret for JOVE. Across four reasoning benchmarks, JOVE achieves competitive accuracy against standard inference baselines while reducing average cost and latency by at least 3.17 times.

</details>


### [163] [Optimal Planning in a Dynamic World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03312)
*Devin Wild Thomas,Solomon Eyal Shimony,Wheeler Ruml,Erez Karpas,Shahaf S. Shperberg,Andrew Coles*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces algorithms for planning when the feasibility of states/actions changes over time and the execution start time is unknown, by representing optimal plans as functions of start time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world planning problems occur in dynamic environments where the set of feasible states or actions varies with time, such as path planning with moving obstacles or boarding a vehicle only when it is available. Existing approaches typically assume a fixed, known start time, which simplifies reasoning about feasibility but is unrealistic when the actual start time is determined later or by another agent. This mismatch can lead to failures or inefficiencies when plans must be valid and near-optimal regardless of when execution begins.

Method: The authors formally define the "any-start-time planning" setting, where the plan must be valid and optimal for any eventual execution start time. They introduce a data structure called the compound arrival time function (cATF), which compactly encodes, for each possible start time, the corresponding optimal plan and its arrival time. They then design heuristic graph-search-based planning algorithms that propagate these time-dependent functions over edges in the state-space graph, instead of propagating simple scalar costs as in standard shortest-path search.

Result: The authors prove that cATFs have size at most linear in the problem size, ensuring that the representation remains compact and scalable. In experiments on the Safe Interval Path Planning (SIPP) domain with moving obstacles, they compare any-start-time planning with standard replanning approaches. On harder instances, replanning agents often fail to find valid solutions in time, whereas the any-start-time algorithms precompute cATFs and can then rapidly retrieve the optimal plan once the true start time becomes known.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that representing plans as functions of start time via cATFs enables efficient planning in dynamic environments without assuming a known execution start time. This establishes a practical framework and theoretical guarantees for any-start-time planning and suggests that such representations can substantially improve robustness and responsiveness in time-dependent planning tasks.

Abstract: Background: We address the problem of planning when the set of feasible states or actions changes over time. For example, in the problem of path planning among moving obstacles (sometimes known as SIPP), the feasibility of being at a particular location can change as the obstacles move. Or, the action of boarding a particular train is feasible only while it is stopped at the station. This dynamism means that the optimal plan and its duration can change depending on when execution begins. In practice, execution start time is often unknown until planning has completed or another agent gives the go-ahead. However, most prior planning work either ignores dynamism or assumes a known start time. This makes it straightforward to assess state and action feasibility but is impractical for some applications. Objectives: In this paper, we relax the assumption of a known start time. We define the setting of {\em any-start-time planning} and provide algorithms for it. Methods: We present a data structure called a compound arrival time function (cATF) that compactly encodes the optimal plan as a function of start time. We provide general-purpose planning algorithms, based on heuristic graph search, that assemble cATFs by propagating functions along edges instead of scalar costs. Results: We prove that the size of a cATF is at most linear in the problem size. An experimental evaluation of an implementation for the specific problem of SIPP shows that, on difficult problems, agents that rely on replanning often fail, while any-start-time algorithms using cATFs can quickly look up the optimal plan once the execution start time is known. Conclusions: By enabling efficient representations and reasoning for time-dependent plans, this work provides a foundation for planning in dynamic worlds.

</details>


### [164] [Lightweight, Rubric-Guided Trajectory Evaluation for Production AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03315)
*Linh-An Phan,MingXue Wang,Guangyu Wu,Feng Pan,Zhaoyu Pang,Yanbin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LiteTrajEval is a lightweight, budget-bounded evaluator for LLM agent trajectories that compresses traces and uses a single rubric-guided LLM judge to diagnose failures more cheaply and accurately.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating LLM-based agent trajectories is crucial for reliability but is expensive in production because traces are long, full of tool calls, retries, and observations, and many tokens are not equally informative for debugging. Existing trajectory evaluators can be costly and less aligned with human failure localization, creating a need for a more efficient and accurate method that works under strict cost and latency budgets.

Method: LiteTrajEval first derives compact, domain-specific rule profiles offline. At evaluation time, it preprocesses each trajectory, marks heuristic failure signals, and serializes the trajectory into a compact representation that fits within a fixed global token budget. It then uses a single rubric-guided LLM judge over this compressed representation to generate structured diagnostic reports that localize failures along the trajectory.

Result: On public Magentic-One-style and τ-bench-style datasets, LiteTrajEval improves alignment of failure localization with human annotations by approximately 20–35 percentage points on Magentic-One and up to 23 percentage points on τ-retail compared with AgentRx. It also reduces evaluation cost by about 6× and latency by more than 8×, and has been successfully deployed in an enterprise agent platform.

Conclusion: LiteTrajEval provides an effective and practical approach to budget-bounded trajectory evaluation for LLM agents, achieving significantly better failure localization at substantially lower cost and latency than a prior system (AgentRx), and is mature enough for real-world deployment in enterprise settings.

Abstract: Trajectory evaluation is essential for improving the reliability of LLM-based agents, but production use makes it expensive to run repeatedly. Modern agents generate long traces containing tool calls, observations, retries, and external outputs, while not all raw tokens are equally useful for diagnosis. We present \textit{LiteTrajEval}, a lightweight architecture for budget-bounded trajectory evaluation. LiteTrajEval derives compact domain-specific rule profiles offline, then preprocesses each trajectory online, marks heuristic failure signals, serializes it under a fixed global budget, and invokes a single rubric-guided LLM judge to produce structured diagnostic reports. Evaluated on public Magentic-One-style and $τ$-bench-style trajectory datasets, LiteTrajEval improves failure-localization alignment with human annotations by roughly 20--35 percentage points on Magentic-One and up to 23 percentage points on $τ$-retail compared with AgentRx, while reducing cost by about 6$\times$ and evaluation time by more than 8$\times$. This solution has also been deployed in our enterprise agentic platform.

</details>


### [165] [Multi-Task Evolution for Zero-Shot Cross-Problem Generalization using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03316)
*Zhouliang Xie,Changliang Zhou,Genghui Li,Zhenkun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MECo is an LLM-driven multi-task evolutionary framework that learns reusable heuristics from multiple source optimization problems and transfers them zero-shot to new problems, outperforming existing automated heuristic design baselines on vehicle routing and job-shop scheduling variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing good heuristics for combinatorial optimization is labor-intensive, requiring expert knowledge and repeated trial-and-error for each new problem variant. While LLMs can help generate and refine heuristics, they still typically rely on direct feedback from the specific problem being optimized, which makes it hard to generalize to new, unseen problem definitions. There is a need for a method that can leverage feedback from some source tasks to learn heuristics that transfer well, without additional search, to target tasks that may differ in structure or domain.

Method: MECo is a multi-task evolutionary framework that uses an LLM to generate and evolve populations of heuristics conditioned on different source tasks. It tracks cross-task performance of these heuristic populations and defines a transfer gap to guide how heuristics are shared and recombined between tasks. Through these interactions, MECo encourages heuristics that are both strong on their own tasks and transferable to others. A complementary selection criterion then picks a compact subset of heuristics by rewarding each one's additional coverage of source-task combinations, yielding a small but diverse set of reusable heuristics. This final set is then applied directly, without further adaptation, to new target problems (zero-shot).

Result: On 32 variants spanning vehicle routing problems (VRP) and flexible job-shop scheduling problems (FJSP), MECo achieves the lowest average solution cost compared to eight state-of-the-art automated heuristic design methods under equal computational budgets. On out-of-domain target problems—problems outside the distribution of training/source tasks—it also outperforms the strongest baseline within each problem family. Furthermore, when MECo’s framework is combined with other automated heuristic design methods, it consistently improves both in-domain (ID) and out-of-domain (OOD) performance across VRP and FJSP, showing that its transfer mechanism is broadly beneficial.

Conclusion: MECo demonstrates that an LLM-driven, multi-task evolutionary approach can learn a compact set of heuristics that generalizes zero-shot across different combinatorial optimization problems and variants. By using cross-task performance and a transfer gap to manage heuristic sharing, and by explicitly selecting for broad coverage, MECo overcomes the usual dependence on target-task feedback. Its empirical superiority over multiple baselines in VRP and FJSP, and its ability to enhance other heuristic design frameworks, indicate that it is a powerful, general tool for automated heuristic generation and transfer in combinatorial optimization.

Abstract: Designing effective heuristics for diverse combinatorial optimization problems requires substantial expertise and repeated search. Large language models (LLMs) automate heuristic generation and refinement, but heuristic search typically depends on evaluation feedback from the problem being optimized. Generalizing to new problem definitions using only source-task feedback therefore remains a central challenge. We introduce MECo, an LLM-driven multi-task evolutionary framework for zero-shot cross-problem generalization. MECo maintains task-conditioned heuristic populations and uses a transfer gap based on cross-task population performance to guide their interactions. These interactions enable the transfer and recombination of heuristics. A complementary selection criterion then constructs a compact heuristic set by rewarding each member's additional coverage of source combinations. The selected set is applied to target problems without further search or adaptation. Experiments on 32 problem variants across vehicle routing (VRP) and flexible job-shop scheduling (FJSP) show that MECo achieves the lowest mean costs compared with eight automated heuristic design (AHD) baselines under the same budgets. On out-of-domain problems, it outperforms the strongest baseline in each family. Moreover, integrating the framework of MECo with different AHD methods improves their ID and OOD performance in both families, supporting its effectiveness across different methods.

</details>


### [166] [Refinement Buys Intelligibility, Search Buys Identity: What Test-Time Compute Buys in Masked-Diffusion TTS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03320)
*Nityanand Mathur,Hamees Sayed,Ayush Pratap Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how two scaling axes of diffusion-based text-to-speech systems—model depth (parameters) and number of refinement steps at inference—differentially affect intelligibility and speaker identity, finding they are not interchangeable and optimize different bottlenecks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion language models for TTS rely both on model size and iterative refinement during inference, but it is unclear whether increasing parameters or refinement steps yields similar improvements across key capabilities like intelligibility and speaker identity. Understanding this scaling behavior is important for efficiently allocating training and inference resources and for designing better TTS systems.

Method: The authors train 15 masked-diffusion codec TTS models with varying parameter counts (19–133M, three random seeds) on 2,000 hours of speech. At inference, they sweep the number of refinement steps T from 1 to 16. They evaluate zero-shot synthesis on 174 held-out speakers using ASR word error rate for intelligibility and speaker verification metrics for identity. They compare gains from additional refinement steps against baseline floors, examine the effect of longer training schedules (3x and 6x), test best-of-K sample selection for identity recovery, and fit scaling models to distinguish separable effects of depth and refinement steps, also analyzing how much of the remaining identity deficit is due to the codec versus the generator.

Result: Refinement steps recover most of the possible intelligibility improvement (86.2% of the measured range) but much less of the identity improvement (46.4%), yielding about a 1.86x asymmetry. Extending the training schedule reduces but does not eliminate this gap (from 1.84x to 1.36x to 1.23x) because intelligibility saturates with more steps while identity continues to improve more slowly. Best-of-K sampling significantly improves speaker identity with win rates of 64.6–79.0% across four speaker encoders. A factorized model B(d)B(T) that treats depth and steps as separable contributions explains performance better (Delta AICc=+69.3) than models where steps can substitute for depth. Analysis attributes about 62% of the remaining speaker identity deficit to the codec component rather than the generator.

Conclusion: Model depth and refinement steps in diffusion-based TTS systems do not scale equivalently across intelligibility and speaker identity; they address different bottlenecks and cannot be treated as interchangeable. Refinement primarily boosts intelligibility and saturates quickly, while identity depends more on aspects that are not resolved simply by more refinement steps, including codec limitations. Therefore, depth and refinement must be tuned and designed separately, and additional strategies like better codecs and best-of-K search are needed to close the speaker identity gap.

Abstract: Diffusion language models for text-to-speech combine two forms of computation: model depth (parameters) and refinement steps (inference budget). We ask whether they scale equally across capabilities. We train 15 masked-diffusion codec TTS models varying depth (19-133M parameters, 3 seeds) on 2,000 hours of speech and sweep refinement steps T in [1,16] at inference, measuring zero-shot synthesis via ASR word error rate (intelligibility) and speaker verification (identity) on 174 held-out speakers. Against measured floors, refinement closes 86.2% of the intelligibility range but only 46.4% of the identity range - a 1.86x asymmetry robust across multiple error metrics. Retraining at 3x and 6x schedule attenuates but does not reverse this gap (1.84 to 1.36 to 1.23x), because intelligibility saturates with steps while identity continues improving. Best-of-K search recovers speaker identity where refinement fails, with 64.6-79.0% win rates across four independent encoders. Depth and steps are not interchangeable: separable B(d)B(T) fits significantly better (Delta AICc=+69.3) than substitution models. Analysis shows 62% of remaining identity deficit lies in the codec, not the generator. We conclude that refinement and depth target different bottlenecks and should be optimized separately.

</details>


### [167] [Preserving Mathematical Reasoning in Compressed Diffusion Language Models via Trajectory-Aware Low-Rank Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03326)
*Tian Liang,Zishan Shao,Yiran Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a trajectory-aware low-rank compression method for diffusion language models that better preserves mathematical reasoning than standard clean-activation calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing low-rank compression methods for diffusion language models calibrate on clean, fully visible activations, even though inference operates over partially masked intermediate states. This mismatch can harm performance, especially for mathematical reasoning, and it is unclear how to characterize low-rank optimality or its effect on reasoning when states are drawn from the full generation trajectory rather than clean inputs.

Method: The authors formulate a trajectory-aware low-rank objective that averages approximation quality over corruption levels and masking realizations along the diffusion trajectory. They then introduce Traj-MC, a Monte Carlo estimator of the trajectory second-moment matrix that allows efficient computation of low-rank approximations. Traj-MC provides exact optimality on the sampled states and is consistent with the population-level objective as the number of samples grows.

Result: Under equal compression budgets, using trajectory-aware calibration improves activation reconstruction quality over the actual generation trajectory compared to clean-state calibration. Empirically, this leads to substantially better preservation of mathematical reasoning abilities on math reasoning benchmarks for compressed diffusion language models.

Conclusion: Taking the full diffusion trajectory into account when calibrating low-rank compression for diffusion language models yields theoretically grounded, sample-consistent low-rank factors and empirically preserves significantly more mathematical reasoning ability than traditional clean-activation calibration. This establishes a link between trajectory-aware low-rank optimality and retained reasoning capability after compression.

Abstract: Diffusion language model (dLLM) compression faces a known challenge because calibration is typically performed on clean, fully visible activations, whereas inference traverses partially masked intermediate states. For low-rank compression, this raises two questions. First, can low-rank optimality still be characterized when approximation quality is measured over trajectory-distributed states, and second, does the choice of calibration states affect mathematical reasoning preservation under compression? We address these questions by formulating a trajectory-aware low-rank objective over corruption levels and masking realizations. To estimate this objective efficiently, we propose Traj-MC, which estimates the trajectory second moment through Monte Carlo sampling and yields exact sampled-state optimality and population consistency. Under matched compression budgets, trajectory-aware calibration improves reconstruction over the generation trajectory and preserves substantially more mathematical reasoning than clean calibration on mathematical reasoning benchmarks. Our results connect trajectory-aware low-rank optimality to the reasoning capability retained after dLLM compression. Our code is available at: https://github.com/Zishan-Shao/traj-mc.git.

</details>


### [168] [ReFract: Benchmarking Perspective Awareness in Language Model Agents with Text World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03356)
*Hainiu Xu,Vítor N. Lourenço,Mohnish Dubey,Yunfei Bai,Yulan He,Caroline Catmur,Aline Paes,Marco Caserta,Akash Chandrayan,Luca D'Angelo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ReFract, a benchmark to test whether LLM agents can adapt their actions to different user roles in high‑stakes industrial settings, showing that current models frequently violate role constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In industrial maintenance and fault troubleshooting, LLM agents are starting to assist workers with diverse roles and capabilities. A single query (e.g., about fixing a machine) should elicit different, role‑appropriate actions for a field technician, a trainee, or a manager. Unlike coding assistance, mistakes here can lead to physical damage, production loss, or personal harm. Existing benchmarks don’t assess whether agents can infer the user’s role and respect constraints on what tools or actions each role is allowed to use. The authors identify this gap and define it as the challenge of Perspective Awareness.

Method: The authors construct ReFract, a benchmark of 150 tasks derived from anonymized real-world domain support conversations in industrial settings. For each entry, they build a Text World Model to simulate the operating environment, define user roles with differing permissions and knowledge, and create perspective-aware action trajectories that specify what an agent should or should not do for each role given the same query. They then evaluate state-of-the-art LLM agents on these tasks, measuring both task success (correct, useful behavior) and the frequency of perspective-violating actions (attempts to use tools or perform actions outside the user’s legitimate role).

Result: On ReFract, state-of-the-art LLMs successfully solve at most 69% of tasks. More than half of the action trajectories produced by these models include attempts to take perspective-violating actions, i.e., actions that disregard the user’s role-based constraints. This indicates that current LLM agents are not reliably perspective aware in high-stakes, role-differentiated industrial environments.

Conclusion: Perspective awareness—calibrating agent behavior to the user’s role, capabilities, and legitimate tool access—is a critical but underexplored dimension of LLM agent evaluation. ReFract formalizes this as a benchmark and demonstrates that even strong models often overstep role boundaries. The work argues that progressing beyond generic task success metrics requires developing and testing agents that reason explicitly about for whom they are acting, not only about what to do, in order to be safe and effective in high-stakes real-world applications.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in high-stakes settings such as industrial maintenance and equipment fault troubleshooting, where workers occupy a variety of roles. A capable agent must therefore act in a way that is calibrated to user's role: taking actions and providing information that respect the role's knowledge and capability boundaries. Unlike coding, where mistakes are usually recoverable, agent responses in these settings are enacted on physical equipment, and can therefore cause irreversible equipment damage, production loss, or personnel harm. Existing benchmarks, however, largely overlook the need for agents to infer what a role intends and acting only through tools that role may legitimately use, a capability which we term Perspective Awareness. To this end, we introduce ReFract, a benchmark of 150 expert-validated entries in which an agent must act differently in response to the same query depending on user's role. Entries of ReFract are grounded in anonymized queries from domain support conversations, against which we construct Text World Models that simulate the agent's operating environments and assemble perspective-aware action trajectories. State-of-the-art LLMs solve at most 69% of the tasks with more than 50% of their trajectories contain attempts of taking perspective-violating actions. ReFract exposes perspective awareness as a distinct, largely unsolved axis of agent evaluation and motivates agents that calibrate not just how to act, but for whom.

</details>


### [169] [Geometry Meets Physics: Data-Efficient Pre-Training for Unstructured Neural PDE Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03363)
*Luis Medrano-Navarro,Giacomo Baldan,Qiang Liu,Benjamin Holzschuh,Jan Hagnberger,Mathias Niepert,Nils Thuerey*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a data-disk-free pre-training framework for neural PDE surrogates on complex 3D domains, using geometry-driven representations for steady-state and physics-driven online synthetic data for transients, to improve data efficiency, convergence, and accuracy under low-data regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural surrogates for PDEs on unstructured 3D geometries struggle with poor generalization and require large, expensive training datasets. Existing fixes rely on massive offline pre-training datasets, which are computationally and data intensive to generate. There is a need for a more efficient pre-training strategy that avoids heavy reliance on pre-computed data but still improves robustness and scalability across different PDE regimes.

Method: They design a disk-data-free pre-training framework that differs for steady and transient PDEs. For steady-state problems, they use geometry-driven pre-training that incorporates intrinsic shape descriptors of complex 3D domains to learn geometry-aware representations. For transient problems, they adopt a physics-driven strategy that generates synthetic PDE trajectories online during pre-training, instead of relying on a large pre-stored dataset. This allows the model to be exposed to diverse PDE dynamics in a scalable way without storing or precomputing massive datasets.

Result: In experiments across multiple PDE benchmarks, the proposed pre-training approach leads to faster convergence during fine-tuning and requires fewer task-specific samples to reach a given accuracy level. It also achieves higher final accuracy compared to baselines, with the gains being most pronounced in realistic low-data regimes where only a limited number of training simulations are available.

Conclusion: Disk-data-free, regime-specific pre-training—using geometry-driven representations for steady-state PDEs and physics-driven online synthetic data for transient PDEs—can substantially improve the data efficiency, convergence speed, and accuracy of neural PDE surrogates on complex 3D geometries. This offers a practical route toward scalable, robust neural emulators for large-scale PDE simulations without the prohibitive cost of assembling massive offline training datasets.

Abstract: Neural surrogate models for Partial Differential Equations (PDEs) on unstructured 3D geometries are often limited by poor generalization and the high cost of generating large-scale training datasets. Consequently, pre-training on massive datasets of related PDE dynamics has emerged as a critical alternative to enhance the robustness and scalability of these models. However, this strategy is neither compute- nor data-efficient, as it relies on massive pre-computed data that is very costly to generate. In this work, we introduce a disk-data-free pre-training framework tailored to both steady-state and transient regimes. For steady-state problems, we propose a geometry-driven strategy that leverages intrinsic shape descriptors to learn representations of complex 3D domains. For transient problems, we introduce a physics-driven approach based on online generation of synthetic PDE data, enabling scalable pre-training without reliance on expensive datasets. Across multiple experiments, our approach achieves faster convergence, greater data efficiency, and higher accuracy during fine-tuning, particularly under realistic low-data regimes. This methodology provides a practical pathway toward data-efficient neural emulators for large-scale simulations.

</details>


### [170] [CVE2AP: Automated Generation of PDDL-Encoded Attack Paths via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03383)
*Lin Cui,Vincenzo Scotti,Raffaela Mirandola*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CVE2AP, an LLM‑based system that automatically converts natural language CVE vulnerability descriptions into formal PDDL attack paths, with iterative error-feedback to improve quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing attack path (AP) models in cybersecurity are powerful but mostly built manually by experts in PDDL, which is slow, hard to scale, and cannot keep up with fast-evolving threats and large volumes of threat intelligence like CVEs. The authors aim to automate this translation from natural-language vulnerability descriptions to machine-verifiable attack path models so that security analysis can be done faster and more consistently, without requiring deep PDDL expertise.

Method: They design CVE2AP, which uses large language models guided by structured prompts to transform CVE descriptions into PDDL-encoded attack paths. After an initial generation, a planner is run on the PDDL; its syntactic and solvability errors are collected and fed back to the LLM in an iterative error-feedback loop so the model can refine and correct the generated attack paths. The approach is evaluated across several LLMs and configuration settings, measuring syntax correctness, solvability, semantic correctness, token use, and generation time.

Result: CVE2AP automatically produces PDDL attack paths with high quality: up to 86.9% syntactically correct outputs, 78.6% solvable instances, and 93.1% semantic correctness as judged by an LLM acting as an expert. Among the tested models, GPT‑5.5 delivers the best balance between quality and computational cost, and incorporating error feedback consistently improves output quality across settings.

Conclusion: LLMs can reliably convert natural-language CVE descriptions into formal PDDL attack paths when guided by structured prompts and an error-feedback loop. CVE2AP significantly reduces manual effort in AP modeling, scales better to large and evolving vulnerability corpora, and offers a practical way to keep attack path analysis up to date, with GPT‑5.5 currently providing the best trade-off between accuracy and efficiency.

Abstract: Attack Path (AP) modeling is fundamental to cybersecurity analysis, where the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) has been widely adopted to encode APs into formal and machine-verifiable representations for automated reasoning about vulnerability exploitation, attack progression, and their potential impacts. However, existing AP modeling approaches largely rely on expert-driven manual construction, limiting their scalability and ability to keep pace with rapidly evolving cyber threats. Large language models (LLMs) are promising candidates, as their extensive pre-trained knowledge and reasoning capabilities enable them to interpret and transform threat intelligence into formal representations. In this paper, we propose \textbf{CVE2AP}, an LLM-based approach for automatically generating PDDL-encoded attack paths from natural language CVE (Common Vulnerability Exposure) descriptions. CVE2AP leverages structured prompting and incorporates an error-feedback mechanism that iteratively refines the generated paths using planner-reported syntactic and solvability errors. We conduct a systematic empirical evaluation across multiple LLMs and generation configurations, assessing generation quality across syntactic, solvability and semantic dimensions, together with token consumption and generation time. The results demonstrate that CVE2AP effectively generates high-quality PDDL-encoded attack paths, achieving up to 86.9\% syntax correctness, 78.6\% solvability, and 93.1\% semantic correctness under LLM-as-expert evaluation, while \texttt{GPT-5.5} offers the best quality-cost trade-off and error feedback yields the most consistent quality improvement.

</details>


### [171] [Becoming Suspicious Across Borders: Algorithmic Extraterritoriality and AI-Driven Financial Surveillance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03425)
*Georgios Pavlidis,Savvas Chatzichristofis,Eleni Gavriil*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper examines how the rise of AI-driven financial surveillance is changing the nature, location, and accountability of “suspicion” in anti-money laundering and counter-terrorist financing systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional AML/CFT frameworks rely on human legal judgments formed within specific jurisdictions to decide when suspicion exists and intervention is justified. With AI becoming central to financial surveillance, this human-centered, jurisdiction-bound notion of suspicion no longer accurately describes how people are flagged or investigated. The paper is motivated by the need to conceptualize this shift and understand its implications for regulatory power, territoriality, and individual rights.

Method: The paper uses conceptual and legal-theoretical analysis rather than empirical methods. It reinterprets the concept of suspicion in AML/CFT through the lens of AI, data infrastructures, and transnational regulation, and introduces the new conceptual frame of “algorithmic extraterritoriality.”

Result: The analysis shows that suspicion in AML/CFT is increasingly produced by data-driven, AI-based systems operating across borders, rather than by localized human legal judgments. Regulatory reach now flows through transnational data infrastructures, making visibility in data systems more important than the physical location of conduct. Individuals become “datafied subjects of suspicion,” governed through dispersed and opaque evaluative processes. The paper crystallizes this transformation in the concept of algorithmic extraterritoriality: regulatory power exerted via data infrastructures rather than formal jurisdictional claims.

Conclusion: The transformation of suspicion through AI-based, transnational financial surveillance reconfigures how and where regulatory power is exercised, shifting it into algorithmic and infrastructural domains. This raises serious challenges for accountability, transparency, and the ability of individuals to contest being treated as suspicious, because suspicion is no longer easily attributable to specific decision-makers, locations, or clear legal judgments. New frameworks are needed to address these accountability gaps created by algorithmic extraterritoriality.

Abstract: Suspicion is an important, yet elusive concept in anti-money laundering and counter-terrorist financing (AML/CFT), which allows for intervention below the threshold of proof. In its traditional form, suspicion can be understood as a situated legal judgement by human actors within identifiable jurisdictions. It is argued that this understanding is no longer adequate. As artificial intelligence (AI) becomes an integral part of financial surveillance, suspicion is increasingly produced through data-driven processes. This transformation is epistemic, but also spatial. Since AI-driven financial surveillance operates through transnational data infrastructures, regulatory reach is less a matter of where conduct occurs than a question of whether such conduct becomes visible within data systems. This article develops the concept of algorithmic extraterritoriality, understood as a form of regulatory power mediated by data infrastructures rather than formal assertions of jurisdiction. Moreover, since individuals are increasingly constituted as datafied subjects of suspicion, they are rendered governable through dispersed and opaque processes of evaluation. This constitutes a challenge for accountability and contestability because suspicion becomes more difficult to locate, explain or contest.

</details>


### [172] [Jumping the Line: Exploiting Length Predictions in LLM Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03430)
*Yuyang Dai,Rana Shahout,Mahmood Sharif*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies an adversarial attack, JIL, that manipulates output-length predictions used by LLM request schedulers, letting malicious users gain priority and complete faster, and explores defenses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prediction-based schedulers for LLM serving rely on estimated output length to prioritize shorter requests and reduce completion time, but these estimates can be exploited. The authors aim to understand whether and how adversaries can game such schedulers by manipulating the prediction signal, and what impact this has on system performance and fairness.

Method: They propose JIL, an adversarial suffix optimization attack targeting a lightweight output-length prediction probe (using TRAIL as the case study). JIL crafts suffixes that cause the probe to systematically underestimate the request’s length. They empirically evaluate JIL on two datasets and four LLMs under varied request mixes and deployment settings, and they measure both the discrepancy between predicted and actual lengths and the resulting scheduling advantages and response quality. They also test scheduler-side defenses, especially coarsening length prediction into intervals.

Result: JIL reduces predicted output lengths by up to 83.4%, giving adversarial requests up to a 1.53× faster average completion time in end-to-end serving experiments. The change in predicted length is much larger than any change in realized output length, showing a significant mismatch exploitable by adversaries. The attack’s impact on response utility depends on the model and task, exposing a trade-off between performance gains and quality degradation. Coarsening predictions into bins substantially reduces JIL’s scheduling advantage and mitigates harm to benign users.

Conclusion: Length-prediction-based schedulers for LLM serving are vulnerable to adversarial manipulation of the prediction signal. Simple, lightweight probes can be fooled into large underestimates of output length, enabling unfair prioritization with relatively small changes in actual response size. However, scheduler-side defenses such as grouping predictions into coarse length intervals can meaningfully reduce this vulnerability and limit the impact on benign traffic, though they may also reduce scheduling optimality. More robust scheduling and prediction mechanisms are needed for secure, fair LLM serving systems.

Abstract: Efficient request scheduling is increasingly important for reducing completion time in large language model (LLM) serving. Size-based policies such as Shortest Job First prioritize shorter requests, but output lengths are unknown before generation, so practical schedulers rely on predicted lengths. We introduce JIL, an attack on prediction-based LLM schedulers that manipulates the scheduling signal to obtain higher priority and reduce completion time. Using TRAIL as a case study, JIL optimizes an adversarial suffix that causes a lightweight output-length probe to underestimate a request's length. We evaluate JIL on two datasets and four LLMs across varied request profiles and deployment configurations. JIL reduces predicted output lengths by up to 83.4 percent, and adversarial requests complete up to 1.53 times faster on average in end-to-end serving experiments. The reduction in predicted length is substantially larger than the change in actual output length, revealing a mismatch between the scheduler's estimate and the request's realized size. Response utility varies across models and tasks, exposing a trade-off between scheduling advantage and response quality. We also evaluate scheduler-side defenses and find that grouping length predictions into coarse intervals reduces JIL's scheduling advantage and mitigates delays to benign requests.

</details>


### [173] [Efficient Reasoning Training Does Not Always Harm CoT Faithfulness and Monitorability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03509)
*Samuel Lewis-Lim,Xingwei Tan,Mario Sanger,Zhixue Zhao,Nikolaos Aletras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how methods that force language models to produce shorter chain-of-thought (CoT) explanations affect whether those explanations truly reflect the model’s decision-making and whether they still reveal when inputs change the output.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought reasoning is useful because it makes model behavior inspectable, but generating long rationales is computationally expensive. To reduce cost, various training schemes encourage shorter CoTs. There is a crucial open question: does compressing CoTs make them less faithful to the underlying decision process, undermining their value for oversight? Prior work lacked a systematic comparison of different ‘length pressure’ methods and their concrete impact on CoT faithfulness and reliability for monitoring models.

Method: The authors fine-tune several language models using three efficiency-focused training strategies that each impose pressure on response length in different ways: (1) a fixed generation budget limiting the total number of tokens; (2) a per-example length target that encourages each CoT to stay near a desired length; and (3) a group-relative length reward that incentivizes explanations shorter than peers’ while still solving the task. They then design evaluations to measure two properties: CoT faithfulness—whether explanations are predictive of the model’s behavior on related, intervention-style inputs—and monitorability—whether the CoT still reveals when controlled input changes influence the final answer.

Result: Across these different length-pressure schemes, CoTs generally become shorter and inference more efficient, but this often reduces faithfulness: shortened CoTs are less consistent with the model’s decisions across related cases. The main driver of reduced faithfulness is increased inconsistency in model behavior after efficiency fine-tuning. In contrast, monitorability remains relatively robust: even when CoTs are significantly compressed, models typically still indicate that certain input interventions affect their answers.

Conclusion: Imposing efficiency pressure on chain-of-thought reasoning creates a trade-off: it commonly harms the faithfulness of explanations, largely by making model behavior less consistent, yet it does not proportionally degrade monitorability. In other words, shorter CoTs can still flag when inputs matter for decisions, but they become less reliable as faithful descriptions of the underlying reasoning process. Designers of efficient CoT methods must therefore balance token savings against potential losses in explanation faithfulness, especially for safety and oversight applications.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning allows humans to inspect how large language models reach their answers, and oversee model behaviour. This reasoning comes at an increased inference cost, motivating efficient methods that train models to solve tasks using fewer tokens. However, a common concern is that such training may cause models to skip important reasoning steps, so the CoT no longer faithfully reflects the model's decision. It is unclear whether or when this occurs in practice, since different efficiency methods apply length pressure to models' CoT in distinct ways, and faithfully explaining a model's decision takes more tokens on some tasks than others. To understand these dynamics, we fine-tune a variety of models with three methods that apply length pressure differently, namely a fixed generation budget, a per-example length target, and a group-relative length reward. We evaluate how efficient reasoning affects CoT faithfulness (i.e., how well the CoT reflects model decisions on related inputs) and monitorability (i.e., whether the CoT reveals when input interventions alter the output). We find that it affects faithfulness and monitorability differently. Faithfulness falls in most settings, primarily because the trained models are less consistent. Monitorability is more robust, as models keep acknowledging the influence on their answer even when the CoT is much shorter.

</details>


### [174] [Reasoning Models Are Accurate but Unsound on Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03519)
*Arman Behnam,Binghui Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CERTID, a formal pipeline to rigorously test whether reasoning models correctly determine and express causal identifiability from observational data, using provably non-identifiable queries and semantic (not string-based) formula verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of reasoning models on causal inference tasks are unreliable because they (1) lack systematically constructed, provably non-identifiable queries needed to measure when models wrongly assert identifiability, and (2) rely on brittle string-matching instead of checking whether model-produced formulas are mathematically equivalent to correct causal expressions. Since claiming a formula for a non-identifiable effect is a serious error that real data cannot expose, there is a need for a rigorous benchmark and certification process that distinguishes correctness from confident but unsound reasoning.

Method: The authors construct CERTID, a formal identification and grading pipeline built around the sound and complete ID algorithm for causal identification. For each graph–query pair, CERTID: (a) uses ID to certify whether the causal effect is identifiable; (b) when identifiable, derives a ground-truth expression; (c) when non-identifiable, ensures the query is provably non-identifiable; and (d) verifies model-generated formulas via evaluation on structural causal models with exactly known interventional distributions, checking semantic equivalence rather than textual similarity. The pipeline also introduces theoretical techniques to reduce structural leakage, repair non-identifiable queries to create identifiable variants, and provide guarantees on the grading process. Using this infrastructure, they generate 1,200 certified instances across graphs of size 4–50 nodes and test multiple reasoning models.

Result: On the 1,200 CERTID instances, three advanced reasoning models (Gemini Flash, Gemini Pro, GPT5.5) display large differences in soundness even when their raw accuracy appears similar. In particular, the rate at which they falsely claim identifiability on provably non-identifiable queries differs by a factor of 17 across models, revealing that accuracy alone poorly reflects the risk of unsound causal reasoning. Additionally, when evaluated on graphs generated after the strongest model’s training cutoff, all models still decide identifiability with very high accuracy (97–100%), suggesting strong generalization of identifiability decisions under the CERTID framework.

Conclusion: CERTID provides a rigorous, formally grounded methodology for evaluating whether reasoning models correctly determine and express causal identifiability. By combining the complete ID algorithm with semantic verification against known interventional distributions, it overcomes prior limitations based on heuristic datasets and string matching. The findings show that models can generalize well on identifiability decisions for new graphs, but that ostensibly similar accuracies can mask large differences in soundness, especially in how often they produce unjustified formulas for non-identifiable queries. CERTID and its released instances, verifier, and logs offer a robust benchmark and toolset for developing and comparing safer, more reliable causal reasoning models.

Abstract: A reasoning model asked whether a causal effect is recoverable from observational data can fail in two ways: it refuses an identifiable query or answers a nonidentifiable one. The latter is more consequential, as no observational data can validate the claimed formula. Measuring this failure requires queries that are provably non-identifiable, which prior evaluations lack, and grading that accepts correct formulas in any equivalent form, which string matching cannot provide. We build CERTID, a formal identification pipeline that addresses both limitations. CERTID uses the sound and complete causal identification algorithm ID to certify whether an effect is identifiable from a given graph and query, and verifies returned formulas against structural causal models whose interventional distributions are known exactly. CERTID further develops theoretical results to mitigate structural leakage, repair non-identifiable queries, and establish grading guarantees. We evaluate three frontier reasoning models (Gemini Flash, Gemini Pro, and GPT5.5) on 1,200 certified instances spanning 4 to 50 vertices. Accuracy proves a poor proxy for soundness: on identical instances, the false-claim rate on non-identifiable queries varies by seventeen-fold across models. We also find that models decide identifiability with 97-100% accuracy on graphs generated after the strongest model's training snapshot. Instances, the certification procedure, the verifier, and per-instance records are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/certid-D718.

</details>


### [175] [From Benchmarks to Production: A Text-to-SQL System for Complex Financial Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03524)
*Arijit Sehanobish,Bruno Gomes Coelho,Guillaume Michel,Sophia Zhi,Valerie Faucon-Morin,Kristen Howell*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FLINT is a domain-specialized Text-to-SQL system for financial databases that boosts accuracy by resolving opaque IDs, reusing expert query templates, and pruning schemas via foreign-key paths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose Text-to-SQL systems perform well on academic benchmarks with simple, human-readable schemas, but they fail on real-world financial databases where meanings are encoded as opaque integer keys and queries require several joins. There is a need for a system that can handle complex production financial schemas and bridge the gap between natural-language questions and SQL in this domain.

Method: FLINT introduces three components: (1) a lookup agent that translates natural-language concepts into question-specific constraints over reference tables, effectively mapping human terms to opaque IDs; (2) an embedding-based retrieval mechanism that finds structurally similar, expert-authored query templates from a compact template bank; and (3) a schema-linking mechanism that narrows down the large financial schema by following foreign-key chains to a relevant subgraph, instead of relying solely on string similarity between column or table names and the question text.

Result: On two datasets with a total of 359 natural-language questions over real production financial schemas, FLINT consistently outperforms multiple state-of-the-art Text-to-SQL baselines that use the same underlying LLM, indicating that its architectural additions provide substantial benefits in realistic financial settings.

Conclusion: Domain-specialization via concept lookup, template retrieval, and foreign-key-based schema pruning allows FLINT to close the performance gap of Text-to-SQL systems on complex, production financial databases. Its successful deployment in a live financial data retrieval service demonstrates its practical effectiveness and robustness beyond academic benchmarks.

Abstract: General-purpose Text-to-SQL systems achieve strong performance on academic benchmarks like Spider and BIRD, where schemas are relatively shallow and column values are often human readable. In production financial databases, where concepts are stored as opaque integer keys rather than human-readable strings, these methods fall below 50%, as even simple queries require multiple joins and filter predicates reference opaque IDs. We present Financial LINking Text-to-SQL (FLINT), a domain-specialized Text-to-SQL system that closes this gap through three key components: (1) a lookup agent that dynamically resolves natural-language concepts to question-specific reference table constraints, (2) embedding-based retrieval of structurally similar query templates from a compact, expert-authored bank, and (3) schema linking that prunes a large table schema to the relevant subset by traversing foreign-key chains, rather than relying on name similarity alone. We evaluate on two datasets totaling 359 questions over production financial schemas. FLINT outperforms various state-of-the-art baselines using the same LLM. The system is deployed in production as part of a financial data retrieval service.

</details>


### [176] [Recursive Harness Self-Improvement for Frontier Reasoning Data Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03548)
*Wenlong Zhang,Zhengbo Jiao,Chenxu Zhang,Lekang Jiang,SiYuan Ma,Qituan Zhang,Guo Chen,Linfeng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework that co-evolves both the task generator and its control harness to automatically create progressively harder reasoning problems, improving downstream model training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for generating reasoning problems typically reuse prior problems as seeds but keep the generation harness (prompts, workflows, skills) fixed, limiting how much task difficulty can grow. There is a need for an automated, scalable way to generate increasingly harder, high-quality reasoning data that remains solvable and useful for training large models.

Method: They introduce task-harness co-evolution, a recursive harness self-improvement (RSI) framework. It has two complementary schedules: (1) online self-improvement, which converts intermediate solver failures encountered during problem generation into reusable skills; and (2) post-task self-improvement, which periodically revises skills, prompts, and workflows after each batch of tasks, only adopting changes that produce harder but still valid tasks under a bounded cost increase. The underlying model weights and verification criteria are kept fixed. They evaluate on mathematics, coding, and science domains with ablation studies comparing different update schedules and fixed harness baselines.

Result: Over fourteen rounds of evolution, the generated tasks become much harder, as indicated by the mean solver accuracy dropping from 100.0% to 54.8%. Ablations show that combining online and post-task self-improvement produces harder tasks than either alone or than fixed-harness recursion. Training on the synthesized data improves downstream supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and GRPO performance. A 27B-parameter student model fine-tuned on 10K synthesized math problems reaches 62.5% mean-16 accuracy on the APEX benchmark, comparable to some frontier-model references chosen for comparison.

Conclusion: Allowing the task-generation harness to adapt—rather than only recycling tasks—enables automatic construction of increasingly challenging reasoning datasets with clear downstream benefits. Co-evolving prompts, skills, and workflows alongside tasks, while keeping model weights and verification fixed, yields harder problems and better-trained models, suggesting a promising direction for scalable reasoning data synthesis.

Abstract: Generating progressively harder reasoning problems requires synthesis procedures that adapt as the task distribution evolves. Existing task-level recursion reuses generated problems as seeds but leaves the construction harness unchanged. We present task-harness co-evolution, a framework for recursive harness self-improvement (RSI) in reasoning-data synthesis. Online self-improvement converts intermediate solver failures into reusable skills during generation. Post-task self-improvement revises skills, prompts, and workflows after each batch, adopting candidates only when they generate harder valid tasks within a bounded cost increase. Model weights and verification criteria remain fixed. Across mathematics, coding, and science, mean solver accuracy decreases from 100.0% to 54.8% over fourteen evolution rounds. Ablations show that combining both update schedules produces harder tasks than fixed-harness recursion or either schedule alone. The resulting data improves downstream SFT and GRPO performance. In particular, a 27B student fine-tuned on 10K synthesized mathematics examples achieves 62.5% mean-16 accuracy on APEX, competitive with selected frontier-model references. These results support adapting the synthesis harness alongside the tasks to generate increasingly challenging data with downstream training value.

</details>


### [177] [Knowledge or Calculator? Decomposing the Skill Premium in Verifiable Financial Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03564)
*Jermyn Zhen Yong Bek,Zhuang Qiang Bok,Zhongtian Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FinSkillBench, a benchmark to evaluate how well AI models perform realistic financial workflows, showing that curated external tools and documents significantly improve performance compared to ad‑hoc in‑context skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing financial AI benchmarks mostly test factual recall or simple question answering, which misses the core demands of real investment workflows: precise quantitative execution, correct use of complex procedures and tools, and transparent, auditable outputs. The authors aim to build an evaluation suite that reflects these requirements and to measure how much value is added when models are given structured skills, tools, and documentation rather than relying purely on base model capabilities or single‑episode prompt engineering.

Method: They design FinSkillBench: 2,603 time‑stamped episodes spanning 12 subtasks in portfolio construction, risk management, and fundamental analysis. Each task has regenerable hidden ground truth and deterministic verifiers for automatic grading. They evaluate 9 models under 3 resource conditions, running 17,820 episodes in total. They compare three settings: (1) base models, (2) models with curated skill packages (human‑authored procedures and tools), and (3) models with skills that are generated within a single episode (on‑the‑fly). They then decompose the performance gains (“skill premium”) by separately providing procedural documents and executable domain tools, and analyze performance across task types and multiple scoring variants, including robustness checks like cluster bootstrapping and a second, independently implemented evaluation harness.

Result: Curated skill packages substantially improve performance across models, increasing mean scores by about 16.2 percentage points (from 0.366 to 0.528). Skills that are generated within a single episode yield almost no benefit (+0.5 points) despite higher token and interaction costs. When decomposing curated skills, human‑authored procedural documents alone add roughly +5.6 points, executable tools alone add about +19.5 points, and using both together yields a subadditive gain. The benefits vary by workflow type: executable tools are most helpful for numerically heavy workflows, documentation is more impactful where procedure or schema guidance is the bottleneck, and interpretive tasks see gains from both. These patterns are consistent across 10 different scoring variants and validated with cluster bootstrap analyses and a separately built evaluation harness, though effect magnitudes vary with how tools and data are integrated.

Conclusion: FinSkillBench provides a rigorous, workflow‑faithful benchmark for evaluating financial AI agents on complex, auditable tasks. The study demonstrates that the “skill premium” in performance is largely a property of the combined system—model plus tools, documents, and evaluation harness—rather than the base model alone. Carefully curated external tools and procedural resources, especially executable domain tools, deliver large and reliable gains over relying on in‑context skill generation, and their value depends strongly on the type of financial workflow being executed.

Abstract: Financial AI agents must do more than retrieve facts: investment workflows require correct quantitative execution, reliable use of procedural resources, and auditable structured outputs. We introduce FinSkillBench, an evaluation suite of 2,603 point in time episodes across 12 subtasks in portfolio construction, risk management, and fundamental analysis, with hidden regenerable ground truth and task specific deterministic verifiers. Executing 17,820 episodes across 9 models and 3 resource conditions, the paired analysis across 8 models shows that curated skill packages raise mean scores by +16.2 points (0.366 to 0.528), whereas skills generated within a single episode add only +0.5 points while consuming more tokens and turns. We then decompose the curated premium by granting human authored procedural documents and executable domain tools separately: documents alone add +5.6 points, tools alone add +19.5 points, and their combination is subadditive. The premium is strongly workflow dependent: executable tools dominate numerically intensive workflows, documentation matters more when procedural or output schema guidance is the bottleneck, and interpretive tasks benefit from both. The effects are sign stable across 10 scoring variants and cluster bootstrap analyses, and an independently implemented second harness reproduces the directional pattern while showing that effect magnitudes depend on how tools and data are exposed. Overall, a measured "skill premium" is a property of the full model, resource, and harness system rather than of the underlying model alone.

</details>


### [178] [Learning to Assess Heartbeat Observability for mmWave Heart-Rate Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03570)
*Yuxuan Hu,Shilin Shan,Jianfei Yang,Feng Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose HEAR, a Transformer model that learns to assess when mmWave radar measurements contain a readable heartbeat and to estimate heart rate, trained purely on simulated data and transferring well to real datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contactless heart-rate sensing with mmWave radar is attractive, but in practice some radar measurements do not clearly contain the heartbeat signal due to coherent interference of scatterers. Existing methods typically estimate heart rate without a reliable, learned notion of when the underlying measurement actually supports a trustworthy estimate. There is a need for an automatic observability assessment so that systems can selectively trust or discard heart-rate estimates, improving reliability without manual tuning, and ideally trained without expensive labeled real-world data.

Method: They define heartbeat observability as how readable the heartbeat component is in the phase spectrum of an FMCW mmWave radar signal. To generate supervised training data over a wide range of observability conditions, they build a controllable multi-scatterer FMCW simulator. For each simulated measurement, they automatically label observability by checking whether the dominant spectral peak in the heartbeat band matches the known ground-truth heart rate. Using these simulated spectra, they train HEAR, a compact dual-task Transformer model that ingests spectral magnitudes together with frequency values expressed relative to the respiration fundamental (to provide respiratory-harmonic context). HEAR jointly predicts a continuous observability score and a heart-rate estimate. The learned observability score is then used as a confidence measure to select which predictions to trust, and can also be applied to outputs of existing heart-rate estimators. They evaluate zero-shot transfer of the simulator-trained HEAR on two public real-world mmWave datasets at 60 and 120 GHz without fine-tuning, and implement the pipeline on an edge device, measuring end-to-end latency.

Result: Trained solely on simulated multi-scatterer FMCW data, HEAR generalizes zero-shot to real-world mmWave datasets collected at 60 and 120 GHz from 134 subjects. The learned observability score effectively identifies reliable measurements: on the 120 GHz dataset, using score-based selection with the HEAR heart-rate head reduces mean absolute error from 17.9 BPM when using all samples to 1.6 BPM when retaining only the most observable 50% of measurements. The same observability score can be used to improve the reliability of multiple existing heart-rate estimators as well. Their implementation achieves an end-to-end processing latency of 50.8 ms on an edge device, indicating suitability for real-time applications.

Conclusion: Assessing heartbeat observability directly from mmWave radar phase spectra substantially improves the reliability of contactless heart-rate estimation. A simulator-driven approach can provide rich supervision for learning such an observability metric without requiring labeled real-world data. The proposed HEAR dual-task Transformer effectively learns both an observability score and heart rate, transfers zero-shot to real datasets at different carrier frequencies, and enables selective prediction that dramatically lowers heart-rate error at partial coverage while remaining efficient enough for edge deployment.

Abstract: Contactless heart-rate sensing with millimeter-wave (mmWave) radar requires assessing whether individual measurements support reliable estimation. We study learning to assess heartbeat observability, defined as the readability of the heartbeat component in an acquired phase spectrum, for selective heart-rate estimation. Coherent superposition of scatterer returns can suppress this component even under similar macroscopic observation geometry, motivating assessment directly from acquired measurements. To obtain training supervision across different observability conditions, we develop a controllable multi-scatterer frequency-modulated continuous-wave (FMCW) simulator. Agreement between the dominant heartbeat-band peak and the known heart rate provides an automatic observability label for each simulated measurement. We propose HEAR (Heartbeat Estimation with Assessed Reliability), a compact dual-task Transformer that jointly predicts an observability score and heart rate. Its input combines spectral magnitudes with frequencies relative to the respiration fundamental, providing context for respiratory harmonics. Trained solely on simulated observations, HEAR transfers zero-shot to two public real-world datasets collected at 60 and 120 GHz from 134 subjects. The same learned score supports selective prediction with both HEAR's own heart-rate head and multiple existing estimators. On the 120 GHz dataset, score-based selection reduces the HR head's mean absolute error from 17.9 BPM at full coverage to 1.6 BPM at 50% coverage. The complete pipeline achieves an end-to-end processing latency of 50.8 ms on an edge device. Project page: https://yuxuanhu9.github.io/HEAR/.

</details>


### [179] [HyperBrowseComp: A Multilingual and Multimodal Stress Test for Web-Browsing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03574)
*Alham Fikri Aji,Faiz Rizki Ramadhan,Zayd M. K. Zuhri,Seung Hun Eddie Han,Ryandito Diandaru,Qinrong Cui,Jan Christian Blaise Cruz,Badrinath Chandana,Peerawat Chomphooyod,Ahmed Attia,Jonibek Mansurov,Emilio Villa-Cueva,Canh Duong Nguyen,Imran Turganov,Minghao Wu,Peerat Limkonchotiwat,Irina Nikishina*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyperBrowseComp is a difficult multilingual, multimodal web-browsing benchmark that tests models’ ability to find and connect obscure evidence online rather than rely on memorized knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing browsing and QA benchmarks often allow models to answer using parametric knowledge or relatively simple retrieval, and they are mostly monolingual and text-only. There is a need for a benchmark that really stresses persistent web browsing, complex evidence discovery, and multilingual/multimodal reasoning, so that we can better evaluate and improve web-enabled AI agents.

Method: The authors construct HyperBrowseComp: 423 hard questions in 13 languages authored and validated by native or highly proficient speakers. Each question has a concise, verifiable answer but requires difficult evidence discovery: obscure web pages, multi-step clue following, and diverse modalities (videos, scanned documents, images, maps). They filter out easier questions by testing them on models without internet access to ensure they cannot be solved from parametric knowledge alone. They then evaluate multiple models using both provider-native search tools and a unified external retrieval system under a shared agent protocol, and they run human evaluations on a subset of questions for comparison.

Result: The paper finds that many questions in HyperBrowseComp remain very challenging for current models, even with access to web search and external retrieval. Model performance varies, and substantial effort (multiple steps, complex browsing) is required to solve questions. Human evaluators are used as a reference point, showing that the benchmark is solvable but still difficult, highlighting clear gaps between human and model browsing capabilities in multilingual and multimodal settings.

Conclusion: HyperBrowseComp serves as a rigorous benchmark for testing web-browsing agents on persistent information seeking across languages and modalities. Difficulty primarily stems from discovering and linking scattered, obscure evidence on the open web, rather than recalling memorized facts. This benchmark can drive development and comparison of more capable, search-aware, and robust multilingual and multimodal AI agents.

Abstract: We introduce HyperBrowseComp, a multilingual and multimodal browsing benchmark comprising 423 manually authored and human-validated questions across 13 languages, written by native or highly proficient speakers. Questions are designed to be extremely challenging. Each question targets a concise, publicly verifiable answer whose discovery requires locating obscure evidence, following multi-step clue chains, or inspecting heterogeneous sources such as videos, scanned documents, images, or maps. Easier questions are filtered out by evaluating them with models without internet access to reduce the likelihood that they can be answered with parametric knowledge alone. We evaluate several models using provider-native search and a shared external retrieval harness under a common agent protocol. To contextualize model performance and effort, we also conduct a human evaluation on a sample of the questions. HyperBrowseComp provides a challenging testbed for persistent information seeking across languages and evidence modalities, with difficulty arising from discovering and connecting evidence on the open web.

</details>


### [180] [HazardWeaver: Scientific Route Selection for Hazard Analysis Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03591)
*Wangshu Zhu,Xueqi Cheng,Liang Wu,Yushun Dong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HazardWeaver is an AI agent framework that dynamically selects and executes scientific workflows for natural hazard analysis, adapting its choices as new evidence arrives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural hazard analysis requires combining diverse scientific data, models, and tools, but current AI agents struggle to decide which methods are scientifically appropriate and actually executable with the data and tools available, especially as the situation evolves. The paper aims to enable reliable, automated decision-making about which scientific workflows to run for a given hazard scenario.

Method: The authors formulate the task as state-dependent scientific route selection. They build: (1) a Hazard Knowledge Compiler to extract evidence-linked conditions that define when scientific methods are applicable; (2) a Hazard Capability Graph that encodes executable capabilities and input–output compatibility; and (3) the Hazard Weaver Agent that uses these structures to select, execute, and iteratively revise scientific routes as analysis state changes. They also construct the Hazard Weaver Benchmark with 141 instances covering seven single-hazard domains and four multi-hazard interaction classes, designed to support multiple valid scientific routes and to evaluate correctness, route validity, and justified abstention.

Result: On the Hazard Weaver Benchmark, HazardWeaver consistently outperforms existing AI agent systems, with especially large performance gains on tasks where multiple scientific routes are possible. The framework more reliably identifies valid, executable workflows and produces more accurate scientific outputs, while also better recognizing when it should abstain from making a decision.

Conclusion: State-dependent route selection grounded in explicit applicability conditions and capability graphs enables more robust and flexible AI agents for natural hazard analysis.
