HazardWeaver shows that encoding both scientific preconditions and tool I/O structure improves workflow selection, execution, and revision over prior agents, particularly in complex, multi-route settings, and the new benchmark provides a standardized way to evaluate such systems.

Abstract: Understanding and assessing natural hazards is essential for disaster preparedness and risk reduction. Recent advances in large language models have spurred growing interest in AI agents for hazard analysis, particularly their ability to integrate scientific data, models, and tools into automated workflows. However, effective automation requires agents to determine which scientific methods are appropriate for a given event and executable with the available data and tools. As new evidence and execution results become available, these conditions can change, requiring agents to reconsider their choices. We formulate this problem as state-dependent scientific route selection and introduce HazardWeaver. Specifically, HazardWeaver first leverages the Hazard Knowledge Compiler to extract evidence-linked conditions governing scientific applicability, then its Hazard Capability Graph represents executable scientific capabilities and checks compatibility between their inputs and outputs. Using these complementary representations, the Hazard Weaver Agent component selects applicable and executable routes, carries out their workflows, and revises its decisions as the analysis state changes. To evaluate both the scientific outputs and the decisions that produce them, we introduce the Hazard Weaver Benchmark, comprising 141 instances across seven single-hazard domains and four multi-hazard interaction classes. The benchmark accommodates multiple valid scientific routes and evaluates output correctness, route validity, and justified abstention. Extensive experiments on this benchmark show that HazardWeaver outperforms existing agent systems, with the largest gains on tasks with multiple eligible scientific routes. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/LabRAI/HazardWeaver.

</details>


### [181] [Low-Cost Video--Time Priors as a Strong Baseline for EEG--fNIRS Emotion Regression on Familiar Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03618)
*Minghao Kong,Jiurun Chen,Ying Gao,Xiangbin Meng,Rongjie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies continuous emotion regression during video watching and finds that a simple video-time prior is already a very strong baseline, while EEG-fNIRS only adds modest, participant- and video-specific residual improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether complex physiological signals like EEG and fNIRS meaningfully improve continuous emotion prediction (valence and arousal) when viewers watch known videos, beyond what can already be inferred from knowing which video is being watched and the time within the video.

Method: They perform continuous valence/arousal regression on video stimuli, comparing models built from participant-specific responses and a strong prior based on video identity and within-video time. They fuse EEG-fNIRS with this prior and conduct five-fold subject-held-out evaluation on 24 participants, including internal and external evaluations, plus source-explicit ablations to quantify contributions from video-time versus physiology.

Result: The fused model with EEG-fNIRS performs best, but the prior based solely on video identity and time within the video nearly matches it, within 0.05 MAE on internal data and 0.32 MAE on external data. Ablations indicate that most performance gains come from video identity and within-video time, with EEG-fNIRS contributing smaller, heterogeneous gains depending on participant and video.

Conclusion: For familiar videos, a video-time prior provides a strong, low-cost baseline for continuous emotion regression. EEG-fNIRS can be treated as an optional residual signal that sometimes improves predictions but offers limited and inconsistent advantage over the video-time prior alone.

Abstract: Continuous emotion regression estimates moment-to-moment valence and arousal while a viewer watches a video. In familiar-video deployment, responses fron training participant-specific estimate, and prior-dominating fixed fusion tests whether physiology adds residual correction. In five-fold subject-held-out evaluation on 24was within 0.05 and 0.32 MAE of fusion in the internal and external evaluations, respectively. Source-explicit ablations showed that video identity and within-video tine accounted for most of the reduction, while EG-FNIRS gains were smaller and varied across participants and videos. These results identify the video-time prior as a strong, low-cost baseline and position EEG-fNIRS as an optional residual signal for familiar-video emotion regression.

</details>


### [182] [Depth as Time in One-Step Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03626)
*Arnold Caleb Asiimwe,William Yang,Sanghyuk Chun,Esin Tureci,Olga Russakovsky*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper empirically studies how multi-step diffusion denoising trajectories are reorganized inside modern one-step generative models and shows that temporal diffusion behavior reappears as a depthwise computation, enabling strong model compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: One-step generative models (via distillation or learned flows) now match the quality of multi-step diffusion, but it is unclear what happens to the original denoising trajectory when many diffusion steps are compressed into a single forward pass. Understanding this is important both for theory (how diffusion computation is represented) and practice (how to further compress or modify such models).

Method: The authors probe intermediate layers of various one-step image generators by decoding features at each depth using the model’s own output head. They compare models trained with explicit time-indexed transport tasks (e.g., MeanFlow) and those trained without such temporal structure (e.g., drifting models). They analyze the evolution of images across depth to characterize denoising/renoising dynamics and experiment with treating layerwise computation as a flow, training a single time-conditioned block to reproduce the depthwise behavior and compress the model.

Result: They find a “depth as time” phenomenon: in one-step models derived from diffusion or trained with time-indexed transport (like MeanFlow), the stepwise denoising trajectory of diffusion reappears as a progression across network depth. Probing shorter transport intervals in MeanFlow reveals both denoising and subsequent renoising within one forward pass. Models without an explicit temporal transport objective do not show this structured depthwise denoising. Exploiting this structure, they successfully compress a MeanFlow SiT-L/2 model by 16.6× in parameters into a single time-conditioned block that still reproduces the denoising across layers.

Conclusion: Temporal diffusion computation is not removed by one-step generation but reorganized along network depth. When models are trained with explicit transport time, their internal layerwise dynamics mirror the denoising trajectory, making them more amenable to aggressive compression via time-conditioned blocks that model the depthwise flow.

Abstract: The recent wave of one-step generative models, which compress the multi-step trajectory of diffusion via either distillation or learned flow maps, has reached an inflection point where they can generate high-quality images. Here, we ask a natural question that follows from these advances: what happens to the denoising trajectory of multi-step diffusion when generation is compressed into a single forward pass? We offer an empirical observation we call \textit{depth as time}: the denoising computation that multi-step diffusion performs across sampling steps appears to unfold across the depth of a single forward pass, and can be recovered by decoding intermediate layers with the model's own output head. Most interestingly, we show that this depthwise computation depends on the transport task a flow map is trained to solve. The most surprising case is MeanFlow, where probing shorter transport intervals reveals both denoising and renoising within a single network evaluation. In contrast, generators trained without a time-indexed transport task, such as drifting models, do not exhibit the same depthwise denoising. Consequently, we show that models that exhibit the depthwise denoising phenomenon are more compressible across the layerwise computation: a MeanFlow \texttt{SiT-L/2} model can be compressed by $16.6\times$ in parameters into a single time-conditioned block. We offer an explanation for this denoise-then-renoise behavior and show that, when we treat the layerwise computation explicitly as a flow, a single time-conditioned block can be trained to denoise across layers, compressing a MeanFlow \texttt{SiT-L/2} model by $16.6\times$ in parameters. Together, these results suggest that the temporal computation of diffusion is not eliminated by one-step generation, but reorganized across network depth.

</details>


### [183] [NeutronGym: Physics-Graded Neutron Instrument Design for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03631)
*Lijie Ding,Changwoo Do*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents NeutronGym, an executable benchmark and training environment to test whether language-model agents can design neutron scattering instruments using real physics simulations rather than memorization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM evaluations in scientific domains mostly test factual recall or shallow reasoning, and often rely on subjective LLM-based grading. The authors want a physics-grounded, automatic, and unambiguous way to assess whether agents can actually perform scientific design tasks—in this case, neutron instrument design—by interacting with realistic simulators. They also want to separate genuine design/generalization ability from memorization of known instruments or formulas.

Method: They build NeutronGym, an environment where agents specify design parameters of neutron instruments. The environment validates designs, then uses the McStas ray-tracing simulator to evaluate performance. A multi-level grading ladder scores four aspects: syntax (is the design valid), runtime (does it run), structure (is the configuration plausible), and science (does it achieve physical performance goals), all without LLM judges. They construct procedural task families that share a fixed layout but require setting continuous design parameters, with held-out parameter regimes to test generalization. They also build McStasBench, a curated subset of 16 tasks derived from published instruments, along with memorization probes and a sandbox. They benchmark seven LLMs and then apply reinforcement learning on NeutronGym rewards to train Qwen3-8B on specific task families, comparing against larger models and classical optimizers.

Result: On the 16 curated McStasBench tasks from real instruments, seven tested models solve at most 7/16 tasks; none can simply retrieve a reference design nor reach predefined improvement targets. In contrast, reinforcement learning on NeutronGym rewards dramatically improves Qwen3-8B: it goes from 11% to 77% success on held-out instances of a task family derived from a hidden reference design (69% on a second random seed), surpassing an untrained Qwen3-32B. Similar gains are observed on three additional gated task families (one seed each). Analysis shows that partial-credit grading is crucial: removing the ladder and giving only sparse success/failure reward reduces performance by about 60 percentage points. The RL-trained agent matches what a classical optimizer achieves under the same simulation budget when that optimizer is given closed-form physics (77% vs 81% success, a small gap at this scale), while top frontier LLMs still achieve 98–99% when given that extra knowledge. During development they identify and reject four task designs that non-LLM baselines could solve but for which the evaluation was not trustworthy, and they release the diagnostic probes used to detect these flaws.

Conclusion: NeutronGym provides a rigorous, physics-based environment for evaluating and training language-model agents on scientific instrument design, avoiding LLM-as-judge subjectivity and minimizing dependence on memorization. Current off-the-shelf models perform poorly on realistic neutron instrument tasks, but reinforcement learning on carefully shaped, ladder-based rewards can train a mid-sized model to match the performance of classical optimizers operating with full closed-form physics knowledge, under the same computational constraints. The framework thus offers a scalable path to scientifically grounded benchmarks and training regimes, while the accompanying probes and rejected tasks illustrate how to audit and refine such environments to ensure trustworthy evaluation.

Abstract: Designing a scientific instrument tests whether language-model agents can do physics rather than recall it, provided the grading cannot be argued with. We introduce NeutronGym, to our knowledge the first executable environment for neutron instrument design: agents build instruments through validating tools, McStas ray-traces what they build, and a level-resolved ladder grades syntax, runtime, structure and science with no LLM judge. Procedural families supply unlimited instances of a fixed layout whose design parameters the agent must set, with held-out parameter regimes; a curated slice, McStasBench, adds 16 tasks from published instruments behind memorization probes and a sandbox. Seven models reproduce at most 7 of the 16, none retrieves a reference, and none meets an improvement target. The environment also trains. Reinforcement learning on its reward takes Qwen3-8B from 11% to 77% of held-out instances of a family whose targets come from a hidden design (69% at a second seed), past an untrained Qwen3-32B, and the recipe holds, at one seed each, on three further gated families. The analysis says what that gain is. Without the ladder's partial credit it collapses by 60 points. From reward alone the trained model reaches what a classical optimizer reaches, at the agent's simulation budget, only when handed the closed-form physics (77% against 81%, a gap that does not separate at this size), while frontier models still solve 98-99%. Getting a trustworthy result meant failing four task designs that no-model baselines could solve, and we release the probes that found them.

</details>


### [184] [Credit Where It Matters: Dependency-Aware Policy Optimization for Terminal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03634)
*Yu Li,Guangfeng Cai,Long-Fei Li,Shuo Han,Shengtian Yang,Han Luo,Kaibing Yang,Lei Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose DepGPO, a dependency-aware credit assignment method for RL-based terminal agents that improves learning by only rewarding steps that causally affect task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-step terminal tasks like coding and debugging, successful outcomes depend on a chain of earlier commands. Existing RL credit assignment either spreads reward across the whole trajectory or each step independently, without explicitly using which commands actually read or write the resources that matter for final success. This can reward irrelevant actions and dilute the learning signal. The authors want a more precise way to attribute credit based on true execution dependencies.

Method: They introduce Dependency-Aware Group Policy Optimization (DepGPO). From each terminal execution trace, they build a command dependency graph where nodes are commands and edges encode read-write relationships over resources (files, outputs, etc.). Starting from the resources checked by a task verifier, they trace backward along this graph to find the commands that wrote or read the influential resources. They then mark these commands as relevant and redistribute the trajectory-level advantage only across these steps, effectively reweighting or grouping updates during policy optimization based on dependency paths.

Result: Across a variety of complex, multi-step terminal tasks (e.g., coding and debugging scenarios), experiments and ablation studies show that DepGPO yields higher task success rates and more stable training compared to standard trajectory-level and step-level baselines that lack explicit dependency-aware credit assignment.

Conclusion: Exploiting execution-time read-write dependencies between terminal commands enables more accurate credit assignment in RL for terminal agents. By focusing learning signals on commands that causally affect the verifier-inspected resources, DepGPO improves both performance and training stability on complex terminal tasks, suggesting that structured dependency information is a valuable signal for optimizing multi-step tool-using agents.

Abstract: Terminal-using agents benefit from reinforcement learning (RL) in coding, debugging, and other multi-step terminal tasks. In these tasks, later commands often depend on information or intermediate results produced by earlier commands. However, existing trajectory-level and step-level credit assignment methods do not explicitly trace the read-write dependencies through which commands affect the final outcome. Consequently, training signals could still be assigned to irrelevant operations, weakening learning from relevant steps. In this paper, we propose Dependency-Aware Group Policy Optimization (DepGPO), which uses execution dependencies between commands to guide credit assignment for terminal agents. Specifically, we construct a command dependency graph from execution traces and trace backward from the resources inspected by the task verifier. We then assign credit to relevant writes and their supporting reads along these paths, and use it to redistribute trajectory advantages across steps. Extensive comparative experiments and ablation studies demonstrate that DepGPO improves task performance and training stability on complex terminal tasks.

</details>


### [185] [Do Large Language Models Know Colombian Law? A Reliability Benchmark for the Colombian Legal System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03639)
*Rubén Manrique,Michelle Castellanos,Jorge Morales,Juan David Gutiérrez,Antonio Barreto Rozo,Joaquín Vélez Navarro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They build and release an expert-validated benchmark to test how reliable LLMs are on Colombian law, finding that models look good on multiple-choice but are often wrong on free-text legal reasoning and need expert oversight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are rapidly entering legal practice and education worldwide, but nearly all systematic evaluation focuses on U.S. law. For a national system like Colombia’s, stakeholders lack evidence on whether models are accurate, what tasks they handle well or poorly, and how apparent fluency relates to actual legal correctness. This gap is risky for non-expert users and for institutions considering LLM deployment. The authors aim to create a rigorous, domain-specific benchmark and use it to characterize LLM performance and failure modes in Colombian law.

Method: They design a 1,042-item benchmark covering ten Colombian legal domains and three task types: closed multiple-choice questions, semi-open questions, and open-ended IRAC-style prompts. Items are created via a human-in-the-loop pipeline with several rounds of expert review to ensure validity. They then evaluate 15 leading proprietary and open-weight LLMs, using metrics matched to format (e.g., accuracy for multiple-choice, graded factual correctness and relevance for free-text). They compute correlations between metrics (e.g., relevancy vs correctness, closed vs free-text performance), and employ both blind human experts and an independent rubric-based LLM judge to score and cross-validate free-text responses, including checking the validity of cited legal norms and analyzing performance variation across legal areas and question complexity.

Result: On closed multiple-choice questions, models span a relatively high but variable accuracy range (about 0.58 to 0.91, with Gemini 3.1 Pro best). However, on open-ended legal answers, no model surpasses 0.45 factual correctness. Relevance and correctness are negatively correlated (Spearman rho = -0.46): answers often appear on-topic but are wrong. Yet the rankings of models by closed-question accuracy and free-text correctness are strongly correlated (rho = 0.94), meaning multiple-choice results predict which models are better but exaggerate their absolute reliability. Both human experts and the rubric-based LLM judge reproduce similar model rankings on free-text tasks (rho ≥ 0.88). The judge also shows that only about half of the legal norms cited by models are correct, with many references being incorrect or fabricated. Reliability differs across legal areas and follows an inverted-U pattern as question complexity increases.

Conclusion: State-of-the-art LLMs are not yet reliably trustworthy for Colombian legal work without expert oversight, especially on open-ended reasoning and citation-heavy tasks. Multiple-choice benchmarks can approximate relative model ranking but mask substantial accuracy problems in realistic free-text use. The frequent production of plausible but incorrect legal arguments and citations poses particular risks for non-experts. Improving grounding in authoritative legal sources, and using rigorous, system-specific benchmarks like the one they release, are key paths toward safer and more reliable deployment of LLMs in national legal systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to support legal practice, education, and research, yet their reliability in national legal systems outside the United States remains largely undocumented. We introduce an expert-validated benchmark for evaluating LLM reliability on the Colombian legal system. The benchmark comprises 1,042 items spanning ten areas of law and three question formats (closed multiple-choice, semi-open, and open-ended IRAC), built through a human-in-the-loop pipeline with multi-stage expert review. We evaluate 15 contemporary proprietary and open-weight models with format-appropriate metrics. Accuracy on closed questions ranges widely, from 0.905 (Gemini 3.1 Pro) to 0.577, but on free-text legal answers factual correctness never exceeds 0.45 (on a 0-1 scale) for any model. We find a dissociation between answer relevancy and correctness (Spearman rho = -0.46): models reliably sound responsive while frequently being wrong, a pattern of particular concern for non-expert users. Closed-question accuracy and free-text correctness are strongly rank-correlated (rho = 0.94), so cheap multiple-choice screening predicts model ranking but overstates absolute reliability. An independent rubric-based LLM judge and blind human expert scoring both reproduce the free-text ranking (rho >= 0.88). The judge further reveals that only about half of the norms models cite are correct; the rest are wrong or non-existent. Reliability varies systematically by legal area and follows an inverted-U across question complexity. Our results indicate that current LLMs require expert supervision for Colombian legal tasks, and that grounding answers in authoritative sources is a promising path to higher reliability. We release the benchmark construction pipeline to support reproducible evaluation.

</details>


### [186] [MRVQ: One Resident Index for Dimension- and Rate-Elastic Vector Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03651)
*Sean Culatana,Shang-En Huang,Kang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Matryoshka Residual Vector Quantization (MRVQ), a post-hoc quantization method for frozen dense-retrieval embeddings that supports multiple bitrate and dimensionality configurations from a single index, greatly reducing memory while maintaining competitive retrieval quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense-retrieval systems must adapt to changing latency, quality, and memory constraints by varying embedding dimensionality and index bit rate. Traditional approaches train a separate quantizer for each operating point, leading to multiple code streams and quantizer states co-existing in memory, which is expensive. The authors aim to design a single, compact quantization scheme that can flexibly support many (dimension, bitrate) configurations without retraining or maintaining multiple indices, thereby improving memory efficiency for elastic retrieval services.

Method: They propose Matryoshka Residual Vector Quantization (MRVQ), a residual quantizer applied post-hoc to frozen embeddings. MRVQ’s maximum-rate code is structured so that it can be truncated along two axes: (1) by dropping later residual stages to reduce bitrate, and (2) by dropping trailing embedding coordinates to reduce dimensionality. This yields a ‘matryoshka’ structure where one stored artifact can serve multiple bitrate-dimension combinations. The method is evaluated on FiQA and NFCorpus across four embedding families and code sizes of 4, 8, and 16 bytes, against baselines including per-rate QINCo2, product quantization (PQ), optimized PQ (OPQ), and AdANNS-OPQ. They also design and test a low-build-cost PCA-scalar quantization baseline and examine two hypotheses: training QINCo2 at high rates and a ranking-bound hypothesis.

Result: MRVQ achieves the lowest RAM usage among all evaluated designs across both datasets and all tested settings, using 17.8–22.0× less memory than three separately trained QINCo2 indices and 1.89–2.02× less than a lean shared-model steelman while serving all (dimension, rate) pairs from a single artifact. In terms of retrieval quality, per-rate QINCo2 remains better, outperforming MRVQ on FiQA by 0.026–0.107 nDCG@10. However, at matched code sizes, MRVQ outperforms PQ, OPQ, and AdANNS-OPQ. The PCA-scalar design matches the quality of RaBitQ and its extension while being 420× faster to fit at the median. They also find that QINCo2 training collapses at high bit rates and that a tested ranking-bound hypothesis fails its pre-defined acceptance criteria.

Conclusion: MRVQ offers a highly memory-efficient operating point for elastic dense-retrieval: a single post-hoc residual quantizer over frozen embeddings that flexibly supports multiple bitrate and dimensionality configurations. It does not universally maximize retrieval quality—per-rate QINCo2 can achieve higher nDCG—but MRVQ strikes a strong balance between memory usage and accuracy, outperforming traditional PQ-style methods at the same code size. The additional experiments show that a very cheap PCA-scalar baseline can be competitive with more complex methods and that some seemingly promising ideas (high-rate QINCo2 training and a ranking-bound hypothesis) do not pan out, clarifying the trade-off landscape. Overall, MRVQ is positioned as a practical, low-memory design choice rather than a universal quality champion.

Abstract: Dense-retrieval services must switch among embedding-prefix dimensions and index bit rates as latency, quality, and memory budgets change. Tuning a quantizer separately for each rate gives the best quality, but the retrieval tier then holds several code streams and quantizer states at once. We introduce Matryoshka Residual Vector Quantization (MRVQ), a post-hoc residual quantizer for frozen embeddings. Its maximum-rate code can be truncated two ways: dropping residual stages lowers the rate, and dropping embedding coordinates lowers the dimension. One resident artifact therefore serves every (dimension, rate) pair we evaluate. Across FiQA and NFCorpus, four embedding families, and {4, 8, 16}-byte codes, MRVQ is the lowest-RAM design we evaluate. It uses 17.8-22.0x less memory than three separately trained QINCo2 indices, and 1.89-2.02x less than a lean shared-model steelman. The saving is not free: per-rate QINCo2 is 0.026-0.107 nDCG@10 better on FiQA. But MRVQ beats PQ, OPQ, and AdANNS-OPQ at matched code size. We also evaluate a low-build-cost PCA-scalar design that attains quality comparable to RaBitQ and its extension while fitting 420x faster at the median. Finally, we report two negative results: QINCo2 collapses when trained at high rates, and a ranking-bound hypothesis misses its pre-specified acceptance criteria. MRVQ is therefore a low-memory operating point for elastic retrieval, not a universal quality winner.

</details>


### [187] [Transcriptome-informed multi-modal AI for predicting neoadjuvant therapy response from breast cancer biopsies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03693)
*Jungkyu Park,Dhruva Biswas,Joseph Cappadona,Cerise Tang,Ken G. Zeng,Bartosz Machura,Chuwen Liu,Paolo Tarantino,Coral Omene,Francisco J. Esteva,Rohit Bhargava,Marcin Braun,Kamila Paździerz,Jakub Czerwiński,Hanna Romańska-Knight,Albert Grinshpun,Bareket Daniel,Michele Buchinger,Frederick Howard,Piotr Wysocki,Brie Chun,Freya Schnabel,Rich Caruana,Jan Witowski,Krzysztof J. Geras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a two-stage AI model that first learns gene expression from pathology images and then predicts breast cancer treatment response, achieving strong and robust performance even with limited labeled data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Developing deep learning biomarkers in oncology is hindered by the scarcity of labeled outcome data and the cost/limited availability of genomic assays. The authors aim to exploit large cross-cancer histopathology datasets to learn transcriptomic representations, so that accurate treatment response prediction is possible with fewer task-specific labels and without relying on expensive gene panels.

Method: Stage 1: Train a model on histopathology images from 8,742 patients across 32 cancer types to infer whole-transcriptome gene expression, validating this by pathologist review and spatial agreement with measured expression. Stage 2: Use the inferred gene expression plus clinical variables as inputs to a downstream model that predicts pathological complete response (pCR) to neoadjuvant therapy in breast cancer. The model is developed on 1,080 patients from five cohorts and evaluated on 1,412 patients from nine independent cohorts. Ablation studies compare against models using only clinical variables, conventional one-stage pathology models, and histopathological biomarkers.

Result: The two-stage model attains a pooled AUROC of 0.79 (95% CI 0.73–0.85) for predicting pCR, and can discriminate responders within established molecular subtypes. It outperforms existing histopathological biomarkers, is robust to differences in intratumoral sampling, and maintains performance even with minimal biopsy tissue. Ablation experiments show that transcriptome-wide inference yields better discrimination than models using only clinical variables or one-stage pathology, and improves robustness by avoiding the gene selection biases inherent in fixed genomic panels.

Conclusion: Learning a transcriptome-level representation from large, heterogeneous histopathology datasets and then using it for downstream prediction effectively compresses biologically relevant information. This “biologically informed compression” strategy enables accurate and robust treatment-response prediction in settings with limited labeled data and without reliance on predefined gene panels, suggesting it may generalize to other data-sparse precision oncology tasks.

Abstract: Scarcity of labeled data limits development of deep learning biomarkers in oncology. We develop a two-stage AI model predicting pathological complete response (pCR) to neoadjuvant therapy in breast cancer. The first stage learns the transcriptome from histopathology using 8,742 patients across 32 cancer types, corroborated by pathologist review and spatial agreement with measured expression. This simplifies the second stage to predicting pCR from inferred expression and clinical variables. Developed using 1,080 patients (five cohorts) and evaluated in 1,412 patients (nine cohorts), the model achieves a pooled AUROC of 0.79 (95% CI, 0.73-0.85), discriminating responders within molecular subtypes. It outperforms histopathological biomarkers, remaining stable across intratumoral sampling and with minimal biopsy tissue. Ablations show transcriptome-wide inference improves discrimination over clinical variables alone or one-stage pathology models, and robustness by avoiding genomic assays' gene selection constraints. These results indicate that biologically informed compression may generalize to data-sparse applications in precision oncology.

</details>
